# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 32

## Page 15607

PART THIRTY-TWO
THE BOOK OF MARRIAGE

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

All praise belongs to Allah alone, and may blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

The shaykh and imam, the learned and eminent scholar, Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked about someone who had been struck by one of Iblīs’s poisoned arrows.

He replied:

Anyone suffering from a poisoned wound must use an antidote and an ointment to draw out the poison and heal the wound. This can be achieved through several measures:

Among them is to marry or take a slave woman as a concubine. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When one of you looks at a woman’s beauty, let him have relations with his wife, for she has what that woman has.”

This helps diminish desire and weaken infatuation.

Second: He should consistently observe the five daily prayers, supplicate, and humbly beseech Allah in the hours before dawn. His prayer should be offered with an attentive heart and humble devotion. He should frequently supplicate, saying:

“O Turner of hearts, keep my heart steadfast upon Your religion. O Director of hearts, direct my heart toward obedience to You and obedience to Your Messenger.”

If he persists in supplication and humble entreaty to Allah, his heart will be turned away from that attachment, as Allah Most High said:

“Thus it was, that We might turn evil and indecency away from him. Indeed, he was one of Our chosen servants.”

---

## Page 15608

Third: He should keep away from that person’s home and avoid meeting those who associate with him, so that he hears no news of him and sees neither him nor any trace of him. Distance breeds estrangement, and the less someone is remembered, the weaker his influence on the heart becomes. Let him take these measures and report any changes in his condition. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about an unmarried man who longs to marry but fears taking on obligations toward a wife that he cannot fulfill. He has pledged to Allah not to ask anyone for anything for himself that would leave him beholden to them, yet he greatly desires marriage. Does he sin by remaining unmarried or not?

He replied:

It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Young men, whoever among you can afford to marry should do so, for it is more effective in lowering the gaze and safeguarding chastity. Whoever cannot should fast, for fasting restrains his desire.”

“The ability to marry” means being able to meet its expenses, not being physically capable of intercourse. The hadith addresses those who are physically capable of intercourse; this is why he instructed those who could not afford marriage to fast, since fasting restrains their desire.

As for someone who has no money, is it recommended that he borrow and marry? There is disagreement on this within the school of Imam Ahmad and elsewhere. Allah Most High has said:

“Let those who cannot find the means to marry remain chaste until Allah enriches them out of His bounty.”

As for a “righteous man,” he is one who fulfills the rights that Allah and His servants have over him.

---

## Page 15609

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who had proposed marriage, after which another man made a competing proposal: Is this permissible?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is not lawful for a man to make a competing marriage proposal over his brother’s proposal, nor to bargain over a purchase his brother is negotiating.”

Accordingly, the four imams, in their explicitly recorded statements, and other imams agreed that this is forbidden. They differed only over the validity of the second man’s marriage, holding two views.

The first is that it is invalid. This is the view of Mālik and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him.

The other is that it is valid. This is the view of Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad according to the other report, on the grounds that the prohibition concerns what preceded the marriage contract, namely the proposal.

Those who deemed it invalid argued that this entails, with even greater reason, a prohibition of the marriage contract itself. There is no disagreement among these imams that whoever does this disobeys Allah and His Messenger, although some of their followers disputed this. Knowingly persisting in sin undermines a man’s religious integrity, moral probity, and fitness to exercise authority over Muslims.

---

## Page 15610

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman separated from her husband, and another man proposed to her during her waiting period and is supporting her financially. Is this permissible or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Explicitly proposing marriage to a woman during her waiting period is impermissible, even if she is observing the waiting period following her husband’s death, by the consensus of the Muslims. How much more so if her waiting period follows divorce! Whoever does this deserves a punishment that deters him and others like him. Both the suitor and the woman are to be punished, and he is to be deterred from marrying her, as a punishment that thwarts his intended purpose. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man divorced his wife three times. She completed her waiting period in his home and then left. After completing her waiting period, she married another man and was divorced that same day. Her former husband did not learn of this until the following day. May he agree with her that he will take her back in marriage once she has completed her waiting period?

He replied:

During her waiting period following divorce from another man, he may neither propose to her nor support her financially with a view to marrying her. If the divorce is revocable, he may not even hint at a proposal. If it is irrevocable, there is disagreement over whether hinting at a proposal is permissible.

This applies if she entered into a marriage out of a genuine desire for married life. If, however, she entered into a marriage intended to make her lawful again for her former husband:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed the man who marries a woman to make her lawful again for her former husband, and the man for whose benefit this is done.”

---

## Page 15611

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man proposed to the daughter of a man known for his uprightness and agreed with him on the dowry, part payable immediately and part deferred. He delivered the immediate portion to her father four years ago and has continued to provide them with financial support, though nothing had been put in writing between them. Then another man proposed to her, offered a larger dowry, and prevented the first husband from proceeding?

He replied:

It is not permissible for a man to propose marriage to a woman whose proposal from his brother has been accepted and whose family has settled on him. The leading scholars agree on this, as it is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is not permissible for a man to propose marriage to a woman to whom his brother has already proposed.”

Whoever does this, and whoever assists him, must receive a punishment that deters them and others like them from doing so. Is the second man’s marriage valid or invalid? Scholars hold two views on this within the schools of Mālik, Ahmad, and others.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man visits his brother’s wife and the daughters of his paternal and maternal uncles. Is this permissible for him or not?

He replied:

He may not be alone in seclusion with any of them. However, if he enters with someone else, without seclusion or grounds for suspicion, that is permissible for him. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15612

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man entered into a marriage arrangement with a young woman. For years he supported her financially and made payments to her. When he resolved to consummate the marriage, he discovered that her father had married her to someone else. What is the ruling?

He replied:

It is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A Muslim is the brother of another Muslim. It is not permissible for a Muslim to propose marriage to a woman to whom his brother has already proposed, to supplant his brother in negotiations for a purchase, or to sell over his brother’s sale.”

If a man proposes marriage to a woman and the person responsible for contracting her marriage—such as a father with binding authority in marriage—has accepted him, no one else may propose to her. How much more serious, then, is the case when they have accepted him, had witnesses attest to the preliminary marriage arrangement preceding the contract, received gifts from him, and allowed a long time to pass! These people have undoubtedly committed a prohibited act for which they deserve punishment. But is the second marriage contract valid or void? The scholars hold two views:

The first—which is one of the two views within the schools of Mālik and Ahmad—is that the second man’s marriage contract is void. She must therefore be separated from him and returned to the first man.

The second is that the marriage is valid. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī. Those who committed the prohibited act are to be punished, and everything taken from the first man must be returned to him.

The first view is more in keeping with the Quran and Sunnah.

---

## Page 15613

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man divorced his wife three times. They have two children, and she has continued living in his house for years, with each seeing the other. Is the food she receives from him lawful for her to eat? Does he have any authority over her?

He replied:

A woman divorced three times is, in relation to the man, like any other woman who is neither his wife nor an unmarriageable relative. He may not be alone with her, just as he may not be alone with any such woman. Nor may he look at any part of her that he may not look at in such a woman. He has no authority over her whatsoever. He may not arrange with her for her to marry another man, then obtain a divorce from him and return to her first husband. Nor may he give her money to spend on that.

Indeed, even if she married another man in an ordinary marriage of the kind customary among Muslims, and her husband then died or divorced her three times, the first husband would not be permitted to propose marriage to her explicitly during her waiting period. This is agreed upon by the Muslims. As Allah Most High says:

“There is no blame upon you for hinting at a proposal of marriage to women or for concealing it within yourselves. Allah knows that you will think of them, but do not make secret promises to them.”

He also forbade him to resolve upon the marriage contract until the prescribed term reaches its end—that is, until the waiting period is over.

If He has forbidden such promises and resolve during the waiting period, how much more so while she is still married to her husband? And how much more so when the man has not yet married her, and a mutual agreement is made

---

## Page 15614

that she will marry him, then divorce him, and the man who made the arrangement will marry her.

This is forbidden by agreement of the Muslims, whether a marriage intended to make her lawful again for her former husband is held to be valid or not. They have not disputed that it is impermissible to propose marriage explicitly to a woman observing a waiting period from another man, a woman married to another man, or a woman whom the proposer has divorced three times. Anyone who does so deserves punishment in this world and the Hereafter, by agreement of the imams.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man speaks in a manner resembling women’s speech and is a “tinjīr.” Is it permissible for him to enter women’s quarters? What is the ruling concerning him?

He replied:

Rather, a man like this must be banished and expelled; he must not live among either men or women. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, banished an effeminate man, ordered that effeminate men be banished, and said:

“Expel them from your homes.”

Yet that man was not a tinjīr; how much more, then, does this apply to a tinjīr! Al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and others explicitly stated this.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section: On the bonds between Allah and His servants, and among the servants themselves: those arising from creation and those acquired; those established by divine law and those established by stipulation.

Allah Most High says:

“O mankind, fear your Lord, who created you from a single soul and created from it its mate, and spread from the two many men and women. Fear Allah, in whose name you ask one another, and be mindful of the ties of kinship. Surely Allah is ever Watchful over you.”

He opened the surah by mentioning the creation of humankind from a single soul, that its mate was created from it, and that He spread men and women from the two—the most complete and exalted of bonds. Then

---

## Page 15615

He mentioned the bonds among human beings that arise from creation and are established by divine law, such as parentage, and those that are acquired and established by stipulation, such as marriage. Then He said:

“Fear Allah, in whose name you ask one another, and be mindful of the ties of kinship.”

A group of early Quranic commentators explained “in whose name you ask one another” as meaning: “In whose name you enter into covenants and contracts.” This is indeed as they said, for each party to a contract of sale, marriage, truce, or any other agreement asks the other for what he seeks. One requests delivery of the goods sold, and the other requests payment of the price. Each has undertaken to provide what the other seeks. Thus, each makes a request of the other and undertakes to fulfil the other’s request.

Then He said:

“And the ties of kinship.”

Covenants and ties of kinship together encompass all the bonds between human beings. These bonds arise either through Allah’s action or through their own actions. The former are ties of kinship, and the latter are covenants. Allah therefore mentions them together in several places, as in His words:

“They observe neither ties of kinship nor covenant toward a believer.”

Here, ill means kinship and blood ties, while dhimma means a covenant or solemn pledge. Allah Most High also says near the beginning of al-Baqara:

“Those who break Allah’s covenant after it has been confirmed and sever what Allah has commanded to be maintained.”

And He says:

“Those who fulfil Allah’s covenant and do not break the solemn pledge, and those who maintain what Allah has commanded to be maintained,”

up to His words:

“And those who break Allah’s covenant after it has been confirmed and sever what Allah has commanded to be maintained.”

Know that Allah’s right is included in both kinds of rights and takes precedence over them. Hence He mentioned it first in His words:

“Fear your Lord, who created you.”

Allah created the servant, created his parents, and created him from his parents. The bond between him and Allah is thus the complete bond of creation, unlike his bond with his parents. The original substance from which he is made comes from them, but he also has substance from other sources. Moreover, they did not shape him in the womb. The servant has no source of bodily substance other than

---

## Page 15616

his parents. Allah is his Creator, his Maker, his Fashioner, his Provider, his Helper, and his Guide. The right of his parents bears only a partial resemblance to this. For this reason, Allah coupled their right with His own in His words:

“Be grateful to Me and to your parents.”

And in His words:

“Worship Allah and associate nothing with Him, and be good to your parents.”

And in His words:

“Your Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him, and that you be good to your parents.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, described disowning one’s parents as disbelief because of its resemblance to disowning one’s Lord. An authentic hadith states:

“Whoever knowingly claims descent from someone other than his father has disbelieved.”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded it in their Sahīh collections. He also said:

“Whoever disowns a tie of descent, however remote, has disbelieved in Allah.”

And he said:

“Do not disown your fathers, for it is disbelief on your part to disown your fathers.”

Thus, the rights of lineage, kinship, and blood ties are preceded by the right belonging to Allah as Lord and His right as the Near, the Responsive, the Most Merciful. For the ultimate end of those ties is to be joined to this bond, as Allah said:

“I am the Most Merciful. I created the ties of kinship and derived their name from My name. Whoever maintains them, I shall maintain My bond with him; and whoever severs them, I shall cut him off.”

He also said:

“Kinship is a branch linked to the Most Merciful.”

And he said:

“When Allah created kinship, it clung to the waist of the Most Merciful and said: ‘This is the place of one seeking refuge in You from being severed.’”

It has also been said that, in His words,

“They observe no ties of kinship toward a believer,”

the word ill means “the Lord,” as in al-Siddīq’s remark upon hearing Musaylima’s “Quran”: “These words have not come from the Lord.”

As for the inclusion of the Lord’s right within covenants and contracts, an example is the servant’s entering into peace and testifying that none has the right to be worshipped but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. This is the covenant of Islam: the noblest, most binding, most comprehensive, and most complete of all covenants.

---

## Page 15617

Chapter: The Essential Elements and Conditions of Marriage

Shaykh al-Islām Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Section

Some hold that marriage is valid only through a formula using inkāh or tazwīj (giving in marriage), except for the formula, “I have freed you, and your freedom has been made your dower.” These include the followers of al-Shāfiʿī, Ibn Hāmid, and those of our school who agree with them, such as Abū al-Khattāb, al-Qādī, his followers, and those who came after him. Their principal argument is that any wording other than these two expressions is indirect, and indirect wording produces its legal effect only when accompanied by the corresponding intention. Intention resides in the heart and cannot be known. Thus, a marriage contract cannot validly be concluded through indirect wording, because its validity requires witnesses, whereas an intention cannot be witnessed. This differs from divorce, emancipation, and sale, which may validly be effected through indirect wording, since witnesses are not a condition of their validity.

Some regard this requirement as a matter of prescribed worship, on account of the acts of worship established through it. This is the position of those, both within our school and outside it, who consider marriage valid only when contracted in Arabic. This view is weak for several reasons.

First: We do not concede that every expression other than these two is indirect. Rather, some expressions refer literally to the marriage contract in customary usage and convey it more clearly than the word ankahtu (I have given in marriage). This word can refer either to intercourse or to the contract, whereas imlāk (contracting a marriage) refers exclusively to the contract. When someone says, “So-and-so has been married to so-and-so,” nothing but the contract is understood. Thus, the two Sahīh collections contain the wording:

“I have given her to you in marriage for what you know of the Quran.”

This holds whether the report preserves the exact wording or conveys its meaning.

---

## Page 15618

Second: We do not concede that indirect wording invariably requires an intention. Rather, when accompanied by an explicit expression or a provision governing the contract, it becomes explicit. Thus, they have stated that an endowment may be established through indirect wording, such as “I have given it in charity,” “I have made it inviolable,” or “I have made it perpetual,” when accompanied by such an expression or provision.

Accordingly, if someone says, “I have made her yours,” and the other replies, “I accept this marriage”; or says, “I have given her to you as a wife,” and the other replies, “I accept”; or says, “I have made her yours in accordance with Allah’s command to retain her honourably or release her with kindness,” or something similar, he has accompanied the wording with expressions and provisions that make it explicit.

Third: Applying such wording to a free woman clarifies its meaning. If someone says concerning his daughter, “I have made her yours,” “I have given her to you,” “I have married her to you,” or something similar, the subject matter itself rules out ambiguity and multiple meanings.

Fourth: Their argument is contradicted by the prescribed practice of having witnesses when taking back a wife after a revocable divorce. This is either obligatory or recommended, and, according to one view, it is a condition for the validity of taking her back. It is also contradicted by having witnesses to sales and other contracts, for this is prescribed in all cases, whether the contract is concluded through explicit wording or indirect wording whose meaning has been clarified.

Fifth: Testimony concerning the contract is valid, and the contract can be established through it before the judge, whatever form it took. Thus, requiring witnesses to the contract does not preclude this.

Sixth: The two contracting parties can explain what they mean, and the witnesses can testify to the meaning they have explained.

---

## Page 15619

Seventh: In our view, when indirect wording is accompanied by indications arising from the circumstances, it is indisputably treated as explicit for purposes of outward legal judgment. Clearly, people gathering, a preliminary sermon being delivered, the bridal dower being mentioned and negotiated, and marriage being discussed conclusively establish that marriage is intended. As for requiring particular wording as a devotional observance, this requires evidence from Islamic law. Moreover, contracts belong to a category in which adherence to particular wording is not prescribed as a devotional observance, since faith is not a condition of their validity. Rather, they are valid when concluded by a disbeliever, and whatever a disbeliever can validly perform does not involve devotional observance. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man appointed a dhimmī (a non-Muslim under Muslim protection) to accept a marriage contract with a Muslim woman on his behalf. Is the marriage valid?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. There is disagreement concerning this issue. An agent appointed to accept a marriage contract must be someone capable, in principle, of validly accepting a marriage contract for himself. Thus, appointing a woman, an insane person, or a child lacking discernment would not be valid.

However, suppose the agent is someone whose acceptance of a marriage contract is valid with his guardian’s permission but not without it, and he is appointed to act in this capacity: for example, a slave appointed to accept a marriage contract without his master’s permission, a person under legal interdiction for financial imprudence appointed without his guardian’s permission, or a discerning child appointed without his guardian’s permission. Scholars within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere hold two views on this.

If, however, the agent can validly accept a marriage contract without permission, but is prohibited from doing so in the particular case because of an impediment pertaining to him—for example, someone appoints an agent to contract marriage with a slave woman whom the agent himself is not permitted to marry—then the appointment is valid.

---

## Page 15620

As for “a dhimmī acting as an agent” in accepting a marriage contract on his behalf, this resembles a dhimmī giving his dhimmī daughter in marriage to a Muslim. If he gave her in marriage to a dhimmī, it would be permissible. If, however, he gives her in marriage to a Muslim, there are two views within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. According to one, it is permissible. According to another, it is not permissible; rather, he must appoint a Muslim as his agent. It has also been said that only the judge may give her in marriage, with her father’s permission. His acting as a guardian in arranging marriage to a Muslim is comparable to his acting as an agent in the case under discussion.

Those who consider all of this permissible argue that, by scholarly agreement, the marital rights established by marriage accrue to the husband, not the agent. Ownership in other transactions is different, for the jurists have disagreed about it. The position of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others is that the rights arising from the contract attach to the principal, and ownership accrues to him. Thus, if a Muslim appointed a dhimmī to purchase wine, that would not be permissible. Abū Hanīfah disagrees on this point.

Since marital rights accrue to the husband, who is the Muslim principal, appointing a dhimmī is comparable to appointing one of a woman’s unmarriageable relatives, such as her maternal uncle, to act as an agent in contracting marriage with her. He may accept the marriage contract with her on behalf of the principal, even though he himself may not marry her. The same applies when a dhimmī acts as an agent in a Muslim man’s marriage, even though he himself may not marry a Muslim woman.

Nevertheless, the more cautious course is not to do this, because the matter is disputed and because marriage has an element of worship. It is recommended to “conclude its contract in mosques,” and it has been related in the transmitted reports:

“Whoever witnesses the conclusion of a Muslim’s marriage contract is as though he had witnessed a conquest in Allah’s cause.”

For this reason, according to one of the two views within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere, the marriage contract must be concluded in Arabic, like the prescribed formulas of remembrance. Since this is so, a disbeliever should not be entrusted with concluding a Muslim’s marriage. Nevertheless, it does not appear that the contract is invalid, for there is no evidence in Islamic law establishing its invalidity. A disbeliever can validly conclude a marriage contract, although he is not qualified to perform acts of worship. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15621

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A sick man married during his illness. Is the marriage contract valid?

He replied:

A sick man’s marriage is valid, and his wife inherits from him according to the overwhelming majority of Muslim scholars among the Companions and the Successors. She is entitled only to the customary dower for a woman of her standing; by scholarly agreement, she is not entitled to anything beyond that.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man has a daughter who has not yet reached puberty. She was married off during her father’s absence, with no guardian acting for her. They claimed that her father had died, although he was alive, and testified that her maternal uncle was her brother. Is the marriage contract valid or not?

He replied:

If they testified that her maternal uncle was her brother, this is false testimony, and it does not make the maternal uncle her guardian. Rather, she has married without a guardian, so her marriage is invalid according to most scholars and jurists, including al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others. Her father may renew the marriage contract.

Anyone who testified that her maternal uncle was her brother and that her father had died is a false witness and must receive a discretionary punishment. The maternal uncle must also receive a discretionary punishment. If the husband has consummated the marriage with her, she is entitled to the dower. Her father may marry her off during the waiting period arising from the invalid marriage, according to most scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad, according to the well-known view reported from him. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15622

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

A woman has a father and a brother, and her father’s representative in matters of marriage and other affairs is present. She went to the witnesses, gave a different name for herself and her father, and claimed that a man who had divorced her wished to renew their marriage. She brought an unrelated man and stated that he was her brother. The witnesses drew up her marriage contract on that basis. What she had done was subsequently discovered and established before the court.

Should she receive a discretionary punishment for this? Must those who vouched for the identities, the man who claimed to be her brother, and the person who supplied the witnesses with the information mentioned also receive discretionary punishment? Is imposing such punishment the exclusive prerogative of the judge, or may a public authority, such as an officer responsible for upholding public order or another official, punish them?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. She should receive a severe discretionary punishment. It would be appropriate for the public authority to punish her on several occasions, just as ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb would impose repeated discretionary punishments for an act involving several prohibited offences. He would administer one hundred lashes on the first day, one hundred on the second, and one hundred on the third, spreading out the punishment so that it would not damage any bodily organs.

This woman has claimed descent from someone other than her father and appointed her purported brother to act on her behalf. This is among the major sins. It is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever claims descent from someone other than his father, or declares allegiance to patrons other than his own, incurs the curse of Allah, the angels, and all people. Allah will accept neither voluntary nor obligatory worship from him.”

Indeed, it is authentically established that Sa‘d and Abū Bakrah heard the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say: “Whoever claims descent from someone other than his father

---

## Page 15623

will be forbidden Paradise.” An even stronger warning is authentically reported from Abū Dharr, who narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever knowingly claims descent from someone other than his father is not one of us and has committed disbelief. Whoever claims what is not his is not one of us; let him take his seat in the Fire. Whoever accuses a man of disbelief or calls him an enemy of Allah, when he is not so, has that accusation return upon himself.”

This is an extremely stern warning, warranting a severe punishment of a hundred lashes or the like. Moreover, she deceived the witnesses and caused them to draw up invalid contracts, and she entered into an invalid marriage. The majority of scholars hold that marriage without a guardian is invalid. Following the example of ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, they impose discretionary punishment on those who do this. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and others. Indeed, some of them impose the prescribed punishment in such cases, whether stoning or otherwise.

Even those who permit marriage without a guardian, either unconditionally or in the city, do not permit it in this manner, involving a false claim of lineage and the appointment of an illegitimate guardian. Thus, Muslims agree that this woman should be punished. She should also be punished for her lies, including the claim that he had previously been her husband and had divorced her. The husband should also be punished, as should the man who claimed to be her brother: he should be punished for these two suspect acts.

As for those who vouched for their identities, they should be punished for their false testimony concerning her lineage, the marriage and divorce, and the absence of an available guardian. Their punishment should be particularly severe. The jurists have expressly stated that a false witness’s face should be blackened, on the basis of what has been reported from ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb

---

## Page 15624

may Allah be pleased with him: he used to blacken the false witness’s face, symbolizing how his lies had blackened his face. He would also seat him backwards on a mount, symbolizing how he had reversed the account, and have him paraded among the people to expose him as a false witness.

Imposing discretionary punishment on such people is not the exclusive prerogative of the judge. Rather, the judge, the official responsible for public oversight, and other authorities capable of doing so may punish them. This becomes obligatory in circumstances such as these, when widespread corruption among women has become apparent and false testimony is rife. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When people see wrongdoing and do not put a stop to it, Allah may soon afflict them all with a punishment from Him.”

And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is it permissible for a father to compel his adult virgin daughter to marry?”

He replied:

Regarding a father compelling his adult virgin daughter to marry, there are two well-known views, both transmitted from Ahmad.

“The first” is that he may compel her, as is the position of Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī. This is the view preferred by al-Khiraqī, al-Qādī, and his followers.

“The second” is that he may not compel her, as is the position of Abū Hanīfa and others. This is the view preferred by Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz ibn Ja‘far, and it is the correct view. Scholars disagree over the “grounds for compelling marriage”: is it

---

## Page 15625

virginity, minority, both together, or either one independently? These are four views within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. The correct view is that minority is the ground for compelling marriage, and that no one may compel an adult virgin woman to marry.

For it is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A virgin must not be married until her permission is sought, nor a previously married woman until she is consulted.” He was asked, “But is a virgin not too shy?” He replied, “Her silence is her permission.”

Another authentic wording states:

“A virgin’s father must seek her permission.”

Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited marrying her without first seeking her permission. This applies to her father and anyone else. The other authentic narration makes this explicit: the father himself must seek her permission.

Furthermore, if she possesses sound judgment, her father may not dispose of her property without her permission. Her marriage is a far weightier matter than her property. How, then, may he arrange her marriage against her will when she possesses sound judgment?

Moreover, minority is a ground for restricting legal capacity, as established by the scriptural texts and scholarly consensus. Treating virginity as necessitating such restrictions, however, contradicts the fundamental principles of Islam. The Lawgiver has not made virginity a ground for restricting legal capacity in any matter on which there is consensus. To justify such restrictions on that basis is therefore to invoke an attribute that has no legal effect under Islamic law.

Furthermore, those who permit compulsion have differed over the case in which she chooses a suitable match and her father chooses another suitable match: should her choice prevail, or her father’s? There are two views within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. Whoever gives decisive weight to her choice contradicts his own underlying principle, whereas whoever gives

---

## Page 15626

decisive weight to the father’s choice advances a view whose corruption, harm, and evil are plain. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“A previously married woman has more right concerning herself than her guardian, while a virgin must be asked for her permission, and her silence is her permission.”

Another narration states:

“A woman who has previously been married has more right concerning herself than her guardian.”

They argue that, since he gave the previously married woman greater right concerning herself, this indicates that a virgin does not have greater right concerning herself; rather, her guardian has greater right, and this applies only to the father and grandfather. This is the principal argument of those who permit compulsion. Yet they have abandoned the hadith’s explicit wording and apparent meaning, clinging instead to its implied converse, without understanding the Messenger’s intent, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

For his statement, “A previously married woman has more right concerning herself than her guardian,” encompasses every guardian, whereas they restrict it to the father and grandfather.

Second, he said, “A virgin must be asked for her permission.” Yet they do not regard seeking her permission as obligatory; rather, they say it is recommended. Some even carried their analogy to its logical conclusion, saying that silence suffices because seeking permission is merely recommended, and claiming that wherever seeking a virgin’s permission is obligatory, she must speak. Some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad expressed this view. It contradicts the consensus of the Muslims before them and the explicit statements of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. For the authentic, widely transmitted Sunnah and the agreement of the imams before these scholars establish that when a virgin’s brother or paternal uncle arranges her marriage, he must seek her permission, and her silence is her permission.

As for the implied converse, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, distinguished between a virgin and a previously married woman, as he said in another hadith:

“A virgin must not be married until her permission is sought, nor a previously married woman until she is consulted.”

For the former, he used the term “permission,” and for the latter, the term “instruction.” He made silence the permission of the former, just as speech constitutes the permission of the latter. These, then, are the two distinctions that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, drew between a virgin

---

## Page 15627

and a previously married woman; he did not distinguish between them in whether or not they may be compelled to marry. This is because a “virgin,” being too shy to speak about her marriage, is not approached directly with a proposal. Rather, the proposal is addressed to her guardian, who seeks her permission, and she grants it. She does not take the initiative by instructing him; rather, she gives him permission when he asks for it, and her silence is her permission. As for a previously married woman, she no longer has a virgin’s shyness, so she speaks about marriage, receives proposals directly, and instructs her guardian to arrange her marriage. She thus gives him instructions, and he must grant her request by marrying her to a suitable match when she instructs him to do so. Thus, the guardian receives instructions from a previously married woman but seeks permission from a virgin. This is what the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, indicate.

As for marrying her against her wishes, this runs contrary to both the foundational principles of the religion and sound reason. Allah has not permitted her guardian to force her into a sale or a lease without her permission, nor to compel her to accept food, drink, or clothing she does not want. How, then, could he compel her to have sexual relations and share a life with someone with whom she detests having sexual relations and whose companionship she detests? Allah has placed affection and mercy between spouses. If the marriage can take place only while she hates him and feels aversion towards him, what affection and mercy can there be in that?

Moreover, when discord arises between spouses, Allah has commanded that an arbitrator from his family and an arbitrator from hers be appointed. The “two arbitrators,” as Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has called them, are indeed arbitrators according to the scholars of Medina. This is also one of the two opinions held by al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. According to Abū Hanīfa and the other opinion, they are “agents.” The first view is more correct, because an agent

---

## Page 15628

is not an arbitrator; his appointment requires no order from the authorities, he need not be a member of the family, his role is not confined to cases of marital discord, and none of this requires a specific scriptural text. When discord arises, however, the spouses need someone with authority over them to oversee their affairs, since neither can have sole authority to judge the other.

Allah therefore commanded that their affairs be entrusted to two people from their respective families, who should do whatever serves their best interests: reconcile them or separate them, with or without compensation. Here, one arbitrator, acting together with the other, has the authority to pronounce divorce without the husband’s permission; the other, acting together with the first, has the authority to offer compensation from the wife’s property without her permission, because the two have become guardians over them.

A consistent implication of this view is that a father may pronounce divorce on behalf of his minor son or his mentally incapacitated son when he considers it beneficial, as stated in one of the two opinions reported from Ahmad. Likewise, he may secure his daughter’s release from marriage in return for compensation when he considers it beneficial to her.

More strikingly, if a husband divorces his wife before consummation, her father may waive her entitlement to half the dowry, if one holds that the father is “the one in whose hand is the marriage tie.” This is the view of Mālik and one of the two opinions reported from Ahmad. The Quran indicates that this view is correct. The dowry is not like the rest of her property: it was originally prescribed as a gift, and the marital right over her has reverted to her undiminished. Treating divorce like annulments would therefore entail that she should not receive half the dowry. Yet the Lawgiver compensated her by awarding her half the dowry for the distress caused to her by divorce. Hence Ibn ‘Umar, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad, according to one of the opinions reported from him, regarded this as a substitute for the consolatory gift. They therefore held that a consolatory gift is obligatory for every divorced woman, except one divorced after her dowry has been fixed and before

---

## Page 15629

consummation and intercourse; the amount allotted to her suffices.

Ahmad, according to the other opinion reported from him, together with Abū Hanīfa and others, holds that the consolatory gift is obligatory only for a woman divorced before her dowry has been fixed and before consummation. They regard the consolatory gift as a substitute for half the dowry, saying: “Every divorced woman receives a dowry except this one.”

The proponents of the former view say: “Entitlement to the dowry was already established before divorce through the marriage contract and consummation, whereas the consolatory gift becomes due because of divorce. It is therefore obligatory for every divorced woman. However, a woman divorced after her dowry has been fixed but before intercourse has received half the dowry as her consolatory provision, so she is not entitled to anything further.”

This view is stronger than the other, for Allah made divorce the basis for entitlement to the consolatory gift. It should therefore not be treated as a substitute for what became due through the marriage contract and consummation. Yet, on this reasoning, it may be said that the third view is more correct: namely, the other opinion reported from Ahmad, that every divorced woman is entitled to a consolatory gift. This is indicated by the apparent and general meaning of the Quran, where Allah says:

“Divorced women are entitled to reasonable provision.”

He also says:

“When you marry believing women and then divorce them before you have touched them, you have no waiting period to count for them. So provide for them and release them graciously.”

He thus commanded that women divorced before intercourse be given a consolatory gift, without restricting this to those whose dowries had not been fixed, even though most women are divorced after their dowries have been fixed.

Furthermore, just as divorce is the basis for entitlement to the consolatory gift, the marriage contract is the basis for entitlement to the dowry. A woman whose dowry has not been specified is entitled, by virtue of the marriage contract, to the customary dowry of women of comparable standing. This entitlement becomes definitive upon death, according to the correct view supported by the hadith of Barwa‘ bint Wāshiq. She married, and her husband died before a dowry had been fixed for her. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ruled that:

“She is entitled to a dowry equal to that of the women of her family, neither less nor more.”

But if this woman were divorced before

---

## Page 15630

intercourse, she would not be entitled to half the dowry, according to the explicit text of the Quran, because she had not stipulated a specified dowry. The hurt caused to her by divorce is remedied by the consolatory gift. This is not the place to discuss these issues at length.

The point, however, is that the Lawgiver does not compel a woman to marry against her wishes. Indeed, if she dislikes her husband and discord arises between them, the Lawgiver entrusts her case to someone other than her husband: someone from her family who considers their best interests, together with someone from his family who does likewise, thereby freeing her from the husband without his consent. How, then, could she be held captive with him forever without her consent? A woman is a captive with her husband, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Fear Allah concerning women, for they are captives in your care. You have taken them as a trust from Allah, and intimacy with them has become lawful to you through the word of Allah.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“A young woman who has reached puberty received a marriage proposal from a relative, but she refused. Her family told the person conducting the marriage contract, ‘Conclude the contract,’ while her father was present. Is it permissible to marry her off?”

He replied:

If the prospective husband is not a suitable match for her, she undoubtedly cannot be compelled to marry him. If he is a suitable match, the scholars hold two well-known views on the matter. However, the view more clearly supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound reasoning is that she cannot be compelled, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A virgin must not be married until her father seeks her permission, and her silence is her permission.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15631

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man married a virgin under her father’s guardianship, without her permission being sought when the contract was concluded. She had previously been married to another man, but was divorced before consummation, without intercourse having taken place. The second husband then consummated the marriage and found her to be a virgin, but concealed this. She became pregnant by him, and their married life became settled. When the husband learned that her permission had not been sought when the marriage contract was concluded, he asked about this and was told: ‘The contract is void because she was a virgin and her permission was not sought.’ Is the contract therefore void? Does their intercourse constitute intercourse under a mistaken belief in its lawfulness? Must the marriage contract be renewed or not?”

He replied:

If she is no longer a virgin as a result of marital intercourse and has reached puberty, she may not be married without her permission, by agreement of the imams. However, if she is married without her permission and subsequently approves the contract, it is valid according to Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and one of the two reports from Imam Ahmad. It is not valid according to al-Shāfi'ī and the other report from Ahmad.

If she has lost her virginity through unlawful intercourse, she is treated like a woman who has lost it through marriage, according to al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad, and Abū Hanīfah’s two principal disciples. There is another view: that she is treated as a virgin. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah himself and Mālik. If her hymen has been broken through jumping, by a finger, or the like, she is treated as a virgin according to all four imams.

If she is a virgin, her father may compel her to marry, even if she has reached puberty, according to Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī, and one of the two reports from Ahmad. According to the other report, which is

---

## Page 15632

the position of Abū Hanīfah and others, the father may not compel her if she has reached puberty. This is the soundest view, supported by the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and corroborated by fundamental legal principles.

It is thus clear that most scholars hold the following view in this case: if she approves the contract, it is valid; otherwise, a fresh contract is required.

It may be said that this is the stronger view here, especially since the father concluded the contract believing that she was a virgin and that he did not need to seek her permission. If the actual situation was otherwise, he was excused. If she then approves the marriage, this is not equivalent to a transaction undertaken by someone acting without authorization. Whether a contract may remain suspended pending ratification is a well-known point of disagreement among scholars. The more persuasive view is to distinguish between different cases, as explained at length elsewhere.

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

“Neither parent has the right to compel a child to marry someone they do not wish to marry, and refusing does not make the child undutiful. If no one has the right to force a child to eat something they find repugnant when they can eat something they desire, the same applies even more strongly to marriage. The unpleasantness of eating disliked food lasts only a short while, whereas living with an unwanted husband or wife causes prolonged distress to the other spouse, who cannot part from them.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man under his father’s guardianship married without his father’s permission. Well-known men testified that his father had died, although the father was still alive. Is the contract valid or not? Does marrying without his father’s permission entail any obligation for the son or not?”

---

## Page 15633

He replied:

“If he is under legal interdiction on account of his imprudence, his marriage is not valid without his father’s permission, and the couple must be separated. If they are separated before consummation, he owes nothing. If, however, he is of sound judgment, his marriage is valid even without his father’s permission.

“If the spouses disagree over whether he was of sound judgment or imprudent when he married, the word of the party maintaining that the marriage is valid is accepted.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man proposed to a woman who had a son. The marriage officiant, who was a Mālikī, requested the son’s presence, but he was unable to attend. Another man was brought in and gave the officiant his consent to her marriage. Is the contract valid?”

He replied:

“This contract is not valid, because her son is her marriage guardian. If he is present and does not refuse, she may not be married without his permission. If, however, he is far away, guardianship passes to a more distant guardian or to the judge.

“Had a Shāfiʿī married her off, believing that her son had no right of guardianship, this would have been a matter open to juristic interpretation. But the person who married her off was a Mālikī who believed that only her son could give her in marriage. Thus, if he was deceived and someone whom he believed to be her son gave her in marriage, while this judge neither married her off by virtue of his own authority nor was she married under the authority of a guardian entitled to it through kinship or ties of manumission, then she was married without any guardian’s permission whatsoever. Such a marriage is invalid according to the majority of scholars, as the scriptural texts establish.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A woman’s brother left her in a place to complete the waiting period following the end of her marriage. When the waiting period ended, she fled to a town a day’s journey away and married without her brother’s permission, although she had no guardian other than him. Is the contract valid or not?”

---

## Page 15634

He replied:

“If her brother was not wrongfully preventing her from marrying and was qualified to act as her guardian, her marriage without his permission is invalid in these circumstances. And Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man married an adult woman whose paternal grandfather gave her in marriage, although he had not declared her to be of sound judgment and had received no testamentary appointment from her father. As her grandfather’s death approached, he appointed an unrelated man as her guardian in his will. Does the grandfather retain guardianship over the wife after her husband has consummated the marriage? And may he appoint a testamentary guardian for her?”

He replied:

“If she is of sound judgment, neither her grandfather nor anyone else has guardianship over her, by agreement of the imams. If, however, she is someone who warrants legal interdiction, the scholars hold two views:

“The first is that the grandfather has guardianship over her. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah.

“The second is that he has no guardianship over her. This is the position of Mālik and the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

“If a young woman has married, a year has passed, and her husband has had a child by her, it is possible for her to be of sound judgment, by agreement of the scholars.”

He was asked:

“A man bribed a woman’s guardian to give her to him in marriage. The guardian did so, and he then reached a settlement with the owner of the money concerning it. Does the woman bear any liability for this?”

He replied:

“He has sinned by what he did. The marriage, however, is valid, and the woman bears no liability for any of this.”

---

## Page 15635

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man owned a slave woman, whom he freed and married, and then he died. A suitable man subsequently proposed to her. May her former master’s children give her in marriage?”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. If a suitable man proposes to her, her former master’s children must give her in marriage. If they refuse, the judge or the manumitter’s agnatic relatives may give her in marriage, if he has agnatic relatives other than his children.

“Some scholars, however, give precedence to the judge when the nearest guardian wrongfully prevents her from marrying. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and one position reported from Ahmad. Others give precedence to the agnatic relatives, as does Abū Hanīfah according to the well-known position reported from him. If the manumitter has no agnatic relatives, the judge gives her in marriage, by agreement of the scholars. Likewise, if all the agnatic relatives refuse, the judge gives her in marriage, by agreement. If the agnatic relatives authorize the judge to give her in marriage, that is permissible by agreement of the scholars.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man married a woman who had been freed by another man, then divorced her. She married someone else, who also divorced her. She then came to the town where her first husband lived, and he wished to remarry her. She had no certificate of release with her, and he feared that he would be asked to produce one. They therefore appeared before the town’s judge, and he claimed that she was his slave woman, that he had fathered a child by her, and that he wished to free her and draw up a document for her. Is this marriage contract valid or not?”

---

## Page 15636

He replied:

“If the judge gives her in marriage in his capacity as her guardian, she is free of any impediments to marriage under Islamic law, and she has no guardian with a stronger claim than the judge, the marriage is valid. Even if the judge supposes that she is a freedwoman when she is actually freeborn, this supposition does not impair the marriage’s validity. This is clear according to al-Shāfiʿī’s principle, since, in his view, the husband cannot act as her guardian.

“As for those who hold that a freedwoman’s husband who freed her may be her guardian, with the judge acting as his deputy: if the judge gives her in marriage in this delegated capacity, and acceptance of the marriage does not come from her side but rests on her being freeborn, then this requires further consideration. Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Concerning Bedouins dwelling by the sea and people of the desert who have no judge among them or nearby, and whose custom is to conclude marriages only before the imams of the surrounding villages: are marriages conducted for them by these village imams valid in all cases, whether the woman has a guardian or not? Some of these imams may have no authorization from a governing official. Are the marriages they conduct valid under Islamic law when witnessed by whichever Muslims happen to be present? Do the imams incur any sin if there is no impediment to the marriage other than this circumstance—namely, that the judge has not authorized the imam to conduct it?”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. As for a woman who has a guardian through kinship—that is, an agnatic relative—or through the bond of manumission, such as her father, grandfather, brother, paternal uncle, brother’s son, paternal uncle’s son, father’s paternal uncle, or father’s paternal uncle’s son; or, if she is a freedwoman, the person who freed her or his agnatic relatives: her guardian gives her in marriage with her permission. According to the majority of scholars, her son may also be her guardian. By agreement of the scholars, this does not require a judge.

---

## Page 15637

If the marriage takes place in the presence of two Muslim witnesses, it is valid, even if none of the imams is present. Even if the two witnesses have not been certified as upright before the judge, their character being unverified but not openly sinful, the marriage is valid according to the apparent position of the four imams’ schools, provided it is publicly announced and not concealed. Even if the two witnesses are openly sinful, the marriage is still valid according to Abū Hanīfa and one of the two reported positions of Ahmad.

Even if no witnesses are present, but her guardian gives her in marriage and it becomes widely known among the people, the marriage is valid according to Mālik’s school and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad ibn Hanbal. This is the stronger of the two scholarly views. Muslims regularly gave women in marriage during the Prophet’s lifetime—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—yet he did not instruct them to have witnesses present. There is no established hadith stipulating witnesses as a condition of marriage, whether in the Sahīh, Sunan, or Musnad collections.

As for a woman who has no guardian, if there is a deputy exercising judicial authority in the village or encampment, he may give her in marriage, as may the Bedouin chief or village head. If there is an imam among them whose authority is obeyed, he too may give her in marriage with her permission. Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

“Concerning a man who has embraced Islam: does he retain guardianship over his children who remain People of the Book?”

He replied:

“He has no guardianship over them in marriage, just as he has no right to inherit from them. A Muslim may not give a non-Muslim woman in marriage, whether she is his daughter or someone else. Nor may a non-Muslim inherit from a Muslim, or a Muslim from a non-Muslim. This is the position of the four imams and their followers among the earlier and later generations.

---

## Page 15638

But if a Muslim owns a slave woman, he may give her in marriage by virtue of his ownership. Likewise, if he is charged with overseeing her marriage, he may give her in marriage by virtue of that authority. He may not, however, give her in marriage on the basis of kinship or having emancipated her. The only dissent on this point is an anomalous view reported from some of Mālik’s followers concerning a Christian giving his daughter in marriage, just as it has been reported from some of the early generations that he may inherit from her. Both views are anomalous.

The Muslims are agreed that a non-Muslim does not inherit from a Muslim and that a non-Muslim man may not marry a Muslim woman. Allah—glorified is He—has severed ties of allegiance between believers and disbelievers in His Book, required mutual disavowal, and established allegiance among believers. He, Most High, says:

“There has already been an excellent example for you in Abraham and those with him, when they said to their people: ‘We disavow you and whatever you worship besides Allah. We reject you, and enmity and hatred have appeared between us and you forever, until you believe in Allah alone.’”

He, Most High, also says:

“You will not find a people who believe in Allah and the Last Day showing affection to those who oppose Allah and His Messenger, even if they were their fathers, their sons, their brothers, or their clan. Those are the ones in whose hearts He has inscribed faith and whom He has strengthened with a spirit from Him.”

He, Most High, also says:

“O you who believe, do not take the Jews and Christians as allies. They are allies of one another. Whoever among you takes them as allies is indeed one of them. Surely Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

Then, further on, He says:

“Your ally is none but Allah, His Messenger, and those who believe.”

And further on:

“Indeed, it is the party of Allah who will prevail.”

Allah Most High has established such ties between blood relatives only on condition of faith, as He, Most High, says:

“Blood relatives have a greater claim upon one another in the Book of Allah than other believers and emigrants.”

He, Most High, also says:

“Indeed, those who believed, emigrated, and strove with their wealth and their lives in the cause of Allah, and those who

---

## Page 15639

gave shelter and aid—those are allies of one another.” Further on, He says:

“Those who disbelieve are allies of one another.”

And further on:

“And those who believed afterwards, emigrated, and strove alongside you are of you. Blood relatives have a greater claim upon one another.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had emancipated a slave woman. Another man asked him for her hand in marriage, whereupon he swore by divorce: “I will not give her to you.” Does divorce take effect if he appoints someone as his agent to give her in marriage to that man?

He answered:

Whenever he does what he swore not to do, whether personally or through his agent, he breaks his oath. However, if the suitor is a suitable match, a more distant guardian—such as the emancipator’s son, father, or brother—may give her in marriage to him. Alternatively, the judge may give her in marriage with her consent, without the emancipator’s permission. Since he is obstructing her marriage, his permission is not required, and he does not break his oath if she is married in any of these ways.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

May the judge prevent someone from concluding marriage contracts with a guardian and two upright witnesses?

He answered:

The judge may not prevent the person mentioned from acting as the guardian’s agent and concluding the marriage contract in accordance with Islamic law. However, a woman who has no guardian may only be given in marriage with the permission of the governing authority, namely, the judge. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15640

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man proposed marriage to a free woman who has a guardian other than the judge. He brought witnesses whom he knows to be sinful, but whose testimony the judge would accept were they to testify before him. Is the woman’s marriage valid with them as witnesses? If it is valid, is it disliked?

He answered:

Yes, the marriage is valid under these circumstances. The “uprightness” required of the two marriage witnesses means only that their outward standing is unblemished and that they display no manifest sinfulness. If they are sinful in private, but this is not apparent and their outward standing remains unblemished, the marriage contracted with them as witnesses is valid according to the sounder of the two scholarly views within the schools of Ahmad, al-Shāfiʿī, and others.

If the two marriage witnesses were required to have their uprightness formally attested before the judge, most people’s marriages would not be valid without that attestation. Yet it is known that, during the time of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and of Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, and ʿAlī, people contracted marriages in the presence of others among them, even though those present had not had their uprightness formally attested before those in authority.

Some jurists hold that the two witnesses must be demonstrably upright. These witnesses, however, are accepted witnesses before the judges, who recognize their uprightness, even if some of them are sinful in reality. Thus, on either view, the marriage is valid with them as witnesses, even if they are sinful in private. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15641

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about the hadith of Abū Hurayrah—may Allah be pleased with him—who said: The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“A previously married woman must not be married until her consent is sought, nor a virgin until her permission is sought.” They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, how does she give permission?” He replied, “By remaining silent.”

Reported by al-Bukhārī and Muslim.

And about the report from Ibn ʿAbbās—may Allah be pleased with him—that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“A previously married woman has more right to decide for herself than her guardian, while a virgin must be asked for her permission, and her silence is her permission.”

Another version reads:

“A virgin’s father must ask her permission concerning her marriage, and her silence is her assent.”

Reported by Muslim in his Sahīh.

And about the report from ʿĀ’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—who said:

“I asked the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—whether a girl whose family arranges her marriage should be consulted. The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—answered, ‘Yes, her consent must be sought.’” ʿĀ’ishah said: “I said to him, ‘But she is shy.’ The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—replied, ‘Her remaining silent is her permission.’”

And about the report from Khansā’ bint Khidām that her father married her off when she was a girl, but she disliked it. She went to the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and he annulled the marriage her father had arranged. Reported by al-Bukhārī.

He answered:

No one should marry a woman off without her permission, as the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded. If she objects, she must not be compelled to marry. The exception is a minor who is a virgin: her father may marry her off without her permission. As for an adult woman who has previously been married, it is not permissible to marry her off without her permission

---

## Page 15642

—whether by her father or anyone else—according to the consensus of the Muslims. Likewise, by the consensus of the Muslims, no one other than her father or grandfather may marry off an adult virgin without her permission. As for her father and grandfather, they should seek her permission. The scholars differed over whether seeking her permission is obligatory or recommended. The correct view is that it is obligatory.

A woman’s guardian must fear Allah concerning the man to whom he marries her and consider whether he is a suitable match. He arranges her marriage for her benefit, not his own. He may not marry her to a husband with shortcomings to serve some purpose of his own: for example, marrying a woman under that husband’s guardianship in exchange for her. This would be a form of the exchange marriage prohibited by the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Nor may he marry her into a group with whom he seeks an alliance for his own corrupt purposes, or marry her to a man in return for money paid to him when another suitor would be better for her, thus preferring the suitor who bribed him over the suitable suitor who did not—and al-Āmidī.

The underlying principle is that a guardian’s authority over his ward’s marriage is like his authority over her property: just as he may administer her property only in her best interests, he may arrange her marriage only in her best interests. A father, however, has greater latitude in using his child’s property than anyone else, as the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“You and your wealth belong to your father.”

This does not apply to anyone other than the father.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Regarding a woman whose permission for marriage is required under Islamic law: must witnesses attest that she has given her guardian permission, or not? And if the guardian says, “She has authorized me to marry her to this

---

## Page 15643

man,” may the person conducting the marriage contract proceed solely on the guardian’s word, or is her own statement required? What are the scholars’ rulings on this matter?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Having witnesses attest to her permission is not a condition for the validity of the marriage contract according to the vast majority of scholars. There is only an isolated dissenting view within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad that makes it a condition. The well-known position in both schools, like that of the majority, is that it is not required.

If the guardian says, “She has authorized me to conclude the marriage contract,” then concludes it with witnesses attesting to the contract, and the wife subsequently confirms that she gave him permission, the marriage is established and valid both in reality and in outward legal judgment. If, however, she denies having given permission, her word, supported by her oath, is accepted, and the marriage is not established. His claim that she gave permission is comparable to a claim of marriage made after the witnesses have died, and similar cases.

The marriage witnesses should nevertheless witness the woman’s permission before the contract is concluded, for three reasons:

The first is that the resulting contract is unanimously considered valid. Whenever it is possible to conclude a contract whose validity is unanimously accepted, one should not depart from it in favour of a disputed form—even if the dissenting view is weaker—unless there is an overriding consideration.

The second reason is that this helps secure the purpose of the contract and safeguards against its denial, particularly in places and times where denials and lies by women are common. Failing to have witnesses attest to her permission often leads to the opposite outcome. It may then result in her being a wife in reality but not in outward legal judgment, which entails numerous harmful consequences.

---

## Page 15644

“The third reason” is that the guardian may be lying when he claims to have obtained her permission. He may use this as a stratagem to secure testimony that he has married her off, while uninformed people imagine that the marriage is valid without her permission, since ordinary people believe that once a woman is married before a judge, she becomes a wife. This may lead to her being coerced and made a wife without her consent.

As for the “person conducting the contract,” who acts as the judge’s deputy: if he is marrying her off by virtue of guardianship over her, rather than as the guardian’s agent, he must not do so until he knows that she has given permission. This differs from merely witnessing the contract. If the guardian marries her off without her permission, this is a marriage contracted by an unauthorized person. According to Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, it remains suspended pending her permission; according to al-Shāfiʿī and the well-known position reported from Ahmad, it is invalid and rejected.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A girl lost her virginity through an unfortunate incident, although she has never entered into a marriage contract. A man sought to marry her, was informed of this, and accepted it. Would the contract be valid in these circumstances if the recognized witnesses testified that she was a maiden, to facilitate the matter?

He replied:

If they testify that she has never been married, they would be telling the truth, and this would not deceive the husband, since he knows her circumstances. She should be asked tactfully to express her consent verbally, for the scholars disagree over whether a woman who has lost her virginity through unlawful sexual intercourse gives permission through silence or through speech. The “former” is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, as well as Abū Hanīfah’s two companions. According to Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, her permission is expressed through silence, just as with a woman whose virginity remains intact.

---

## Page 15645

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

An orphan girl is ten years old, has no one to care for her, and urgently needs someone to look after her. Is it permissible for someone to marry her with her permission?

He replied:

According to most of the early generations and jurists, it is permissible to marry her to a suitable match. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, the apparent position of Ahmad’s school, and the position of others besides them.

The Quran and Sunnah provide evidence for this, including the words of Allah Most High:

“They ask you for a ruling concerning women. Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning them, as does what is recited to you in the Book concerning orphan girls’”—to the end of the verse.

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim both recorded ʿĀ’ishah’s explanation of this verse in their Sahīh collections. It provides evidence concerning an orphan girl and her marriage to someone who treats her fairly regarding her dowry.

However, these scholars disagreed over whether she may be married off with or without her permission. Abū Hanīfah held that she may be married off without her permission, but that she has the option to rescind the marriage upon reaching puberty. This is also one position reported from Ahmad. The apparent position of Ahmad’s school is that she may be married off without her permission once she is nine years old, and that she has no option to rescind the marriage upon reaching puberty. This is based on the Prophet’s statement—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—recorded in the Sunan collections:

“An orphan girl must be asked for her permission concerning her marriage. If she remains silent, she has given permission; if she refuses, the marriage may not be imposed on her.”

Another wording states:

“An orphan girl must not be married off until her permission has been sought. If she remains silent, she has given permission; if she refuses, the marriage may not be imposed on her.”

---

## Page 15646

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a girl who had not yet reached puberty and whose father had died:

“May the judge or his deputy marry her off? Does she have the option to rescind the marriage upon reaching puberty?”

He replied:

Once she is nine years old, her guardians—her agnatic relatives, the judge, or his deputy—may marry her off, according to the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. This is also the position of Abū Hanīfah and others, as indicated by the Quran and Sunnah, including the words of Allah Most High:

“They ask you for a ruling concerning women. Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning them, as does what is recited to you in the Book concerning orphan girls whom you do not give their prescribed rights, yet whom you desire to marry.’”

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded in their Sahīh collections that ‘Urwah ibn al-Zubayr asked ‘Ā’ishah about the words of Allah, Mighty and Majestic:

“If you fear that you will not deal justly with orphan girls, then marry other women of your choice—two, three, or four.”

She said: “My sister’s son, this refers to an orphan girl in her guardian’s care who shares in his property. He is attracted to her wealth and beauty and wishes to marry her without giving her a fair dowry equal to what another man would give her. They were therefore forbidden to marry such girls unless they treated them fairly and paid them their customary dowry. They were instructed instead to marry other women of their choice.”

‘Urwah said:

‘Ā’ishah said: “Then, after this verse, people asked the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for a ruling concerning these girls, so Allah revealed

---

## Page 15647

—Mighty and Majestic—:

‘They ask you for a ruling concerning women. Say, “Allah gives you a ruling concerning them”’—and the rest of the verse.”

‘Ā’ishah said: “What Allah described as ‘recited to you in the Book’ is the earlier verse in which Allah, Mighty and Majestic, said:

‘If you fear that you will not deal justly with orphan girls, then marry other women of your choice.’”

‘Ā’ishah said: “The words of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, in the other verse, ‘and you desire to marry them,’ refer to a man’s reluctance to marry the orphan girl in his care when she has little wealth or standing.”

In another wording:

“If she possessed wealth and beauty, they wished to marry her, paying the dowry in full. But if she was considered undesirable because she had little wealth or beauty, they would turn away from her and marry other women.”
