He said: “Just as they leave her when they have no desire to marry her, they may not marry her when they do desire her unless they treat her justly and give her the dowry that is rightfully hers.”

This demonstrates that Allah permitted them to marry off orphan girls provided they stipulated for them the dowry customary for women of their standing. He did not permit them to marry them off for less than that customary dowry, because such a girl is not legally competent to make gratuitous dispositions of her property. The evidence for this is manifold.

The majority who permit marrying her off hold two positions:

The first, which is the position of Abū Hanīfah and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, is that she may be married off without her permission, but she has the option to rescind the marriage upon reaching puberty.

The second, which is the well-known position in Ahmad’s school and that of others, is that she may be married off only with her permission, and she has no option to rescind upon reaching puberty. This is the correct position, supported by the Sunnah, as Abū Hurayrah narrated: The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said: “Permission must be sought

---

## Page 15648

from the orphan girl concerning her marriage. If she remains silent, that is her consent; but if she refuses, the marriage may not be imposed on her.” Narrated by Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī and al-Nasā’ī.

Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī also narrated that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“The orphan girl must be consulted concerning her marriage. If she remains silent, she has consented; but if she refuses, the marriage may not be imposed on her.”

This Sunnah explicitly supports the third view, which is the most balanced: that she may be married off. This is contrary to the view that she may not be married off until she reaches puberty, when she is no longer an “orphan girl.” The Quran and Sunnah explicitly include orphan girls who have not yet reached puberty in this ruling, since a girl who has reached puberty and has authority over her property may agree to less than the dowry customary for women of her standing. Moreover, this is what the term denotes in its literal sense. Even if someone who has reached puberty is figuratively called an orphan, this means, at most, that such a person falls within the term’s general scope.

As for interpreting “orphan girl” to mean a girl who has reached puberty to the exclusion of one who has not, the wording cannot possibly bear such an interpretation. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

An orphan girl has no father and no guardian other than her brother. She is twelve years old and has not reached puberty. Her brother has concluded a marriage contract on her behalf with her permission. Is this permissible or not?

He answered:

This contract is valid according to Ahmad’s school, as expressly stated in most of his responses and adopted by the majority of his followers, and according to Abū Hanīfah’s school as well. However, according to the well-known position reported from Ahmad, if she is married off with her own permission and that of her brother, she has no option to rescind the marriage when

---

## Page 15649

she reaches puberty.

Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad according to one report, hold that she may be married off without her permission, but she has the option to rescind the marriage upon reaching puberty. This is also one of the two views in Mālik’s school. Another report from him states that if there is a need for her to marry and she is capable of intercourse, it is permissible. It has also been said that she may be married off, with the option to rescind the marriage upon reaching puberty.

Ibn Bashīr said: The later scholars agreed that she may be married off if there is fear that she might fall into corruption.

The “third” view, held by al-Shāfiʿī and by Ahmad according to the other report, is that she may not be married off before reaching puberty if she has neither a father nor a grandfather. They argue that she has no guardian empowered to compel her to marry, while her own permission is not legally valid before puberty. Thus, she cannot be married off on the basis of either her own permission or that of her guardian.

The “first view” is more correct, as indicated by the Quran, the Sunnah and sound reasoning. Allah Most High says:

“They ask you for a ruling concerning women. Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning them, and concerning what is recited to you in the Book about orphan girls whom you do not give what is prescribed for them, yet whom you wish to marry, and concerning helpless children, and that you should uphold justice for orphans.’ Whatever good you do, Allah is ever aware of it.”

It is authentically reported from ʿĀ’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—that this verse was revealed concerning an orphan girl in the care of her guardian. If she possessed wealth and beauty, he would marry her without giving her a fair dowry; but if she had no wealth, he would not marry her. He was therefore forbidden to marry her unless he gave her a fair dowry, because he would otherwise be disinclined to marry her if she had no wealth.

His words,

“Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning them, and what is recited to you in the Book,’”

mean: it gives you a ruling, and We give you a ruling concerning the helpless. It has thus been reported by

---

## Page 15650

‘Ā’ishah, in this authentic hadith recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, that this verse was revealed concerning an orphan girl in her guardian’s care, and that Allah permitted him to marry her provided he gave her a fair dowry. The account states that she was in his care, indicating that she was legally restricted in managing her own affairs.

Furthermore, it is established in the Sunan collections, through narrations from Abū Mūsā and Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“An orphan girl must not be married off until her permission is sought. If she remains silent, she has given permission; but if she refuses, no marriage may be imposed upon her.”

Thus, she may be married off with her permission, but not without it. He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—also said:

“There is no orphan status after puberty.”

Even if “orphan” were intended to include someone past puberty, this would be figurative usage, and the term would therefore have to encompass those both before and after puberty. Restricting the word “orphan” exclusively to those past puberty, however, is not a meaning the wording can bear under any circumstances.

Moreover, a discerning minor’s declarations are legally valid with his guardian’s permission, just as his entering the consecrated state for Hajj is valid with his guardian’s permission. Likewise, according to most scholars, his transactions involving sales and other matters are valid with his guardian’s permission. This is indicated by the Quran, where Allah says:

“Test the orphans until they reach marriageable age”—to the end of the verse.

He thus commanded that they be tested before puberty, and this may only be possible through sales transactions—although, according to the majority, neither a minor’s will nor his provision for a slave’s emancipation upon his death is valid. His embracing Islam is likewise valid, as are his fasting, prayer and other such acts, because of the benefit they bring him.

Thus, if her guardian marries her to a suitable match with her permission, the marriage is valid. This is an act undertaken with her permission and serves her interests; either of these two grounds suffices to validate the act of a discerning minor. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15651

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Regarding a girl who has not reached puberty, whom someone has come forward wishing to marry: may the judge marry her off?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the suitor is a suitable match for her, she may be married off according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and the well-known position reported from Ahmad. Some scholars hold that she may be married off without her consent, while retaining the right of choice. This is Abū Hanīfah’s position and one position reported from Ahmad. Others hold that once she reaches nine years of age, she may be married off with her permission, but has no right of choice upon reaching puberty. This is the apparent position of Ahmad’s school, based on the Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“An orphan girl must not be married off until her permission is sought. If she remains silent, she has given permission; but if she refuses, no marriage may be imposed upon her.”

This was narrated by Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasā’ī and others. The permissibility of marrying off an orphan girl is established by the Quran and the Sunnah. Allah Most High says:

“They ask you for a ruling concerning women. Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning them, as does what is recited to you in the Book concerning orphan girls whom you do not give what is prescribed for them, yet whom you wish to marry, and concerning helpless children.’”

It is authentically reported from ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—that this verse was revealed concerning an orphan girl whose guardian wishes to marry her if she has wealth, but does not marry her if she has none. They were therefore forbidden to marry such girls unless they treated them fairly regarding their dowries. Allah has thus permitted a guardian to arrange an orphan girl’s marriage, provided he gives her a dowry appropriate to women of her standing. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15652

He was also asked—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

“A man married a young orphan girl, and her marriage contract was concluded by a follower of the Shāfi‘ī school. She did not reach puberty until two months after the contract. Is this contract valid or not?”

He replied:

As for an “orphan girl” who has not yet reached puberty, no one other than the father, grandfather, brother, paternal uncle, or ruler—meaning the judge or his deputies responsible for marriage contracts—may compel her to marry. The jurists hold three opinions on this:

The first is that it is not permissible. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and Mālik, and one position reported from Imam Ahmad.

The second is that the marriage may be contracted without her permission, but she has the right to choose whether to remain married upon reaching puberty. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and one position reported from Ahmad.

The third is that she may be married off with her permission, but has no right of choice upon reaching puberty. This is the well-known position of Ahmad’s school.

Thus, the marriage of this girl who has not reached puberty is permissible according to the schools of Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad, and others.

If a judge who considers this permissible marries her off, does his marrying her off constitute

---

## Page 15653

a judicial ruling that cannot be overturned, or does it require another judge to rule on its validity?

There are two opinions on this within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others. The sounder of the two is the first.

However, the judge who performed this marriage is a Shāfi‘ī. If he followed the opinion of those who consider this marriage valid, observed all its other conditions, and was entitled to do so, then it was permissible. If, however, he proceeded with something he believed to be forbidden, his action was impermissible. If he thought she had reached puberty and married her off, but she had not reached puberty, then he did not in fact conclude her marriage, and the marriage is invalid. Allah knows best.

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

“A man found a little girl and raised her. When she reached puberty, the judge married her to him, and he was blessed with children by her. Later, it was discovered that she had a brother. Is this marriage valid?”

He replied:

If she had a brother who was absent and out of contact, and, because she had become separated from her family in childhood, it was not known until after the marriage that she had a brother, then the marriage in question is not invalidated. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15654

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

“An orphan girl has received a marriage proposal from a man who acts as an agent for various establishments in the city, but her mother’s husband dislikes him. May her paternal uncle and her brother marry her off without her consent?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. By agreement of the imams, no one other than her father or paternal grandfather may marry off a woman who has reached puberty without her consent. Indeed, according to one of the two scholarly opinions—the sounder of them—even her father may not marry her off without her consent. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and one of the two reported positions of Ahmad. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A virgin must not be married until her permission is sought, nor a previously married woman until she is consulted.” They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, but a virgin is shy?” He replied, “Her silence is her consent.”

Another wording states:

“Her father must seek her permission, and her silence is her consent.”

As for her paternal uncle and her brother, neither may marry her off without her consent, by agreement of the scholars. If she accepts a man who is a suitable match for her, her guardian—her brother, followed by her paternal uncle—is obliged to marry her to him. If he obstructs her marriage and refuses to marry her off, then a more distant guardian or the judge is to marry her to him without his permission, by agreement of the scholars.

A guardian has no right to compel her to marry someone she does not accept, nor to prevent her from marrying someone she accepts if he is a suitable match, by agreement of the imams. Such coercion and obstruction are practised only by the people of pre-Islamic ignorance and by wrongdoers, who marry the women under their guardianship to men of their own choosing for some ulterior purpose, rather than for the woman’s welfare. They force her into it or embarrass her into agreeing. They also prevent her from marrying a suitable match out of enmity

---

## Page 15655

or some ulterior purpose. All of this belongs to the practices of pre-Islamic ignorance, injustice, and transgression. Allah and His Messenger have forbidden it, and the Muslims unanimously agree that it is forbidden. Allah has obliged women’s guardians to consider the woman’s welfare, not their own desires. Like all other guardians and agents acting on someone else’s behalf, they must seek the welfare of the person they represent, not pursue their own desires. This is among the trusts that Allah has commanded to be discharged to those entitled to them. He says:

“Indeed, Allah commands you to render trusts to whom they are due and, when you judge between people, to judge with justice.”

This is also part of the obligatory duty of sincere counsel. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Religion is sincerity. Religion is sincerity. Religion is sincerity.” They asked, “Towards whom, O Messenger of Allah?” He replied, “Towards Allah, His Book, His Messenger, the leaders of the Muslims, and their common people.”

And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man married a woman, and she stayed with him for several days. Some people then came, claiming that she belonged to them. They took her from his house and looted his property while he was absent. Is it permissible to take her away when she is pregnant?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If it was not apparent to the husband that she was a slave woman, but he married her without qualification, as is customary, believing her to be free, and was told that she was free, then he has been deceived, and his child by her is free, not a slave. As for the “marriage,” it is invalid if the master does not ratify it, by agreement of the Muslims. If the master ratifies it, it is valid according to the schools of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, in one of the two reported positions; it is not valid according to the schools of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad, in the other reported position. Rather, there is a need

---

## Page 15656

for a new marriage contract.

If, however, she proves to be pregnant by someone other than her husband, the marriage is unquestionably invalid. He owes no dowry if he has not consummated the marriage. They have no right to take any of his property; rather, everything taken from him must be returned to him.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“May male slaves be married to slave women without being emancipated, if they belong to the same owner? Who concludes both sides of the marriage contract on their behalf? And what of their children? May the master take the women as concubines?”

He replied:

It is permissible to marry male slaves to slave women while they remain enslaved, whether they belong to one owner or two. The leading Muslim scholars agree on this. The person who gives a slave woman in marriage is her master or his agent. As for the male slave, if he is an adult, he accepts the marriage on his own behalf, or his agent accepts on his behalf. If he is a minor, his master accepts on his behalf.

If both spouses belong to him, he says in the presence of two witnesses, “I marry my male slave So-and-so to my slave woman So-and-so.” The marriage is thereby contracted.

As for an adult male slave, may his master marry him off without his permission and compel him to marry? The scholars hold two views:

The first is that this is not permissible. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

The second is that he may compel him. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik. As for a slave woman and a minor male slave, he may marry them off without their permission, by scholarly agreement.

---

## Page 15657

As for “children,” they follow their mother in “freedom and slavery,” and their father in lineage and patronage, by agreement of the Muslims. Whoever owns the mother owns her children, whether they are born from marriage or fornication. Likewise, when male animals—horses, camels or donkeys—mate with females, the offspring belong to the owner of the mother. If the mother has been emancipated or was born free, while the father is enslaved, the children are free.

As for “lineage,” their descent is traced through their father. If both parents have been emancipated, the children are affiliated to the father’s patrons. If the father is enslaved, they are affiliated to the mother’s patrons. If the father is subsequently emancipated, the bond of patronage transfers from the mother’s patrons to the father’s patrons. This is the position of the four imams.

Whoever owns the mother owns her children. He may take daughters born to his slave women as concubines, provided he has not had sexual relations with their mother; in that case, he may have sexual relations with her daughters. If, however, he has had sexual relations with the mother, he may not have sexual relations with her daughters. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man of noble lineage married his adult virgin daughter, with her consent and permission, to a man from the Maghrib who was not of noble lineage but was known among the people for his righteousness. The father did not have her consent witnessed. Does this impair the validity of the marriage contract, even though the wife remained consenting both before and after consummation? Someone challenged its validity, whereupon the wife declared before witnesses that she had given her consent and permission. Does the marriage contract therefore need to be renewed?”

---

## Page 15658

He replied:

According to the four schools, the validity of a marriage does not depend on having witnesses attest to the woman’s permission before the marriage, apart from a weak opinion within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. Rather, he said: If the guardian says, “She has given me permission,” the marriage contract may be concluded, and witnesses may attest to the guardian and the husband entering into it. If the woman subsequently denies having given permission, the marriage remains established. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

According to Abū Hanīfah, Mālik and one report from Ahmad, however, if she had not given permission before the marriage contract was concluded, it is valid. This is called “the question of contracts held in abeyance.” The same disagreement applies when a slave marries without his masters’ permission.

As for “compatibility in lineage,” Mālik takes lineage into account. According to Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī and one of the two reports from Ahmad, such compatibility is a right belonging to the wife and both parents. If they agree to someone who is not a compatible match, the marriage is valid. According to Ahmad, it is a right of Allah, and the marriage is therefore invalid where such compatibility is lacking. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man married his brother’s daughter to his own son. The husband is an evildoer who does not pray. They intimidated her until she gave permission for the marriage, telling her, ‘If you do not give permission, you will be married off under Islamic law without your consent.’ He now takes her property and prevents people, including her mother and others, from visiting her to ascertain her circumstances?”

---

## Page 15659

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. By agreement of the imams, neither a paternal uncle nor any other guardian may marry a woman under his guardianship to an incompatible match without her consent. If he does so, he deserves a punishment under Islamic law that will deter him and others like him from such conduct. Indeed, even if she consents to an incompatible match, another guardian besides the one who contracted the marriage may have it annulled. A paternal uncle has no right to compel an adult woman to marry even a compatible match; how much more objectionable, then, is compelling her to marry an incompatible one! Rather, by the consensus of the Muslims, he may marry her only to someone she accepts.

If he tells her, “If you do not give permission, you will be married off under Islamic law without your consent,” and she gives permission because of this, neither that permission nor the marriage based upon it is valid. By agreement of the imams, Islamic law does not empower anyone other than the father and grandfather to compel a minor girl to marry. The scholars differed only concerning “the father and grandfather” in the case of an adult woman, and concerning minor girls generally.

If her husband has married her under a valid marriage contract, he must fulfil his obligations towards her and must not wrong her in her person or her property. He is liable for whatever he has taken of her property. If she complains, he has no right to prevent anyone from ascertaining her circumstances. He must either allow someone, such as her mother or another person, to visit her and ascertain her circumstances, or arrange for her to live next to truthful, religiously upright neighbours who can ascertain her circumstances. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15660

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him—about a man who owned a slave who had restrained himself and wished to marry: may the slave marry or not?

He replied:

Yes, he may marry, according to the principle upheld by those who require the master to arrange his marriage. This is the position of Ahmad and one of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two opinions. Arranging his marriage, when he needs it, is like providing for his maintenance. Allah Most High says:

“Marry off those among you who are unmarried, and the righteous among your male and female slaves.”

He thus commands that male and female slaves be married off, just as He commands that unmarried people be married off. If a female slave requests marriage to a compatible match, arranging her marriage is obligatory by agreement of the scholars. Those who grant the male slave permission to marry are the owner of half of him, or that owner’s agent, and the administrator of the share held as an endowment.

He was also asked about a man who married a freedwoman of one of the daughters of kings who purchase slaves with their own wealth and with the Muslims’ wealth, without the permission of the woman who emancipated her: is the marriage contract valid or not?

He replied:

If the woman’s emancipation was lawfully effected from her mistress’s own wealth, then guardianship belongs to her mistress by agreement of the scholars. She is the one who inherits from the freedwoman, followed after her by her closest male agnatic relatives.

---

## Page 15661

As for marrying this “freedwoman” without her emancipator’s permission, there are two well-known scholarly opinions. Those who do not require the guardian’s permission—such as Abū Hanīfa and, according to one of the two reports from him, Mālik—consider this marriage valid. Those who require the guardian’s permission, such as al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, have two opinions on this issue, both reported from Ahmad.

The “first” is that she may not be married off without her emancipator’s permission, since the latter holds the position of an agnatic relative to her. On this view, may the emancipating woman herself contract the freedwoman’s marriage? There are two opinions, both reported from Ahmad.

The “second” is that contracting her marriage does not require her emancipator’s permission, because a woman cannot be her own marriage guardian and therefore cannot be another woman’s guardian. Moreover, since they hold that she may not herself contract a marriage, her permission is not required. On this view, the freedwoman’s marriage is contracted, with her consent, by the person entitled to contract her emancipator’s marriage—for example, the emancipator’s brother or someone similarly placed—provided that he is qualified to act as a marriage guardian. If he is not qualified and the judge contracts her marriage, it is valid; otherwise, it is not. If they are qualified according to Abū Hanīfa, the right of patronage belongs to them, while the judge contracts her marriage.

He was also asked:

A man proposed to a woman. When inquiry was made about how he supported himself, the answer given was that some of his income came from government sources. The guardian therefore refused to marry her to him. The suitor stated that the Hanafī jurists permitted receiving such funds. Has any scholar stated that receiving funds from these sources is permissible? May the guardian in question reject the suitor on this ground, despite the prospective bride’s consent?

---

## Page 15662

He replied:

None of the leading jurists whose opinions are relied upon in issuing fatwas declared this permissible. However, in the early days of the “Seljuk” dynasty, a group of Hanafī and Shāfiʿī jurists issued fatwas permitting it. Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm reported in “his book” that the scholars unanimously agreed that it was forbidden.

“Nūr al-Dīn Mahmūd, the Turkish martyr,” abolished all the newly imposed levies in Syria, Upper Mesopotamia, Egypt, and the Hijaz. He was exceptionally knowledgeable about jihad. It was he who restored Islam after the “Franks and Qarmatians” had seized even more extensive territories.

Someone who acts according to what he believes permissible on the basis of a defensible interpretation—particularly when he is in need—is not deemed sinful merely for doing so. Nevertheless, a guardian may prevent the woman under his guardianship from marrying someone who receives income of this kind which the guardian believes unlawful, especially since her maintenance would come from it. If, however, the husband provides her food from another source, or she obtains her food elsewhere, the guardian may marry her to him, provided that the husband relies on an interpretive justification concerning what he consumes.

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man married his daughter to someone without knowing his true character. The husband lived with his wife for years, and then the guardian and the wife discovered his filthiness, corruption, wine-drinking, lying, and treacherous oaths. She subsequently became irrevocably divorced from him through a threefold divorce. May the guardian proceed to marry her to him again, or not? The guardian has repeatedly called upon the husband to repent, but he has broken his pledges and has not mended his ways. Is it lawful to marry her to him?

---

## Page 15663

He replied:

If he persists in sinful conduct, the guardian should not give her in marriage to him. As one of the early generations said:

“Whoever marries his beloved daughter to a wicked man has severed her ties of kinship.”

But if the guardian knows that he has repented, she may be married to him, provided he is a suitable match for her and she consents to marry him. As for a marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband, it is established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Allah has cursed the man who marries her to make her lawful for her former husband, and the former husband for whom this is done.”

The scholars unanimously agree that a woman may not be compelled to enter such a marriage.

He was also asked whether one may marry into the Rāfidīs.

He replied:

The Rāfidīs proper are people of deviant inclinations, religious innovations, and misguidance. A Muslim should not give a woman under his guardianship in marriage to a Rāfidī. If, however, he himself marries a Rāfidī woman, the marriage is valid if he hopes that she will repent. Otherwise, it is better not to marry her, lest she corrupt his children. Allah knows best.

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Is marriage to a Rāfidī, or to someone who says that the five daily prayers are not obligatory upon them, valid, whether that person is a man or a woman? If such a person repents of Rāfidī beliefs and observes prayer for a time, but then returns to their former ways, may the existing marriage be allowed to continue?

He replied:

No one may give a woman under his guardianship in marriage to a Rāfidī or to someone who abandons prayer. If they give her in marriage to him on the understanding that he is a Sunnī, and he performs the five daily prayers, but it later becomes apparent that he is a Rāfidī who does not pray—or he returns to Rāfidī beliefs and abandons prayer—then they are to annul the marriage.

---

## Page 15664

Chapter: Women Forbidden in Marriage

A General Rule Concerning Women Forbidden in Marriage through Blood or Marital Ties

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked to explain this briefly.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. As for women forbidden in marriage through blood relationship, the governing rule is that all of a man’s female blood relatives are forbidden to him except the daughters of his paternal uncles, maternal uncles, paternal aunts, and maternal aunts.

These four categories are those whom Allah made lawful for His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, when He said:

“O Prophet, We have made lawful for you your wives to whom you have given their dowries, those whom your right hand possesses from what Allah has granted you, the daughters of your paternal uncles, paternal aunts, maternal uncles, and maternal aunts who emigrated with you, and a believing woman if she offers herself to the Prophet and the Prophet wishes to marry her—a privilege for you alone, not for the other believers.”

And so the verse continues. Thus Allah, glorified be He, made four categories of women lawful for His Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. Of these, He made only the woman who offers herself to the Prophet exclusively lawful for him, to the exclusion of other believers. He made this one of his unique privileges: he could marry a woman who offered herself to him without a dowry. By the unanimous agreement of the Muslims, this is not permitted to anyone else.

Rather, no one else may lawfully have marital relations with a woman unless a dowry is due to her, as Allah Most High says:

“Lawful to you are all women beyond these, provided that you seek them with your wealth in marriage, not in fornication.”

The scholars agree that if a man marries a woman without specifying a dowry, the marriage is valid, and she becomes entitled to a dowry if he consummates the marriage with her. If he divorces her before consummation, she is not entitled to a dowry; rather,

---

## Page 15665

she is entitled to a parting gift, as expressly stated in the Quran. If her husband dies, there are two opinions concerning her case.

This is the case of Birwa‘ bint Wāshiq, concerning which Ibn Mas‘ūd was consulted over the course of a month. He then said:

“I shall give my own judgment concerning her. If it is correct, it is from Allah; if it is mistaken, it is from me and from Satan, and Allah and His Messenger are innocent of it. She is entitled to the dowry customary among women of her standing, neither less nor more. She must observe the waiting period and is entitled to inherit.”

Men from Ashja‘ then stood up and said: “We testify that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, ruled concerning Birwa‘ bint Wāshiq just as you have ruled concerning this woman.”

‘Alqamah said: “I never saw ‘Abd Allāh rejoice at anything as much as he rejoiced at that.”

The ruling given by Ibn Mas‘ūd is the position of the jurists of Kufa, such as Abū Hanīfah and others, and of the hadith jurists, such as Ahmad and others. It is also one of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two opinions. His other opinion, which is also Mālik’s position, is that she is not entitled to a dowry. This is also reported from ‘Alī, Zayd, and other Companions.

They also differed over whether a marriage is valid when it is stipulated that there shall be no dowry. There are two opinions within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. The first is that the marriage is invalid, as Mālik held. The second is that it is valid and that a dowry customary for women of her standing is due, as Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī held.

Those who hold the first opinion say that this is nikāh al-shighār (reciprocal marriage), which the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, declared invalid, because the dowry is excluded and marital access to one woman is made the dowry for marital access to the other. This is the reasoning Ahmad ibn Hanbal gives in several places in his statements, and it is also the reasoning of most of his early followers.

Among those who hold the other opinion, some regard reciprocal marriage as valid, such as Abū Hanīfah. His position is more consistent with this principle, but it contradicts the explicit text and the reports from the Companions, for they declared reciprocal marriage invalid. Others among them regard it as invalid

---

## Page 15666

and explain its invalidity either by claiming that it involves shared marital access or on other grounds, as do the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and those followers of Ahmad who agree with them, such as al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā and his followers.

The first opinion accords more closely with the revealed text and sound analogy, as has been explained at length elsewhere. They also differed over whether a marriage with a dowry can be contracted using expressions such as “transfer of ownership,” “gift,” and the like. The majority permitted this, including Mālik and Abū Hanīfah. Ahmad’s explicit statements and the statements of his early followers also support this position. Al-Shāfi‘ī and most of Ahmad’s later followers, such as Ibn Hāmid, al-Qādī, and their followers, disallowed it. I know of no follower of Ahmad before Ibn Hāmid who held this view.

The point here is that the only form of marriage Allah Most High reserved exclusively for His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, was marriage to a woman who offered herself as a gift, as He says:

“And a believing woman if she offers herself to the Prophet, if the Prophet wishes to marry her—exclusively for you, not for the other believers.”

This indicates that everything else He made lawful for His Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is lawful for his community. His words also establish this:

“So when Zayd had ended his marriage to her, We gave her to you in marriage, so that there should be no restriction upon believers concerning the former wives of their adopted sons once those sons have ended their marriages to them.”

Thus, when He made marriage to the former wife of an adopted son lawful, particularly for the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, so that it would thereby be lawful for the believers, this showed that what He makes lawful for him is lawful for his community. Among his relatives, He permitted him to marry the daughters of his paternal uncles and aunts and the daughters of his maternal uncles and aunts. Singling these out for mention indicates that other female relatives are forbidden, especially since He subsequently said:

“No women are lawful for you beyond these, nor may you exchange them for other wives.”

That is, beyond these women

---

## Page 15667

whom We have made lawful for you. These are those mentioned in the words of the Most High:

“Forbidden to you in marriage are your mothers, your daughters, your sisters, your paternal aunts, your maternal aunts, your brothers’ daughters, and your sisters’ daughters.”

“Mothers” includes one’s paternal and maternal grandmothers, however far up the line, with no disagreement among the scholars that I know of. Likewise, “daughters” includes one’s son’s daughter and one’s daughter’s son’s daughter, however far down the line, with no disagreement that I know of. “Sisters” likewise includes full sisters and half-sisters through either parent. “Paternal aunts” and “maternal aunts” include the paternal and maternal aunts of both parents. “Brothers’ and sisters’ daughters” includes the female descendants of one’s siblings, however far down the line.

Thus, if his ascendants, his descendants, and the descendants of his more distant ascendants are forbidden to him—excluding the daughters of his paternal uncles and aunts and his maternal uncles and aunts—then, concerning “women forbidden through marriage,” he says that all women related to him through marriage are lawful for him except four categories. This contrasts with blood relatives: all a person’s female blood relatives are forbidden to him except four categories, whereas female relatives on either spouse’s side are all lawful except four categories: the wives of fathers, the wives of sons, the mothers of wives, and their daughters.

Each spouse is therefore forbidden to marry the other’s ascendants and descendants. A man is forbidden to marry his wife’s mother, her maternal and paternal grandmothers, and so on, however far up the line. He is also forbidden to marry his wife’s daughter—his stepdaughter—and her daughter’s daughter, however far down the line. His stepson’s daughter is likewise forbidden to him, as expressly stated by the well-known imams, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others. I know of no disagreement concerning this. He is also forbidden to marry his father’s wife, however far up the line, or his son’s wife, however far down the line.

These “four categories” are the women forbidden through marriage in the Book of Allah; and each spouse

---

## Page 15668

has the other’s relatives as in-laws. A man’s relatives are his wife’s ahmā’ (in-laws through her husband), and a woman’s relatives are her husband’s akhtān (in-laws through his wife). These four categories become forbidden in marriage upon conclusion of the marriage contract, except for the stepdaughter: she does not become forbidden until the marriage to her mother has been consummated. Allah stipulated this condition only for the stepdaughter, leaving the prohibition concerning the others unrestricted.

For this reason, the Companions said: “Leave unrestricted what Allah has left unrestricted.” This is the position of the four imams and the overwhelming majority of scholars. As for the daughters and mothers of these two—the father’s wife and the son’s wife—they are not forbidden. A man may therefore marry the daughter of his father’s wife or his son’s wife, by scholarly consensus, since she is not herself one of the wives of fathers or sons. The word halīla means “wife,” and a wife’s daughter and mother are not themselves wives. The stepdaughter is different, since a stepchild’s child is also a stepchild, just as one’s child’s child is also one’s child. Likewise, the mother of one’s wife’s mother is also a mother of one’s wife. A daughter of one’s wife’s mother, however, is not forbidden, since she is not a mother.

For this reason, some jurists have said: “The daughters of women forbidden in marriage are also forbidden, except the daughters of paternal aunts, maternal aunts, wives’ mothers, and fathers’ and sons’ wives.” Thus, they regarded a stepdaughter’s daughter as forbidden, unlike the daughters of the other three categories. I know of no disagreement concerning this.

If a man has intercourse with a woman under what he believes to be a marriage, paternity is attributed to him and the marriage prohibitions arising from affinity are established, by scholarly consensus as far as I know—even if that marriage is invalid before Allah and His Messenger. An example is a disbeliever who contracts a marriage forbidden in Islam: paternity is attributed to him through it, and affinity is established. Each spouse is therefore forbidden to marry the other’s ascendants and descendants, by scholarly consensus. The same applies to any intercourse that a person believes is not unlawful, although it is in fact unlawful, such as

---

## Page 15669

a man who contracts an invalid marriage with a woman, divorces her, and then supposes that the divorce did not take effect, owing to his own error or that of the person who gave him a fatwa. He subsequently has intercourse with her, and she bears him a child. In this case, paternity is attributed to him, and she is regarded as a woman with whom marriage has been consummated. She therefore becomes forbidden in marriage, even if she has a mother with whom he has not consummated marriage, by scholarly consensus.

Thus, if a disbeliever marries a woman in a marriage he considers valid according to his religion, and his son subsequently accepts Islam—as happened among the Arabs whose children accepted Islam, and as frequently happens today—he may not marry his son’s wife, even if her marriage was invalid, by scholarly consensus. Paternity is established on the basis of the belief that intercourse was lawful, even if that belief was mistaken. Affinity follows paternity; when paternity is established, affinity is established all the more readily.

Likewise, “the child’s free status” depends on his father’s belief. A child follows his father in “lineage and free status,” and follows his mother in this, by scholarly consensus. In religion, the child follows whichever parent has the better religion, according to the overwhelming majority of scholars. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad, and one of the two positions in Mālik’s school.

If a man has intercourse with another person’s slave woman, whether through marriage or fornication, his child is a slave belonging to her master. But if he purchases her from someone he believes to be her owner, or marries her believing her to be free, he is called “the deceived man.” Her child is free by agreement of the imams, because he believed he was having intercourse with a woman whose child by him would be free. Paternity and free status thus depend on the belief of the man having intercourse, even if he is mistaken; the same applies to marriage prohibitions arising from affinity.

The scholars differed only concerning outright fornication: does it establish marriage prohibitions through affinity? This is a well-known disagreement among earlier and later scholars. Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad hold that it does establish these prohibitions, whereas al-Shāfi‘ī holds that it does not. Two positions are reported from Mālik.

---

## Page 15670

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had a concubine under a written deed, then passed away into Allah’s mercy. His son’s son subsequently married this concubine of his grandfather. Is that lawful?

He replied:

It is not permissible for him to marry his grandfather’s concubine with whom his grandfather had intercourse, by the consensus of the Muslims. If he marries her, they must be separated; he may not remain with her. If he considers this lawful, he must be called upon to repent three times. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be put to death.

The Shaykh—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—also said:

Section

As for the prohibition of “combining women in marriage,” a man may not be married simultaneously to two sisters, as expressly stated in the Quran, nor to a woman and her paternal aunt, nor to a woman and her maternal aunt. Neither may the older woman be married while the younger is already his wife, nor the younger while the older is already his wife. It is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade this. It is reported that he said:

“If you do that, you will sever your ties of kinship.”

Even if one of the two agrees to his marrying the other alongside her, it remains impermissible, for feelings change. This is illustrated by the report: “When Umm Habība proposed to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, that he marry her sister, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, asked her, ‘Would you like that?’ She replied:

---

## Page 15671

‘I do not have you to myself, and my sister is the one most entitled to share this good with me.’ He said, ‘She is not lawful for me.’ He was then told, ‘We hear that you intend to marry Durra, the daughter of Abū Salama.’ He replied, ‘Even if she were not my stepdaughter under my care, she would still not be lawful for me, for she is the daughter of my brother through breastfeeding. Thuwayba, Abū Lahab’s slave woman, nursed both me and her father, Abū Salama. So do not offer me your daughters or your sisters in marriage.’” This is agreed upon among the scholars.

The “governing rule” here is that a man may not be married simultaneously to any two women whose blood relationship would preclude their marriage to one another if one of them were male. Kinship that precludes marriage entails “four rulings”: two are agreed upon, and two are disputed.

Such relatives may neither enter into marriage with one another nor have sexual intercourse. Thus, a man may neither marry a female relative whom he is permanently forbidden to marry nor take her as a concubine. This is agreed upon. Indeed, in this regard, breastfeeding prohibits what blood relationship prohibits: such a woman is lawful to him neither through marriage nor through ownership as a slave.

Nor may he be married simultaneously to two women related in this way. He may not combine two sisters in marriage, or a woman and her paternal aunt, or a woman and her maternal aunt. This, too, is agreed upon.

He may own both women as slaves, but he may not take both as concubines. Those who prohibit combining them in marriage also prohibit combining them as concubines. Thus, he may not take two sisters as concubines, or a slave woman and her paternal aunt, or a slave woman and her maternal aunt. This is the position on which the majority of the Companions ultimately settled, and it is the view of most scholars.

They agree that a man may not take as a concubine a woman forbidden to him through blood relationship or breastfeeding. Their disagreement concerned only combining two women. Some of the Companions therefore withheld judgment on this matter, saying: “One verse made both lawful, while both were made unlawful by

---

## Page 15672

another verse.” He thought that the prohibition on combining such women might resemble the restriction on their number: a man may take as many concubines as he wishes, but may marry no more than four wives. Both prohibitions are circumstantial, unlike prohibitions arising from blood relationship or marriage, which are permanent. Thus, the latter make a woman one of his permanently unmarriageable relatives, whereas the former do not. His wife’s sister, for example, is not a permanently unmarriageable relative: he may neither be alone with her nor travel with her, just as he may not be alone with other women once he has four wives, since marrying beyond that number is forbidden. The majority, however, categorically held that combining such women as concubines is forbidden. This is the established position of the four imams and others.

They reasoned that everything Allah has forbidden in the verse with respect to marriage is also forbidden with respect to concubinage. The verse granting permission—His words,

“or those your right hands possess”—

merely permits slave women as a general category. It does not specify which forms of concubinage are lawful or unlawful, just as it does not specify which women may or may not lawfully be married with a dowry. Intercourse with a woman is forbidden when she is observing a waiting period or is in the state of consecration for pilgrimage, whether she is a wife or a concubine.

The restriction on the number of wives was imposed because of the obligation to divide one’s time equitably among them, as the Most High says:

“If you fear that you will not deal justly with orphans, then marry women of your choice, two, three, or four. But if you fear that you will not be just, then marry only one, or those your right hands possess. That is more likely to prevent you from being unjust.”

That is, so that you do not act unjustly in dividing your time. This is how the early generations and the majority of scholars explained it. A group of scholars, however, thought that it meant that one’s dependants should not become numerous. They said that this indicates the obligation to provide maintenance for one’s wife. Most scholars judged this interpretation mistaken, both linguistically and in meaning.

As for the wording, one says ‘āla, ya‘ūlu when someone acts unjustly; ‘āla, ya‘īlu when he becomes poor; and a‘āla, yu‘īlu when his dependants become numerous. He, glorified is He, said:

“ta‘ūlū,”

not tu‘īlū. As for the meaning, increased expenditure and numerous dependants can result from concubinage just as they can from marriage. Nevertheless, He has permitted

---

## Page 15673

as many concubines as a man wishes, without any numerical limit, because slave women are not entitled to an equitable division of his time, nor do they have a right to intercourse with him. Thus, he may own a woman with whom intercourse is unlawful for him, such as his wife’s mother or daughter, or his sister or daughter through breastfeeding. Even if he were impotent or had sworn to abstain from intercourse, he would not be required to relinquish ownership of her. With wives, however, he must divide his time equitably among them.

“The best company consists of four.”

The equitable treatment that most people can manage therefore extends to four. As for the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Allah empowered him to deal justly with more than that—according to the well-known view that he was obliged to divide his time equitably. According to the other view, he was exempt from this obligation. Likewise, because he had a greater claim upon the believers than they had upon themselves, he was permitted to marry without a dowry.

They said:

Since the “prohibition on exceeding the permitted number of wives” rests solely on the obligation to divide one’s time equitably, and this consideration does not apply to slave women, he is not forbidden to take more than four concubines. This differs from taking two sisters together: that prohibition serves to prevent the severing of kinship ties between them, a consideration that applies to two slave women just as it does to two wives. If he takes both sisters as concubines, the same jealousy will arise between them as would arise if he married them both, leading to the severing of kinship ties.

Since this consideration is the operative basis of the ruling in Islamic law, he may marry two women together if they have a relationship that bars intermarriage but no blood ties, or blood ties that do not bar intermarriage. An example of the first is marrying both a woman and her husband’s daughter. Thus, after the death of ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar was married to both ʿAlī’s widow and his daughter.

---

## Page 15674

This is permitted according to most scholars—the four imams and others. Although marriage between these two women would be prohibited, that prohibition arises from ties of marriage, not blood ties. The underlying reason for the prohibition is to prevent the severing of kinship ties. Thus, this case falls outside both the wording and the meaning of the verse establishing the prohibition.

As for two women whose blood relationship does not bar marriage, such as female cousins on the paternal or maternal side, marrying both at once is permissible. But is it disliked? There are two views, both reported from Ahmad, because they share blood ties, though not within the prohibited degrees.

As for “the two disputed rulings”: may a person own a blood relative within the prohibited degrees of marriage? And may he separate two such relatives in ownership by selling one but not the other? Both questions are disputed, and this is not the place to discuss the various positions.

“The prohibition on concurrent marriage” ceases when the marriage ends. Thus, if one of four wives, or one of two sisters, dies—or he divorces her or her marriage is annulled, and her waiting period has ended—he may marry a fourth wife or the other sister, by scholarly agreement. If, however, he divorces her revocably, he may not marry the other woman according to the overwhelming majority of scholars—the four imams and others.

ʿUbayda al-Salmānī reported:

“On no matter were the Companions of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as united as they were that a fifth woman may not be married during the fourth wife’s waiting period, nor may a woman be married during her sister’s waiting period.”

This is because a revocably divorced woman retains the status of a wife: each spouse inherits from the other. Although she is heading toward an irrevocable separation, that does not prevent her from remaining a wife. The same applies when he makes her divorce contingent on a future date, as when he says, “If you give me a thousand at the end of the year, you are divorced.” This woman, too, is heading toward an irrevocable separation

---

## Page 15675

of the lesser degree; nevertheless, she remains a wife by scholarly agreement. If it is objected that she cannot give him the compensation on which the divorce is conditional, so the marriage continues, the reply is that he may likewise take back a revocably divorced wife, so the marriage continues.

The same applies if he says, “If you do not give birth this month, you are divorced,” when she has only one divorce remaining. Here, she remains his wife, and her marriage does not end unless the month passes without her giving birth, even though she is heading toward an irrevocable separation. The scholars disagree only over whether he may have intercourse with her, just as they disagree over intercourse with a revocably divorced wife.

If, however, the divorce is irrevocable, may he marry a fifth woman during the fourth wife’s waiting period, or marry a woman during her sister’s waiting period? This is a well-known point of disagreement among both the early and later scholars. Permissibility is the position of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, while prohibition is the position of Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15676

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

About people among whom one man marries another’s sister, and the other marries his sister or daughter. Whenever one provides maintenance, the other does likewise; whenever one provides clothing, the other does likewise. The same applies in everything, including pleasing and angering: if one is pleased, so is the other, and if one provokes anger, the other does likewise. Is this permissible?

He replied:

Each husband must either keep his wife on honorable terms or release her with kindness. He may not make this conditional upon the other husband’s conduct. A woman has rights over her husband, and her rights are not forfeited because of wrongdoing by her father or brother. Allah Most High says:

“No bearer of burdens will bear the burden of another.”

If one of them wrongs his wife, her rights must be enforced against him. The other man may not wrong his own wife because she is the first man’s daughter. If each wrongs his wife because of the other’s wrongdoing, both deserve punishment, and each wife is entitled to demand her rights from her husband.

Even if this were stipulated in the marriage contract, it would be an invalid condition of the same kind as “exchange marriage,” whereby a man gives his sister or daughter in marriage on condition that the other man give him his daughter or sister in marriage. How much worse, then, if he gives her in marriage on condition that, if one treats his wife fairly, the other does likewise, but if one wrongs his wife, the other wrongs his own wife! This is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. Anyone who does this deserves a punishment that will deter him from such conduct.

---

## Page 15677

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man is married to someone’s maternal aunt. That person has a daughter, whom the man also marries, thus being married to both his maternal aunt and his daughter. Is this marriage valid?

He replied:

It is not permissible for a man to be married simultaneously to another man’s maternal aunt and daughter, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“forbade being married simultaneously to a woman and her paternal aunt, or to a woman and her maternal aunt.”

The four imams agree on this. They also agree that this hadith encompasses the maternal aunt of one’s father, mother, or grandmother, as well as the paternal aunt of either parent. Thus, according to the four imams, a man may not be married simultaneously to a woman and her father’s maternal aunt, or her mother’s maternal aunt.

He was also asked:

A man has married both another man’s maternal aunt and the daughter of that man’s full brother. Is it permissible for him to be married to both women simultaneously?

He replied:

Being married to both these women constitutes being married simultaneously to a woman and her father’s maternal aunt; for if her father is the other man’s brother through their mother, or through both their mother and father, then

---

## Page 15678

the maternal aunt of one would also be the maternal aunt of the other. This is unlike the case where they are brothers through their father alone: the maternal aunt of one would not be the maternal aunt of the other; rather, she would be his paternal aunt.

According to the imams of the Muslims, being married simultaneously to a woman and her father’s maternal aunt, her mother’s maternal aunt, her father’s paternal aunt, or her mother’s paternal aunt is like being married simultaneously to a woman and her own paternal or maternal aunt. They unanimously agree that this is forbidden.

If he marries one after the other, the second marriage is void and requires no divorce to dissolve it. The contract creates no entitlement to a dower or inheritance, and he may not consummate the marriage with her. If he has consummated it, he must separate from her, just as he must keep apart from a woman to whom he is not married. If he wishes to marry the second woman, he must separate from the first; once her waiting period has ended, he may marry the second.

If he marries the second woman during the first woman’s waiting period following a revocable divorce, the second contract is invalid by agreement of the imams. If the divorce is irrevocable, this is not permissible according to the schools of Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad, but it is permissible according to the schools of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī.

If he divorces her once or twice without compensation, the divorce is revocable, and marriage to the second woman is invalid until the first woman’s waiting period has ended, by agreement of the imams. If he nevertheless marries the second woman, he may not consummate the marriage with her. If he has intercourse with her under this invalid marriage, he must keep apart from her, for she is not his lawful wife. He may not enter into a marriage contract with her until the waiting period of the first, divorced woman has ended, by agreement of the imams.

May he marry this woman, with whom he had intercourse under an invalid marriage, during her waiting period resulting from that intercourse? The scholars hold two views:

The first: It is permissible. This is the position of the schools of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī.

The second: It is not permissible. This is the position of Mālik’s school. Both views are found in Ahmad’s school.

---

## Page 15679

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man purchased a slave woman and had intercourse with her, then transferred ownership of her to his son. Is his son permitted to have intercourse with her?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. In these circumstances, the son is not permitted to have intercourse with her after his father has done so, by the consensus of the Muslims. Anyone who regards this as lawful must be called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is to be executed. The Sunan collections contain the following report:

Al-Barā’ ibn ‘Āzib said: “I saw my maternal uncle Abū Burda carrying his banner and asked him, ‘Where are you going?’ He replied, ‘The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has sent me to a man who married his father’s wife, ordering me to behead him and take one-fifth of his property.’”

The imams do not dispute that there is no difference between the father having intercourse with her within marriage and his doing so by right of ownership.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man married a woman a year ago but did not consummate the marriage, and divorced her before having intercourse with her. May he consummate a marriage with her mother after divorcing the daughter?

He replied:

He is not permitted to marry his wife’s mother, even if he did not consummate the marriage with her daughter. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15680

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man divorced his wife while she was breastfeeding his child. She remained divorced for eight months, then married another man. She stayed with him for a full month, after which he divorced her. She then remained divorced for three months. She did not menstruate during the first eight months, during her marriage to the second man, or during the final three months. Then her first husband, the child’s father, married her again. Are both of these marriage contracts valid, or is either one valid?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Neither the first nor the second contract is valid. Rather, she must complete the waiting period from her first husband, then observe the waiting period from her second husband. Once both waiting periods have ended, she may marry whichever of them she wishes. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man married a woman three years ago and had a child with her who is now two years old. She stated that, when she married him, she had experienced only two menstrual periods, and her husband accepted her statement as true. He had divorced her a second time under this marriage contract. Can a divorce take effect under this annulled marriage contract?

---

## Page 15681

He replied:

If the husband accepts her statement that she married before her third menstrual period, the marriage is invalid. He must separate from her, and she must complete the waiting period from her first husband, then observe a waiting period on account of intercourse with the second husband. If she had experienced her third menstrual period before the second husband had intercourse with her, her waiting period from the first husband had ended. When the second husband separates from her, she must observe a waiting period of three menstrual periods on his account. She may then marry whomever she wishes under a new marriage contract. His child is legitimate and legally recognized as his offspring, even if born of intercourse under an invalid marriage contract whose invalidity was unknown.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A divorced woman claimed and swore that she had completed her waiting period, so a second husband married her. Then another woman came forward and alleged that she had experienced only two menstrual periods, and the husband believed her. What is the ruling?

He replied:

If she had experienced only two menstrual periods, the second marriage is invalid by agreement of the imams. If the husband believes this woman's statement, the couple must be separated. The wife must complete her waiting period from the first husband with one further menstrual period, then observe a full waiting period on account of intercourse with the second husband. After that, if the second husband wishes to marry her, he may do so.

He was asked:

What is the ruling concerning a woman who was irrevocably divorced and then married a month and a half later, having experienced only one menstrual period?

He replied:

She must separate from this second husband and complete her waiting period from the first husband with two further menstrual periods. After that, she must observe a waiting period of three menstrual periods on account of intercourse with the second husband. He may then marry her under a new marriage contract.

---

## Page 15682

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man entered into a marriage contract on the understanding that the bride was of age. He neither consummated the marriage nor had intercourse with her, then divorced her three times. Another man then married her, but likewise neither consummated the marriage nor had intercourse with her, then divorced her three times. May the first husband marry her again?

He replied:

If he divorces her before consummating the marriage, the ruling, according to the four imams, is the same as if he divorced her after consummation: she is not lawful for him until she marries another husband who consummates the marriage with her. If the second husband divorces her before consummation, she does not become lawful for the first.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a virgin girl, then divorced her three times without having had intercourse with her. May he enter into a second marriage contract with her or not?

He replied:

According to most imams, divorcing a virgin three times has the same ruling as divorcing three times a woman with whom the marriage has been consummated.

---

## Page 15683

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning someone who says that a woman who has been divorced three times becomes lawful again for the man who divorced her three times without a second marriage: has any Muslim held this view? What are the consequences for someone who says this? And what are the consequences for someone who considers her lawful after three divorces have taken effect, without a second marriage? What are the requirements of the second marriage that makes her lawful for the first husband? Give us your ruling—may you be rewarded and recompensed, and may Allah have mercy on you.

He—may Allah be pleased with him—replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. When three divorces have taken effect concerning a woman, she is forbidden to the man who divorced her until she marries another husband, according to the Book, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the Muslim community. No Muslim scholar has said that she becomes lawful after three divorces have taken effect without a second husband. Anyone who attributes this to any of them has lied.

If someone says this or considers intercourse with her lawful after three divorces have taken effect, without her marrying a second husband, and his ignorance is excusable—for example, because he grew up among people in a place where the laws of Islam are unknown, or because he has only recently embraced Islam, or something similar—then he is to be taught the religion of Islam.

If he persists in maintaining that she becomes lawful after three divorces have taken effect without a second marriage, or in considering this act lawful, he is to be called upon to repent. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be put to death, like others of his kind among

---

## Page 15684

apostates who deny the obligatory nature of duties, the prohibition of forbidden acts, or the lawfulness of permissible things known to be part of the religion of Islam, as established by the community’s transmission from its Prophet through numerous independent channels—may the finest blessings and peace be upon him—and widely known among scholars and laypeople alike. Examples include someone who denies the obligatory nature of “the pillars of Islam,” namely the two testimonies of faith, the five daily prayers, fasting the month of Ramadan, and pilgrimage to the Sacred House; or denies “the prohibition of injustice in its various forms,” such as usury and gambling; or denies the prohibition of shameful acts, whether open or concealed. This includes the prohibition of “marriage to relatives,” other than female cousins on either the paternal or maternal side, and the prohibition of marriage to “women forbidden through marital ties”: the mothers and daughters of one’s wives, and the wives of one’s fathers and sons, along with other such prohibitions. The same applies to denying the lawfulness of bread, meat, marriage, clothing, and other things whose permissibility is necessarily known to be part of the religion of Islam.

These are matters over which Muslims have not disagreed, whether adherents of the Sunnah or adherents of innovation. They have, however, disagreed over many questions concerning “divorce and marriage” and other legal rulings. For example, the Companions and the jurists after them disagreed over “a declaration that one’s wife is forbidden to oneself”: does it constitute a divorce, an oath, or something else?

They also disagreed over “clear allusions to divorce,” such as “You are free,” “You are released,” and “It is final”: do these effect a single revocable divorce, an irrevocable divorce, or three divorces? Or does the ruling vary according to the circumstances?

They likewise disagreed concerning “a man who swears to abstain from intercourse with his wife”: does divorce take effect when the prescribed period expires if he has not resumed marital relations within it? Or, after it expires, must he be required either to resume marital relations or to divorce her?

Scholars also disagreed concerning divorce pronounced by an intoxicated person or a person under coercion, divorce in writing, and divorce pronounced by

---

## Page 15685

a discerning child, divorce pronounced by a father on behalf of his son, and divorce pronounced by an arbitrator from the husband’s family without the husband’s authorization. They likewise disagreed over offering the compensatory payment without the wife’s authorization, and over other questions familiar to scholars.

They also disagreed over questions of “making divorce conditional” and “oaths invoking divorce, emancipation, zihār (likening one’s wife to a forbidden female relative), declarations of prohibition, and vows,” such as saying: “If I do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj, fast for a month, or give one thousand in charity.” They also disagreed over many questions concerning “oaths” generally and the obligations they entail.

This includes their disagreement over making divorce conditional upon marriage: does the divorce take effect or not? Or should a distinction be drawn between a general declaration and a specific one? Or between cases involving a purpose recognized by Islamic law and cases involving a form of ownership or something not owned?

They held three views concerning divorce made conditional after marriage. One view is that it takes effect in all cases; another is that it does not take effect; and a third distinguishes between a condition upon whose occurrence divorce is intended and a condition whose occurrence is unwanted, with divorce likewise unintended if it occurs.

“The first” is exemplified by his saying: “If you give me one thousand, you are divorced.” “The second” is exemplified by his saying: “If I do such-and-such, my slaves are free, my wives are divorced, and I must perform Hajj.”

As for a conditional vow, they agreed that it is binding when the person wishes the condition to occur, as when he says: “If Allah heals my sick relative or safeguards my property that is elsewhere, I must fast for a month or give one hundred in charity.” They disagreed, however, over the case in which he does not wish the condition to occur, but rather wishes to prevent it, using the vow as an oath—as when he says: “I shall not travel, and if I do travel,

---

## Page 15686

I must fast, perform Hajj, give charity, or free a slave,” and similar statements. They held three views on this:

The Companions and the majority of the early generations held that expiation for an oath suffices. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, the later of the two views reported from Abū Hanīfah, and the view of a group of Mālikī scholars, including Ibn Wahb, Ibn Abī al-Umar, and others. Must he specifically offer this expiation, or does fulfilling the vow also suffice? There are two views within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad.

Another view is that he must fulfil the vow. This is Mālik’s position and one of the two views reported from Abū Hanīfah. Some later scholars also attributed it to al-Shāfi‘ī, but this has no basis in his statements.

A third view is that nothing is required of him under any circumstances. This was held by a group of the Successors and is the position of Dāwūd and Ibn Hazm.

They likewise differed along these same three lines concerning someone who swears by emancipation or divorce not to do something, saying, for example: “If I do such-and-such, my slave is free,” or “my wife is divorced.” If he breaks his oath, does the emancipation or divorce take effect, does expiation for an oath suffice, or is nothing required of him? There are three views. Some scholars distinguished between divorce and emancipation.

They agreed that if he says, “If I do such-and-such, I must divorce my wife,” this does not effect a divorce. Indeed, he is not even obliged to divorce her if doing so is not an act of devotion. But must he offer expiation for an oath? There are two views.

“The first” is that he must offer expiation for an oath. This is Ahmad’s position according to the well-known report from him. It is also Abū Hanīfah’s position, as related by Ibn al-Mundhir, al-Khattābī, Ibn ‘Abd al-Barr, and others, and it is what has reached us in the books of his followers. Al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā and others also related from him that no expiation is required.

“The second” is that nothing is required of him. This is al-Shāfi‘ī’s position.

---

## Page 15687

Section

If someone says, “If I do it, I must then emancipate my slave,” they agreed that emancipation does not take effect merely by his doing it. However, he must emancipate the slave. This is Mālik’s position and one of the two views reported from Abū Hanīfah.

Another view is that nothing is required of him. This was held by a group of the Successors and is the position of Dāwūd and Ibn Hazm.

A further view is that he must offer expiation for an oath. This is the position of the Companions and the majority of the Successors, and the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. According to the well-known view reported from both, he may choose between offering expiation and emancipating the slave.

Another view is that expiation specifically is obligatory.

As for swearing by divorce, no report from the Companions has reached us despite extensive research and examination of the books of earlier and later scholars. Rather, what is attributed to them is either weak—indeed, falsely transmitted—or does not constitute evidence concerning swearing by divorce, for people did not swear by divorce in their time.

However, a number of them are reported to have held that expiation for an oath suffices when someone swears by emancipation, as when he says, “If I do such-and-such, my slave is free.” The opposite view—that the slave becomes free—has also been reported from some of these same Companions. We have discussed the chains of transmission for these reports elsewhere.

For those among the Companions and Successors who held that emancipation does not take effect, divorce would all the more not take effect, as some of the Successors explicitly stated.

Some scholars assumed that there was no disagreement concerning divorce. This led them to reverse what the evidence entails, saying that divorce takes effect, but emancipation does not. These questions have been discussed at length, with an explanation of the views held concerning them by the Companions and those who followed them

---

## Page 15688

in righteousness, as well as the four imams and other Muslim scholars, together with each group’s supporting evidence, elsewhere.

The scholars disagreed concerning someone who swears by Allah, or invokes divorce, zihār, a declaration of unlawfulness, or a vow, that he will not do something, but then does it, forgetting his oath or unaware that it is the very thing his oath concerns.

Has he broken his oath, as held by Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and Ahmad, and according to one of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two views and one report from Ahmad? Or has he not broken his oath in either case, as held by the Meccan scholars, according to al-Shāfi‘ī’s other view and the second report from Ahmad? Or should a distinction be drawn between oaths invoking divorce or emancipation and other oaths, according to the third report from Ahmad? This last position was preferred by al-Qādī, al-Khiraqī, and other followers of Ahmad, and by al-Qaffāl among the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī.

Likewise, what if he believed that his wife had become irrevocably divorced through his doing what his oath concerned, but then discovered that she had not? There are two views on this. Similarly, if he swears by divorce or otherwise that something is as he believes it to be, but it turns out otherwise, there are three views, as mentioned above.

If he swears to something about which he is uncertain, and it then proves true, there are two views. According to Mālik, divorce takes effect; according to the majority, it does not.
