The latter is the well-known position in Ahmad’s school. However, Ahmad’s explicit statement, as reported by Harb, is to withhold judgment on this question. Two juristic views may therefore be derived concerning it, just as there are two views concerning whether someone has broken his oath if he swears that he will do such-and-such that day and the day passes, or he is unsure whether he has done it.

They agreed that an oath is interpreted according to the oath-taker’s intention when his wording admits that intention and it does not conflict with the apparent meaning—or when it does conflict with that meaning but he is being wronged. They disagreed over whether account should be taken of the reason for

---

## Page 15689

the oath, its context, and what prompted it. There are two views. The Medinan scholars, such as Mālik, Ahmad, and others, hold that these factors should be taken into account. The well-known position in the schools of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī is that they should not, although some of their rulings imply otherwise. If the reason for the oath is broader than its wording, those who take the reason into account give effect to it. If it is narrower, should the oath be restricted to it? There are two views in Ahmad’s school and elsewhere.

If someone swears concerning a particular thing, believing it to have a certain attribute, but it proves otherwise, there are likewise two views. The same applies if he divorces his wife on the basis of a circumstance that subsequently proves untrue. For example, he says, “You are divorced because you entered the house”—using the particle an, pronounced with an a-vowel, meaning “on account of your entering the house”—when she had not entered it. Does the divorce take effect? There are two views in Ahmad’s school and elsewhere.

The same applies if he says, “You are divorced because you did such-and-such,” or something similar, when she had not done it. Likewise, if he is told, “Your wife did such-and-such,” and says, “She is divorced,” but it subsequently becomes clear that they had lied about her, there are two views.

They also disagreed concerning prohibited divorce, such as divorce during menstruation, or pronouncing three divorces together according to the majority, who consider this prohibited. Nevertheless, the four imams and the majority of scholars hold that its being prohibited does not prevent it from taking effect. Similarly, zihār is prohibited, yet when a man pronounces it, its legal consequences apply. The same applies to “vows”: it is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “forbade them.” Nevertheless, fulfilling a vow is obligatory according to the scriptural texts and scholarly consensus.

Those who held that such a divorce does not take effect believed that everything Allah has forbidden is invalid when performed and produces no legal effect. The majority, however, distinguished between a legal effect that encompasses it but is not commensurate with committing

---

## Page 15690

a prohibited act—such as the lawfulness of property and sexual relations, and the validity of acts of worship—and a form of worship commensurate with committing a prohibited act, such as the imposition of obligations and prohibitions.

When someone does something he has been forbidden to do, his action may render him liable to an expiation, a prescribed punishment, or some other penalty. Likewise, someone may be forbidden to do something, yet doing it may subject him to obligations and prohibitions. But no one is forbidden to do something such that committing it makes good things lawful for him and discharges his obligations. That belongs to “the category of honor and beneficence.” Prohibited acts cannot be a cause of unqualified honor and beneficence; rather, they are a cause of punishment when those who commit them do not fear Allah, Blessed and Exalted.

As the Exalted says:

“Because of wrongdoing on the part of the Jews, We forbade them good things that had been lawful to them.”

And the Exalted says:

“To the Jews We forbade every animal with claws,”

through to His words, Blessed and Exalted:

“Thus We repaid them for their transgression.”

Similarly, what the Exalted relates in the account of the cow—their excessive questioning and their hesitation to obey His command—became a cause for further obligations. Another example is His statement:

“Do not ask about things which, if made known to you, would distress you.”

And the statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The Muslim who commits the gravest offense against other Muslims is the one who asks about something that has not been forbidden, and it is then forbidden because of his question.”

When they asked him about Hajj, “Is it required every year?” he said:

“No. Had I said yes, it would have become obligatory; and had it become obligatory, you would not have been able to bear it. Leave me be so long as you refrain from asking. Those before you perished only because of their excessive questioning and their disagreements with their prophets. When you are forbidden something, avoid it; and when I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

On this basis, a group of scholars said that pronouncing three divorces makes the woman unlawful to her husband as a punishment for him, to deter him from divorcing. Allah hates divorce; it is the devils and sorcerers who urge it. As the Exalted says concerning sorcery:

“They learn from the two of them that by which they cause separation between a man and his wife.”

And in

---

## Page 15691

an authentic hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“Satan sets his throne upon the sea and sends out his troops. Those closest to him in rank are those who cause the greatest strife. One of them comes and says, ‘I kept after him until he drank wine.’ Satan replies, ‘He will soon repent.’ Another comes and says, ‘I kept after him until I separated him from his wife.’ Satan kisses him between the eyes and says, ‘You are the one! You are the one!’”

Scholars of Quranic commentary, hadith, and jurisprudence have related that, in the early days of Islam, people divorced without any numerical limit. A man would divorce his wife, then leave her until her waiting period was nearly over, take her back, and then divorce her again to harm her. Allah therefore limited them to three divorces, because three is the lowest number counted as many and the highest counted as few. Were there no need for divorce, the evidence would require its prohibition, as indicated by the transmitted reports and fundamental principles. Allah Most High, however, permitted it out of mercy for His servants, because they sometimes need it. He prohibited it in certain circumstances, by scholarly consensus: for example, when a man divorces his wife during menstruation without her having requested a divorce. Such a divorce is forbidden by scholarly consensus.

Allah Most High sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with the finest religious law: the easy way of pure monotheism. As he said:

“The religion most beloved to Allah is the easy way of pure monotheism.”

He therefore permitted His believing servants to have intercourse through marriage and through ownership of female slaves. Jews and Christians have intercourse only through marriage, not through ownership of female slaves. The “initial basis of enslavement” is capture in war alone. The spoils of war were made lawful only for the community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as established in the authentic hadith in which he said:

“We have been favored above the prophets in five ways: our ranks have been made like the ranks of the angels

---

## Page 15692

and the earth has been made for me a place of prayer and a means of purification; war spoils have been made lawful for me, though they were not lawful for anyone before us; each prophet was sent specifically to his own people, whereas I have been sent to all mankind; and I have been granted intercession.”

Thus Allah, glorified be He, permitted the believers to marry, to divorce, and to remarry a divorced wife after she has married another man.

Christians forbid marriage to some of their members, and those whom they allow to marry are not allowed to divorce. Jews permit divorce, but if a divorced woman marries another man, they consider her forbidden to her former husband. Christians do not allow divorce, while Jews do not allow a man to take back his former wife after she has married someone else. Allah Most High has permitted the believers both.

If divorce were permitted without numerical limit—as it was initially—people would divorce continually, with nothing to deter them. The resulting harm and corruption necessitated prohibiting this. Nor does the harm caused by divorce concern only the wife’s rights—as in the case of divorce during menstruation—such that it would always become permissible at her request. Rather, scholars agree that divorce itself, when there is no need for it, is either forbidden or discouraged. Whatever is permitted because of a need is limited to the extent of that need. Three is the limit of what has been permitted to meet the need, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is not lawful for a Muslim to shun his brother for more than three nights: they meet, and each turns away from the other. The better of the two is the one who offers the greeting of peace first.”

Likewise, he said:

“It is not lawful for a woman who believes in Allah and the Last Day to mourn a deceased person for more than three days, except for her husband

---

## Page 15693

, for whom she mourns for four months and ten days.” Likewise, he permitted an emigrant to remain in Mecca for three days after completing his pilgrimage rites. These hadiths are recorded in the Sahīh.

This is among the evidence cited by those who hold that divorce takes effect only where there is intent, and that divorce pronounced under coercion does not take effect. Similarly, according to explicit scriptural evidence and scholarly consensus, someone compelled to utter words of disbelief does not become a disbeliever. Yet someone who utters words of disbelief in mockery of Allah’s verses, Allah, and His Messenger becomes a disbeliever. Likewise, someone who pronounces divorce in jest effects a divorce.

If someone swears an oath invoking disbelief, saying, “If I do such-and-such, I disavow Allah and His Messenger,” or, “Then I am a Jew or a Christian,” he does not become a disbeliever by doing the act to which his oath relates. This is so even though the wording makes that outcome contingent upon a condition, because his purpose is to invoke it in an oath out of hatred and aversion for it, not because he desires it. This differs from someone who says, “If you give me a thousand, I will disbelieve,” for such a person becomes a disbeliever. The same reasoning is advanced by those who distinguish between swearing an oath by divorce, or making divorce contingent upon a condition one does not intend to occur, and intending divorce to take effect when the condition occurs.

For this reason, many scholars of the early and later generations held that khul' (release from marriage in return for compensation) is an annulment of the marriage, not one of the three divorces. This was the view of Ibn ‘Abbās, and one of the two views held by each of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. The woman has ransomed herself from her husband, just as a captive is ransomed; this is not the divorce that is, in principle, discouraged. Thus it is permissible during menstruation, unlike divorce. If, however, the husband turns away from khul' and instead grants her one of the three divorces in return for compensation, the fault lies with him.

A group of the early generations, such as ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān and others, took a position on this matter and transmitted concerning it a hadith traced back to the Prophet. Some later scholars among the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad regarded it as an annulment when concluded with a third party, like mutual rescission. The correct view is that its status when concluded with a third party is the same as when concluded with the woman herself. For if the woman’s release is secured by ransom just as ransom is paid for

---

## Page 15694

a captive, a captive may secure his ransom with his own money or with money provided by someone else. Likewise, a slave may obtain his freedom in return for money provided either by himself or by a third party. A settlement may likewise be concluded with the defendant or with a third party. All these involve the relinquishment of rights and the dissolution of legal ties. Since khul‘ terminates the marriage and does not count as one of the three divorces, it makes no difference whether the money is provided by the woman or by a third party.

The analogy between annulment of marriage and rescission of a sale is questionable. A sale can be rescinded only with the consent of both parties; neither may rescind it unilaterally. Marriage is different: the woman does not have the authority to dissolve it, whereas the husband may do so unilaterally. Nevertheless, her ransoming herself from him is equivalent to a third party ransoming her. Questions of divorce, including the points of consensus and disagreement concerning them, are discussed at length elsewhere.

The point here is that once three divorces have taken effect, the woman becomes unlawful to him by the consensus of the Muslims, as indicated by the Quran and Sunnah. According to the overwhelming majority of earlier and later scholars, she becomes lawful to him again only after marrying a second husband and having intercourse with him. For when marriage is prescribed, both the contract and intercourse are prescribed, whereas when it is prohibited, both the contract and intercourse are prohibited. Thus, in an obligatory or recommended marriage, intercourse pursuant to the contract is prescribed, whereas in a prohibited marriage, even the contract alone is forbidden.

It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to the wife of Rifā‘a al-Qurazī, when she wanted to return to Rifā‘a without having had intercourse with her second husband:

“No, not until you taste his sweetness and he tastes yours.”

No disagreement on this is reported except from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib. Although he was the most learned of the Successors, the Sunnah concerning this matter had not reached him.

The marriage that makes her lawful again is the marriage recognized among Muslims: the marriage in which Allah has placed between

---

## Page 15695

the spouses affection and mercy. This is why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said concerning it:

“Until you taste his sweetness and he tastes yours.”

As for a marriage contracted to make her lawful again for her former husband, it does not make her lawful for him according to the overwhelming majority of the early scholars. It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the man who marries a woman to make her lawful again for her former husband, and the man for whom this is done.”

‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb said: “Whenever a man who contracts such a marriage and the man for whom he does so are brought before me, I will stone them both.”

Likewise, ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, Ibn ‘Abbās, Ibn ‘Umar and others said that she becomes lawful again only through a marriage entered into out of genuine desire, not a marriage contracted to make her lawful again for her former husband. No Companion is known to have permitted such a marriage. They did, however, disagree about temporary marriage, which is better than a marriage contracted to make a woman lawful again for her former husband in three respects.

First, temporary marriage was permissible in the early days of Islam, unlike marriage contracted to make a woman lawful again for her former husband.

Second, Ibn ‘Abbās and a group of the early scholars permitted temporary marriage, whereas none of the Companions permitted marriage contracted to make a woman lawful again for her former husband.

Third, a man entering a temporary marriage desires the woman, and she desires him, for a specified period. By contrast, a woman has no desire whatsoever for the man who marries her to make her lawful again for her former husband, and he has no desire for her, but only for the payment he receives. Even if he does desire her, his desire is for intercourse, not for taking her as a wife; it is the same kind of desire as that of an adulterer.

This is why Ibn ‘Umar said: “They remain adulterers, even if they stay together for twenty years.” For Allah knows that in his heart he intends to make her lawful again for her former husband. Thus, such a marriage lacks the distinctive qualities of marriage. Marriage as ordinarily understood is as Allah Most High describes it:

“And among His signs is that He created for you spouses from among yourselves, that you may find tranquillity in them, and He has placed between you

---

## Page 15696

affection and mercy.” By contrast, marriage contracted to make a woman lawful again for her former husband involves hatred and aversion. This is why those involved do not publicize it, but conceal it as illicit intercourse is concealed. Among the outward signs of marriage is its public announcement, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Announce marriages and beat the frame drum for them.”

According to one group of scholars, having a marriage witnessed suffices to make it public. Another group requires both witnesses and public announcement; if the parties agree to conceal it, it is invalid. Other signs include the wedding feast, scattering treats, perfume, drinks, and similar customary wedding practices.

As for a “marriage to make her lawful again for her former husband,” none of these things takes place, because those involved do not intend the man to become the woman’s husband or the woman to become his wife. Their sole purpose is to borrow him to mount her, as reflected in his being called “the borrowed billy goat” in a hadith traced back to the Prophet. He has likewise been compared to the donkey of al-‘Ashriyyayn, hired to mount females. Consequently, after this arrangement, the woman’s relationship with her husband does not remain as it was before; rather, a degree of aversion develops between them.

Since such an arrangement serves no sound purpose commanded by the Lawgiver, Satan has made other practices contrary to scholarly consensus seem analogous to it. Some ordinary people have thus come to think that a woman’s giving birth to a boy makes her lawful again for her former husband, or that his stepping with his foot on her foot or head, or on a roof or ladder beneath which she is, makes her lawful again for him. Some think that if the two meet at ‘Arafāt, as Adam and his wife met, this makes her lawful again for him.

Some women, upon marrying a man for this purpose, do not allow him intercourse with them, but instead give him access to a slave woman they own. Others give him something and instruct him to acknowledge having had intercourse with them. Some men perform this role for both a mother and her daughter. There are other such practices, discussed at length elsewhere, which we have explained

---

## Page 15697

in An Exposition of the Evidence for the Invalidity of Marriages Contracted to Make a Woman Lawful Again for Her Former Husband. Undoubtedly, abrogated provisions of the Sacred Law and matters over which the early generations differed are better than anything of this sort. Had the Sacred Law prescribed no limit to the number of divorces, that would have been conceivable, even though such a provision has been abrogated. But to say that a man who divorces his wife cannot lawfully remarry her until he hires someone to have intercourse with her is something no divine law would prescribe.

Many of those involved in such marriages engage in practices that are forbidden by Muslim consensus. No one other than a woman’s husband may explicitly propose marriage to her during her waiting period, whether that period follows divorce or her husband’s death. Allah Most High says:

“There is no blame upon you for hinting at a proposal of marriage to women or keeping it concealed within yourselves. Allah knows that you will think of them, but do not make secret promises to them; you may only speak to them in a proper manner. And do not resolve to conclude the marriage contract until the prescribed period has come to an end.”

Allah Most High thus forbids making secret promises and resolving to conclude the marriage contract before the prescribed period has ended. If this applies to the waiting period following a husband’s death, it applies even more strictly to the waiting period following divorce, by Muslim consensus, since a divorced woman may return to her husband, unlike a woman whose husband has died.

As for indirect proposals, they are permissible during a widow’s waiting period, but not during the waiting period following a revocable divorce or in other cases. Thus, by Muslim consensus, no one may make secret promises to a woman divorced three times or resolve to conclude a marriage contract with her before her prescribed waiting period has ended. If she marries a second husband and he divorces her three times, then, by Muslim consensus, her first husband may not make secret promises to her or resolve to conclude a marriage contract with her before her prescribed waiting period has ended. That is more serious still.

---

## Page 15698

While a woman is with her husband, no one may propose marriage to her, either explicitly or indirectly, by Muslim consensus. If she has not yet married another man, the man who divorced her three times may not propose to her, either explicitly or indirectly, by Muslim consensus. Proposing to her in this situation is more serious than proposing to her after she has married the second husband.

Yet one of “those involved in such marriages” may make an arrangement with a woman divorced three times, and the two resolve—before her waiting period has ended and before she has married the second husband—to marry after that second marriage, the marriage intended to make her lawful again for her former husband. He gives her money to pay the witnesses to this marriage contract and the intermediary husband, and provides for her during the waiting period following that marriage. The intermediary husband gives her neither a dowry nor maintenance during her waiting period nor maintenance upon divorce.

If Muslims agree that the first husband may not propose to her—either explicitly or indirectly—while she is married to the second husband, how much more serious is it if he proposes before she has married the second husband? Likewise, if, after the second husband divorces her, the first husband may neither make secret promises to her nor resolve to conclude the marriage contract until the prescribed waiting period has ended, how much more serious is it if he does so before the second husband divorces her—indeed, before he even marries her, or even before her waiting period following divorce from the first husband has ended? All of this is forbidden by Muslim consensus, yet many of those involved in such marriages do it.

There is no form of marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband whose permissibility Muslims unanimously accept, nor any form expressly permitted by a scriptural text. Rather, some forms are forbidden by Muslim consensus, while others are disputed among the scholars. As for the Companions, it is not established that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) cursed anyone among them as either the man who contracts such a marriage or the former husband for whose benefit it is contracted. This and other considerations show that some forms of such marriages are worse than temporary marriage and the like

---

## Page 15699

among the forms of marriage over which the early generations differed.

In any event, the Companions are the best of this community, followed by the Successors, as established in an authentic report from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), who said:

“The best generation is the generation among whom I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them.”

A marriage whose permissibility the early generations disputed is therefore closer to being permissible than one whose prohibition they unanimously affirmed, even if later generations dispute it, for the former possessed greater knowledge and piety. What they unanimously regarded as gravely forbidden has an even stronger claim to prohibition than what they simply agreed was forbidden, even if its prohibition was unclear to those who came after them. And Allah Most High knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married an orphan girl whose mother testified that she had reached puberty. She remained with him for four years, after which she became irrevocably divorced from him through three divorces. Her sisters and other women then testified that she had not reached puberty until nine days after her husband consummated the marriage with her. Her mother also testified to this account. The mother has since died, and the husband wishes to take her back. What is the ruling?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

According to the majority of scholars, the husband may not marry her after divorcing her three times. The position of Abū Hanīfah and the well-known position reported from Ahmad is that her marriage is valid even if it took place before puberty. The well-known position of Mālik and Ahmad is that divorce takes effect in a defective marriage over whose validity scholars differ. Such questions are reprehensible, for they are

---

## Page 15700

posed by transgressors, who do not question the validity of the marriage while the husband is having intercourse with her and enjoying intimacy with her. Only after she has been divorced three times do they begin seeking grounds to invalidate the marriage so that it cannot be said that the divorce took effect.

This is opposition to Allah’s command. When intercourse was unlawful, he neither exercised due care nor asked about it; yet once Allah forbade it, he began asking about grounds on which intercourse might be made lawful. A man who behaves in this way commits what is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims and is a sinner. For this woman’s first marriage must either have been valid or not.

If it was valid, the three divorces took effect, and intercourse with her before she marries another husband is forbidden. If the first marriage was invalid, intercourse within it was forbidden, and this husband has not repented of that intercourse. He only asked after divorcing her, so that the divorce would not take effect. Thus, they sought grounds that would make the earlier intercourse unlawful in order to deem subsequent intercourse lawful. This is opposition to Allah and His Messenger and the spreading of corruption in the land.

If this man has divorced her three times, let him fear Allah, stay away from her, and observe Allah’s limits, for whoever transgresses Allah’s limits has wronged himself. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man married a woman with an unrelated man acting as her marriage guardian, although her own guardian was less than the distance permitting the shortening of prayers away. He believed that the unrelated man was a judge. He consummated the marriage with her, and she bore him offspring. He then divorced her three times and subsequently wished to

---

## Page 15701

take her back before she married another husband.

May he do so on the grounds that the first marriage was invalid, without the prescribed punishment being waived, while the dowry is obligatory, paternity is established, and the legal status associated with a consummated marriage is attained?

He replied:

No prescribed punishment is due for this marriage if he believed it to be valid. Rather, paternity is established through it and the dowry is obligatory. An invalid marriage, however, does not confer the legal status associated with a consummated marriage. Divorce takes effect in a marriage whose validity is disputed if he believed it to be valid. But if it becomes clear that the man who contracted the marriage had no authority to do so whatsoever, and the husband separates from her upon learning this, pronouncing three divorces, no divorce takes effect in these circumstances. He may marry her without her first marrying another husband.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman two years ago and then divorced her three times. Her marriage guardian was openly sinful. Is a marriage contracted by such a guardian valid, so that, if she is divorced three times, she is not lawful for him until she has married someone else? Or is the marriage he contracted invalid, allowing the husband to marry her under a new contract with a rightly guided guardian, without her first marrying another man?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he has divorced her three times, the divorce has taken effect. After three divorces, no one may investigate whether the guardian was upright or openly sinful in order to use the guardian’s sinfulness as a pretext for claiming that the divorce did not take effect, for most

---

## Page 15702

jurists recognize the validity of an openly sinful guardian’s authority, and most regard divorce as taking effect in such a marriage—indeed, even in other invalid marriages. If he proceeds on the premise that the marriage is invalid and that divorce does not take effect within it, only someone who also treats what is forbidden as forbidden may avail himself of what is lawful. No one may regard the same thing as both lawful and unlawful.

This husband had intercourse with her before divorcing her, and had she died, he would have inherited from her. He was therefore acting on the basis that the marriage was valid. How, then, can he act after the divorce on the basis that it was invalid, making the marriage valid when its validity serves his purpose, and invalid when its invalidity serves his purpose? This position contradicts the consensus of the Muslims. They agree that whoever believes something to be lawful must maintain that belief whether it serves his purpose or not, and whoever believes it to be forbidden must maintain that belief in both circumstances.

These men who divorce their wives only consider whether the guardian’s sinfulness invalidates the marriage when they have pronounced three divorces—not when enjoying marital intimacy or inheriting from one another. Thus, at one time they follow those who regard it as invalid, and at another they follow those who regard it as valid, according to their interests and desires. Such conduct is impermissible by the consensus of the Muslim community.

A comparable case is that of a man who believes in the existence of “the right of pre-emption by virtue of being a neighbour” when he seeks to exercise it, but believes that no such right exists when he is the purchaser. This is impermissible by consensus. In the present case, the guardian’s authority, despite his open sinfulness, was treated as valid at the time of the marriage, but was treated as invalid

---

## Page 15703

at the time of his divorce. This is impermissible by the consensus of the Muslims.

Even if the particular person seeking the ruling were to say, “I did not know this, and from today onward I shall abide by it,” that would not entitle him to do so, because this opens the door to making a game of religion and allows what is lawful and unlawful to be determined according to personal desires. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman whose guardian is openly sinful, consuming unlawful wealth and drinking wine. The witnesses are likewise, and he has now divorced her three times. Does this afford him a dispensation to take her back?

He answered:

If he has divorced her three times, the divorce takes effect. Whoever begins examining the nature of the marriage contract after divorce, without having examined it beforehand, is transgressing the limits set by Allah, for he seeks to treat as lawful what Allah has forbidden, both before and after divorce.

According to Mālik, Ahmad and other imams, divorce takes effect in an invalid marriage whose validity is disputed. Moreover, a marriage contracted under the guardianship of an openly sinful person is valid according to the vast majority of imams. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15704

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman “by a handshake,” agreeing to a bridal due of five dinars, payable at half a dinar each year. He has consummated the marriage and had intercourse with her. Is the marriage valid? If they have a child, will the child inherit? Are they liable to the prescribed punishment?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah.

If he married her without a guardian or witnesses, and they concealed the marriage, then this marriage is invalid by agreement of the imams. Indeed, the scholars hold that:

“There is no valid marriage without a guardian.”

“Any woman who marries without her guardian’s permission—her marriage is invalid, her marriage is invalid, her marriage is invalid.”

Both these wordings are reported in the Sunan collections from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

More than one of the early generations said, “There is no valid marriage without two witnesses.” This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. Mālik requires that the marriage be publicly announced.

“Secret marriage” is akin to the marriages of prostitutes. Allah Most High has said:

“Chaste, neither committing fornication nor taking secret lovers.”

Thus, secret marriage is akin to the conduct of women who take secret lovers. Allah Most High has also said:

“Marry off the unmarried among you.”

And He Most High has said:

“And do not give women in marriage

---

## Page 15705

to polytheistic men until they believe.”

Thus, He addressed men concerning giving women in marriage. For this reason, some of the early generations said: “A woman does not give herself in marriage; it is the prostitute who gives herself in marriage.”

However, if he believed this marriage to be lawful, intercourse within it was intercourse under a mistaken belief in its lawfulness. The child’s paternity is established, and the child inherits from his father. As for punishment, both deserve punishment for entering into such a contract.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman “by a handshake,” and she stayed with him for several days. It then emerged that she had another husband. The husband, the wife, and her first husband were brought in, and she was asked, “Do you want the first or the second?”

She replied, “I want only the second husband.” The first then divorced her, and she was ordered to complete the waiting period following her separation from him. The husband remained with her. Is this valid for her or not?

He answered:

If she married the second man before completing the waiting period following her separation from the first, and the first had parted from her—whether because his marriage was invalid, because he divorced her, or because a judge separated them—then her marriage is invalid. She, he, and whoever gave her in marriage all deserve punishment.

Rather, she must complete the waiting period following her separation from the first. Then, if the second has had intercourse with her, she must observe another waiting period on his account. Once both waiting periods have ended, she may marry whomever she wishes: the first, the second, or someone else.

---

## Page 15706

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An enslaved woman was married, and her husband went away on a journey. Her master sold her, stipulating that she was married. She stayed with her purchaser for several days; then, as death approached him, he freed her. She subsequently married another man, who did not know that she already had a husband.

When her first husband returned from his journey, the master who had sold her gave him the marriage document, which recorded a valid marriage contract under Islamic law. Which marriage contract is valid—that recorded in the first husband’s document, or the second husband’s?

He answered:

If the first husband married her in a manner valid under Islamic law—whether according to Abū Hanīfah’s view that a free man’s marriage to an enslaved woman is valid, or according to the view of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad, on condition that he lacked sufficient financial means and feared falling into sexual sin—then her emancipation does not invalidate his marriage to her. Rather, she remains his wife after being freed.

However, according to one report from Abū Hanīfah, she has the right to annul the marriage. She may therefore annul it and, after completing the waiting period following its annulment, marry someone else if she wishes. According to Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and the well-known position reported from Ahmad, she has no such choice; rather, she remains his wife.

If she marries before the marriage has been annulled, her new marriage is invalid by agreement of the imams. If, however, her first marriage was invalid, the two must be separated, and she may marry whomever she wishes after the waiting period has ended.

---

## Page 15707

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man acknowledged before upright witnesses that he had divorced his wife longer ago than the legally prescribed waiting period. May they now marry her to him again?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If the man making this acknowledgment is openly sinful or of unknown moral standing, his statement is not accepted as grounds for dispensing with the waiting period, which involves a right of Allah. This is not merely an admission against himself, such that it would be accepted even from an openly sinful person. Rather, it also involves a right of Allah, for the waiting period involves both Allah’s right and the husband’s right.

If, however, he is upright and not under suspicion—for example, he was away and, upon returning, told her that he had divorced her such-and-such a time earlier—must she reckon her waiting period from when he informed her, if no evidence establishes his claim, or from the time of the divorce, as she would if evidence established it?

There is a well-known disagreement on this, with differing views reported from Ahmad and others. The well-known position reported from him is the second. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man married a woman but neither consummated the marriage nor had intercourse with her, and she gave birth two months later. Is the marriage valid, and is he obliged to pay the bridal due or not?

---

## Page 15708

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. By agreement of the Muslims, the child is not attributed to him, nor does the full bridal due become binding upon him. However, the scholars hold two views concerning the marriage contract.

The sounder view is that the contract is invalid, as held by Mālik, Ahmad and others. Accordingly, the couple must be separated, and he owes neither the bridal due nor half of it, nor any compensatory gift, just as with other invalid marriage contracts when separation occurs before consummation. However, a judge who considers the contract invalid should pronounce their separation, so as to settle the dispute.

“The second view” is that the contract is valid, but he may not have intercourse with her until she gives birth, as held by Abū Hanīfah. It has also been said that he may have intercourse with her before she gives birth, as held by al-Shāfi‘ī. According to these two views, if he divorces her before consummation, he owes half the bridal due.

But this disagreement applies where she is pregnant through intercourse under a mistaken belief of lawfulness, or by a master or a husband; the marriage is invalid by agreement of the Muslims, and he owes no bridal due if he separates from her before consummation. As for a woman pregnant through fornication, there is no dispute concerning the validity of marrying her. The disagreement concerns a man who married her willingly. If, however, he was forced to marry her, the marriage is invalid according to the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and others.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning a man who is a “frequent traveller,” travelling through different lands and spending a month or two in each city, who practises withdrawal with her and fears falling into sin: may he marry during his stay

---

## Page 15709

in that town, then divorce her when he leaves and give her what is due to her, or not? And would the marriage be valid?

He answered:

He may marry, but he must enter into a marriage with no fixed term stipulated, so that he may keep her as his wife if he wishes or divorce her if he wishes. If he firmly intends to divorce her when his journey ends, that is disliked in such circumstances, and there is disagreement over the validity of the marriage. If, however, he intends to keep her as his wife upon departing if she pleases him, and otherwise to divorce her, that is permissible.

As for stipulating a fixed term, this is “temporary marriage,” which the four imams and others unanimously hold to be forbidden. Although some permit it, either unconditionally or in cases of necessity, as was the case in the early days of Islam, the correct view is that this permission has been abrogated. As established in an authentic report, after permitting temporary marriage in the year of the Conquest, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has forbidden temporary marriage until the Day of Resurrection.”

The Quran forbids a man to have intercourse with anyone other than his wife or a woman he owns, through His words:

“And those who guard their private parts, except from their wives or those their right hands possess, for indeed, they are not to be blamed. But whoever seeks beyond that—then those are the transgressors.”

A woman in a temporary marriage is neither a wife nor a woman whom one’s right hand possesses. Allah has prescribed rulings for wives, including inheritance, a waiting period of four months and ten days following the husband’s death, a waiting period of three periods following divorce, and other such rulings that do not apply to a woman in a temporary marriage. If she were

---

## Page 15710

a wife, these rulings would apply to her. For this reason, some of the early generations said that these rulings abrogated temporary marriage. A detailed discussion would be lengthy, and this is not the place for it.

If he stipulates a fixed term before concluding the marriage contract, this is equivalent to stipulating it when the contract is concluded, according to the more correct of the two scholarly views. The same applies to a “marriage intended to make a woman lawful again for her former husband.”

If, however, the husband intends a fixed term without disclosing it to the woman, there is disagreement: Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī permit it, while Mālik, Ahmad and others disapprove of it. If his intention were to make her lawful again for her former husband, the Companions agreed that this was forbidden and regarded it as a marriage contracted for that purpose.

Yet a marriage intended to make a woman lawful again for her former husband is worse than temporary marriage. The former has never been permitted, since the man’s purpose is not marriage itself, but merely to return her to the man who previously divorced her. He establishes the marriage contract only to dissolve it, and this cannot be lawful under any circumstances. By contrast, a man entering a temporary marriage does seek marital enjoyment. Nevertheless, fixing a term undermines the purposes of marriage—affection, mercy and tranquillity—and places the wife in the position of a woman hired for a fee. Thus, intending a fixed term is less serious than intending to make the woman lawful again for her former husband. The question is whether the former is prohibited or merely discouraged.

As for “withdrawal,” a group of scholars have forbidden it. However, the position of the four imams is that it is permissible with the woman’s consent. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15711

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about someone who said:

“If a man has anal intercourse with a divorced woman, she becomes lawful again for her former husband.” Is this correct or not?

He replied:

This is a false claim that contradicts the well-known imams of the Muslims and other Muslim imams. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to a woman who had been divorced three times:

“No, not until you taste his sweetness and he tastes yours.”

This explicitly states that such sweetness is indispensable. It is not attained through anal intercourse, and no disagreement on this is known. As for the view attributed to some Mālikī scholars—an attribution they themselves dispute—and the report from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab that intercourse is not a prerequisite, no mention is made there of anal intercourse. It is an anomalous view contradicted by the authentic Sunnah, and consensus was established both before and after it.

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

“Marrying a woman who has committed unlawful sexual intercourse” is forbidden until she repents, whether her partner in that act was the prospective husband or someone else. This is undoubtedly the correct view. It is the position of a group of earlier and later scholars, including

---

## Page 15712

Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others. Many earlier and later scholars held that such a marriage is permissible; this is the position of the three imams. Mālik, however, requires a waiting period to establish that the womb is clear of pregnancy, while Abū Hanīfa permits the marriage contract before this period has elapsed if she is pregnant, but does not permit intercourse with her until she gives birth. Al-Shāfi‘ī permits both the marriage contract and intercourse without qualification, because the fornicator’s semen has no legally protected status, and the child’s lineage is not attributed to him. This is the basis of his position.

Abū Hanīfa distinguishes between a pregnant woman and one who is not pregnant: if a man has intercourse with a pregnant woman, he would be acknowledging as his own a child who is certainly not his, unlike the case of a woman who is not pregnant. Mālik and Ahmad require a “precautionary waiting period,” and this is correct. Mālik, and Ahmad according to one reported position, require one menstrual period. The other position reported from Ahmad, adopted by many of his followers, including Judge Abū Ya‘lā and his followers, requires three menstrual periods.

The correct view is that only the precautionary waiting period is required. This woman is not a wife obliged to observe a marital waiting period. Her case warrants no greater requirement than that of a slave woman observing a precautionary waiting period whose child would be attributed to her master. Since only that precautionary period is required of the latter, this applies all the more to the former. Even supposing she is free—as in the case of a woman freed after her master has had intercourse with her, whom it is proposed to marry either to the man who freed her or to someone else—the majority require her to observe a precautionary waiting period, not a marital waiting period. Nor is this woman who committed unlawful sexual intercourse like a woman with whom intercourse took place under an appearance of lawfulness, whose child is attributed to the man who had intercourse with her; moreover, there is disagreement over whether even the latter must observe a marital waiting period.

The indications of the Quran, the explicit Sunnah, and the statements of the Companions establish that a “woman who obtains release from marriage in return for compensation” need only observe a precautionary waiting period of one menstrual period, not a waiting period like that of a divorced woman. This is one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, and it is the view of ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, Ibn ‘Abbās, and Ibn ‘Umar in the latter of his two opinions.

Makkī stated that this was the consensus of the Companions. It is also the view of Qabīsa ibn Dhu’ayb

---

## Page 15713

and Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, Ibn al-Mundhir, and other hadith jurists. This is the correct view, as we have discussed at length elsewhere. If a woman who obtains release from marriage in return for compensation is not required to observe the waiting period of a divorced woman, since she is not divorced, but only a precautionary waiting period—which is also called a waiting period—then this applies all the more to a woman with whom intercourse took place under an appearance of lawfulness, and still more to a woman who committed unlawful sexual intercourse.

There is also the case of a woman who emigrates from a land of disbelief, such as the woman whose faith was tested, concerning whom Allah revealed:

“O you who believe, when believing women come to you as emigrants, test them”—to the end of the verse.

Elsewhere, we have mentioned the hadith transmitted concerning her and explained that this would take place after she had observed a precautionary waiting period of one menstrual period, even though she had been married. The separation, however, resulted from her embracing Islam and choosing to leave her husband, not from his divorcing her.

Likewise, His words:

“And married women, except those whom your right hands possess.”

Thus, when they took a woman captive, intercourse with her became permissible after the precautionary waiting period. According to the Sunnah and scholarly consensus, a captive woman is required to observe only the precautionary waiting period, which may also be called a waiting period. In the Sunan collections, the hadith concerning Barīra upon her emancipation states: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered her to observe a waiting period.” For this reason, some literalist scholars, such as Ibn Hazm, held that a woman who is not divorced need only observe a precautionary waiting period of one menstrual period, except for this woman. This view is weak, however, because in their usage, “observe a waiting period” means observing the precautionary waiting period, as we have mentioned, sū (1). Ibn Mājah also narrated from ‘Ā’isha that “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered her to observe a waiting period of three menstrual periods.” That is how he worded it, but this hadith is defective.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in both originals.

---

## Page 15714

First, it is established through several routes of transmission that ‘Ā’isha held the waiting period to be three periods of purity, and that once a woman enters her third menstrual period, she becomes free to remarry. How, then, could she report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered her to observe a waiting period of three menstrual periods? From the time of the Companions until today, Muslims have disagreed over whether the waiting period consists of three menstrual periods or three periods of purity. Yet we have never heard of any scholar citing this hadith as evidence that it consists of three menstrual periods. Had this report had any basis from ‘Ā’isha, it would not have escaped all the scholars.

Moreover, this would be a momentous ruling of the Sunnah, which there would be every reason and incentive to learn, for it establishes two important matters: first, that an emancipated woman married to a slave must observe a waiting period of three menstrual periods; and second, that the waiting period consists of three menstrual periods. Furthermore, had this been established, it would have been cited by those who hold that an emancipated woman’s choice to leave her husband constitutes one irrevocable divorce, as Mālik and others maintain. On this view, the waiting period would arise only from divorce. But this, too, is a weak view. The Quran, the Sunnah, and sound reasoning indicate that divorce can only be revocable, and that any irrevocable separation does not count as one of the three divorces—even release from marriage in return for compensation—as has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Our purpose here is to discuss marriage to a woman who has committed unlawful sexual intercourse. This involves two issues. The first concerns her precautionary waiting period, which is her waiting period. We have already mentioned the view of those who say that no inviolability attaches to the semen of a man who commits unlawful sexual intercourse.

The response is that the precautionary waiting period is prescribed not out of regard for the inviolability of the first man’s semen, but for that of the second man’s. A man has no right to acknowledge as his own a child who is not his. The same applies if he does not wait for her precautionary waiting period to end when she has already conceived through unlawful sexual intercourse. Moreover, regarding a man’s acknowledgment of paternity of his child conceived through unlawful sexual intercourse, if the woman was not

---

## Page 15715

another man’s lawful sexual partner, scholars hold two views on the matter. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The child belongs to the man whose lawful bed the woman shares; the man who commits unlawful intercourse has no claim.”

He thus assigned the child to the man whose lawful bed she shares, not to the man who commits unlawful intercourse. If the woman does not share any man’s lawful bed, the hadith does not cover that case. ‘Umar attributed children born during the pre-Islamic period to their fathers. This is not the place to discuss this issue at length.

The second issue is that marriage to her is not lawful until she repents. This is what the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound reasoning indicate. The well-known evidence here is the verse in Sūrat al-Nūr, in which Allah Most High says:

“A man who commits unlawful sexual intercourse marries only a woman who does likewise or an idolatress; and a woman who commits unlawful sexual intercourse is married only by a man who does likewise or an idolater. This is forbidden to the believers.”

The Sunan collections also contain the hadith of Abū Marthad al-Ghanawī concerning ‘Anāq. Those who did not apply this verse offered an alternative interpretation or claimed that it had been abrogated.

As for their interpretation, they said that marriage here means sexual intercourse. The unsoundness of this becomes apparent with even a little reflection.

First, wherever the word “marriage” occurs in the Quran, it necessarily refers to the marriage contract, even if sexual intercourse is also included in its meaning. Nowhere in Allah’s Book does it refer solely to sexual intercourse.

Second, the verse was revealed specifically because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked for a ruling on marrying a woman who committed unlawful sexual intercourse. How, then, could the very occasion of its revelation fall outside the scope of its wording?

Third, saying, “A man who commits unlawful sexual intercourse has intercourse only with a woman who does likewise,” or, “A woman who commits unlawful sexual intercourse has intercourse only with a man who does likewise,” is like saying, “An eater eats only food, and food is eaten only by

---

## Page 15716

an eater; a husband marries only a wife, and a wife is married only by a husband.” Such language is unworthy of the speech of Allah.

Fourth, a man may force a woman to have intercourse with him, making him guilty of unlawful sexual intercourse without her being guilty of it. Likewise, a woman may commit unlawful sexual intercourse with a man who is asleep or under coercion, according to one of the two views, without his being guilty of it.

Fifth, the Muslims already knew that unlawful sexual intercourse was forbidden through verses revealed in Mecca. Its prohibition was too widely known for this verse to have been revealed to prohibit it.

Sixth, Allah says:

“She is married only by a man who commits unlawful sexual intercourse or an idolater.”

If sexual intercourse were intended, there would be no need to mention the idolater, since he would himself be committing unlawful sexual intercourse. Likewise, if a man has unlawful sexual intercourse with an idolatress, she too is guilty of it. There would therefore be no need for this distinction.

Seventh, Allah had already said:

“As for the woman and the man who commit unlawful sexual intercourse, flog each of them a hundred lashes.”

What need would there then be to mention the prohibition of unlawful sexual intercourse after that?

As for abrogation, Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab and a group of scholars said that this verse was abrogated by His words:

“Marry off the unmarried among you.”

When the proponents of this view realized that the claim of abrogation by this verse was very weak, and could find nothing to abrogate it, they assumed that no one had upheld its ruling and said, “It has been abrogated by consensus,” as Abū ‘Alī al-Jubbā’ī and others claimed. As for

---

## Page 15717

the view held by some of these scholars that consensus abrogates scriptural texts—as is reported from ‘Īsā ibn Abān and others—it is utterly unsound. It implies that the Muslim community may change its religion after its Prophet, and that doing so is permissible for them, just as the Christians claim that their scholars were permitted to abrogate whatever they saw fit of the Messiah’s law. This is not a position held by Muslims.

Among those who suppose that a consensus exists are those who say, “The consensus indicates an abrogating text that has not reached us.” Yet there is no report of a consensus contrary to this verse. Anyone who sets a consensus against a scriptural text and claims that the text has been abrogated, without adducing another text that conflicts with it, is mistaken. This has been discussed at length elsewhere, where it was explained that no scriptural text has been abrogated except by another text that remains extant and preserved within the Muslim community. Knowledge of the abrogating text, which must be acted upon, is more important to the community than knowledge of the abrogated text, which may no longer be acted upon. There is even greater reason for Allah to preserve the abrogating texts than the abrogated ones.

The claim that this verse was abrogated by His words,

“Marry off the unmarried among you,”

is exceedingly weak. A woman’s being guilty of unlawful sexual intercourse is a temporary condition that entails a temporary prohibition on marrying her, just as her being in a state of ritual consecration, observing a waiting period, or being married to another man, and similar circumstances, prohibit marriage until a certain point. Even if she were assumed to be permanently forbidden in marriage, she would be like an idolatress. It is well known that this verse does not address the attributes that make marriage to a woman forbidden, whether permanently or temporarily. Rather, He gave a general command to marry off unmarried women, subject to the conditions He has explained. Just as she may not be married during her waiting period or while in ritual consecration, she may not be married until she repents.

---

## Page 15718

They have also cited the hadith containing the following exchange:

“My wife does not repel the hand of anyone who touches her.” He said, “Divorce her.” The man said, “I love her.” He replied, “Then enjoy her company.”

This hadith was reported by al-Nasā’ī. Ahmad and others graded it weak, so it cannot serve as evidence against the Quran and Sunnah. Even if it were authentic, its meaning would not be explicit. Some interpret “one who touches” as someone asking for money, but this interpretation is weak. Rather, the expression may refer to someone who touches her with his hand without having intercourse with her. Some women display their charms and do not recoil when a man looks at them or places his hand on them, yet do not allow him to have intercourse with them. Marriage to such a woman is disliked. Hence, he instructed the man to separate from her, but did not require him to do so when he mentioned that he loved her. She had not committed unlawful sexual intercourse, but had sinned by engaging in some of the acts leading to it.

This is why he said, “She does not repel the hand of anyone who touches her,” specifying touching with the hand alone. When the words “touching” and “mutual touching” mean intercourse, they are not restricted to the hand. When touching is expressly associated with the hand, however, it is like the words of Allah Most High:

“Even if We had sent down to you a written scripture on parchment, and they had touched it with their hands.”

Moreover, a woman who commits unlawful sexual intercourse after marriage is not like one who marries while already engaging in it. An existing marriage has a stronger claim to validity than a marriage being initiated. A state of prohibition or a waiting period prevents initiating a marriage, but not continuing it. Thus, if there were evidence from revelation establishing that a woman who commits unlawful sexual intercourse after the marriage contract need not be divorced, such intercourse would be treated like the waiting period: it would prevent initiating a marriage, but not continuing it. This would reconcile the two pieces of evidence.

If it is asked, “What is meant by His words, ‘None marries her except a man guilty of unlawful sexual intercourse or an idolater’?” the answer is: If the man marrying her is a Muslim, he is guilty of unlawful sexual intercourse; if he is not a Muslim, he is a disbeliever. If he believes in the prohibition brought by the Messenger yet does this, he is guilty of unlawful sexual intercourse. If he does not believe in what the Messenger brought, he is an idolater, as was their practice in the pre-Islamic era: they would marry

---

## Page 15719

prostitutes.

He is saying: “If you marry them as you used to do, without believing this to be forbidden, you are idolaters; but if you believe it to be forbidden, you are guilty of unlawful sexual intercourse.” For such a woman allows men other than her husband to have intercourse with her, while her husband continues to have intercourse with her just as they do. Any woman whose sexual partners are two men is guilty of unlawful sexual intercourse, for sexual access cannot be shared; rather, a wife must be chaste.

Thus, since a man who marries a woman who engages in unlawful sexual intercourse is himself guilty of unlawful sexual intercourse, people condemn him. Indeed, they condemn him more severely than someone who commits unlawful sexual intercourse with women from other households. Hence, when describing an insult, people say, “He reviled him with zāy and qāf”—meaning that he called him, “You husband of a whore!” This is among the gravest insults people exchange, because the reprehensibility of such conduct is firmly established among Muslims. How, then, could it be permissible?

For this reason, accusing a woman of unlawful sexual intercourse also impugns her husband. If he were permitted to marry a prostitute, such an accusation would not impugn him. Hence the statement made by some of the early generations: “No prophet’s wife ever committed unlawful sexual intercourse.” Allah Most High allowed the prophets to marry unbelieving women, but did not allow them to marry prostitutes, because the latter undermine the purpose of marriage, unlike unbelieving women.

For this reason, Allah allowed a man whose wife commits unlawful sexual intercourse to swear the oaths of imprecation instead of producing four witnesses, and waived the prescribed punishment against him upon his taking those oaths, because of the harm her conduct causes him. The hadith states:

“A cuckold will not enter Paradise.”

A man who marries a prostitute is a cuckold. Allah has instilled in all His believing servants a natural disposition to condemn and reproach such a man for this—indeed, even in non-Muslims, whether People of the Book or others: they all condemn a man whose

---

## Page 15720

wife is a prostitute, and they insult and shame him for it. How, then, can permission for this be attributed to Islamic law? No prophet could bring such a ruling, let alone the finest of all revealed laws.

Rather, the revealed law must be cleared of such a claim. When a believer considers this claim and its implications, he finds it outrageous to attribute anything of the sort to the revealed law. He sees that clearing it of this attribution is even more important than clearing ‘Ā’ishah of what those who spread the slander said about her. Allah commanded the believers to say:

“Glory be to You! This is a monstrous slander!”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not part with ‘Ā’ishah because he initially did not believe what was said. When doubts arose in his mind, he consulted ‘Alī and Zayd ibn Hārithah and questioned the maidservant to ascertain whether it was true, in which case he would part with her. Then Allah sent down her exoneration from heaven, and this secured the continuation of her marriage. No Muslim has ever said that it is permissible to retain a prostitute as a wife.

By speaking against her, the hypocrites intended to impugn the Messenger. Had marrying a prostitute been permissible, he would have said, “There is no blame upon me for this.” His wives would sometimes distress him to the point that he stayed apart from them; a woman’s sins do not impugn her husband. Her prostitution, however, does impugn him in the eyes of everyone. No one shields from reproach a man who marries a woman whom he knows to be a prostitute persisting in prostitution. Thus the hypocrites used this as a means of attacking him, until Allah sent down her exoneration from heaven.

When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said, “Who will support me in dealing with a man whose abuse of my family has reached me? By Allah, I have known nothing but good of my family. They have also mentioned a man of whom I have known nothing but good,” Sa‘d ibn Mu‘ādh stood up—Sa‘d ibn Mu‘ādh, at whose death the Throne of the Most Merciful shook—and said:

“I will support you against him. If he is one of our brothers from al-Aws, I will strike off his head; and if he is from among

---

## Page 15721

our brothers of al-Khazraj, give us your command and we will carry it out against him.” Then Sa‘d ibn ‘Ubādah was overcome by partisan zeal.

‘Ā’ishah said: “Before that, he had been a righteous man, but tribal fervour overcame him, for Ibn Ubayy was a leader among his people. He said, ‘You lie! By Allah, you shall not kill him, nor will you be able to kill him.’

“Then Usayd ibn Hudayr stood up and said, ‘You lie! By Allah, we shall certainly kill him! You are a hypocrite defending the hypocrites.’ The two tribes rose against one another, until the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, came down and began to calm them.”

Had the allegations against ‘Ā’ishah not impugned the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, the believers would not have sought to kill whoever among al-Aws and al-Khazraj had made them, on account of his slander against the Prophet’s wife. This is why anyone who accuses the Prophet’s mother of unchastity is to be put to death: he has impugned the Prophet’s lineage. Likewise, anyone who accuses his wives of unchastity is to be put to death, because he has impugned his religion. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, refrained from putting them to death only because they had spoken before he knew of her innocence and that she was one of the Mothers of the Believers whom he had not divorced—upon him ... (1)

For he could have divorced her, thereby ending her status as a Mother of the Believers, according to the stronger of the scholars’ two views. Concerning a woman whom the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, divorced, there are “three views” within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. “The first” is that she is not one of the Mothers of the Believers. “The second” is that she is one of the Mothers of the Believers. “The third” distinguishes between a woman whose marriage was consummated and one whose marriage was not consummated.

The first is more correct, for when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, gave his wives the choice between remaining married and separation, the intention was that any woman from whom he parted could marry someone else. Thus, if this had been permissible, it would not have impugned his religion.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in both originals.

---

## Page 15722

In short, this matter is too weighty in the hearts of believers to require extensive evidence, for faith and the Quran forbid such things. Nevertheless, many Muslim scholars among the Successors and later generations—whose learning, piety, and high standing are beyond doubt—have permitted such things on the basis of an interpretation they adopted. It has therefore been necessary to discuss the matter at length.

There are many comparable cases: an opinion may be exceedingly weak, yet its weakness remains unclear to many people of learning and faith, including eminent figures. For when Muslims disagree, Allah has made reference to the Book and the Sunnah the only safeguard against error. Everyone’s statements may be accepted or rejected, except those of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, who does not speak from his own desire.

If it is asked: What, then, of His statement:

“A fornicator marries none but a woman who fornicates or a polytheist woman”?

The answer is: This indicates that a fornicator who has not repented may not marry a chaste woman, as stated in one of the two opinions reported from Ahmad. For if he continues to have intercourse with this woman, that woman, and another, as before, his intercourse with this wife is of the same kind as his intercourse with the other women who fornicate. Al-Shaʿbī said: “Whoever marries his daughter to an immoral man has severed his bond of kinship with her.”

Moreover, if he commits adultery with other men’s wives, this is something that prompts his own wife to make herself available to another man, as frequently happens. I have never seen a man who has illicit intercourse with other men’s wives or with males without thereby driving his own wife to commit adultery with someone else, in retaliation and to spite him.

Furthermore, if illicit intercourse is his habitual practice, he finds satisfaction with prostitutes and fails to satisfy his wife sufficiently to keep her chaste, leaving her in need of illicit intercourse.

---

## Page 15723

Moreover, when he commits adultery with other men’s wives, those men seek to commit adultery with his wives, as actually happens. Thus, an adulterer’s wife is led into adultery in many ways. If she regards as lawful what Allah has forbidden, she becomes a polytheist. Even if she does not commit adultery with her private parts, she does so with her eyes and in other ways. Among the wives of men who persist in illicit intercourse without repenting, scarcely any woman is known to remain entirely untainted. A woman’s nature draws her towards other men when she sees her husband going to other women. It is stated in the hadith:

“Be dutiful to your fathers, and your children will be dutiful to you; remain chaste, and your wives will remain chaste.”

Thus, His statement, “A fornicator marries none but a woman who fornicates,” may mean either that his very marriage to her and intercourse with her constitute fornication, or that this leads her to commit fornication. As for a woman who fornicates, having intercourse with her while she persists in fornication is itself fornication.

Likewise, “chaste women from among the believers” refers to free women. Ibn Abbās is also reported to have said: “They are chaste women.” Thus, Ibn Abbās has been reported to interpret muhsanāt as both free women and chaste women, and this is correct. We say that the evidence for this includes His statement, Exalted is He:

“They ask you what has been made lawful for them. Say, ‘Lawful for you are all good things and the game caught by the hunting animals you have trained, teaching them from what Allah has taught you. So eat of what they catch for you, and mention Allah’s name over it. And fear Allah; indeed, Allah is swift in reckoning.’”

“This day all good things have been made lawful for you. The food of those who were given the Scripture is lawful for you, and your food is lawful for them. And lawful for you are chaste women from among the believers and chaste women from among those who were given the Scripture before you, provided that you give them their bridal dues, seeking chastity, not fornication.”

The Quran commentators have said that muhsanāt means chaste women. This was the view of al-Shaʿbī, al-Hasan, al-Nakhaʿī, al-Dahhāk, and al-Suddī. Ibn Abbās is reported to have said: “They are free women.” If the term muhsanāt is intended to mean “free women,” then chastity is all the more encompassed by the term, for the basic meaning of

---

## Page 15724

muhsana is that of a chaste woman whose chastity is guarded. Allah, Exalted is He, says:

“And Mary, the daughter of Imrān, who guarded her chastity.”

He also says:

“Indeed, those who slander chaste, unsuspecting, believing women.”

These are chaste women. Hassān ibn Thābit said:

“Chaste and dignified, beyond suspicion,
She greets the dawn without feeding on unsuspecting women’s flesh.”

It was customary among the Arabs that free women were not known to commit fornication; rather, slave women were known for it. Thus:

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, took the pledge of Hind, the wife of Abū Sufyān, that she would not commit fornication, she said: ‘Does a free woman commit fornication?’”

This was not something known among them. Thus, in common usage, “free woman”—as opposed to a slave woman—came to mean a chaste woman, because a woman who was free rather than enslaved was known among them for chastity. The term ihsān therefore came to encompass freedom together with chastity, because slave women were not chaste. Likewise, Islam forbids indecency and wrongdoing. Similarly, a married woman’s husband safeguards her chastity, because he meets her needs and jealously guards her honour. Thus, the term “ihsān” came to encompass Islam, freedom, and marriage, although its fundamental meaning is chastity. A chaste woman is one whose private parts are guarded from anyone other than her husband, just as a chaste man abstains from anyone other than his wife. Since Allah has permitted marriage only to chaste women from among the Muslims and the People of the Scripture, and prostitutes are not chaste, Allah has not permitted marriage to them.

Further evidence of this is His statement:

“Provided that you give them their bridal dues, seeking chastity, neither committing fornication nor taking secret lovers.”

A musāfih is a fornicator who spills his semen with one woman after another.

---

## Page 15725

and likewise a woman who fornicates, or who takes a secret lover whose mistress she becomes and with whom alone she has illicit relations. For marriage to be lawful, the man must neither fornicate nor take a secret lover. If the woman is a prostitute who has intercourse with one man after another, her husband is not safeguarding her chastity against other men. Were he safeguarding her chastity, she would be chaste; if she is promiscuous, she is not chaste. Allah has permitted marriage only when men safeguard chastity rather than commit fornication. And if it is stipulated that he must not commit fornication with another woman—and thus must not spill his semen with anyone other than her—then this reinforces the point all the more.

The lexicographers say that sifāh means fornication. Ibn Qutaybah explained “seeking chastity” as “being married.” Regarding “not committing fornication,” he said: “Its root comes from the expression safahtu al-qirbah, meaning ‘I poured out the waterskin’s contents.’ Fornication was therefore called sifāh because the man pours out his reproductive fluid, and the woman pours out hers.”

Ibn Fāris said: “Sifāh is the spilling of sexual fluid without a contract or marriage; she is thus a woman who spills her fluid.”

Al-Zajjāj explained “seeking chastity” as “entering into a marriage contract.” Others besides these two explained it as “remaining chaste, not committing fornication.” Likewise, He says in Sūrat al-Nisā’:

“And lawful to you are all beyond these, provided that you seek them with your wealth in marriage, not in fornication.”

In these two verses, He stipulates that men be muhsinīn, not fornicators, with the letter sād pronounced with an i vowel. A muhsin is someone who safeguards another’s chastity; this is not muhsan, with an a vowel, the status stipulated in connection with the prescribed punishment. Thus, permission to marry is granted only to a man who safeguards the woman’s chastity rather than committing fornication. Whoever marries a prostitute while she continues in prostitution, without safeguarding her chastity against other men—so that she continues to have illicit relations with others just as she did before marriage—is engaging in fornication with her, not safeguarding her chastity. This is forbidden, as the Quran indicates.

---

## Page 15726

If it is objected:

“The meaning is merely that you should seek marriage with your wealth, not fornication: you give her the dowry on the understanding that she will be your wife, with no one else having any right to her. This differs from giving her money on the understanding that she will fornicate with whomever she wishes, or that she will be your mistress, having illicit relations with you alone. That is forbidden.”

The answer is:

“If the purpose of marriage is that she should be yours alone, and no one else’s, yet she has not repented of fornication, would she not be failing to fulfil the requirements of the contract?”

If it is objected:

“He can safeguard her chastity against her will by housing her somewhere she cannot commit fornication.”

The answer is:

“If he safeguards her chastity by force, he is indeed unlike someone who allows her to go out to men and allows men to come in to her. Yet common experience has shown that when a woman desires someone other than her husband, she devises many ways to achieve this without her husband knowing. She may even impair her husband’s reason through what she feeds him, and she may also cast spells on him. This happens frequently: men have been fed such things by their wives and bewitched by them so that the woman can do as she pleases. Alongside this, her intention may be to keep him from going to other women. She thus seeks to prevent him from lawful relations, or from both unlawful and lawful relations. She may also intend to make him let her do whatever she wishes, so that he no longer safeguards her chastity or exercises his responsibility over her; rather, she becomes the one who rules him.
