“If this happens with women who married without having been prostitutes, what, then, of a woman who was a prostitute? Accounts of such things are numerous. If only, once she repented, this necessarily entailed

---

## Page 15727

her remaining steadfast in repentance.

This could be achieved if he were permitted to marry her and told, “Safeguard her chastity and remain vigilant.” Without repentance, however, this is impossible or very difficult.

For this reason, the scholars discussed her repentance. Ibn ʿUmar and Ahmad ibn Hanbal said:

“He should attempt to entice her into illicit relations. If she responds as she used to, she has not repented.”

A group of scholars, including Abū Muhammad, said:

“He should not solicit her, for she may already have repented, only to break her repentance if he solicits her. Moreover, there is a fear that, by soliciting her, he himself may fall into sin with her.”

Those who required her to be tested said:

“The sincerity of her repentance cannot be established merely by her words. This is therefore comparable to His saying:

‘When believing women come to you as emigrants, test them.’”

The term “emigrant” may also encompass someone who repents. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The emigrant is one who abandons what Allah has forbidden, and the emigrant is one who abandons evil.”

Thus, if this woman claims to have abandoned evil, she is tested concerning that claim. In short, he must have a firm conviction that her repentance is sincere.

His saying, Exalted is He, “nor taking secret lovers,” forbids a man to take a secret mistress who commits fornication with him but with no one else. In the verse concerning slave women, He, Glorified is He, says:

“If any of you cannot afford to marry free believing women, then let him marry from among your believing slave women. Allah knows best your faith; you are of one another. So marry them with their owners’ permission and give them their dowries according to what is fair, as chaste women, neither engaging in fornication nor taking secret lovers. Once they are married, if they commit an indecency, their punishment is half that of free women.”

Thus, concerning slave women, He mentioned:

“Chaste women, neither engaging in fornication nor taking secret lovers.”

As for free women, He stipulated that the men be chaste, not fornicators, and in Sūrat al-Mā’idah He mentioned “nor taking secret lovers”

---

## Page 15728

when He mentioned the women of the People of the Book, whereas in Sūrat al-Nisā’ He mentioned only “not fornicators.” This is because slave women, unlike free women, were known for fornication. He therefore stipulated that, for them to be married, they must be chaste, neither engaging in fornication nor taking secret lovers. This also indicates that a slave woman who engages in prostitution may not be married unless she is married on the basis that she will be chaste, with her husband safeguarding her chastity, so that she neither commits fornication with men nor takes a lover. Alongside what has already been discussed, this is among the clearest indications that marrying an unchaste slave woman is forbidden.

It is related from Ibn ʿAbbās that he explained “chaste women” as women who are virtuous, not fornicators, and “nor taking secret lovers” as meaning intimate companions. The people of pre-Islamic ignorance regarded open fornication as forbidden but considered what was concealed permissible.

Another report from him states:

“Women engaging in fornication” are those who do so openly, while “those taking secret lovers” are women who each have a single lover.

Some Quran commentators said:

“A woman would take a lover with whom she committed fornication, but she would not do so with anyone else.”

Thus Ibn ʿAbbās and others among the early generations explained “chaste women” as virtuous women, and their explanation is correct. They mentioned that fornication in pre-Islamic times was of two kinds: one involving multiple partners and another confined to a single partner. The former was ordinarily open, whereas the latter was ordinarily concealed. Since Allah prohibited the latter, which resembles marriage—for in marriage a woman is exclusively bound to one man—it became necessary to distinguish lawful marriage from the forbidden practice of taking secret lovers. For if he commits fornication with this woman alone, he cannot know that no other man has had intercourse with her, nor can he know that the child she bears is his; nor do the distinctive provisions of marriage apply to her.

For this reason, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb used to administer beatings for “secret marriage.” Secret marriage is akin to taking secret lovers and resembles it, especially if she marries herself off without a guardian or witnesses

---

## Page 15729

and the two of them conceal it: this is like a man taking a mistress. There is no outward distinction, recognizable to people, that sets the two apart. Anyone committing fornication with a woman he has taken as a mistress can simply say, whenever he wishes, “I married her.” Likewise, anyone wishing to accuse a man who has married secretly of committing fornication with her can do so. There must therefore be a clear distinction between the lawful and the unlawful. Allah Most High says:

“Allah would never lead a people astray after guiding them until He makes clear to them what they must avoid.”

And He Most High says:

“He has already explained to you in detail what He has forbidden you.”

When it becomes apparent to people that he has safeguarded this woman’s chastity, she is distinguished from women who commit fornication or take secret lovers. But if he allows her to go to unrelated men, chaste women are not distinguished from the others. Similarly, if he conceals his marriage to her so that no one knows of it, she is not distinguished from women who take secret lovers.

Scholars have differed over what distinguishes one from the other. Some held that only public announcement is obligatory, whether witnesses are present or not. This is the position of Mālik, many jurists of the hadith tradition, the Zāhirīs, and Ahmad according to one report. Others held that having witnesses is obligatory, whether the marriage is publicly announced or not. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī, and one report from Ahmad. Others held that both are obligatory; this is the third report from Ahmad. Still others held that either one is required; this is the fourth report from Ahmad.

Requiring “witnesses” alone is a weak position, with no basis in the Quran or the Sunnah, for no hadith concerning it has been authentically established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is inconceivable that something Muslims continually practise should have conditions that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not explain. This is a matter that concerns everyone, and all Muslims need to know about it. If this were a condition, mentioning it would have been more important than mentioning the dowry and other matters, yet it finds no mention in the Book of Allah or in any authentic hadith from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It thus becomes clear that it is not among the things that

---

## Page 15730

Allah has required of Muslims in their marriages.

Ahmad ibn Hanbal and other leading hadith scholars said: “Nothing has been authentically established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning having witnesses to marriage.”

Had he made this obligatory, that obligation could only have been known through the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It would have been among the rulings that had to be made known and publicly proclaimed. Stipulating the dowry would have a stronger claim; yet the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus establish that its amount need not be specified in the marriage contract.

Had he publicly proclaimed this requirement, it would have been transmitted from the Companions. They would not have neglected to preserve knowledge indispensable to Muslims generally, for every incentive and motive favors transmitting it, as does the one who commands its preservation. They preserved his prohibition of shighār marriage and marriage contracted by someone in the state of pilgrimage consecration, and similar matters that occur infrequently. What, then, of marriage without witnesses? If Allah and His Messenger had forbidden and invalidated it, how could no explicit statement from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, have been preserved concerning it?

Indeed, even if something concerning this had been transmitted through solitary reports, it would have been rejected by those who take that position on such reports. This is among the matters that concern everyone most widely—far more widely than many other rulings. It is therefore inconceivable that no Muslim marriage should be valid without witnesses, when Muslims have concluded marriage contracts in numbers known only to the Lord of the heavens.

It is thus clear that making witnesses alone a condition is unquestionably invalid. This is why those who stipulate witnesses hold inconsistent positions, with an inconsistency that reveals the unsoundness of their underlying principle. None of their positions withstands scrutiny against the standard of the revealed law: some of them permit marriage witnessed by two sinful men, whereas, even for testimony they do not consider obligatory, Allah has commanded that two upright men be called as witnesses. How much more, then, should this apply to witnessing that is obligatory!

---

## Page 15731

It is also remarkable that Allah commanded “having witnesses when taking back a divorced wife,” but did not command this for marriage. Yet they require it for marriage, while most of them do not require it when taking a wife back. Allah commanded witnesses when taking a wife back lest the husband’s marital relationship be denied while he continues to live with his wife, leading to his living with her unlawfully. He did not command witnesses to a divorce in which the wife is not taken back, because the husband then releases her graciously once the waiting period ends, making the divorce publicly apparent.

For this reason, Yazīd ibn Hārūn said, criticizing the adherents of juristic opinion: “Allah commanded witnesses for sales, but not for marriage; yet they require witnesses for marriage, but not for sales.” It is as he said. Having witnesses to a sale must be either obligatory or recommended, and the Quran and Sunnah indicate that it is recommended. As for marriage, the revealed law has prescribed neither obligatory nor recommended witnessing. Rather, marriage is to be publicly announced, and its public announcement, together with its continuing nature, makes witnesses unnecessary. A woman lives with a man, and people know that she is his wife. This enduring public visibility dispenses with witnesses, just as with lineage: a man need not call anyone to witness his wife giving birth. Rather, this becomes apparent, and it is known that his wife gave birth to this child, making witnesses unnecessary. A sale is different: it may be denied, and establishing proof of it may prove difficult.

Thus, if a marriage takes place somewhere it would not become publicly known, it is publicized by calling witnesses. Having witnesses to a marriage may therefore become obligatory because this makes it public and known—not because no marriage can be validly contracted without two witnesses. Rather, if a guardian marries his ward to a man, and the two then go out and speak about it within people’s hearing, or witnesses and other people arrive after the contract and are told that he has married her, that is sufficient. This was the practice of the early generations: they did not insist on bringing two witnesses or putting the dowry in writing.

---

## Page 15732

Among those who hold witnessing to be obligatory are some who require two witnesses whose uprightness is unverified, even though they accept testimony in court only from those known to be upright. This, too, fails to achieve the intended purpose. Some have taken the anomalous position of requiring witnesses known to be upright. This view is certainly unsound, for Muslims have not observed such a requirement in their marriages.

These three positions exist within Ahmad’s school, on the view that he requires witnesses. One holds that two openly sinful witnesses suffice, as Abū Hanīfah maintains. Another holds that two witnesses whose uprightness is unverified suffice; this is the well-known position in Ahmad’s school and that of al-Shāfiʿī. A further position within the school requires witnesses known to be upright. It has also been said that, if a judge concludes the marriage contract, he must use witnesses known to be upright, unlike anyone else who concludes it, since judges are the ones who distinguish those of established uprightness from those whose uprightness is unverified.

Moreover, requiring their uprightness to be known to the local judge contradicts the Muslims’ consensus, past and present: they conclude marriages among themselves while the judge lives among them yet does not know them. If, instead, they require witnesses renowned among their community for goodness, having such a reputation is not a condition for a witness to be upright and his testimony admissible.

Furthermore, witnesses die and their circumstances change. Yet these scholars say: “The purpose of witnessing is to establish the marital relationship when it is disputed, safeguarding the child’s lineage.”

The response is that this is achieved by publicly announcing the marriage, whereas having witnesses while keeping it secret does not achieve it at all. What admits of no doubt is that a publicly announced marriage is valid even if two witnesses did not witness it. As for a marriage kept secret but witnessed, that requires examination. When both witnessing and public announcement are present, its validity is undisputed.

If there is neither witnessing nor public announcement, the marriage is invalid according to scholars generally. If any disagreement on this is supposed to exist,

---

## Page 15733

it is confined to very few. It may be thought that there is disagreement on this within Ahmad’s school. But one must then ask: what distinguishes this from women taking secret lovers? Among Abū Hanīfah’s followers who require witnesses, some do not explain this requirement as a means of establishing the marital relationship. Rather, the purpose is for two people to attend in recognition of the solemnity of marriage. This ultimately serves the purpose of public announcement.

Where people are unfamiliar with one another’s circumstances and do not know whether the woman living with a man is his wife or his lover—as in places with many people whose circumstances are unknown—it may be said that witnessing is obligatory in such cases.

The Companions did not draw up “dower documents,” because they did not marry with deferred dowers. Rather, they paid the dower immediately, and if they deferred it, it remained known. When people began marrying with deferred dowers, however, lengthy periods would pass and the dower would be forgotten. They therefore began recording the deferred amount, and this became evidence establishing both the dower and that the woman was the man’s wife. Such witnessing achieves the intended purpose whether the witnesses attend the marriage contract or come afterward and witness the acknowledgment of the husband, wife, and guardian, knowing that the marriage has been publicly announced. Having them witness it without any agreement to conceal it is itself a public announcement.

This differs from the requirement of a “marriage guardian,” which is established in several places in the Quran and several places in the Sunnah. It was also the practice of the Companions: men were the ones who married women off, and no woman is known to have contracted her own marriage.

This is one of the distinctions between marriage and women taking secret lovers. Hence ʿĀ’ishah said:

“A woman may not contract her own marriage, for it is the prostitute who contracts her own marriage.”

Yet the guardian’s involvement alone is not sufficient; the marriage must also be publicly announced, for some guardians are indulgent toward their female relatives.

---

## Page 15734

Allah Most High says:

“Marry off the unmarried among you and the righteous among your male and female slaves.”

He also says:

“Do not give women in marriage to polytheistic men until they believe.”

He thus addresses men, instructing them to marry off the unmarried, just as He instructs them to arrange marriages for slaves. There is a distinction between His words, “Do not give women in marriage to polytheistic men,” and His words, “Do not marry polytheistic women.” Some of the early generations from the Prophet’s household cited this distinction as evidence.

Moreover, Allah has made the dower obligatory in other passages, but has not made witnessing obligatory. Whoever says that marriage is valid even when a dower is expressly excluded, yet invalid without witnesses, has dispensed with something Allah made obligatory and imposed something Allah did not make obligatory.

This helps demonstrate that the position of the scholars of Medina and the scholars of hadith, who prohibit “shighār marriage,” is sounder than that of the scholars of Kufa. It also demonstrates that the reason for this prohibition is specifically the exclusion of a dower. Wherever there is a dower, the marriage is valid, as the scholars of Medina maintain. This is also the more explicit and unequivocal of the two reports from Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and the position preferred by his early followers.

This and similar examples demonstrate the greater strength of the positions held by the scholars of hadith and transmitted reports, and by the scholars of the Hijaz—such as those of Medina—over contrary positions based on legal opinions that conflict with the scriptural texts. Nevertheless, those jurists who arrived at opinions contrary to the texts after exercising independent judgment and exhausting their efforts—may Allah be pleased with them—did all they could to seek knowledge and strove to reach the correct judgment. Allah rewards them, and in doing so they are obedient to Allah, Glorified is He. Allah rewards them for their efforts: He has thus rewarded them for this, even though those who knew what the scriptural texts taught

---

## Page 15735

were superior to those to whom the texts were unknown. The former receive two rewards, and the latter one, as Allah Most High says:

“And remember David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field into which some people’s sheep had strayed by night. We were witness to their judgment.”

“We gave Solomon understanding of the case, and to each We gave judgment and knowledge.”

Whoever reflects upon the texts of the Quran and Sunnah will find that they explain marriage without imposing the conditions required by certain jurists. Some, for example, stipulate that marriage must be contracted using the words inkāh or tazwīj (giving in marriage). Some require it to be in Arabic. These jurists, together with others, require the presence of two witnesses. Yet despite this, they deem a marriage valid even when a dower is expressly excluded.

They then divide into two groups. One considers “shighār marriage” valid, since its only potentially invalidating feature is the exclusion of a dower, which they do not regard as invalidating. The other considers it invalid, but offers unsound reasons for doing so, as we have explained at length elsewhere. They also deem valid a “muhallil marriage,” in which the husband intends to make the woman lawful for her former husband.

Thus, the position of the scholars of hadith and the scholars of Medina—who did not require a particular marriage formula or the presence of two witnesses when the marriage was publicly announced and made known, and who invalidated shighār marriage, every marriage in which a dower was expressly excluded, and muhallil marriage … (1)—was more consistent with the Quran, the Sunnah, and the reports from the Companions.

Furthermore, many jurists of the Hijaz and Iraq who relied on legal opinion broadened the “scope of divorce.” They gave effect to divorce pronounced by an intoxicated person and to divorce invoked in an oath, and these jurists also gave effect to the divorce pronounced by

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 15736

a person under coercion, while others gave effect to divorce whose occurrence was doubtful in cases involving an oath of divorce. They treated an irrevocable separation as a divorce counting toward the limit of three, and thus regarded khulʿ (marital dissolution in return for compensation) as an irrevocable divorce counting toward that limit. These and other positions broadened the scope of divorce, which makes the lawful unlawful, while restricting lawful marriage. Then, having broadened the scope of divorce, some increasingly resorted to legal stratagems to enable a woman to return to her husband, while others recognized no way for her to return. Thus, some were burdened with shackles and fetters, while others resorted to deception and stratagems.

Whoever reflects upon the Quran, the Sunnah, and the reports from the Companions will see that Allah has made all this unnecessary. Allah sent Muhammad with the pure and accommodating faith of monotheism, in which He enjoined what is right, forbade what is wrong, permitted wholesome things, and prohibited foul things. Allah, Glorified is He, knows best. May Allah bless Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them peace.

Shaykh al-Islām Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a daughter born of illicit intercourse:

“May she be married to her father?”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. The position of the majority of scholars is that marrying her is impermissible, and this is unquestionably correct. The majority even differed over whether someone who does so should be executed, holding two views. The position transmitted from Ahmad is that someone who does so should be executed.

It may be said that this applies when he was not acting on an interpretation of the law. As for “one acting on an interpretation of the law,” he is not to be executed, even if mistaken. Alternatively, it may be said that this applies without qualification, just as the majority held that someone who drinks nabīdh whose permissibility is disputed, acting on an interpretation of the law, is to be flogged—even though al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him, do not regard him as morally corrupt. He was, however, judged morally corrupt by

---

## Page 15737

Mālik and Ahmad, according to the other report from him.

The correct view is that someone whose interpretation of the law is excusable is not morally corrupt; indeed, he incurs no sin. Ahmad had not heard that there was any disagreement on this issue, for the disagreement only emerged in his own time, not in the time of the early generations. That is why he was unaware of it.

Those who permitted “marrying one’s daughter born of illicit intercourse” argued: “She is not legally his daughter, as shown by the fact that neither inherits from the other, he is not obliged to maintain her, he does not act as her marriage guardian, and she is not automatically emancipated if he acquires ownership of her. The same applies to other legal consequences of kinship. Since she is not legally his daughter, she is not included in the verse prohibiting marriage, and thus remains included in His words:

‘Lawful to you are all beyond these.’”

The majority’s argument, however, is as follows: Allah Most High’s words,

“Forbidden to you in marriage are your mothers and your daughters,”

and the rest of the verse encompass everyone to whom these terms apply, whether literally or figuratively, and whether mutual inheritance and other legal consequences apply or only the prohibition of marriage applies. The general scope of the verse prohibiting marriage is not the same as that of the verse prescribing inheritance shares and similar verses, such as His words:

“Allah instructs you concerning your children: a male shall have a share equal to that of two females.”

This can be explained in three ways.

First: the verse prohibiting marriage encompasses one’s daughter, son’s daughter, and daughter’s daughter, just as the term “paternal aunt” encompasses the paternal aunts of one’s father, mother, and grandfather. Likewise, it encompasses one’s sister’s daughter, sister’s son’s daughter, and sister’s daughter’s daughter. Such breadth of scope does not apply to the verse prescribing inheritance shares, nor to similar verses and texts in which legal rulings are attached to kinship.

---

## Page 15738

Second: The prohibition of marriage is established by breastfeeding alone, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Breastfeeding prohibits what birth prohibits.”

Another wording reads:

“What blood relationship prohibits.”

This hadith is agreed to be authentic and [is followed] by the imams. Allah has forbidden a woman to marry a child whom she has nourished with her milk, or to marry his descendants. He has likewise forbidden such marriages to her female ascendants, paternal aunts, and maternal aunt. Indeed, He has forbidden a girl breastfed by a woman to marry the man responsible for that milk—that is, the man whose intercourse with the woman caused her to produce milk.

If a man is forbidden to marry his daughter through breastfeeding, although none of the legal consequences of blood relationship apply to her apart from the prohibition of marriage and its attendant sanctity, how could he be permitted to marry a daughter created from his semen? How can a girl created from his semen be compared to one nourished with milk produced as a result of his intercourse? This establishes the prohibition through the general scope of the wording, its indications and implied meaning, and a fortiori analogy.

Third: Allah Most High said:

“And the wives of your sons who are from your own loins.”

The scholars said that this qualification excludes an adopted son, as Allah said:

“So that there should be no restriction upon the believers concerning the wives of their adopted sons once the latter no longer have need of them.”

It is well known that, in the pre-Islamic period, they acknowledged children born of illicit intercourse as their own even more emphatically than they acknowledged adopted children. If, then, Allah Most High qualified this with His words,

“From your own loins,”

it follows that the term “daughters” and similar terms encompass everyone included under those designations in their language.

As for the assertion that inheritance and similar legal consequences do not apply to her, the answer is that the legal consequences of kinship are separable: some may apply without others, just as

---

## Page 15739

most of those who dispute this agree that the child of a woman who has undergone mutual imprecation is forbidden in marriage to the man who invoked it against her, yet does not inherit from him. Scholars hold two views on acknowledging paternity of a child born of illicit intercourse when the mother has no husband.

It is established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, attributed the son of Zamʿa ibn al-Aswad’s slave woman to Zamʿa ibn al-Aswad, although ʿUtba ibn Abī Waqqās had made her pregnant. Saʿd and ʿAbd ibn Zamʿa disputed over the child (*). Saʿd said, “He is my brother’s son. My brother entrusted me with the matter, saying, ‘This son of Zamʿa’s slave woman is my son.’”

ʿAbd said, “He is my brother, the son of my father’s slave woman, born on my father’s bed.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said, “He is yours, O ʿAbd ibn Zamʿa. The child belongs to the lawful bed, and the adulterer has no claim. Conceal yourself from him, O Sawda.”

This was because he saw the child’s clear resemblance to ʿUtba. Thus, he treated him as her brother for purposes of inheritance, but not as a close relative before whom she could unveil.

Scholars have also differed over whether a child born of illicit intercourse is automatically emancipated upon coming into the ownership of the biological father. There are two views within the schools of Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad. This issue requires a fuller discussion than this sheet can accommodate.

No one may attribute such a weak opinion to any imam of the Muslims, whether to disparage him or to follow him in it. Doing so involves both impugning the imams and following weak opinions. Through such means, the vizier of the Tatars began stirring up discord among the schools of Ahl al-Sunnah, seeking to induce them to abandon the Sunnah and the united community and draw them into the doctrines of the Rāfidah and the proponents of heresy. Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 232):
> In the Sahīh, his lineage is not specified. He is in fact the son of Zam‘a ibn Qays, of Banū ‘Āmir ibn Lu’ayy. As for Zam‘a ibn al-Aswad, he belonged to Banū Asad ibn ‘Abd al-‘Uzzā and is a different person.

---

## Page 15740

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man committed illicit intercourse with a woman in his youth. Recently, he saw a daughter with her whom he wishes to marry. He does not know whether she is his daughter or another man’s, and is therefore hesitant to proceed. May he marry her?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. According to most scholars, he may not marry her. Even if she is another man’s daughter by the woman with whom he committed illicit intercourse, marrying her is unlawful according to Abū Hanīfa and Mālik, and according to one of the two reports from Ahmad. If she is his own daughter born of illicit intercourse, the prohibition is even more severe. If he cannot distinguish her from another woman, both are forbidden to him in marriage.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man committed illicit intercourse with a woman, who became pregnant by him and gave birth to a girl. May he marry the daughter?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. This is unlawful according to the overwhelming majority of scholars. None of the Companions or those who followed them in righteousness permitted it. Hence Ahmad ibn Hanbal and other scholars, despite their extensive knowledge, knew of no disagreement concerning this among the early generations. A ruling was therefore given by Ahmad

---

## Page 15741

ibn Hanbal: “If he does so, he is to be put to death.” He was then asked, “But has not so-and-so reported a contrary view from Mālik?” He replied, “So-and-so is lying.”

A number of scholars reported from him that a child born of illicit intercourse is attributed to his biological father if the latter acknowledges him as his child, and that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb “affiliated”—that is, attributed—the children born in pre-Islamic times to their fathers. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The child belongs to the marital bed, and the fornicator has no claim.”

This applies when the woman has a husband. As for a “prostitute” who has no husband, scholars disagree over whether the man who committed illicit intercourse with her may establish the child’s lineage by acknowledging him.

According to the overwhelming majority of scholars, the “daughter of a woman who has undergone mutual imprecation” is not lawful in marriage to the man who invoked it. Only an isolated dissenting view exists, even though her lineage is severed from her father. Yet if he were to acknowledge her as his child, her lineage would be attributed to him. The two do not inherit from one another, by agreement of the imams.

This is because the rulings concerning “lineage” are separable: a man may be considered someone’s son for some rulings but not others. Thus, the son of a woman who has undergone mutual imprecation is not treated as a son in matters of inheritance: he neither inherits from his father nor does his father inherit from him. Yet he is a son under “marriage law”: the daughter of a woman who has undergone mutual imprecation is forbidden in marriage to her father.

Allah, Glorified and Exalted, has forbidden through breastfeeding the same marriages that He has forbidden through lineage. A man may therefore marry neither his daughter nor his sister through breastfeeding, even though none of the “rulings concerning lineage”—inheritance, blood-money obligations, guardianship, maintenance, or anything else—apply to her. What applies to her is only the prohibition of marriage and mahram status. The “Mothers of the Believers” are mothers only with respect to the prohibition of marriage, not mahram status.

Thus, if a girl whom his wife breastfed with milk resulting from his intercourse with her is forbidden in marriage to him, even though she is not attributed to him for inheritance or other purposes, what then of a girl created from his own semen? Her connection to him is stronger than that of the former. And His words, Most High, in the Quran

---

## Page 15742

“Forbidden to you in marriage are your mothers and your daughters” (and the rest of the verse), encompass anyone designated a daughter, so that both his daughter’s daughter and his son’s daughter are forbidden to him in marriage. This differs from His words concerning inheritance:

“Allah instructs you concerning your children.”

This encompasses only his own children and his son’s children, not his daughter’s children. Since the terms “son” and “daughter,” when unqualified, encompass anyone so designated, Allah Most High said:

“And the wives of your sons who are from your own loins.”

This excludes an adopted son, such as Zayd, who used to be called Zayd ibn Muhammad. They called him a “son,” so had the wording been left unqualified, he might have been thought to fall within its scope. Thus, Allah Most High said, “who are from your own loins,” to exclude that case.

He permitted Muslim men to marry the former wives of their adopted sons, saying:

“So when Zayd had no further need of her, We gave her to you in marriage, so that there would be no restriction upon the believers concerning the wives of their adopted sons when they have no further need of them.”

Thus, if the terms “son” and “daughter” encompass everyone affiliated to a person—to the extent that Allah has forbidden him to marry his daughter through breastfeeding—then his daughter born of illicit intercourse is also called his “daughter.” She therefore has an even stronger claim to be forbidden to him in marriage under Islamic law and to be included within the verse establishing that prohibition. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and his followers, Mālik and his followers, Ahmad ibn Hanbal and his followers, and the overwhelming majority of the imams of the Muslims.

The well-known disagreement among the Companions, the Successors, and those after them concerns whether illicit intercourse establishes marriage prohibitions through affinity. Thus, if a man wishes to marry the woman’s mother or her daughter by another man, this is a matter of longstanding disagreement among the early generations. Many scholars have upheld each position. Al-Shāfiʿī and Mālik, according to one of the two reports from him, permit it; Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad, and Mālik, according to the other report, prohibit it. In this matter, it is permissible for a person to follow either of the two scholarly positions. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15743

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who went to his house and found an unrelated man with his wife. He paid her what was due to her and divorced her, then returned and reconciled with her. He subsequently heard that she had been found beside an unrelated man.

He replied:

It is reported in a hadith from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When Allah, glorified and exalted be He, created Paradise, He said: ‘By My might and majesty, neither a miser, nor a liar, nor a dayyūth shall enter you.’”

A dayyūth is someone who has no protective jealousy.

An authentic hadith reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The believer has protective jealousy, and Allah has jealousy. Allah’s jealousy is aroused when a servant does what He has forbidden him.”

Allah Most High has said:

“An adulterer marries none but an adulteress or a polytheist woman, and an adulteress is married by none but an adulterer or a polytheist man. That is forbidden to the believers.”

Therefore, the correct one of the two scholarly opinions is that a woman who commits illicit intercourse may not be married until she repents. Likewise, if a man’s wife is committing illicit intercourse, he may not keep her as his wife while she remains in that state. Rather, he must part with her; otherwise, he is a dayyūth.

---

## Page 15744

He was also asked:

About a man who married his own daughter born of illicit intercourse.

He replied:

According to the majority of Muslim imams, he may not marry her. Indeed, Imam Ahmad denied that there had been any disagreement among the early generations on this matter. He said: “Whoever does this is to be put to death.” When told that Mālik had permitted it, he rejected that attribution to Mālik as false.

Its prohibition is the position of Abū Hanīfa and his followers, Ahmad and his followers, Mālik and the majority of his followers, and many of al-Shāfiʿī’s followers. He also denied that al-Shāfiʿī had explicitly stated otherwise. They said: “His explicit statement concerned his daughter through suckling, not the woman with whom he had committed illicit intercourse.” Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

About a man who committed illicit intercourse with a woman and then died: may that man’s son marry her or not?

He replied:

Marrying her is forbidden according to the schools of Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad, and according to one of the two positions in Mālik’s school. The other position permits it, which is also the position of al-Shāfiʿī’s school.

---

## Page 15745

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who has intercourse with his slave woman, knowing that another man also has intercourse with her, and does not safeguard her chastity.

He replied:

He is a dayyūth (a man who tolerates sexual immorality among his women), and “No dayyūth will enter Paradise.” Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

About a man whose slave woman commits illicit intercourse: is it lawful for him to have intercourse with her?

He replied:

If she commits illicit intercourse, he may not have intercourse with her until she menstruates and he establishes that she is not pregnant from illicit intercourse. For:

“A man who commits illicit intercourse marries none but a woman who commits illicit intercourse or a polytheist.”

This applies both to the marriage contract and to intercourse. Whenever he has intercourse with her while she engages in illicit intercourse, he is a dayyūth. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

About a hadith concerning the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in which “A man said to him: ‘O Messenger of Allah, my wife does not repel the hand of anyone who touches her.’” Does this mean that she does not withhold herself from anyone, or that she does not withhold her hand from giving to anyone? And is this hadith authentic or not?

---

## Page 15746

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds. Ahmad and others graded this hadith as weak. Some have interpreted it to mean that she does not turn away anyone asking for money, but the apparent meaning and context of the hadith indicate otherwise.

Others have held that it is established and that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded the man to keep her as his wife even though she did not refuse men. Several leading scholars rejected this, for Allah says in His Mighty Book:

“A man who commits illicit intercourse marries none but a woman who commits illicit intercourse or a polytheist, and a woman who commits illicit intercourse is married by none but a man who commits illicit intercourse or a polytheist. This has been forbidden to the believers.”

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd and elsewhere, it is reported:

“A man had a prostitute named ‘Anāq as his companion during the pre-Islamic period. He asked the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—about marrying her, whereupon Allah revealed this verse.”

He—glorified and exalted be He—also says:

“And whoever among you cannot afford to marry free believing women may marry believing slave women from among those whom your right hands possess. Allah knows best your faith; you are of one another. So marry them with their owners’ permission and give them their dowries in a fair manner, provided they are chaste, neither promiscuous nor taking lovers.”

Allah permitted marriage to slave women only when they are neither promiscuous nor taking lovers. A promiscuous woman is one who engages in illicit intercourse with anyone, whereas a woman who takes a lover has a single intimate companion. If a woman in this condition may not be married, then what of one who does not repel the hand of anyone who touches her, but instead engages in illicit intercourse with whoever comes along? And if this is the ruling concerning slave women in such a condition, then what of free women?

Allah Most High also says:

“And chaste women from among the believers and chaste women from among those who were given the Scripture before you, when you give them their dowries, being chaste yourselves, neither promiscuous nor taking lovers.”

Thus He stipulated these conditions for men here,

---

## Page 15747

just as He stipulated them for women there. This accords with His words in Sūrat al-Nūr:

“A man who commits illicit intercourse marries none but a woman who commits illicit intercourse or a polytheist, and a woman who commits illicit intercourse is married by none but a man who commits illicit intercourse or a polytheist. This has been forbidden to the believers.”

For whoever marries a woman who has illicit intercourse with other men does not keep his semen protected and safeguarded; rather, it becomes mixed with the semen of others, and the private parts with which he has intercourse are shared with others. This is illicit intercourse. Likewise, if a woman’s husband has illicit intercourse with other women, making no distinction between the lawful and the unlawful, his intercourse with her is of the same kind as an adulterer’s intercourse with the woman with whom he commits adultery, even if no other man has intercourse with his wife. Taking lovers is itself one form of illicit intercourse.

Scholars have differed over whether it is permissible to marry a woman who commits illicit intercourse before she repents, holding two well-known views. However, the Quran, the Sunnah, and reasoned consideration indicate that this is not permissible. As for those who interpret the verse in Sūrat al-Nūr as referring to the marriage contract and hold that this ruling was abrogated, the falsity of their view is evident on several grounds. Moreover, Muslims agree in condemning the toleration of sexual immorality in one’s household. By consensus, a man who marries a prostitute is a man who tolerates such immorality.

A hadith states:

“Neither a miser, nor a liar, nor a man who tolerates sexual immorality in his household will enter Paradise.”

Allah Most High says:

“Impure women are for impure men, and impure men are for impure women; pure women are for pure men, and pure men are for pure women.”

That is, pure men are for pure women, and impure men are for impure women; the same applies to women. If a woman is impure, her partner is impure; and if her partner is impure, she is impure. This is why the condemnation of those who slandered ‘Ā’ishah and others among the Mothers of the Believers was so severe. Were it not for the disgrace this brings upon the husband, there would not have been such stern condemnation.

For this reason, the early generations said:

“No prophet’s wife ever committed illicit intercourse.”

If marrying a prostitute were permissible, it would be necessary to shield

---

## Page 15748

the prophets from something permissible. How could that be, when some prophets’ wives were unbelievers, just as some believing women had unbelieving husbands? Allah Most High says:

“Allah sets forth an example for those who disbelieve: the wife of Noah and the wife of Lot. They were married to two of Our righteous servants, but betrayed them, so their husbands could not avail them at all against Allah. They were told, ‘Enter the Fire along with those who enter.’”

“And Allah sets forth an example for those who believe: the wife of Pharaoh, when she said, ‘My Lord, build for me a house near You in Paradise, and save me from Pharaoh and his deeds, and save me from the wrongdoing people.’”

As for prostitutes, none of the prophets or the righteous married a prostitute, because prostitution corrupts a man’s marriage bed. Hence, a Muslim is permitted to marry a Jewish or Christian woman, provided that he is chaste, neither engaging in illicit intercourse nor taking a lover. Thus, marrying an unbelieving woman may be permissible, whereas marrying a prostitute is not: the harm of her religion does not extend to him, whereas the harm of her prostitution does. And Allah knows best.

Section: Taking Intention into Account in Marriage

This has been discussed at length elsewhere, where it was explained that the purpose underlying contracts must be taken into account.

In all these matters, legal stratagems should be invalidated, as should a marriage contracted to make a woman lawful again for her former husband when that is its intended purpose, and a compensatory divorce effected to circumvent a divorce oath. For the former did not intend

---

## Page 15749

marriage, while the latter did not intend to part with his wife. Rather, he intends her to remain his wife, which is incompatible with intending a compensatory divorce. The former, meanwhile, intends her to become the wife of the man who divorced her three times, which is incompatible with intending her to be his own wife. Hence, he pays no dowry; indeed, they may pay him themselves. Nor does he seek to establish paternity over any child or to form ties of kinship through marrying her. He may even act as an intermediary husband for both a mother and her daughter. These and other considerations show that he did not intend marriage.

“As for temporary marriage”: if a man intends to enjoy married life with a woman for a time and then part with her—for example, a traveller who goes to a town where he will stay for a time and marries there, intending to divorce her when he returns home—but concludes the marriage contract without stipulating a fixed term, there are three opinions on this in Ahmad’s school.

One holds that the marriage is permissible. This is the position chosen by Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī and is the view of the majority. Another holds that it is a marriage contracted to make a woman lawful again for her former husband and is impermissible. This view is reported from al-Awzā‘ī and is the position defended by al-Qādī and his associates in juristic debate. A third holds that it is disliked but not forbidden.

The correct view is that this is not temporary marriage and is not forbidden. This is because he genuinely intends marriage and desires it, unlike the intermediary husband; he simply does not intend the woman to remain with him permanently. Permanence, however, is not a requirement: he is not obliged to keep her with him permanently, but may divorce her. Thus, if he intends to divorce her after a time, he intends something permissible. Temporary marriage, by contrast, resembles a lease: the marriage ends when its term expires, and he has no marital rights over her once that term has ended.

In the present case, however, his marital rights are established without a fixed term. His intention may change, and he may keep her permanently, which is permissible for him. Likewise, if he marries intending to keep her permanently but later decides to divorce her, that is permissible. And if he married her

---

## Page 15750

with the intention of keeping her if she pleased him and parting with her otherwise, that would be permissible. However, this is not to be stipulated in the contract. If, instead, he stipulates that he will either retain her honourably or release her with kindness, this is already what the contract requires under Islamic law. Such a condition is valid according to the majority of scholars, and he is bound by what the Law requires. This resembles the Prophet’s stipulation, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in a sale contract:

“A Muslim’s sale to another Muslim, free of disease, deception, or unlawful taint.”

This, too, is already required by the contract.

Al-Hasan ibn Alī divorced frequently, so perhaps he intended, when marrying most of his wives, to divorce them after a time. Yet no one said that this was temporary marriage. The man under discussion likewise does not intend to divorce her at a specified time, but when his purpose in marrying her and staying in that town has been fulfilled. Even supposing that he intended to divorce her at a particular time, his intention might change. Nothing here makes the marriage fixed-term or turns it into a lease for a specified period.

If the resolve to divorce arose after the marriage contract, it would not invalidate the marriage, nor would his remaining with the woman be considered disliked—even if he intended to divorce her. We know of no disagreement on this, although scholars differ concerning a fixed term introduced into an existing marriage, as when a man stipulates that their divorce will take effect at a future time. There are two opinions on this, both reported from Ahmad.

The first is that the separation takes effect immediately. This is Mālik’s view, so that the marriage does not become fixed-term.

The second is that it does not take effect immediately, because this time limit was introduced after the marriage had been established, and continuing an existing marriage has greater legal force than initiating one. Thus, a waiting period, apostasy, and the state of consecration for pilgrimage prevent a marriage from being initiated, but not from continuing. Consequently, prohibiting a fixed term at the outset does not necessarily entail prohibiting it once the marriage is established. It may, however, be objected that some impediments prevent both continuation and initiation. This is therefore a matter for juristic reasoning, just as scholars have differed concerning

---

## Page 15751

subsequently arising defects and the loss of marital compatibility: do they establish grounds for annulment?

As for an intention to divorce arising later—when he intends to divorce her after a month—we know of no one who has said that this invalidates the marriage. He may or may not divorce her when that time comes. The same applies to someone who has this intention when contracting the marriage. Each spouse marries the other until death, so separation is inevitable.

A man may marry a slave woman whose master intends to emancipate her. If she were emancipated, the choice would be hers, and he knows that she would not choose to remain with him; nevertheless, the marriage is valid. The same applies if her emancipation is deferred to a specified time, or she is designated to be freed upon her master’s death, and he marries her, even though she will have the option of leaving him when that time comes.

Marriage is founded on the husband’s having the right to divorce from the moment the contract is concluded. It is therefore not binding upon him, whereas it is binding upon the woman. If it is known that, after a period, it will cease to be binding upon her and remain terminable at her option, this does not impair the marriage’s validity. Thus, marriage to a man whose penis has been amputated or to an impotent man is valid, as is marriage with conditions stipulated by the husband, even though the woman has the option of ending it if he does not fulfil those conditions.

It is therefore clear that the marriage’s becoming terminable at the woman’s option does not impair its validity, even though this deprives both spouses of complete assurance. His resolve to acquire marital rights with only partial assurance is comparable to this, since the woman enters the marriage on the understanding that he may divorce her if he wishes. This is inherent in marriage: he has resolved only to exercise a right that he possesses by virtue of the contract. It is as though he resolved to divorce her if she committed a sin, or if his wealth diminished, or something similar.

His resolve to divorce her when he travels back to his family, when his absent wife arrives, or when he has satisfied his desire for her falls into this same category.

---

## Page 15752

Zayd had resolved to divorce his wife, yet this did not end their marriage. She remained his wife until he divorced her, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him:

“Fear Allah and keep your wife.”

It has been said that Allah had informed the Prophet that he would marry her, but he concealed this information from the people. Allah reproached him for concealing it, saying:

“And you concealed within yourself what Allah was to disclose”—

namely, what Allah had told him concerning this. Another explanation is that what he concealed was his intention to marry her if Zayd divorced her. In either case, Zayd’s resolve to divorce her did not impair the continuation of their marriage. We know of no disagreement on this point.

If, then, it is established by scriptural evidence and consensus that resolving to divorce her has no immediate effect, this refutes those who say that divorce takes effect merely through intending it in one’s heart. Zayd’s heart had turned away from her, yet she remained his wife until he pronounced her divorce. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has pardoned my community for what occurs within their minds, so long as they neither speak nor act upon it.”

This is the position of the majority, including Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, and it is one of the two positions reported from Mālik.

It does not follow that, because stipulating a fixed term invalidates a marriage, intending to divorce later also invalidates it. An intention invalidates a contract when it contradicts the contract’s purpose. Divorce after a period of time is permissible and does not contradict the purpose of the marriage contract up until the divorce. This differs from the case of a man who marries a woman to make her lawful again for her former husband: he has no desire whatsoever to be married to her, but only for her to become the first husband’s wife. If that could be achieved without such an intervening marriage, this man would not have married her to make her lawful for him.

If his aim is payment, he would not marry her if he could obtain it without doing so. If, in this case, his aim is to have intercourse with her that day, then this falls into the category of

---

## Page 15753

a prostitute with whom one intends to have intercourse for a day or two. This differs from a man who marries intending to remain married: the choice rests with him, and no one has stipulated that he divorce her, as is stipulated for a man marrying her to make her lawful again for her former husband.

Suppose a man marries her in an unrestricted marriage, involving neither a condition nor a promise, but intends to enjoy marital relations with her for a few days and then divorce her, without intending that she return to her former husband. This is the case under discussion. Even if this makes her lawful again for her former husband, he is not regarded as marrying her for that purpose unless he intends it or a condition to that effect is imposed upon him, explicitly or by custom, whether before or after the marriage contract. If there is neither any intention to make her lawful again for her former husband nor any condition whatsoever, then this is a marriage like other marriages.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is this practice of ‘tahlīl’ (marrying a divorced woman to make her lawful again for her former husband), as people practise it today, valid when carried out through asserting claims, having witnesses attest, and the various other familiar stratagems they employ? If someone follows the opinion of those who permit it, is a distinction to be made between one belief and another? Is it preferable to keep the woman as one’s wife, or not?”

He replied:

Tahlīl in which they agree with the husband—explicitly or by custom—that he will divorce the woman, or in which the husband himself intends this, is forbidden. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed

---

## Page 15754

the one who practises it in several hadiths, calling him “the borrowed billy goat,” and said:

“May Allah curse the man who performs tahlīl and the man for whose benefit it is performed.”

Likewise, well-known reports from ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, Ibn ʿUmar and others explicitly state that anyone who inwardly intends tahlīl is performing it, even if he does not stipulate it in the marriage contract. They called it “fornication.” Such a contract does not make her lawful again for the husband who first divorced her. Nor may the husband who performs tahlīl retain her as his wife on the basis of this arrangement; rather, he must separate from her. However, if the former husband had concluded that this was permissible, whether through independent legal reasoning or by following a scholarly opinion, and she underwent tahlīl and he subsequently married her, then later he came to recognize that this was forbidden, the stronger view is that he is not required to separate from her. Rather, he is to be prevented from doing this in the future, while Allah has pardoned what occurred in the past.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“An upright imam divorced his wife, and she remained with him in his house until she underwent tahlīl as practised by the people of Egypt, after which he married her.”

He replied:

If a man marries a woman intending to divorce her after having intercourse with her, so as to make her lawful again for her former husband, or if the two agree to this before the marriage contract, or stipulate it in the contract itself—explicitly or by custom—then this and its various forms constitute the ‘marriage for tahlīl’ whose invalidity the Muslim community has unanimously affirmed. It is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the man who performs tahlīl and the man for whose benefit it is performed.”

---

## Page 15755

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man divorced his wife three times. After completing her waiting period, she married a second husband, the “man who makes her lawful again.” Is such tahlīl permissible in light of what happened between Rifāʿah and his wife during the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or not?

She then came to her first husband’s house to claim some of what was due to her, and he forced himself upon her. After staying for several days, she became afraid and claimed that she had menstruated so that her first husband would take her back. He therefore remarried her under a lawful marriage contract and lived with her for several days. When her pregnancy became apparent, he learned that she had lied about menstruating. He then abstained from relations with her pending guidance on the ruling of the noble Sharia.

He replied:

If a man marries her in order to make her lawful again for the husband who divorced her, he is the man who performs tahlīl. It is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the man who performs tahlīl and the man for whose benefit it is performed.”

As for the hadith concerning Rifāʿah, the second husband had married her in a lasting marriage; he had not married her to make her lawful again for the husband who divorced her.

If she marries a man who performs tahlīl and he then divorces her, she must observe a waiting period, by scholarly consensus. For even if her situation amounts only to intercourse within an invalid marriage, she must observe a waiting period following it. It was not lawful for the first husband to have intercourse with her. If he does so, he is an adulterer and a fornicator. Her marriage to the first husband before she has menstruated three times is invalid by the consensus of the imams, and he must abstain from relations with her. If she then gives birth

---

## Page 15756

to a child, its paternity is attributed to the man who performed tahlīl, for he had intercourse with her within an invalid marriage. The child is not attributed to the first husband by virtue of their earlier marriage, because its waiting period had ended and she subsequently married another man who had intercourse with her. This ends the legal effect of the former marital bed, without disagreement among the imams. Nor is the child attributed to the first husband on account of his adulterous intercourse, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The child belongs to the marital bed, and the adulterer has no claim.”

However, if the man who performed tahlīl knows that the child is not his but is instead the child of this adulterer, he must disavow paternity through liʿān (the prescribed mutual oaths). He must exchange these oaths with her, thereby severing the child’s paternal lineage. The child’s lineage is traced to its mother, not to the adulterer.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Is the arrangement involving a male slave valid or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. A divorced woman’s marriage to a male slave who has intercourse with her, after which she becomes lawful for her former husband, is one form of tahlīl. It is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the man who performs tahlīl and the man for whose benefit it is performed.”

He was also asked:

A man broke an oath concerning his wife, and she then married another man so that he would make her lawful again for the first husband. Is this marriage valid or not?

---

## Page 15757

He replied:

It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the man who performs tahlīl and the man for whose benefit it is performed.”

It is also reported that he said:

“Shall I tell you about the borrowed billy goat?”

They said, “Yes, O Messenger of Allah.”

He said, “It is the man who performs tahlīl. May Allah curse the man who performs tahlīl and the man for whose benefit it is performed.”

The Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and those who followed them in righteousness agreed that this is forbidden. These included ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, and others. Some of them even said:

“They remain adulterers, even if they stay together for twenty years, if Allah knows that in his heart he intends to make her lawful for her former husband.”

Some of them said:

“No marriage is valid except one entered into out of genuine desire for marriage, not one entered into through deception.”

Some of them said:

“Whoever seeks to deceive Allah, Allah will deceive him.”

Some of them said:

“During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, we used to regard it as illicit intercourse.”

All the imams who issued fatwas agreed that if tahlīl is stipulated in the marriage contract, the contract is invalid. Some of them, however, did not consider a prior stipulation or an established custom to have any legal effect, and treated the contract, despite this, as an ordinary marriage entered into out of genuine desire.

The Companions, their successors, and most imams who issued fatwas, however, made no distinction between this customary understanding and an express stipulation. This is the position of the scholars of Medina, the scholars of hadith, and others. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

If women seek to become lawful again for their former husbands by marrying a young male slave who has not reached puberty, does he count as a husband when he has no understanding of intercourse?

---

## Page 15758

He replied:

It is established in the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he “cursed the recipient of usury, its payer, its two witnesses and its scribe; and Allah cursed the man who performs tahlīl and the man for whose benefit it is performed.” Al-Tirmidhī said: “An authentic hadith.”

The consensus of the Companions on this is also established, including ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, Ibn Mas‘ūd, Ibn ‘Abbās and others. Indeed, ‘Umar said:

“Whenever a man who performs tahlīl and the man for whose benefit it is performed are brought before me, I will stone them both.”

‘Uthmān said:

“No marriage is valid except one entered into out of genuine desire for marriage, not one entered into through deception.”

Ibn ‘Abbās was asked about a man who had pronounced divorce upon his wife a hundred times. He replied:

“She became irrevocably divorced from him with three pronouncements; with the rest, he has made a mockery of Allah’s verses.”

The questioner then said to him:

“What if I marry her without his knowledge, intending to make her lawful for him, and then divorce her?”

Ibn ‘Abbās replied:

“Whoever seeks to deceive Allah, Allah will deceive him.”

When asked about this, he also said:

“They remain adulterers, even if they stay together for twenty years, if Allah knows that in his heart he intends to make her lawful for her former husband.”

We have discussed this issue at length in the book “Setting Forth the Evidence for the Invalidity of Tahlīl.” All this, by my life, concerns a man performing tahlīl who is an adult, has intercourse with her, and tastes her sweetness as she tastes his.

As for a male slave who is incapable of intercourse, or whose intercourse does not count as such—as in the case of someone whose penis does not become erect—the imams are in agreement that this does not make her lawful for her former husband.

“Tahlīl marriage” is among the things for which Christians reproach Muslims. They even say: “The Muslims’ Prophet told them: ‘If one of you divorces his wife, she does not become lawful for him until she commits adultery.’”

Our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is free of this, as are his Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, and the majority of the Muslim imams. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15759

Chapter: Stipulations in Marriage

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant him abundant peace.

Section

Invalid stipulations in marriage are numerous, as in “shighār (exchange marriage),” “tahlīl marriage,” and “temporary marriage.” Other examples include marrying a woman on condition that she receive no dower, or that her dower consist of something unlawful, and similar invalid stipulations. Scholars hold several views concerning these.

“The first” is that the marriage is invalid. Then, is it valid to give effect to the invalid stipulation thereafter? This is disputed.

This is one of the two views within the schools of Mālik and Ahmad. It was the view preferred by a number of leading scholars among Ahmad’s followers, such as Abū Bakr al-Khallāl and Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz.

---

## Page 15760

“The second” is that the marriage is valid but the stipulation is void. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and his followers in all these cases. A number of Ahmad’s followers, including Abū al-Khattāb, Ibn ‘Aqīl and others, inferred this as a view within his school—even in the case of an invalid marriage. Abū Hanīfah and his two companions regard it as invalid, whereas Zufar upholds the contract and disregards the underlying basis. Both scholars derived this as a view within Ahmad’s school.

This inference rests on Ahmad’s explicit ruling concerning the statement: “If you bring me the dower by such-and-such a time; otherwise, there is no marriage between us.” Three positions are reported from him concerning this: one upholding both the contract and the stipulation; another invalidating both; and a third upholding the contract but not the stipulation. Likewise, where a man marries a woman on condition that she return the dower to him, Ahmad explicitly affirmed its validity and the invalidity of the stipulation.

“The third view” concerning invalid stipulations is that exchange marriage, temporary marriage and tahlīl marriage stipulated in the contract are invalid, whereas marriage involving an unlawful dower or a stipulation excluding the dower is valid. This is al-Shāfi‘ī’s position and the second position reported from Ahmad. Many of Ahmad’s followers preferred it, including al-Harbī, Judge Abū Ya‘lā and his followers.

These scholars distinguish between the marriage contracts they uphold despite an invalid stipulation and those they invalidate on the following basis: if the stipulation is removed, a marriage remains; otherwise, it is invalid, as in “temporary marriage.” The same applies to “tahlīl marriage” when its duration is defined by the completion of an act, as when someone says: “I marry her to you until you make her lawful again for her former husband.”

But if he says, “On condition that once you have made her lawful again for her former husband, there will be no marriage between you,” or, “On condition that you divorce her once you have made her lawful again for her former husband,” then there is disagreement within al-Shāfi‘ī’s school. Abū Yūsuf agrees with al-Shāfi‘ī’s view that it is invalid.

---

## Page 15761

As for “exchange marriage,” they offer several explanations for its invalidity: is it because it entails shared claims to sexual access, because one marriage is made conditional upon the other, or because one contract constitutes an advance against the other? These and other explanations appear in their writings elsewhere.

As for “marriage involving an invalid dower” and “a stipulation excluding the dower,” they uphold the marriage, agreeing with Abū Hanīfah. Their reasoning is that, since marriage is valid without specifying a dower, it is also valid when the dower is expressly excluded. These scholars take temporary marriage as the basis for the marriages they invalidate, and marriage without a specified dower as the basis for those they uphold, while treating exchange marriage as a separate category.

This is the foundation of Abū Hanīfah’s position concerning invalid stipulations in marriage and his distinction between them and invalid stipulations in sales and leases. He held that a sale or lease is not valid without specifying the consideration; hence it is not valid when the consideration is unknown, nor when it contains invalid stipulations. Such stipulations entail uncertainty about the consideration, since the invalid stipulation must be cancelled along with the portion of the price corresponding to it, leaving the remainder of the price unknown.

The majority have argued against these scholars on the basis of the established reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibiting exchange marriage and tahlīl marriage, just as he prohibited temporary marriage. A prohibition of a marriage entails its invalidity, as with his prohibition of marriage during the waiting period and marriage without a guardian or witnesses.

They also cite the fact that the Companions invalidated these contracts: they separated spouses in exchange marriages, regarded tahlīl marriage as fornication, threatened the man undertaking it with stoning, and prohibited marriages not entered into out of a genuine desire for married life. We have cited numerous reports from them to this effect in “The Book on the Invalidation of Tahlīl Marriage.” Thus, the invalidity of these marriages is established by the textual evidence and the consensus of the Companions.

---

## Page 15762

Moreover, if a marriage is held to be valid and binding, this must mean either that it remains so together with the unlawful, invalid stipulation—which contradicts the textual evidence and consensus—or that it remains so while the stipulation is annulled. The latter would bind the contracting party to a contract to which he did not consent and which Allah did not impose upon him.

It is well known that the obligations arising from a contract become binding either because the Lawgiver imposes them or because the contracting party undertakes them. The first category includes contracts that the Lawgiver requires a person to enter into, just as He requires a non-Muslim in a state of war to embrace Islam, and requires someone who has broken a binding oath to make one expiation through manumission and fasting; and just as He requires someone who needs something else … (1) to buy and sell in various circumstances. The “second” is reciprocal exchange … (2), just as a guarantor becomes liable for the debtor’s debt while the debtor remains liable for it, and just as each party to a sale, settlement or lease becomes bound by what he undertakes towards the other.

That being so, when a marriage contains an invalid stipulation, the Lawgiver has not required the person concerned to enter into it without that stipulation, nor has he himself undertaken to enter into it without the stipulation. Binding him to something that he neither undertook himself nor was required to undertake by the Lawgiver amounts to imposing upon people obligations that neither Allah nor His Messenger imposed upon them. This is impermissible.

Furthermore, stipulations in marriage are more binding than those in sales, as demonstrated by his statement in the authentic hadith:

“The stipulations most deserving of fulfilment are those by which you have made marital intimacy lawful.”

Now, a sale is permissible only by mutual consent, for Allah Most High says:

“Unless it is trade by mutual consent among you.”

Thus, marriage has an even stronger claim to require mutual consent. The contracting party consented to the invalid contract only on those particular terms.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) A lacuna in the original.

---

## Page 15763

so binding him to it without those terms means binding him to a contract to which he did not consent. This runs contrary to the textual evidence and established principles. For this reason, it is impermissible to bind someone, in a sale, to something to which he did not consent.

Accordingly, Ahmad’s followers, such as Judge Abū Ya‘lā and others, said: If we uphold the sale while annulling the invalid stipulation, according to one of the two positions reported from him, the party who made that stipulation—if he did not know that it was prohibited—may rescind the contract or demand compensation for its nonfulfilment. They expressed a similar view concerning a valid stipulation that is not fulfilled. However, a valid stipulation must be fulfilled, just as a valid contract must be honoured. If it is not fulfilled, the stipulating party may rescind the contract in all cases, because he did not consent to it without that stipulation.

An invalid stipulation, by contrast, need not be fulfilled, just as an invalid contract need not be honoured. Nevertheless, the stipulating party may uphold the contract without it, or he may rescind the contract, just as when he stipulates a particular quality in the goods sold and they lack that quality, or when a defect is discovered in them.

Thus, concerning a sale containing an invalid stipulation, Ahmad—may Allah be pleased with him—holds that the sale is valid according to one of the two positions reported from him; indeed, this is the more explicit of the two reports. This is because the failure to meet a stipulation or a specified quality does not invalidate the sale, and the harm suffered by the stipulating party is remedied by releasing him from rescission, as when stipulated qualities are absent because of defects.

In marriage, however, stipulations are more binding. If someone stipulates a quality in either spouse, as with the stipulation most deserving of fulfilment, he has the right to rescind the marriage if that quality is absent, according to one of the two positions reported from Ahmad. This is also one of the two views associated with Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī. Likewise, in Ahmad’s view, he may rescind the marriage because of defects that prevent the purpose of marriage from being fulfilled. … (1)

And he has the right to rescind. As for a marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband, it is not intended for its own sake; and, in Ahmad’s view, the purpose behind contracts is taken into account. Temporary marriage is marriage for a specified term, whereas marriage cannot be made subject to a fixed term.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A lacuna in the original.

---

## Page 15764

And as for “shighār” (exchange marriage), he and many of his followers, such as al-Khallāl and Abū Bakr ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, explained its prohibition by the absence of a dower and by making the right to marital relations with one woman the dower for the other. This is also the explanation given by Mālik’s followers. Many of his followers, however, offered the same explanation as al-Shāfiʿī’s followers.

An objection remains: when an invalid stipulation is included, the contracting party should therefore be given the choice between accepting the contract without it and rescinding the contract, just as with invalid stipulations in a sale.

The answer is: if we say that marriage can be contracted only through the formulas of giving in marriage or marrying, since these are its explicit expressions, and that it cannot be contracted through indirect expressions—as Abū Hāmid, Judge Abū Yaʿlā and their followers among Ahmad’s adherents maintain, in agreement with al-Shāfiʿī’s followers—and we also say that a sale, unlike marriage, admits a stipulated option to rescind, then the distinction becomes clear. A sale can be concluded on a nonbinding basis, whereas marriage cannot.

Those who deem marriages arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband and exchange marriages, or similar arrangements, valid may say: “We do not deem valid what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited.
