We do not uphold such a marriage while it remains an exchange marriage, a marriage arranged to permit remarriage to a former husband, or a temporary marriage. Rather, we invalidate the stipulation making the marriage contract itself the dower, and we invalidate the stipulation concerning remarriage to the former husband. The same applies to the stipulation fixing a term, according to those who hold this view. The contract then remains binding, without being an exchange marriage or a marriage arranged to permit remarriage to a former husband.”

For this reason, according to one of their two views, Abū Hanīfa’s followers held that a marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband is valid, but does not make her lawful for the man who divorced her three times. This gives effect to the Prophet’s words:

“Allah has cursed the man who marries a woman to make her lawful for her former husband, and the man for whom this is done.”

They uphold the marriage only while invalidating the stipulation concerning remarriage to the former husband. It therefore becomes a binding marriage, and they do not deem her lawful

---

## Page 15765

for her former husband; because if she becomes lawful for him, the marriage will have been intended to make her lawful for him. If, however, the marriage does not make her lawful for him, it will not be intended for that purpose, and thus will not be a marriage arranged to permit remarriage to her former husband. According to this view, a man would marry the woman only out of a genuine desire for marriage, not to make her lawful for her former husband.

If he marries her with that intention, or stipulates that purpose, but both subsequently desire a genuine marriage and abandon the stipulation concerning remarriage to her former husband, must they conclude a new contract, or is retaining the original contract sufficient? There is disagreement on this. It resembles the question of abandoning an invalid stipulation in a sale: does the sale thereby become valid or not? Here, too, intention is involved.

A similar question arises when someone concludes a contract without the permission of a person whose permission is required: is the contract void, or is its validity suspended pending approval? There are two well-known views, both held within Ahmad’s school. The first is that the contract is void and is not held in suspense, as al-Shāfiʿī maintains. The second is that it remains suspended pending approval, as Abū Hanīfa and Mālik maintain.

Thus, if someone concludes a contract with an invalid intention or an invalid stipulation, one might say that the same two views concerning suspension apply. Whoever accepts suspension would make its validity contingent upon removing the invalidating factor; whoever does not would not. Removing an impediment is equivalent to establishing the requisite cause. Making a contract contingent upon the fulfilment of some of its conditions is comparable to making it contingent upon the removal of some of its impediments. . . . (1)

For by deeming him a husband who divorces her, while holding that the marriage binds her, you have bound her to a marriage to which she did not consent. This contradicts the established principles and the scriptural texts. The soundest view in this matter is that the decision rests with her. If she accepts him without that stipulation, he is her husband, and no new contract is needed. If she does not accept him, he is not her husband, just as with a marriage whose validity is suspended pending her approval. The same applies to a marriage

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A lacuna in the original.

---

## Page 15766

for a dower that she cannot receive because it is unlawful or rightfully belongs to someone else. If she wishes to accept him as her husband with a different dower, she may do so; if she wishes to leave him, she has that right. Until she consents, the marriage is not binding.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman on the stipulation that he would neither marry another woman alongside her nor move her from her home. She also had a daughter, and it was stipulated that the daughter would remain with her mother and him, without being removed. He entered the marriage on all these terms. Must he fulfil them? If he breaches any of these stipulations, does the wife have the right to annul the marriage?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, these stipulations and others of the same kind are valid according to the school of Imam Ahmad and others among the Companions, the Successors, and their followers, such as ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb and ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀs—may Allah be pleased with them both—as well as Shurayh the judge, al-Awzāʿī, and Ishāq. Hence, old marriage deeds from the Maghrib, dating from when its people followed the school of al-Awzāʿī, can still be found today containing these stipulations.

According to Mālik’s school, if it is stipulated that, should he marry another woman or take a concubine, the decision concerning her marriage will rest with her, or something similar, this stipulation is also valid, and she thereby acquires the right to end the marriage. In substance, this is similar to Ahmad’s position on the matter, on account of the hadith recorded in both Sahīh collections, in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The stipulations most deserving of fulfilment are those by which marital intimacy has become lawful for you.”

ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb said: “Rights are determined by the stipulations.”

---

## Page 15767

Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, declared the stipulations by which marital intimacy becomes lawful more deserving of fulfilment than other stipulations. This explicitly addresses stipulations of this kind, since no stipulation is unanimously agreed to require fulfilment other than the dower and the spoken terms. These stipulations must therefore be what is intended.

As for stipulating that her child remain with her and that he provide for the child, this is comparable to increasing the dower. According to Ahmad’s explicit position—which is also the position of Abū Hanīfa and Mālik—a degree of uncertainty is permissible in the dower that would not be permissible in a sale price or a wage. Any uncertainty less than that involved in determining the customary dower for a woman of comparable standing is all the more deserving of being permitted.

Moreover, an arrangement of this kind is permissible in hiring and similar contracts according to Ahmad’s school and others: a worker may be hired in return for his food and clothing, with customary practice determining what these entail. All the more, then, should customary practice determine the maintenance stipulated for her child.

If he fails to fulfil these stipulations, marrying another woman and taking a concubine, she has the right to annul the marriage. There is, however, disagreement over whether this requires a judge’s ruling, since it is a right of annulment subject to juristic disagreement, like the right arising from impotence or defects.

Alternatively, it may be argued that establishing this right requires no exercise of juristic judgment, even if annulment on its basis is disputed. It is comparable to the right of choice afforded to an emancipated woman: those who recognize that right hold that it applies without a judge’s ruling even in disputed cases, such as exercising annulment after a delay.

The underlying question is whether requiring a judicial ruling for annulment is also due to the need for juristic judgment in establishing the ruling, or whether it is because particular caution is required in matters of marital separation. The stronger view is that an annulment whose validity is disputed, such as annulment on grounds of impotence, does not require a judge’s ruling. However, if it is brought before a judge who considers it valid, he upholds it; if he considers it invalid, he sets it aside. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15768

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man stipulates that he will not marry another woman alongside his wife, take a concubine, or move her from her home or town. If she stipulates these conditions before the marriage contract and they agree to them, but the contract makes no mention of them, are they valid and binding, requiring fulfilment just like conditions stipulated when the contract is concluded, or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, they are valid and binding if the two parties have not revoked them, even if they accompanied the conclusion of the contract. This is the apparent position of Imam Abū Hanīfa, Imam Mālik, and others regarding all contracts. It is also one position within al-Shāfiʿī’s school, inferred from the issue of “the privately agreed dower and the publicly announced dower.” Mālik and Ahmad likewise apply this principle to acts of worship: according to them, an intention formed beforehand is equivalent to one formed when the act begins.

There is a second position within Ahmad’s school: stipulations made before the contract have no effect. There is also a third position, which distinguishes between a stipulation that makes the transaction itself unintended—such as an agreement that a sale is merely a sham with no reality—and a stipulation that does not prevent the transaction from being genuinely intended, such as stipulating an option to rescind or something similar.

However, the general tenor of the explicit rulings of Ahmad, the early scholars of his school, and its meticulous later scholars is that, when stipulations and prior agreements between the contracting parties have not been revoked by the time they conclude the contract, the contract takes effect

---

## Page 15769

subject to them. Ahmad’s rulings on legal stratagems involving sales, leases, pledges, loans, and other transactions are based on this principle. Examples abound in his statements and those of the scholars of his school; this fatwa cannot accommodate a detailed enumeration of the individual cases. Many are well known to anyone with even a little familiarity with Ahmad’s principles and explicit rulings, and would not escape their notice. We have established the evidence for this from the Quran, the Sunnah, the consensus of the early generations, and the principles of Islamic law in “The Question of Tahlīl.”

Anyone who examines the agreements concluded between the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and others, such as the pledge of allegiance between him and the Ansār on the night of al-ʿAqaba, the truce between him and Quraysh in the year of al-Hudaybiyya, and other agreements, will recognize that they first agreed upon the conditions and then concluded the agreement in unqualified terms. The same applies to the general texts of the Quran and Sunnah commanding the fulfilment of contracts, covenants, and stipulations, and forbidding treachery. All three terms encompass such arrangements equally: linguistic and customary usage agree on these designations, and their meanings in Islamic law accord with this.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a girl aged ten. Her family stipulated that he must live with them, not take her away from them, and not consummate the marriage until a year had passed. Yet he took her to his own home, broke that agreement, and consummated the marriage. The midwives stated that he moved her and then

---

## Page 15770

settled with her in a place where he subjected her to severe beatings. He then took her away on a journey; they subsequently returned with her, and he prevented her family from visiting her while continuing to beat her. Is it permissible for her to remain with him under these circumstances?

He replied:

If the situation is as described, it is not permissible to allow her to remain with him under these circumstances. Rather, if he cannot live with her in kindness, they must be separated. He has no right to have intercourse with her in a way that harms her. Indeed, if he does not refrain from abusing her, they must be separated. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man stipulated before witnesses that he would not house his wife in his father’s home. She lived separately for a time, but he is unable to maintain this arrangement. Is he obliged to do so? May she annul the marriage if he wishes to set aside the condition? Must he allow her mother or sister to visit her and stay overnight with her, or not?

He replied:

He is not obliged to do what he is unable to do, particularly if she stipulated her consent to that. Indeed, even if he could provide another dwelling, according to many scholars—including Mālik, one of the two positions in Ahmad’s school, and others—she would be entitled only to what he stipulated for her. How much more so, then, if he is unable? According to these scholars, she has no right to annul the marriage even if he is able. If, however, that dwelling is suitable accommodation for a poor person and he cannot afford any other, she has no right to annul the marriage, without disagreement among the jurists.

He is not obliged to allow either her mother or her sister to enter his home, provided that he treats her kindly. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15771

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married, and it was stipulated in the marriage contract that every woman he subsequently married would be divorced, and every slave woman he took as a concubine would become free. He then married another woman and took a concubine. What is the ruling according to the four schools of law?

He replied:

This condition is not binding according to the school of Imam al-Shāfiʿī. According to the school of Abū Hanīfa, it is binding upon him: whenever he marries another woman, she is divorced, and whenever he takes a slave woman as a concubine, she becomes free. This is also the position of Mālik’s school.

According to Ahmad’s school, neither divorce nor manumission takes effect through this condition. However, if he marries another woman and takes a concubine, the decision rests with his wife: if she wishes, she may remain with him, and if she wishes, she may leave him. This is based on the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The stipulations most deserving of fulfilment are those by which marital intimacy has become lawful for you.”

It is also based on the report that a man married a woman on condition that he would not marry another woman alongside her. The matter was brought before ʿUmar, who said: “Rights are determined by the stipulations.”

Thus, there are three positions on this issue:

“The first”: divorce and manumission take effect through this condition.

“The second”: neither takes effect through it, and his wife has no right to leave him.

“The third”—which is the most just position—is that neither divorce nor manumission takes effect through it, but his wife is entitled to what he stipulated for her: if she wishes, she may remain with him, and if she wishes, she may leave him. This is the most balanced of the positions.

---

## Page 15772

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man swore an oath invoking divorce that he would not marry a certain woman, but then decided to marry her. May he do so? Another man married a woman and stipulated in the marriage contract that he would not marry another woman alongside her, but then did so. Does she have the right to choose whether to remain with him?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. He may marry her, and no divorce takes effect if he does so, according to the majority of the early scholars. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others.

If he stipulated in the marriage contract that he would not marry another woman alongside her, and that if he did, the decision would rest with her, then this condition is valid and binding according to the schools of Mālik, Ahmad and others. Whenever he marries another woman alongside her, the decision rests with her: if she wishes, she may remain with him, and if she wishes, she may leave him. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15773

Chapter: Defects Affecting Marriage

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman married a man, and after the marriage was consummated, she noticed vitiligo on his body. Does she have the right to annul the marriage?

He replied:

If either spouse is found to have insanity, leprosy or vitiligo, the other has the right to annul the marriage. However, anyone who accepts the defect after it becomes apparent no longer has the right to annul the marriage. If the wife annuls the marriage, she may not take anything from her trousseau. If she annuls it before consummation, her entitlement to the bridal dower lapses; if she annuls it after consummation, it does not lapse.

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A married man was found to have leprosy. Does his wife have the right to annul the marriage?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah. If the husband is found to have leprosy, the wife has the right to annul the marriage without his consent. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15774

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a virgin and discovered that she suffered from continuous non-menstrual bleeding, which had persisted since she was in her mother’s home, and that her family had deceived him. Does he have the right to annul the marriage and recover the bridal dower from whoever deceived him? Must her mother and father swear an oath if they deny this? And may he have intercourse with her?

He replied:

This is a defect that establishes the right to annul the marriage, according to the stronger of the two opinions within the school of Ahmad and among others, for two reasons:

First, intercourse in this condition necessarily entails harm that he fears and discomfort that he experiences.

Second, according to the well-known position reported from Ahmad, intercourse with a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding is impermissible except out of necessity.

Whatever prevents intercourse physically, such as an obstruction of the vaginal passage, or through natural aversion, such as insanity or leprosy, establishes the right to annulment according to Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, as has also been reported from ʿUmar.

As for conditions that prevent intercourse from being fully enjoyed, such as impurity in the vagina, there is a well-known disagreement concerning them. The condition of a woman suffering from non-menstrual bleeding is more severe than other such conditions.

If the marriage is annulled before consummation, he owes no bridal dower. If it is annulled afterwards, one opinion holds that seclusion of this kind makes the bridal dower fully due. If he has had intercourse with her, he may recover the dower from whoever deceived him.

Another opinion holds that the dower does not become fully due, so he owes nothing. He may require anyone whom he accuses of deceiving him to swear that they did not deceive him.

There is a well-known disagreement concerning intercourse with a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding. One view permits intercourse with her, as held by

---

## Page 15775

al-Shāfiʿī and others.

Another view holds that it is impermissible except out of necessity. This is the well-known position reported from Ahmad. The husband retains the option to annul the marriage so long as he has not indicated acceptance by word or deed. If he then has intercourse with her, he no longer has that option, unless he claims ignorance. Whether he retains the option in that case is a matter of well-known disagreement; the stronger view is that he does have the right to annulment. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman on the understanding that she was a virgin, but she turned out not to be. Is he entitled to annul the marriage and seek reimbursement from whoever deceived him?

He replied:

He is entitled to annul the marriage and may claim a proportionate reduction in the bridal dower, based on the difference between the customary dower of a virgin and that of a non-virgin. The stipulated dower is reduced accordingly. If he annuls the marriage before consummation, no dower is due. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15776

Chapter: Marriage among Disbelievers

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the meaning of the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: “I was born of marriage, not fornication”?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. This hadith is well known among the reports that ‘Alī ibn al-Husayn—may Allah be pleased with them both—and others attributed directly to the Prophet without naming a Companion. Its wording is:

“I was born of marriage, not fornication. None of the marriage practices of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance touched my lineage.”

Their marriage practices during the pre-Islamic age of ignorance took various forms.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Were marriages contracted before the messengers were sent valid or not?

He replied:

(*)
Their marriage practices during the pre-Islamic age of ignorance took various forms, including the form of marriage practised today. According to the majority of scholars, that form of marriage was valid during the pre-Islamic age of ignorance. The same applies to all other marriages among polytheists that are not prohibited in Islam. The legal consequences of a valid marriage apply to them, including inheritance, oaths to abstain from marital intercourse, mutual imprecation, declarations likening one’s wife to a permanently forbidden female relative, and other matters.

It is reported that Mālik said: “Marriage among polytheists is not valid.” What he means by this is that if a divorce were pronounced

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 233):
> The second fatwa is, in fact, a continuation of the first; they constitute a single, continuous fatwa. It appears to me—and Allah knows best—that the second question was inserted by the copyist, for three reasons:
> First: the context clearly indicates this, since the first fatwa does not answer the question.
> Second: the beginning of the answer to the second question continues the Shaykh’s preceding words.
> Third: in the second fatwa, the Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—states: “People have cited this hadith as evidence that marriage in the pre-Islamic period is valid.” Yet the second fatwa contains no hadith to which this statement could refer; rather, the hadith is mentioned in the preceding question.
> Accordingly, the text should be corrected by deleting the question and joining its answer to the first answer. Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 15777

by a disbeliever three times, no divorce would take effect. Likewise, if a Muslim divorced his dhimmī wife—a non-Muslim woman under Muslim protection—three times, and a dhimmī man then married her and consummated the marriage, this would not, in Mālik’s view, make her lawful again for her former husband. If a dhimmī man had intercourse with a dhimmī woman within marriage, he would not thereby qualify as having consummated a valid marriage for the purposes of the prescribed punishment for adultery. Most scholars disagree with him on these matters.

As for such a marriage being valid for establishing lineage and the marital presumption of paternity, there is no disagreement among Muslims. It is not equivalent to intercourse under a semblance of lawfulness. Indeed, if both disbelieving spouses embrace Islam, their marriage is upheld by consensus, whereas they would not be permitted to continue intercourse under a mere semblance of lawfulness. Scholars have cited this hadith as evidence that marriages contracted during the pre-Islamic age of ignorance were valid. They have also cited His words:

“And his wife, the carrier of firewood,”

and His words:

“the wife of Pharaoh.”

They said: Allah called her a “wife,” and words used without qualification are presumed to carry their literal meaning. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—also said:

In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, it is stated:

‘Atā’ reported from Ibn ‘Abbās: “The polytheists stood in two different relationships to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the believers. Some were hostile polytheists: he fought them and they fought him. Others were polytheists bound by a treaty: he did not fight them, nor did they fight him. When a woman from among the hostile polytheists emigrated, she was not to receive a marriage proposal until she had menstruated and become pure. Once she became pure, she could lawfully marry. If her husband emigrated before she married, she was restored to him. If a male or female slave from among them emigrated, they became free and enjoyed the same rights as the emigrants.”

He then mentioned concerning those bound by a treaty something similar to Mujāhid’s report: “If a male or female slave belonging to the polytheists bound by a treaty emigrated, they were not returned, but their owners were reimbursed their value.”

‘Atā’ also reported from Ibn ‘Abbās: “Qurayba bint Abī Umayya was married to ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb. He divorced her, and Mu‘āwiya ibn Abī Sufyān married her. Umm al-Hakam, the daughter of Abū Sufyān, was married to ‘Iyād ibn Ghanm al-Fihrī. He divorced her, and ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Uthmān married her.”

---

## Page 15778

Then, in a later chapter, he cited the following:

Ibn Jurayj said: “I asked ‘Atā’: ‘If a woman from among the polytheists comes to the Muslims, is her husband to be compensated for losing her, in accordance with the words of Allah Most High, “And give them what they have spent”?’

“He replied: ‘No. That applied only between the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and those bound by a treaty.’”

Mujāhid said: “All of this concerned a peace treaty between the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and Quraysh.”

I say: The report of Ibn ‘Abbās contains several points.

First: A woman who emigrates from among those at war with the Muslims is not required to observe the prescribed waiting period; she need only establish that her womb is clear by observing a single menstrual period. This is one of the two scholarly positions on this question, because the prescribed waiting period involves a right belonging to the husband, as Allah Most High says:

“Then you have no waiting period to count for them.”

For this reason, we have held that prescribed waiting periods cannot run concurrently. By embracing Islam and emigrating, this woman has gained freedom over her own person, just as a slave gains freedom over his own person by embracing Islam and emigrating. Her husband therefore has no right over her. Nevertheless, she must establish that her womb is clear, just as an emancipated slave woman must. This may lend support to the view that a single menstrual period suffices for a woman who obtains release from marriage through khul‘, since each woman has freed herself from the marital bond.

Second: If her husband emigrates before she remarries, she is restored to him, even if she has already menstruated. Yet, after this, al-Bukhārī narrates from Khālid, from ‘Ikrima, from Ibn ‘Abbās: “If a Christian woman embraces Islam even an hour before her husband, she becomes unlawful to him.”

What Ibn ‘Abbās mentioned concerning the emigrant woman accords with the well-known report that “Zaynab, the daughter of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was restored to Abū al-‘Ās ibn al-Rabī‘ under the original marriage contract.” In my writings on jurisprudence, I have recorded reports and explicit statements on this matter from Imam Ahmad and others.

---

## Page 15779

Third:

His statement that any of their slaves who emigrates becomes free and enjoys the same rights as the other emigrants, as in the account of Abū Bakra and the slaves belonging to the people of al-Tā’if who emigrated with him. There is no doubt about this: by embracing Islam and emigrating, he gains ownership of his own person. The property of those at war with the Muslims is open to lawful appropriation, so whoever gains possession of something becomes its owner. If he gains control over his own person, he is all the more entitled to own it, and Islam safeguards that ownership.

Fourth:

When a slave belonging to people bound by a treaty emigrates, his owners are compensated for his value, but the slave himself is not returned, because their property is protected. This is comparable to the case of a slave belonging to a non-Muslim under Muslim protection who embraces Islam: the owner is ordered to relinquish ownership of him by selling him, giving him away, or setting him free. If he complies, well and good; otherwise, the slave is sold on his behalf. The slave himself is not returned to them, because they would hold a Muslim in slavery, which is impermissible.

This differs from returning a free man to them, since they do not enslave him. Thus, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stipulated that women should be returned along with men, Allah annulled that stipulation and commanded him not to return Muslim women, saying:

“Neither are these women lawful for those men, nor are those men lawful for them.”

For among the unbelievers, liberties are permitted with a Muslim woman that are not permitted with a man, since a captive woman is like a captive man. Allah commanded him to return the dowry as compensation.

---

## Page 15780

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked: (*)

Concerning the words of Allah Most High, “Do not marry polytheistic women”: scholars have permitted marriage to Christian and Jewish women. Are these women included among the polytheists or not?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah.

Marriage to a woman from the People of the Book is permissible on the basis of the verse in Sūrat al-Mā’idah. Allah Most High says:

“The food of those who were given the Scripture is lawful for you, and your food is lawful for them, as are chaste believing women and chaste women from among those who were given the Scripture before you.”

This is the position of the overwhelming majority of earlier and later scholars, including the four imams and others.

It is reported that Ibn ‘Umar disapproved of marrying a Christian woman, saying: “I know of no greater association of partners with Allah than that of a woman who says that her Lord is Jesus son of Mary.”

Today, this is the position of a group among the proponents of religious innovation. They have cited as evidence the verse in Sūrat al-Baqarah and His words:

“Do not hold on to marriage bonds with unbelieving women.”

The verse in al-Baqarah may be answered in three ways:

First: the People of the Book are not included among the polytheists. They are treated as distinct from the polytheists, as evidenced by His words:

“Indeed, those who believe, the Jews, the Sabians, the Christians, the Magians, and those who associate partners with Allah.”

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd said (pp. 233–234):
> There are two points here:
> First: This fatwa previously appeared at 14/91–93, but it is incomplete there. This was already noted in the discussion of volume fourteen.
> Second: A textual error occurs here, in the passage at 32/179: “Where He describes them as having committed shirk (polytheism), it is because of the shirk they introduced, which Allah did not command; they must be distinguished from the polytheists because the foundation of their religion is adherence to the revealed scriptures, which taught tawhīd (monotheism), not shirk.”
> The correct wording, as found at 14/92, is: “Where He describes them as having committed shirk, it is because of the shirk they introduced, which Allah did not command; and where He distinguishes them from the polytheists, it is because the foundation of their religion is adherence to the revealed scriptures, which taught tawhīd, not shirk.” Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 15781

If it is objected that He has described them as associating partners with Him in His words:

“They have taken their rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah, and also the Messiah, son of Mary, though they were commanded only to worship one God. There is no deity except Him. Exalted is He above whatever they associate with Him.”

The reply is that the religion of the People of the Book contains no polytheism in its original form. Allah sent the messengers only with monotheism; thus, those who believed in the messengers and the Scriptures had no polytheism in the original foundation of their religion. The Christians, however, introduced polytheism, as He says:

“Glorified and exalted is He above whatever they associate with Him.”

Wherever He describes them as having associated partners with Him, it is because of the polytheism they introduced, which Allah did not command. They must nevertheless be distinguished from the polytheists, because the original foundation of their religion is adherence to the revealed Scriptures, which taught monotheism, not polytheism. Thus, when they are called the People of the Book, they are not polytheists in that respect, since the Scripture to which they are affiliated contains no polytheism.

Likewise, when we speak of “the Muslims” and “the community of Muhammad,” there is, in that respect, no belief in the union of Creator and creation, no Rāfidism, no denial of divine decree, nor any other such religious innovation among them—even though some who belong to the community have introduced these innovations. However, the community of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—does not unite upon misguidance. There will therefore always remain within it those who follow the law of monotheism, unlike the People of the Book.

Allah, Mighty and Majestic, did not describe the People of the Book as “polytheists,” using the noun. Rather, He said:

“Whatever they associate with Him,”

using the verb. In the verse in al-Baqarah, however, He used the nouns “polytheistic men” and “polytheistic women.” The noun is more emphatic than the verb.

---

## Page 15782

The second approach is to say: If the term “polytheists” in Sūrat al-Baqarah includes them, just as Allah has described them as associating partners with Him, this can be understood by distinguishing between the term’s meaning when used alone and its meaning when used alongside another term. When “polytheists” is used alone, it includes the People of the Book; when mentioned alongside the People of the Book, it does not include them. A similar distinction has been made concerning the terms “the poor” and “the needy,” and other such expressions.

On this basis, the verse in al-Baqarah is general, whereas the other verse is specific, and the specific takes precedence over the general.

The third approach is to say: The verse in al-Mā’idah abrogates the verse in al-Baqarah, because al-Mā’idah was revealed after al-Baqarah, by scholarly consensus. It is related in the hadith:

“Al-Mā’idah was among the last portions of the Quran to be revealed, so regard as lawful what it declares lawful, and as forbidden what it declares forbidden.”

A later verse abrogates an earlier verse when the two conflict.

As for His words:

“Do not hold on to marriage bonds with unbelieving women,”

this verse was revealed after the Treaty of al-Hudaybiyah, when people emigrated from Mecca to Medina, and Allah revealed Sūrat al-Mumtahanah and commanded that the emigrants be examined. It addresses anyone who had an unbelieving wife. The lām of the definite article indicates a known referent, and the unbelieving women referred to were polytheistic women. Moreover, “disbelievers” may also be distinguished from the People of the Book in certain passages, as in His words:

“Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scripture? They believe in superstition and false deities, and say of those who disbelieve, ‘These are better guided than the believers.’”

For the original foundation of their religion is faith; yet they

---

## Page 15783

became disbelievers, introducing disbelief as an innovation, as Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve in Allah and His messengers and wish to make a distinction between Allah and His messengers, saying, ‘We believe in some and disbelieve in others,’ and wish to take a course in between—

“Those are truly the disbelievers, and We have prepared for the disbelievers a humiliating punishment.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about slave women from the People of the Book:

What evidence is there in the Quran, the Sunnah, scholarly consensus, and legal reasoning for the permissibility of intercourse with them by right of ownership, and for the prohibition of intercourse with Zoroastrian slave women? Please give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. The permissibility of intercourse with “slave women from the People of the Book” by right of ownership is more firmly established than its permissibility through marriage, according to the general body of scholars, including the four imams and others. None of the early generations is reported to have forbidden this, whereas some are reported to have prohibited marriage to women from the People of the Book. Nevertheless, Ibn al-Mundhir said: “It has not been authentically established that any of the early scholars forbade marriage to them.” Rather, prohibition is the position of the Shia.

There is, however, disagreement over whether marrying them is disliked when there is no need to do so. Such disapproval is well known in the schools of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad. Likewise, there is disagreement over whether intercourse with such slave women is disliked. Al-Hasan is reported to have disliked it. Disapproval in this matter is based

---

## Page 15784

on disapproval of marriage. As for declaring it forbidden, no one is known to have held that position.

Indeed, scholars have disagreed over the permissibility of marrying a slave woman from the People of the Book. Abū Hanīfa and his followers permitted it, while Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, al-Layth, and al-Awzā‘ī prohibited it. Two positions are reported from Ahmad, the better-known of which agrees with the latter view. For Allah, glorified is He, permitted marriage only to free women in the verse in which He, Most High, says:

“And free women from among the believers and free women from among those who were given the Scripture before you.”

He thus permitted the free women among them. In the verse concerning slave women, He says:

“And whoever among you cannot afford to marry free believing women may marry believing slave women whom your right hands possess. Allah knows best your faith; you are of one another.”

Thus, He permitted only believing women. This is not the place to discuss this issue at length.

As for the Zoroastrian slave woman, the discussion concerning her rests on two principles. The first is that marriage to Zoroastrian women is impermissible, just as marriage to idolatrous women is impermissible. This is the position of the four imams. Imam Ahmad reported this position from five of the Companions concerning the animals slaughtered by Zoroastrians and their women, and regarded dissent on this matter as akin to the dissent of the people of innovation.

The second principle is that women whom it is impermissible to marry, such as idolatrous women, are also impermissible to have intercourse with by right of ownership. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others. It is related from Abū Thawr that he said: Intercourse with slave women by right of ownership is permissible, whatever their religion.

---

## Page 15785

and I believe this is reported from some of the early scholars. Thus, it is clear that there is disagreement over intercourse with an idolatrous slave woman. As for a slave woman from the People of the Book, the permissibility of intercourse with her is not disputed when marriage to such women is permitted; rather, there is well-known disagreement over marrying her. All this demonstrates that no one has held that marrying such women is permissible while taking them as concubines is prohibited, nor would any jurist hold such a view.

We therefore say:

There are several grounds establishing that taking them as concubines is not forbidden.

First: The basic presumption is permissibility, and no evidence prohibiting them has been established from a scriptural text, scholarly consensus, or analogy. The permissibility of intercourse with them therefore remains the default ruling. This is because the evidence cited by those who dispute the permissibility of marrying them, such as His words:

“Do not marry polytheistic women,”

and His words:

“Do not hold on to marriage bonds with disbelieving women,”

applies only to marriage, not to intercourse by right of ownership. It is known that neither the Sunnah nor analogy provides grounds for prohibiting them, so permissibility remains the default ruling.

Second: His words, Most High:

“And those who guard their private parts, except with their wives or those whom their right hands possess, for then they are free from blame,”

establish the general permissibility of intercourse by right of ownership, without restriction except where evidence establishes an exception. Indeed, ‘Uthmān and other Companions regarded a text such as this as encompassing having two sisters as concubines at the same time, saying: “One verse made them lawful, and another verse made them unlawful.” If they understood its general scope to include a case in which marriage is forbidden, then it is all the more fitting and appropriate that its general scope should include a case in which marriage is not forbidden.

---

## Page 15786

Third: The scholars have unanimously agreed that this is permissible, as we have mentioned. No Muslim has said that marrying these women is permissible while taking them as concubines is forbidden. Rather, it has been said that intercourse by right of ownership is forbidden wherever intercourse within marriage is forbidden; and it has been said that marrying them is permissible. Thus, there is consensus on the permissibility of taking such a slave woman as a concubine. Its permissibility is neither stronger than that of marriage nor weaker. To prohibit taking them as concubines while permitting marriage to them would therefore contradict scholarly consensus.

Fourth: The permissibility of marrying them implies, with still greater force, the permissibility of taking them as concubines. There is no disagreement that any woman with whom intercourse is permissible through marriage may also be lawfully approached by right of ownership. The converse, however, is disputed. This is because ownership allows greater latitude: it imposes no numerical limit, whereas marriage is limited to a specified number. Where having certain women together as wives is forbidden, there has been disagreement over whether having them together as concubines is likewise forbidden. A man may also enjoy intimacy by right of ownership without having to allot turns, obtain permission to practise withdrawal, or observe similar restrictions imposed on him to safeguard a wife’s rights.

The authority established by marriage is a form of bondage, whereas ownership entails complete bondage. Allah has permitted Muslim men to marry women from the People of the Book, but men from the People of the Book may not marry Muslim women. This is because marriage is a form of bondage, as ‘Umar said:

“Marriage is bondage, so let each of you consider to whom he entrusts his cherished daughter in bondage.”

Zayd ibn Thābit said:

“The husband is a master in the Book of Allah.” He then recited His words, Most High:

“And they both found her master at the door.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Fear Allah regarding women, for they are captives in your care.”

Thus, permission was granted

---

## Page 15787

for a Muslim to hold such a disbelieving woman in bondage, whereas a disbeliever was not permitted to hold such a Muslim woman in bondage, because Islam is supreme and nothing is above it. Likewise, a Muslim was permitted to own a disbeliever, whereas a disbeliever was not permitted to own a Muslim. Thus, intercourse with these women by virtue of full ownership is all the more clearly permissible.

This is clarified by the following: the impediment would have to be either disbelief or slavery. Disbelief of this kind is not an impediment, and slavery does not prevent intercourse by right of ownership; it can only serve as an impediment to marriage. Thus, when the ground permitting intercourse exists and no impediment remains, intercourse is permissible. This argument encompasses both “reasoning by analogy” and “a fortiori reasoning.” A “fourth argument” can also be derived from it, framed as “reasoning from the effective cause.”

It may be stated as follows: ownership provides the ground for permitting intercourse with the female slave, as the revealed text indicates when it identifies this effective cause in His words:

“Or those whom your right hands possess.”

Intercourse is prohibited only when there is a reason requiring its prohibition, such as her being forbidden through milk kinship, marriage ties, polytheism, or something similar. In this woman’s case, nothing could serve as an impediment except her being from the People of the Book, and that is not an impediment. When the ground for permissibility exists, and the alleged impediment cannot serve as a countervailing factor, that ground must be acted upon, since nothing of equal force opposes it. Once fully understood, these arguments establish with certainty that it is permissible.

“Fifth argument”: Whoever reflects on the lives of the Companions and the early Muslims during the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and that of the Companions will find many reports showing that they did not regard this as an impediment. Rather, this was the practice of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and of his caliphs. One example is the man who had a slave woman who had borne him a child. She used to revile the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, so he proceeded to kill her.

---

## Page 15788

and Abū Dāwūd and others narrated the report about her. This woman was not Muslim. However, it may be argued that this incident provides no evidence, because it occurred shortly after the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, arrived in Medina, when marriage to polytheistic women was not yet forbidden. That prohibition was established only after al-Hudaybiyya, when Allah Most High revealed:

“And do not hold on to marriage bonds with disbelieving women.”

Umar then divorced his wife who was in Mecca. As for the verse in al-Baqara, the date of its revelation is unknown. Some passages in al-Baqara were revealed later, such as the verses concerning fornication, while others were revealed earlier, such as the verses concerning fasting.

Another example is the report:

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, intended to undertake the expedition to Tabūk, he said to al-Jadd ibn Qays, ‘Would you like some of the women of Banū al-Asfar?’ He replied, ‘Give me leave, and do not tempt me.’”

Another example is his conquest of Khaybar and his distribution of the slaves. He did not require the Muslims to refrain from intercourse with the women until they embraced Islam, although he did require them to observe the waiting period to establish that they were not pregnant.

Indeed, those who permit “intercourse with idol-worshipping women by right of ownership” may cite what occurred at Awtās, when he said:

“No intercourse is to take place with a pregnant woman until she gives birth, nor with a woman who is not pregnant until she has had one menstrual period to establish that she is not pregnant.”

They may cite this as evidence that intercourse with idol-worshipping women by right of ownership is permissible. There is discussion to be had on this, but this is not the place for it. When the Companions conquered the lands, they did not refrain from intercourse with Christian women.

---

## Page 15789

Section

As for a Magian woman, we have already explained that the discussion concerning her rests on two principles.

The first is that animals slaughtered by the Magians are not lawful to eat, nor may their women be married. There are several lines of evidence for this.

One is that they are not among the People of the Book, and the food and women of those who are not among the People of the Book are not lawful.

There is an anomalous dissenting view concerning the first premise. The evidence for this premise is His statement, glorified is He:

“And this is a blessed Book which We have revealed, so follow it and fear Allah, that you may receive mercy.”

“Lest you say, ‘The Scripture was revealed only to two groups before us, and we were indeed unaware of their study.’”

It is thus clear that He revealed the Quran so that they would not say this—to prevent and forestall such a claim. Had Scripture been revealed to more than two groups, this statement would have been false, and there would have been no need to prevent them from saying it.

(Furthermore, He said:

“Indeed, those who believe, the Jews, the Sabians, the Christians, the Magians, and the polytheists—Allah will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection.”

He mentioned the six religious communities and stated that He would judge between them on the Day of Resurrection. But when He mentioned the religious communities whose members include those who will attain felicity in the Hereafter, He said: “Indeed, those who believe, the Jews, and the Christians

---

## Page 15790

and the Sabians—whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does righteous deeds” in two passages. He did not mention the Magians or the polytheists. Had these two religious communities included people who would attain felicity in the Hereafter, as did the Sabians, Jews, and Christians, He would have mentioned them. Had they possessed a scripture, they would have followed guidance before its abrogation and alteration, and would have entered Paradise by observing their religious law, just as the Jews and Christians did before abrogation and alteration. Since He did not mention the Magians among these communities, it is known that they have no scripture. Indeed, He mentioned the Sabians rather than them, even though the Sabians have no scripture unless they embrace the religion of one of the two scriptural communities. This indicates that the Magians are further removed from Scripture than they are.

Furthermore, al-Musnad, al-Tirmidhī, and other works of hadith, Quranic commentary, and accounts of the Prophet’s campaigns contain the well-known report:

“When Persia and Byzantium fought and the Persians prevailed, the polytheists rejoiced at this because the Persians were like them, having no scripture. The Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, welcomed this news because the Christians were closer to them, since they possessed a scripture. Allah Most High revealed: ‘Alif Lām Mīm. The Byzantines have been defeated in the nearest land. Yet after their defeat, they will triumph within a few years’”—and the remainder of the verse.

This shows that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions did not regard the Magians as possessing a scripture.

Furthermore, the report of al-Hasan ibn Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya and other Successors states:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, collected the poll tax from the Magians and said: ‘Treat them in the same manner as the People of the Book, but do not marry their women or eat the animals they slaughter.’”

This report has a missing link between the Successor and the Prophet.

---

## Page 15791

Reports from five Companions agree with it, and no contrary view is known from them. As for Hudhayfa, Ahmad stated that he married a Jewish woman. The general body of scholars has acted upon this report despite its missing link.

According to one of the two scholarly positions, a report lacking the link between a Successor and the Prophet constitutes authoritative evidence. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and Ahmad, according to one of the two views reported from him. According to the other position, it constitutes authoritative evidence if supported by the position of the majority of scholars and the apparent meaning of the Quran, or if transmitted through another chain with such a missing link. This is al-Shāfiʿī’s position. Thus, a report of this kind constitutes authoritative evidence by scholarly agreement. Moreover, this report explicitly addresses the particular issue and does not need to rest upon the two preceding arguments.

If it is said, “It is reported from ʿAlī that they once possessed a scripture, but it was taken away,” the reply is:

Ahmad and others have judged this report weak. Even if it is authentic, it only indicates that they once possessed a scripture which was then taken away, not that they now have a scripture in their possession. In that case, they cannot properly be included under the designation “People of the Book,” since they possess no scripture—whether altered or unaltered, abrogated or unabrogated. However, if they once possessed a scripture which was subsequently taken away, they retain a semblance of scriptural status. This much has a bearing on sparing their lives through payment of the poll tax, if its acceptance is restricted to the People of the Book.

As for marriage to their women and consumption of the animals they slaughter, permission is restricted to the People of the Book. The Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“Treat them in the same manner as the People of the Book,”

is evidence that they are not among the People of the Book. He only commanded that they be treated like them specifically in the collection of the poll tax, as the Companions did, for they understood these words to convey only this ruling. It has also been transmitted with the qualification:

“But do not marry their women or eat the animals they slaughter.”

Thus, those who permit the collection of the poll tax from idolaters reason by analogy

---

## Page 15792

between them and others regarding the poll tax. Those who restrict its collection to them say:

“They retain a semblance of scriptural status, unlike others. Lives are protected where there is doubt, but doubt does not render sexual relations or slaughtered animals lawful.”

Thus, when ʿAlī and Ibn ʿAbbās disagreed about the animals slaughtered by Banū Taghlib, ʿAlī said:

“They have retained nothing of Christianity except drinking wine.”

Ibn ʿAbbās recited the words of the Most High:

“Whoever among you takes them as allies is indeed one of them.”

ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him, prohibited eating the animals they slaughtered, although their lives were protected. He was also the one who transmitted the report concerning the scripture of the Magians. This shows that resembling the People of the Book in certain respects warrants sparing their lives, but does not make the animals they slaughter or marriage to their women lawful.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man uttered words of disbelief and was judged to have disbelieved. He subsequently swore an oath invoking a threefold divorce from his wife. If he returns to Islam, may he enter into a new marriage contract with her without her first marrying another man?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he apostatizes and does not return to Islam until his wife’s waiting period has expired, the marriage bond is severed according to the four imams. If he divorces her after that, he has pronounced divorce upon a woman who is no longer his wife, so the divorce has no effect. If he returns to Islam, he may marry her.

If, however, he divorces her during the waiting period, before returning to Islam, there are two views

---

## Page 15793

among the scholars.

The first is that the marriage bond is severed by the apostasy itself. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik according to the well-known report from him, and Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. On this view, any divorce pronounced thereafter is pronounced upon a woman who is no longer his wife, and therefore has no effect.

The second is that the marriage does not end until the waiting period expires. If he returns to Islam before the waiting period expires, their marriage remains intact. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad according to the other report from him.

On this view, if he pronounces divorce during the waiting period and returns to Islam before it expires, it becomes clear that he divorced his wife, and the divorce therefore takes effect. If, however, he does not return to Islam until the waiting period has expired, it becomes clear that he pronounced divorce upon a woman who was no longer his wife, and the divorce therefore has no effect. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15794

Chapter on the Dower

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

The Sunnah is to keep the dower modest and not exceed the amounts given to the Prophet’s wives and daughters. ‘Ā’isha—may Allah be pleased with her—reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The most blessed women are those who occasion the least expense.”

Ibn ‘Abbās reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best of them are those whose dowers are easiest to afford.”

Al-Hasan al-Basrī reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Marry women to men, and do not demand excessive dowers.”

‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb addressed the people, saying:

“Do not demand excessive dowers for women. If this were an honour in this world or an act of piety before Allah, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would have been the most entitled to it. He never gave any of his wives, nor was any of his daughters given, a dower exceeding twelve ūqiyyas.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “An authentic hadith.”

It is disliked for a man to agree to a dower that would cause him financial hardship if paid outright, or that he would be unable to discharge if left as a debt.

Abū Hurayra said:

“A man came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said:

---

## Page 15795

‘I have married a woman of the Ansār.’

The Prophet asked, ‘For what dower did you marry her?’

He replied, ‘Four ūqiyyas.’

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Four ūqiyyas! It is as though you carve silver from the side of this mountain! We have nothing to give you, but perhaps we can send you on an expedition from which you may gain something.’

He then sent an expedition to Banū ‘Abs and included that man among them.”

Muslim narrated this in his Sahīh.

An ūqiyya was forty dirhams by their reckoning. The amounts mentioned constituted the entire dower, without division into upfront and deferred portions.

Abū Hadrad al-Aslamī related:

“He mentioned that he had married a woman and came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, seeking his help with her dower. The Prophet asked, ‘How much did you pledge as her dower?’ I replied, ‘Two hundred dirhams.’ He said, ‘Even if you were scooping dirhams out of your valleys, you would not have offered more!’”

Imām Ahmad narrated this in his Musnad.

If a man pledges a large dower as a debt he owes her, while intending not to pay it to her, this is forbidden for him. Abū Hurayra reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever marries a woman for a dower that he intends not to pay her is a fornicator, and whoever incurs a debt that he intends not to repay is a thief.”

Some coarse, arrogant and ostentatious people inflate dowers for show and boastfulness, although they do not intend to collect them from the husband, and he intends not to pay them. This is reprehensible and vile, contrary to the Sunnah and outside the bounds of Islamic law.

---

## Page 15796

If the husband intends to pay it, although it is generally beyond his means, he has burdened himself, incurred a liability, and exposed himself to losing some of his good deeds and being held bound by his debt. The woman’s family have also harmed their son-in-law and caused him injury.

What is recommended regarding the “dower,” for someone who has the means and is well-off, is that its total, including both the upfront and deferred portions, should not exceed the dowers of the Prophet’s wives or daughters, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. These ranged from four hundred to five hundred dirhams of pure silver, approximately nineteen dinars. This is the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Whoever does this has followed his Sunnah regarding the dower.

Abū Hurayra, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“Our dowers when the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was among us were ten ūqiyyas.” He brought his hands together. This amounts to four hundred dirhams.

Imām Ahmad narrated this in his Musnad; the wording given here is that of Abū Dāwūd in his Sunan.

Abū Salama said:

“I asked ‘Ā’isha, ‘How much was the dower given by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’

“She replied, ‘The dower he gave his wives was twelve ūqiyyas and a nashsh.’ She asked, ‘Do you know what a nashsh is?’

“I replied, ‘No.’

“She said, ‘Half an ūqiyya. That makes five hundred dirhams.’”

Muslim narrated this in his Sahīh. It has already been related from ‘Umar that the dowers of the daughters of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were approximately the same.

Anyone who feels inclined to set his daughter’s dower higher than those of the daughters of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who are the best of Allah’s creation in every virtue and the finest women in the world in every quality—is an ignorant fool. The same applies to the dowers of the Mothers of the Believers.

This is for someone who has the means and is well-off. As for a poor man or someone in similar circumstances, he should pledge no more as a woman’s dower than he can pay without hardship.

---

## Page 15797

It is preferable to pay the woman her entire dower before consummation, if possible. Paying part upfront and deferring the rest is permissible. The righteous and virtuous early generations kept dowers modest.

“‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf married during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for a date stone’s weight of gold.”

They said that this weighed three and one-third dirhams. Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib married off his daughter for two dirhams, although she was among the finest unmarried women of Quraysh. This was after the caliph had sought her hand for his son, but Sa‘īd had refused to marry her to him. Reports that some of the early generations gave women large dowers concern those whose wealth had become abundant. They paid the entire dower before consummation, deferring none of it.

If a man is wealthy and has ample means, there is nothing wrong with his wishing to give his wife a large dower, as Allah Most High says:

“Even if you have given one of them a great amount of wealth, do not take any of it back.”

As for someone who incurs a dower obligation that he does not intend to discharge or cannot afford to pay, this is disliked, as explained above. Likewise, undertaking a large dower obligation without the means to fulfil it is not in keeping with the Sunnah. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man marries with a specified, recorded dower. He and his wife agree upon an upfront payment, which he pays, and then he dies. Is that upfront payment counted towards the total recorded dower?

He replied:

As for the cash payment that the husband makes to the woman upfront, as agreed upon, separate from the dower recorded in the marriage contract: if the husband gives her that amount, or part of it, or

---

## Page 15798

its equivalent, it is not counted towards the dower recorded in the marriage contract. Indeed, even if he had not paid it to her, she would be entitled to claim it according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions. It would form part of the dower to which her entitlement becomes established upon his death, and she would receive it in full after his death.

For if she agrees to an upfront portion and a deferred portion—what the early generations called “immediate” and “deferred”—and stipulates that he pay a certain amount upfront and defer a certain amount, then, even if she does not mention this when the contract is concluded, a prior stipulation that is not rescinded when the contract is concluded has the same force as one included in the contract, according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions. I have discussed this at length in the substantial work I wrote on “Questions of Means and Legal Stratagems” and “An Exposition of the Evidence for the Invalidity of Marriage Intended to Permit Remarriage to a Former Husband.” The exception is where the intention was that, should he consummate the marriage, he would pay her that amount beforehand. If he does not consummate the marriage, she is not entitled to the amount stipulated as payable before consummation.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman’s husband paid her a sum upfront without specifying it in the dower document, then died. The judge sought to count that upfront payment towards the dower specified in the marriage contract, because the upfront payment had not been mentioned in the dower document.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If they had agreed upon an immediate, upfront portion and a deferred portion—as is customary—the wife is entitled to claim the entire deferred portion if the upfront payment was not mentioned in the contract. The same applies if he had given her gifts, as is customary. But if he had paid her part of the specified dower, that payment is counted towards her dower entitlement. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15799

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man’s wife had him detained by the judge for two months over her dower, but no assets belonging to him could be found. Is the judge permitted to keep him in detention, or should he release him?

He replied:

If he is not known to possess any property, the judge should require him to swear an oath that he cannot pay, then release him. In these circumstances, it is not permissible to imprison him or require him to produce evidence, according to all four schools of jurisprudence.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a virgin and consummated the marriage, then claimed that she had not been a virgin. They brought their dispute before a judge, who sent two women with her. They found that she had been a virgin, but he denied this and refused to pay the dower. What is he obliged to do?

He replied:

He has no right to do that; rather, he must pay the full dower. As Zurārah stated, and as the rightly guided caliphs and imams ruled: once a man has closed the door and drawn the curtain, the waiting period must be observed and the dower paid. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15800

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man proposed to a woman, and they agreed to the marriage without concluding a marriage contract. He gave her father something for that purpose, but she died before the contract was concluded. Is he entitled to recover what he gave?

He replied:

If they fulfilled what they had agreed to and did not prevent him from marrying her before she died, they owe him nothing, and he is not entitled to recover what he gave them. Likewise, had he married her, she would have been entitled to the full dower. This is because he gave them that only so that they would enable him to marry her, and they did so. That was all they could possibly do.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman married, but it then emerged that she already had a husband, so the judge separated her from the second man. Is she entitled to a dower? If so, is it the stipulated dower or a dower comparable to that of women like her?

He replied:

If she knew that she was married and did not believe that her husband had either died or divorced her, then she has willingly committed adultery and is not entitled to any dower. If, however, she believed that he had died or divorced her, this constitutes intercourse under a mistaken belief of lawfulness within an invalid marriage, and she is entitled to a dower. The apparent position of the schools of Ahmad and Mālik is that she is entitled to the stipulated dower. Another opinion reported from Ahmad agrees with al-Shāfiʿī’s position: she is entitled to a dower comparable to that of women like her. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15801

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

May a man who is unable to pay his dower be allowed to pay it in instalments?

He replied:

If he is unable to pay, the dower is to be divided into instalments according to his means, and he must not be imprisoned. Most scholars accept his statement that he is unable to pay when supported by his oath. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. Some, however, do not accept evidence of his inability to pay until after he has been imprisoned; this is the view of some followers of Abū Hanīfa. Thus, if the case is before a judge who rules according to the school of al-Shāfiʿī or Ahmad, he is not to be imprisoned.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman and gave her the dower. A dower of one thousand dinars was also recorded as payable by him, with the express understanding: “We will not take anything from you; this is merely our custom and for appearances.” The husband has now died, and the woman is demanding from his heirs the full amount recorded in her document. What is the ruling?

He replied:

If the circumstances are as described, she may claim only what the two of them actually agreed upon. As for the amount recorded in the manner described, it is not lawful for her to demand it. Rather, she is entitled to what they agreed upon.

---

## Page 15802

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman married a man who then fled and left her for six years without providing her with maintenance. She subsequently married another man, who consummated the marriage with her. When the judge learned of her situation, he annulled their marriage contract. Is the husband obliged to pay the dower?

He replied:

If the first marriage had been annulled because maintenance could not be obtained from the husband, and her waiting period had ended before she married the second man, then her marriage to him is valid. But if she married the second man before the first marriage was annulled, her marriage to him is invalid.

If the husband and wife both knew that the first marriage remained in force and that they were forbidden to marry one another, the prescribed punishment must be imposed on them both.

If, however, the husband was unaware of the first marriage, denied its existence, or did not know that marrying her before its annulment was forbidden, then his marriage is one contracted under a presumption of lawfulness: he must pay the dower, any resulting child’s paternity is legally established, and no prescribed punishment applies.

If the woman or her guardian deceived him by telling him that she was not married to anyone, he is entitled to recover the dower he paid from whoever deceived him, according to the more correct of the two scholarly views.

---

## Page 15803

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

If a man is alone with his wife, but she refuses to allow him intercourse and he does not have intercourse with her, her entitlement to the full dower does not become established. This is the position of Imam Ahmad—as reported by his followers, such as Judge Abū Ya‘lā, Abū al-Barakāt and others—and of the other three imams: Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and Abū Hanīfah. If she acknowledges that she did not allow him intercourse, they unanimously agree that her entitlement to the full dower does not become established. They also unanimously agree that he owes her no maintenance for as long as she remains in that state. If she dislikes him and prefers someone else, she is to secure her release from him by compensation.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An enslaved man married a Muslim woman, after which his enslaved status became known. He had declared that he was free and possessed wealth in Egypt. Claims have been brought against him under the written contract and in respect of marital rights, and he has borrowed something from his wife. Is he liable for anything or not?

Answer

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

A slave’s marriage without his master’s permission, if the master does not approve it, is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims. It is reported in the Sunan collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Any slave who marries without his masters’ permission is a fornicator.”

However, if the master approves it after the contract has been concluded, it is valid according to

---

## Page 15804

the schools of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, and according to one of the two reported views of Ahmad. It is not valid according to al-Shāfi‘ī and the other reported view of Ahmad. If he requests marriage, his master must arrange his marriage, because Allah Most High says:

“Marry off the unmarried among you and the righteous among your male and female slaves. If they are poor, Allah will enrich them out of His bounty.”

If he deceives a woman by claiming to be free, marries her and consummates the marriage, she is unquestionably entitled to a dower. But is she entitled to the stipulated dower, as Mālik held according to one report? Or to the customary dower for a woman of comparable standing, as held by Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad according to one report? Or are two-fifths due, as Ahmad held according to a third report? Scholars disagree on this.

This obligation may be secured against the slave himself, as held by Ahmad according to the best-known report from him, and by al-Shāfi‘ī according to one opinion; I believe this is also Abū Hanīfah’s position. Or is it a personal debt owed by the slave, for which he may be pursued once freed, as held by al-Shāfi‘ī in his later position, and by Abū Yūsuf, Muhammad and others?

The first view is more convincing. His claim to them that he was free was deception and a lie, and his subsequent consummation of the marriage on the strength of that lie was a wrong committed against them. The imams agree that if a slave wrongs someone by destroying his property, wounding him or killing him, liability for his wrongdoing attaches to the slave himself; it does not become a personal debt owed by his master. Rather, the master is told: “You may either redeem your slave from liability for this wrong or surrender him so that compensation for it may be recovered from his person.”

If he wishes to kill him, he must pay the lesser of the two amounts: the compensation due for the wrong or the slave’s value. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī, of Ahmad according to the best-known report from him, and of others.

---

## Page 15805

And according to Mālik and one report from Ahmad, he must redeem him by paying the compensation due for the wrong, however much it may be. This slave has acted wrongfully and aggressively, wronging these people; liability for his wrongdoing therefore attaches to the slave himself. The same applies to whatever he borrowed from his wife’s wealth while claiming to be free: this too constitutes a wrong against them, and liability attaches to the slave himself according to the sounder of the two scholarly views. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman accepted property in satisfaction of her dower after her husband’s death, then sold that property and received its price. She subsequently acknowledged that she had received her dower from a source other than the proceeds of that property. Does this invalidate the purchaser’s right? Or should the amount she acknowledged receiving apart from that property be recovered from her?

He answered:

No right is invalidated merely by this admission. The heirs may demand from her the sale proceeds of the property she accepted in satisfaction of her dower, if she acknowledges having received her dower before that.

Some had issued a ruling that the amount she acknowledged receiving from the estate should be recovered from her, but this is unfounded. Her admission entails that she had received her dower in full and that, after receiving it, she acquired another property. Thus, it was the heirs, not the purchaser, whom she deprived of the real estate.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman, had her marriage contract drawn up, and paid her the immediately payable portion of the dower in full. The portion payable in instalments remained outstanding, but none of it had yet fallen due. He asked her to join him for consummation, but she refused, and she has a maternal aunt who is preventing her from doing so. Is she to be compelled to join him for consummation? And is her aforementioned aunt required to hand her over to him?

---

## Page 15806

He answered:

In these circumstances, she has no right to refuse to join her husband, by agreement of the imams. Neither her maternal aunt nor anyone else may prevent her from doing so. Rather, the aunt is to receive a discretionary punishment for preventing her from fulfilling what Allah has made obligatory upon her, and the woman is to be compelled to join her husband.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman and then divorced her three times. She holds a written claim against him payable at a specified time, but he is insolvent. What is the ruling?

He answered:

If he is insolvent, she may not demand payment from him until he has the means to pay. If witnesses testify to his insolvency, their testimony is to be heard. Indeed, according to the schools of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others, his word, supported by his oath, is accepted if he is not known to possess any wealth.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman while outwardly appearing to be free. She lived with him for eleven years, after which he divorced her and did not take her back. When she demanded her financial rights from him, he said, “I am a slave and must be placed under legal interdiction.” Is he obliged to fulfil his wife’s rights under the rulings of the noble Sharia according to the four schools of law?

---

## Page 15807

He answered:

The wife’s right remains established, and she is entitled to demand it, for two reasons.

The first is that his mere claim to be a slave does not extinguish her right in the circumstances described. The presumption is that people are free.
