If a man claims to be a slave without evidence, and nothing to the contrary is known, scholars within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere hold three opinions regarding the acceptance of his statement:

The first: it is accepted insofar as it counts against him, but not insofar as it affects his rights against others. This is one position held by Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad.

The second: it is not accepted under any circumstances. This is the position of some Mālikī scholars and one of the two reports from Ahmad.

The third: his statement is accepted without qualification. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and a reported position of Ahmad.

If, according to the majority of the imams of Islam, his admission cannot extinguish her right even when another person claims that he is a slave, how could his mere claim to be a slave do so? How much less so when he possesses wealth and a grant, claims a lineage, and previously claimed to be free in order to marry her?

The second reason is that, even supposing he lied, deceived her, and claimed to be free in order to marry her and consummate the marriage, he has thereby committed a wrong through his lies and deception. When a slave commits a wrong, liability for it attaches to his person. She may therefore seek satisfaction of her claim from his value as a slave, unless his master chooses to redeem him by paying her what she is owed; he is entitled to do so.

---

## Page 15808

Chapter on the Wedding Feast

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about wedding meals, meals served at gatherings of condolence, circumcision meals, and meals celebrating a birth.

Answer

He replied:

The wedding feast is a sunnah, and accepting an invitation to it is enjoined. As for a feast held on the occasion of a death, it is an innovation; both holding it and accepting an invitation to it are disliked. A circumcision feast is permissible: whoever wishes may hold one, and whoever wishes may refrain. The same applies to a feast celebrating a birth, unless an ʿaqīqah (birth sacrifice) has been offered for the child, for offering this sacrifice on the child’s behalf is a sunnah. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Is a circumcision meal disliked? Is a distinction drawn between it and a wedding feast?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The wedding feast is an enjoined sunnah by scholarly consensus; indeed, some scholars regard it as obligatory. It publicizes the marriage and makes it known, thereby distinguishing it from fornication and taking secret lovers. For this reason, scholars hold that accepting an invitation to it is obligatory when the relevant conditions are fulfilled and no impediments exist. As for the circumcision feast, it was not

---

## Page 15809

a practice of the Companions, though it is permissible. Some scholars among the followers of Ahmad and other imams disliked it, while others permitted it or even considered it recommended. As for accepting an invitation to it, anyone who does so is sinful. Some scholars considered it recommended, others did not, and still others also disliked accepting an invitation to it. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about the saying attributed to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever eats with someone who has been forgiven will himself be forgiven.”

Is this authentic or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. No one has transmitted this from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—on the basis of a waking encounter with him. Rather, it has only been mentioned that he was seen in a dream saying this. This statement is not true without qualification. And Allah knows best.

He was asked about the meaning of his saying:

“Whoever comes to a meal to which he has not been invited enters as a thief and leaves as a raider.”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. This refers to someone who joins a feast without its hosts’ permission. He enters furtively like a thief and eats without their consent, while they are too embarrassed to stop him. Thus he leaves like a raider who seizes people’s property by force. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15810

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about “the Prophet’s drinking in three stages—may Allah bless him and grant him peace,” meaning that he paused for breath three times:

If someone drinks in a single draught, is that forbidden? Is it reported that he never drank in a single draught? Some of the ten books mention “that he drank in a single draught.” A fatwa has been written on this, and they have said that drinking in a single draught is forbidden, yet none of the scholars has heard this view. A hadith also states “that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—drank while standing.” Is drinking in this manner merely disliked, or is it forbidden? Does someone who drinks standing without an excuse incur sin? And is drinking in a single draught forbidden?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. It is preferable to pause for breath three times while drinking, breathing away from the vessel, since breathing into the vessel is prohibited. If someone drinks in a single breath without pausing, however, that is permissible. For in the Sahīh collection, Anas reports:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to pause for breath three times when drinking from a vessel.”

In a version narrated by Muslim:

“He used to pause for breath three times while drinking, saying, ‘It quenches thirst more fully and is easier on the stomach.’”

This is evidence that pausing for breath three times is recommended. In the Sahīh collection, Abū Qatāda reports that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“When one of you drinks

---

## Page 15811

he must not breathe into the vessel.” This prohibits breathing into the vessel.

Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī reported:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade breathing into one’s drink. A man asked, ‘What about a speck I see in the vessel?’ He replied, ‘Pour it out.’ The man said, ‘I cannot quench my thirst in a single breath.’ He replied, ‘Move the cup away from your mouth.’”

Al-Tirmidhī narrated this and graded it authentic. Thus, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did not prohibit drinking in a single breath. Rather, when the man said, “I cannot quench my thirst in a single breath,” he told him:

“Move the cup away from your mouth.”

That is, if you need to breathe, do so away from the vessel. This indicates that if someone quenches his thirst in a single breath without needing to pause for breath, that is permissible. I know of no imam who made pausing for breath obligatory and drinking in a single breath forbidden. His practice—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—indicates that this is recommended, just as:

“He liked to begin with the right when putting on his shoes, combing his hair, purifying himself, and in all his affairs.”

If someone began with his left side before his right when purifying himself, he would have forgone the preferable practice, but his ablution would be valid, without any disagreement among the imams that I know of.

As for drinking while standing, there are authentic hadiths prohibiting it and authentic hadiths permitting it. The scholars have therefore differed over it, and two positions on it have been reported from Ahmad. However, these hadiths are reconciled by understanding the permission as applying when there is an excuse. Among the hadiths prohibiting it is the report in the Sahīh collection:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade drinking while standing.”

It also reports:

---

## Page 15812

from Qatādah, on the authority of Anas:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—sternly forbade drinking while standing.”

Qatādah said: “We asked, ‘What about eating?’ He replied, ‘That is worse and more reprehensible.’”

The hadiths permitting it include the report in the two Sahīh collections from ʿAlī and Ibn ʿAbbās:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—drank Zamzam water while standing.”

Al-Bukhārī also relates from ʿAlī that he drank while standing in the public square of Kufa. He then said:

“Some people disapprove of drinking while standing, yet the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did as I have just done.”

A report concerning this hadith of ʿAlī states that the water was from Zamzam, as related in the hadith of Ibn ʿAbbās. This took place during the Hajj, when the people there were circumambulating the Kaʿbah, drinking from Zamzam, drawing water, and asking him questions. There was no place to sit. Moreover, this occurred shortly before his death. This and similar circumstances are therefore exceptions to that prohibition.

This accords with the principles of Islamic law: something prohibited becomes permissible when there is a need. Indeed, things more strictly prohibited than this become permissible when there is a need. Even substances forbidden to eat or drink, such as carrion and blood, become permissible out of necessity. As for things whose direct use is forbidden despite their being pure—such as gold and silk—their use becomes permissible when there is a need.

The prohibition here concerns a manner of eating and drinking. It is therefore less stringent than the prohibition against drinking from gold and silver vessels or wearing gold and silk, for these carry a threat of punishment. Yet even these are permitted when there is a need, so this is all the more deserving of such permission. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15813

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked about eating and drinking while standing:

“Is it lawful, forbidden, or merely discouraged without being forbidden? Is eating and drinking permissible when one has an excuse, as in the case of a traveller? And may one eat and drink while walking along the road?”

He replied:

When there is an excuse, there is no harm in doing so. It is established that:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—drank Zamzam water while standing.”

The place was not suitable for sitting. When there is no need, however, it is disliked, since it is established that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade it. This distinction reconciles the texts. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked about a man who said:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—never ate yellow melon in his life.”

Another said:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—ate grapes daw dū.”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The assertion that “he ate grapes dū dū” is a lie with no basis. As for melons, they used to eat them; but the variety commonly known among them was the melon

---

## Page 15814

of the green variety. The report that Imam Ahmad refrained from eating melon because he did not know how the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—ate it is falsely attributed to Imam Ahmad.

He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—would eat the fruit of his land. He never declined fruit offered to him out of misguided asceticism or misplaced scrupulousness. Rather, he would neither refuse what was available nor go to undue lengths to obtain what was not. He followed the words of Allah Most High:

“O you who believe, eat of the good things We have provided for you, and be grateful to Allah, if it is Him that you worship.”

Thus, He commanded both eating and gratitude. Whoever forbids good things to himself and refrains from eating them without a reason sanctioned by Islamic law is blameworthy, an innovator, and falls within His words:

“O you who believe, do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you.”

Whoever eats them without giving the gratitude due for them is also blameworthy. Allah Most High says:

“Then, on that Day, you will surely be questioned about the blessings.”

That is, about gratitude for those blessings. It has been narrated that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“One who eats and is grateful has the same standing as one who fasts and is patient.”

In the Sahīh, it is reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah is indeed pleased with a servant who eats a meal and praises Him for it, or takes a drink and praises Him for it.”

Likewise, “excess in eating” is blameworthy: it means going beyond the proper limit. Whoever eats with the intention of gaining strength for worship is rewarded for doing so. The same applies to what he spends on his household, as the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said in an authentic hadith:

“What a Muslim spends on his family, seeking reward for it, is charity.”

He also said to Saʿd:

“You will never spend anything seeking thereby the Face of Allah without being raised in rank and standing through it—even the morsel you place in your wife’s mouth.”

---

## Page 15815

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about the following saying attributed to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Written on the rind of the melon are these words: ‘There is no god but Allah; Moses is the one to whom Allah spoke. There is no god but Allah; Jesus is a spirit from Allah. There is no god but Allah; Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.’”

And also:

“Whoever eats it with its rind will receive ten good deeds for every bite, and ten bad deeds will be erased from his record. If he eats it with its seeds, then for every thousand, he will receive a rank in Paradise.”

And about the report that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said to Abū Hurayrah:

“Do you have two shirts? Sell one and use the proceeds to buy yellow melon to eat.”

Was it authentically reported that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—ate melon with fresh dates? And, if the hadith is authentic, what is meant by eating melon with fresh dates?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. The preceding reports about melon are all fabricated. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did not encourage people to eat melon. Everything narrated of this kind is a lie.

As for eating “melon with fresh dates,” this is like eating cucumbers with fresh dates, and the hadith concerning the latter is more authentic. The point is to combine the sweetness of the one with the moisture of the other. His favourite drink was sweet and cool. This explains eating green melon with fresh or dried dates. As for eating it with yellow fresh dates, this has no basis, either in a text or in analogical reasoning. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15816

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A group of people visited a man so that he could offer them something to eat. He brought out the table with bread on it, then went away to fetch the food to accompany it. One of the guests said, “When bread is served, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘Do not wait for anything else.’” So they ate the bread. When the accompanying food arrived, there was no bread left to eat with it. They said to him, “You have lied about the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and made this man bear the expense of the bread.” Is the hadith he mentioned authentic or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Nothing concerning this has been reported from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, this is something some people say. Its meaning is to encourage contentment: when bread is served, one should be satisfied with it, neither waiting for anything else nor asking the host for anything more, for this is part of honouring him.

However, if they are waiting for food to be brought to accompany the bread, and eating the bread would leave that food to be eaten on its own, then the proper thing is to wait so that they can eat them together. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

If most of a man’s wealth is lawful, but a small portion of it is doubtful, and he offers someone hospitality or invites him, should that person accept or not?

---

## Page 15817

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If declining would cause harm—such as severing kinship ties, damaging relations, or the like—then he should accept the invitation. Maintaining kinship ties and harmonious relations is obligatory; if this can only be achieved by accepting, then accepting becomes obligatory, and doing so is not forbidden.

Alternatively, it may be said that the benefit of accepting outweighs the feared risk of involvement in what is doubtful.

If, however, declining entails no harm, but instead offers the benefit of shielding him from what is doubtful and discouraging the host from the small amount of wrongdoing involved, while accepting offers only the benefit of responding to the invitation and entails the harm of involvement in what is doubtful—which consideration carries greater weight? I believe there is disagreement on this. This issue has many subsidiary cases, a number of which have been discussed by the scholars of our school and others. Some scholars may give greater weight to abstention and religious scrupulousness, while others give greater weight to obedience and the benefit involved.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man has wealth derived from both lawful and unlawful sources. Is it permissible for someone to eat from his food or not?

He answered:

If he can identify the unlawful portion, he must certainly not eat from it. If he cannot identify it, eating from his food is not forbidden. However, if much of his wealth is unlawful, it should be avoided out of religious scrupulousness. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15818

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Is playing chess forbidden, disliked, or permissible? If you say it is forbidden, what is the evidence for its prohibition? If you say it is disliked, what is the evidence for that? Or if it is permissible, what is the evidence for its permissibility?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

Some forms of playing chess are forbidden by unanimous agreement; others are forbidden according to the majority of scholars and disliked according to some. None of the imams of the Muslims regards playing chess as permissible in the sense that playing and refraining are equally acceptable.

If playing chess involves stakes, it is forbidden by unanimous agreement. Abū ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Barr, the leading scholar of the Maghrib, said: “The scholars unanimously agree that playing chess for stakes is gambling and is not permissible.”

Likewise, if playing chess involves neglecting an obligation or committing something forbidden—such as delaying prayer beyond its prescribed time or omitting any of its obligatory inward or outward acts—then it is forbidden by the agreement of the scholars.

It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“That is the hypocrite’s prayer: he watches the sun until, when it is between the two horns of a devil, he rises and pecks out four units of prayer, remembering Allah only a little during them.”

Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, described this prayer as the prayer of the hypocrites. Allah has condemned their prayer in His words:

---

## Page 15819

“Indeed, the hypocrites seek to deceive Allah, but He deceives them. When they stand for prayer, they stand lazily, showing off before people and remembering Allah only a little.”

Allah Most High also says:

“So woe to those who pray, those who are heedless of their prayer.”

The early generations explained “heedlessness of prayer” as delaying it beyond its prescribed time and neglecting what one is commanded to do within it. Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained that the hypocrite’s prayer involves both delay and shortcomings in its performance. Salmān al-Fārisī said: “Prayer is a measure: whoever gives full measure will be repaid in full; whoever gives short measure—you know what Allah has said about ‘those who give short measure.’”

They similarly explained His words:

“Then there came after them successors who neglected prayer.”

He said: “Neglecting it means delaying it beyond its prescribed time and failing to fulfil its due requirements.” As stated in the hadith:

“When a servant perfects his prayer—its purification, recitation, and humble attentiveness—it ascends with a radiance like that of the sun, saying, ‘May Allah preserve you as you have preserved me.’ But if he does not perfect its purification, recitation, and humble attentiveness, it is folded up like a garment and struck against the face of the one who offered it, saying, ‘May Allah abandon you as you have neglected me.’”

Even if a servant performs the outward form of prayer, he is rewarded only in proportion to how attentive his heart was during it. As reported in the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd and others, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A servant may finish his prayer with only half of it recorded for him—or a third, a quarter, a fifth, a sixth, a seventh, an eighth, a ninth, or a tenth.”

Ibn ʿAbbās, may Allah be pleased with them both, said: “You have only that portion of your prayer of which you were consciously aware.” If distracting thoughts dominate the prayer, there are two views on whether the obligation has been discharged and whether the prayer must be repeated

---

## Page 15820

well known among the scholars:

The first is that the obligation has not been discharged. This is the view of Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Hāmid, Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, and others. The point is that chess is forbidden by scholarly consensus whenever it distracts from any inward or outward obligation. That it distracts from fulfilling obligations properly is too obvious to require elaboration.

The same applies if it distracts from an obligation other than prayer: attending to one’s own or one’s family’s welfare, enjoining what is right, forbidding what is wrong, maintaining kinship ties, honouring one’s parents, or discharging necessary responsibilities in an office of authority, a position of leadership, or any other matter. Rarely does anyone occupy himself with it without its distracting him from an obligation. It should therefore be understood that its prohibition in such circumstances is agreed upon.

Likewise, if it involves or entails something forbidden, it is forbidden by consensus. Examples include lying, false oaths, cheating—which they call mughādāh—or wrongdoing or assistance in it. Such conduct is forbidden by Muslim consensus even in racing and archery contests; how much more so in chess, backgammon, and similar games!

Likewise, if it is assumed to entail some other harm, such as gatherings involving preliminaries to sexual immorality, cooperation in aggression, or the like; or if playing it becomes so frequent and conspicuous that it entails neglecting an obligation or committing something forbidden—Muslims agree that playing it in these and similar circumstances is forbidden.

Even supposing it to be free of all these things, what is transmitted from the Companions is that they prohibited it. It is authentically reported that ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him, passed by some people playing chess

---

## Page 15821

and said:

“What are these statues to which you are so devoted?”

He likened them to those devoted to idols, just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is reported in al-Musnad to have said:

“The wine drinker is like an idol worshipper.”

Wine and gambling are paired together in the Book of Allah Most High. The prohibition of chess is likewise well known from Ibn ‘Umar and other Companions. What is transmitted from Abū Hanīfah and his followers, and from Ahmad and his followers, is that it is “forbidden.”

As for al-Shāfi‘ī, he said: “I disapprove of playing it because of the report. Playing chess and playing with pigeons without gambling, although we disapprove of them, are less serious than backgammon.” Other statements transmitted from him convey the same meaning: that he disapproved of chess and regarded it as less serious than backgammon. There is no doubt that his disapproval denotes prohibition, for he said, “because of the report.” The wording of the report that he himself transmitted from Mālik is:

“Whoever plays backgammon has disobeyed Allah and His Messenger.”

Thus, he disapproved of chess … (1) even though it is less serious than backgammon.

It has also been transmitted from him that he withheld judgment on its prohibition and said: “It is not clear to me that it is forbidden.” Yet we have not learned of anyone transmitting a statement from him that entails denying its prohibition. Moreover, the imams whose followers did not disagree over its prohibition most often expressed their ruling as “disapproval.”

Ibn ‘Abd al-Barr said: “Mālik and his followers unanimously agreed that neither backgammon nor chess may be played. They also said that the testimony of someone who habitually and persistently plays chess is inadmissible.”

Yahyā said: “I heard Mālik say, ‘There is no good in chess or other such things.’ I also heard him express disapproval of playing it and engaging in other vain pursuits, reciting this verse: ‘What, then, is there beyond the truth

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in both source copies.

---

## Page 15822

but error?’”

Abū Hanīfah said: “I disapprove of playing chess and backgammon.” Thus, all four imams prohibit every form of idle amusement.

The majority nevertheless differed over two issues.

The first: Should the greeting of peace be offered to someone playing chess? The explicit position of Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad, al-Mu‘āfā ibn ‘Imrān and others is that he should not be greeted. The position of Mālik, Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad is that he should be greeted. Nevertheless, Mālik’s position is that chess is worse than backgammon, whereas Ahmad’s position is that backgammon is worse than chess, as al-Shāfi‘ī also stated.

The correct assessment is that, when both involve stakes or both are played without stakes, chess is worse than backgammon. It contains the harms of backgammon and more, such as turning the heart away from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer, among other things. Hence the saying that chess accords with the doctrine of free will, while backgammon accords with the doctrine of determinism. Chess occupies the heart more intensely with thought. But when backgammon involves stakes, backgammon is worse. This is why Ahmad, al-Shāfi‘ī and others regarded backgammon as worse: they had in mind that stakes were involved in backgammon but not in chess.

This also makes clear the source of the confusion surrounding this subject. Allah Most High prohibited maysir (gambling) in His Book, and Muslims unanimously agree that maysir is forbidden. They also agree that competitive games involving wagers fall within maysir, whether played with chess, backgammon, nuts, gaming pieces or eggs. Several of the Successors, including ‘Atā’, Tāwūs, Mujāhid and Ibrāhīm al-Nakha‘ī, said: “Anything involving wagering falls within maysir—even games

---

## Page 15823

played by children with nuts.”

Those who did not prohibit chess, including some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and others, believed that the term “maysir” encompasses only gambling, which is forbidden because it involves consuming wealth unjustly. The same applies to racing and archery contests when each competitor puts up a stake and no third contestant participates without staking anything: they prohibited this because it is gambling. The Sunan collections report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever enters a horse between two other horses while assured that it will not win is engaging in gambling; whoever enters a horse between two other horses without being assured that it will not win is not engaging in gambling.”

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited sales involving uncertainty,” because they are a form of gambling. An example is buying a runaway slave or a stray camel: if the buyer finds him or it, he has won at the seller’s expense; if he does not, the seller has won at his expense.

Since they believed that these contests were prohibited only because they involved consuming wealth unjustly, they did not prohibit them when no stakes were involved. Some early followers of al-Shāfiʿī consistently applied this reasoning to backgammon, prohibiting it only when stakes were involved. However, al-Shāfiʿī’s explicit statement and the apparent position of his school prohibit backgammon outright, even without stakes. Hence his statement: “I disapprove of it because of the report.” He thus made clear that his basis for this position was the report, not analogical reasoning as he understood it.

This is one of the arguments the majority advanced against him: if backgammon is forbidden even without stakes, then chess, if not equivalent to it, is certainly no less objectionable. Anyone familiar with the reality of playing chess knows this. Whatever backgammon involves of turning people away from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer, and of stirring up enmity and hatred, is undoubtedly present to an even greater degree in chess. It affects souls as the intoxicating force of wine does. It thus distracts their minds

---

## Page 15824

and hearts from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer more than many kinds of intoxicating drinks and hashish do. A little play leads to much more. Prohibiting backgammon when no stakes are involved while permitting chess is like prohibiting a drop of grape wine while permitting a draught of a fermented wheat drink. Just as the latter position is utterly inconsistent with sound reasoning, analogy, and fairness, so too is this position concerning chess.

“The prohibition of backgammon” is established by an explicit text. The Sunan collections report from Abū Mūsā that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever plays backgammon has disobeyed Allah and His Messenger.”

Mālik also narrated it in al-Muwatta’. He also narrated from ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, that she learned that a family living in her house had a backgammon set. She sent them this message: “Unless you remove it, I will evict you from my house.” She rebuked them for it.

Mālik also narrated from Nāfi‘, from ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, that whenever he found a member of his household playing backgammon, he would beat him and break the set.

In another wording of the hadith, Abū Mūsā said:

“I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say, when backgammon was mentioned in his presence: ‘Whoever throws its dice to play it has disobeyed Allah and His Messenger.’”

He thus made merely playing it an act of disobedience, without stipulating that stakes be involved. Indeed, he explained it as throwing its dice.

Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from Abū Buraydah, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever plays backgammon is as though he had dipped his hand into the flesh and blood of a pig.”

Another wording states:

“Then let him cut pigs into pieces.”

Thus the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in this

---

## Page 15825

authentic hadith, likened someone who plays it to someone who dips his hand into the flesh and blood of a pig, or someone who cuts pigs into pieces—that is, butchers them and cuts up their flesh as a butcher does. This comparison encompasses playing it with one’s hands, whether or not money is taken, just as dipping one’s hand into the flesh and blood of a pig and cutting up its flesh apply whether or not one also eats it. Just as those acts are prohibited even when no wealth is wrongfully taken, so too is backgammon prohibited even when no wealth is wrongfully taken.

This is further established by several arguments that demonstrate the prohibition of backgammon, chess, and similar games.

First: the prohibition of these activities is not confined to cases involving gambling. If the prize were provided by one of the two players or by a third party, this would constitute a reward arrangement. Nevertheless, this too is prohibited, except in beneficial activities such as racing and archery contests, as stated in the hadith:

“No prize may be offered except for camel racing, horse racing, or archery.”

This is because spending money on something that brings no benefit in either religious or worldly affairs is prohibited, even when it does not constitute gambling. Taking wealth wrongfully is forbidden by the explicit text of the Quran, and these games are among vain things, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every diversion in which a man engages is vain, except shooting with his bow, training his horse, or playing with his wife, for these are worthwhile pursuits.”

The phrase “among vain things” means things that bring no benefit, for what is vain stands opposed to what is true and worthwhile. What is true may refer to an existing reality that is the object of belief or of a report. It may also refer to a rightful aim—something that ought to be pursued, namely, something beneficial. Whatever falls outside this is vain: it brings no benefit.

---

## Page 15826

Yet some such activities may be permitted if their harm does not outweigh their benefit; taking money through them, however, is not permitted. Thus foot racing, wrestling, and similar activities are permissible, although taking money through them is forbidden. Likewise, beating the frame drum at celebrations is permitted, although taking money for it is forbidden. It is therefore clear that the prohibition of these activities is not confined to gambling, and it is impermissible to restrict it to that alone. If backgammon and similar games were prohibited solely because of gambling, backgammon would be like horse racing, archery, and similar activities.

For when gambling enters into these activities, they regard them as forbidden, even though they are righteous pursuits that are either obligatory or recommended. As authentically reported, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Practise archery and horsemanship; I prefer that you practise archery rather than horsemanship.”

“Whoever learns archery and then forgets it is not one of us.”

“He and his successors used to hold horse races.”

“He recited from the pulpit the verse, ‘Prepare against them whatever strength you can muster, and steeds of war,’ then said: ‘Indeed, strength is archery! Indeed, strength is archery!’”

How, then, can something commanded by Allah and His Messenger, whose prescription the Muslims unanimously affirm, be likened to something forbidden by Allah, His Messenger, and his Companions after him? If gambling alone were deemed the grounds for prohibition, backgammon and chess would be like archery contests.

The Second Argument

It may be said: Suppose that gambling is the original reason for the prohibition. Nevertheless, the Lawgiver coupled wine and gambling together in prohibition. Allah Most High said:

“Wine, gambling, sacrificial stones, and divining arrows are nothing but defilement from Satan’s handiwork, so shun them that you may prosper.”

“Satan only seeks to stir up enmity and hatred among you through wine and gambling

---

## Page 15827

and keep you from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer. Will you not then desist?”

He described all four as defilement from Satan’s handiwork and commanded that they be shunned. He then singled out wine and gambling, stating that Satan seeks through them to stir up enmity and hatred among you and to keep you from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer. He threatens those who do not desist with His words, Most High:

“Will you not then desist?”

Likewise, He made success conditional upon shunning them, saying:

“So shun them that you may prosper.”

Hence it is said that this verse establishes the prohibition of wine and gambling in several ways. It is well known that, once wine was commanded to be shunned, any involvement with it became forbidden. Thus, it is impermissible to keep it or drink even a little of it. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered that it be poured away, its wineskins cut open, and its jars broken. He forbade turning it into vinegar, even when it belonged to orphans and had been purchased for them before the prohibition.

Thus, the correct view—expressly stated by Ahmad, Ibn al-Mubārak, and others—is that no wine enjoys legal protection, whether intended for vinegar-making or otherwise. Anyone making vinegar must alter the juice before it ferments, by adding vinegar to it or using some other means of preventing fermentation. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “forbade steeping two kinds of fruit together,” lest one intensify the effect of the other, leading someone to drink intoxicating wine unknowingly.

He also forbade preparing steeped drinks in vessels in which intoxicating fermentation develops without one knowing what is happening inside them, such as gourds, earthenware jars, pitch-coated vessels, and hollowed-out wooden vessels. He ordered that such drinks be prepared in waterskins with their mouths tied shut, because the development of intoxicating fermentation can be detected: if it occurs in the drink, the skin splits. There is, however, disagreement over whether these rules, or some of them, were abrogated; this is not the place to discuss it. The point is to block any means that might lead to drinking wine in any way.

---

## Page 15828

Likewise, he would drink a steeped fruit drink for three days, and after three days he would give it to others to drink or pour it away, since by then it might become intoxicating. Indeed, he had ordered that a wine-drinker be put to death on the third or fourth occasion. All of this … (1) was to block the means to wrongdoing. Since people crave wine, keeping it—even for making vinegar—may lead to drinking it, just as drinking a little invites drinking much. This was therefore forbidden. Such, then, is the case of “gambling,” which is mentioned alongside “wine,” if we assume that the reason for its prohibition is the wrongful consumption of wealth, with the resulting harm and loss of benefit.

It is well known that people crave these games. When their desire for them grows strong, monetary stakes are introduced, as commonly happens. It would therefore be consistent with the Lawgiver’s ruling to forbid whatever leads to this, if it offered no overriding benefit. This differs from contests that may be beneficial, such as racing, wrestling, and the like. These offer an overriding benefit by strengthening the body, so they were not prohibited on that account. Nor has it been customary for people to earn money through them.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, drew attention to this meaning when he said:

“Whoever plays backgammon is as though he had dipped his hand into the flesh and blood of a pig.”

Dipping one’s hand into this invites eating pork: it is a preliminary step, a cause, and an inducement to eating it. If that is forbidden, then so too is playing a game that is a preliminary step, a cause, and an inducement to consuming wealth wrongfully.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in one of the two source copies.

---

## Page 15829

This makes clear what the scholars have stated: contests are of three kinds.

Those that help fulfil what Allah has commanded in His words:

“Prepare against them whatever strength you can muster, and horses ready for battle”

are permissible with or without a prize. Those that lead to what Allah has forbidden, such as backgammon and chess, are prohibited with or without a prize. Those that may offer benefit without any overriding harm, such as racing and wrestling, are permissible without a prize.

The Third Argument

The claim that gambling was prohibited solely because it involves wagering is an unsupported assertion. The plain meaning of the Quran and Sunnah, together with rational consideration, demonstrates its falsity.

Allah Most High says:

“Satan only seeks to stir up enmity and hatred among you through wine and gambling, and to turn you away from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer.”

He thus draws attention to the reason for the prohibition: the harm these things cause and the loss of benefits associated with obligatory and recommended acts. The arising of enmity and hatred is among the gravest harms. Likewise, turning the heart away from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer—each of which may be obligatory or recommended—is among the gravest harms.

It is well known that this occurs when playing chess, backgammon, and similar games, even when no monetary stakes are involved. It is more pronounced in chess: a player becomes so absorbed, in heart, mind, and thought, in what his opponent has done, what he himself intends to do, and the consequences of those moves and their further consequences, that he is unaware of his hunger or thirst, of anyone greeting him, of his family’s circumstances, or of other essential matters concerning himself and his property—let alone remembering his Lord or thinking of prayer.

---

## Page 15830

This is like what happens to someone who drinks wine; indeed, many drinkers are more clearheaded than many chess and backgammon players. The player can satisfy his craving only with game after game, just as the wine drinker satisfies his craving only with cup after cup. The effects of these games linger in the mind after play has ended longer than the effects of wine. They intrude upon him during prayer, illness, and riding, and even at the approach of death—times when he should remember his Lord and turn to Him. Images of the pieces come before him, along with thoughts of the king, rook, firzān, and the like.

Thus, these games may turn the heart away from remembering Allah more powerfully than wine does, and they are closer to drinking. As the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him, said to those playing chess:

“What are these images to which you are so devoted?”

He then overturned the board. Likewise, enmity and hatred arise when one player defeats another, together with the mutual wrongdoing, lying, and betrayal involved—among the strongest causes of enmity and hatred. A player scarcely escapes any of these things.

When an activity frequently involves such things, human nature inclines towards them, and it offers no outweighing benefit, the Lawgiver unquestionably prohibits it. How much more so when these things occur more often than not! This is a consistently applicable principle of Islamic law, as we have explained at length in “The Principle of Blocking the Means to Evil” and elsewhere. We have shown that any act which frequently leads to something prohibited is a cause of evil and corruption. If it offers no outweighing benefit recognized by Islamic law, and its harm outweighs its benefit, it is forbidden. Indeed, every means leading to corruption is forbidden when it offers no outweighing benefit.

---

## Page 15831

so how much more does this apply to what frequently leads to corruption! This is why being alone with a woman outside the prohibited degrees of marriage is forbidden. As for looking, since there is a need for some of it, permission is granted to the extent required by that need. Need is a basis for permission, just as corruption and harm are grounds for prohibition. When the two coincide, the weightier consideration prevails. Thus, when harm threatens, eating carrion takes precedence, because the harm of death is worse than the harm of consuming impure food.

“Backgammon and chess” and similar competitive games involve countless harms and offer no benefit recognized by Islamic law, let alone one that could counterbalance those harms. At most, they entertain and relax the soul, just as the wine drinker seeks to do. Yet ample relief can be found in permissible activities that neither obstruct beneficial pursuits nor bring about harm. Allah has made what He permits sufficient for the believer, sparing him what He forbids, and through His bounty has freed him from dependence on anyone else:

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make for him a way out and provide for him from where he does not expect.”

In Sunan Ibn Mājah and elsewhere, Abū Dharr relates that when this verse was revealed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Abū Dharr, if all people acted upon this verse, it would suffice them.”

In this verse, Allah, glorified be He, explains that He wards off harm from the one who fears Him by providing him with a way out of circumstances that constrain others. He also brings him benefit and provides for him from where he does not expect. Everything that sustains a living being—including what gives souls relief and what they need for their well-being and contentment—is part of provision. Allah Most High grants this to those who fear Him by doing what He commands and abandoning what He forbids.

Whoever seeks this through backgammon, chess, and similar forms of gambling is like someone who seeks it through wine. The wine drinker seeks relief, but it only increases his weariness and distress, even though it may afford him

---

## Page 15832

a measure of pleasure: the ensuing harms and the joys he forfeits are many times greater, as those who have experienced this know. The same applies to all other forbidden things.

The following also makes clear that “gambling” was not prohibited merely because it involves consuming wealth unjustly—although consuming wealth unjustly is forbidden even apart from gambling, so how much more so when it occurs through gambling. Rather, as with wine, there is another reason for its prohibition besides consuming wealth unjustly: Allah has coupled wine with gambling and made the reason for prohibiting one the same as the reason for prohibiting the other. It is well known that wine was not prohibited merely because it involves consuming wealth unjustly, although consuming its proceeds does constitute consuming wealth unjustly. The same therefore applies to gambling.

This is illustrated by the fact that when people first asked the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about wine and gambling, Allah Most High revealed:

“They ask you about wine and gambling. Say, ‘In them is great sin and benefits for people, but their sin is greater than their benefit.’”

The “benefits” they offered have been understood to mean financial gain; others have understood them to mean pleasure. It is well known that wine offered both: people benefited from its sale and trade, just as they benefited from the pleasure of drinking it. Then, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited wine, he

“cursed wine, the one who presses it, the one who has it pressed, its seller, its buyer, its carrier, the one to whom it is carried, the one who serves it, the one who drinks it, and the one who consumes its proceeds.”

Likewise, people benefited from “gambling” through the money they obtained from it and the pleasure they derived from playing.

Then Allah Most High said:

“But their sin is greater than their benefit,”

because the loss in

---

## Page 15833

gambling is greater, and the pain and harm involved in playing are greater. Perhaps most people’s primary aim in gambling is simply the enjoyment of playing and competing, just as most people’s primary aim in drinking wine is the pleasure it affords. He prohibited payment for such activities only because it amounts to taking money for something of no benefit, and thus to consuming wealth unjustly, just as He prohibited the proceeds from wine, carrion, swine, and idols.

How, then, can financial harm alone be regarded as the rationale for the prohibition, when it is secondary, while the fundamental harm—the corruption of the mind and heart—is disregarded? Wealth sustains the body, and the body is subordinate to the heart. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, there is a piece of flesh in the body which, when sound, makes the rest of the body sound, and when corrupt, makes the rest of the body corrupt. Indeed, it is the heart.”

The heart is the seat of remembrance of Allah Most High and of the true essence of prayer.

Thus, the gravest harm for which wine and gambling are prohibited is the corruption of the heart, which governs the body: it is diverted from the remembrance of Allah and prayer, for which it was created, and falls into the mutual enmity and hatred that corrupt it. Prayer is the right of the True One, while mutual love and loyalty are the rights of His creatures. How can consuming wealth unjustly compare with this?

It is well known that the welfare of the body takes precedence over the welfare of wealth, and that the welfare of the heart takes precedence over the welfare of the body. Wealth is inviolable only because it sustains the body. For this reason, jurists placed the division on acts of worship before the division on transactions in their books; together, these secure the welfare of the heart and body. They then discussed the division on marriage, for the preceding divisions concern the welfare of the individual, whereas this concerns the welfare of the species, which is perpetuated through marriage. Then, having discussed what promotes welfare, they discussed what wards off harm in the division on criminal offences.

---

## Page 15834

Allah Most High has said:

“I did not create jinn and mankind except to worship Me.”

And “worship of Allah encompasses knowing Him, loving Him, and humbling oneself before Him; indeed, it encompasses everything He loves and is pleased with. Its foundation and greatest aspect are the deeds of the heart: faith, knowledge of Allah, love for Him, reverent fear of Him, turning to Him, reliance upon Him, and contentment with His judgment. These are encompassed by prayer, remembrance, supplication, and recitation of the Quran. All of this falls within the meaning of remembrance of Allah and prayer. Mentioning prayer alongside remembrance of Allah is an instance of conjoining the specific with the general, as in His words, Most High:

‘And His angels and His messengers, and Gabriel and Michael.’

And His words, Most High:

‘And when We took from the prophets their covenant, and from you and from Noah.’

Likewise, Allah Most High said:

‘O you who believe, when the call to prayer is made on Friday, hasten to the remembrance of Allah and leave off trade.’

He thus described hastening to prayer as hastening to the remembrance of Allah.

Since prayer encompasses remembrance of Allah Most High, which is sought for its own sake, and restraint from evil, which is sought for a further purpose, Allah Most High said:

‘Indeed, prayer restrains one from indecency and wrongdoing, and the remembrance of Allah is greater.’

That is, the remembrance of Allah within prayer is greater than its restraining one from indecency and wrongdoing. It does not mean that remembrance of Allah outside prayer is better than prayer and the remembrance of Allah it contains, for that would contradict scholarly consensus.

Since remembrance of Allah is the purpose of prayer, Abū al-Dardā’ said: ‘As long as you remember Allah, you are in prayer, even if you are in the marketplace.’ And since the remembrance of Allah

---

## Page 15835

encompasses all of this, they said: Gatherings devoted to discussing the lawful and unlawful, and similar matters involving mention of Allah’s commands and prohibitions, His promises and warnings, and the like, are gatherings of remembrance.

The aim here is to understand “the degrees of benefit and harm,” what Allah and His Messenger love, and what He does not hate in what Allah and His Messenger have commanded. Such commands serve to secure the benefits He loves and is pleased with and to avert the harms He hates and is displeased with. Whatever has been prohibited involves what He hates and is displeased with and prevents what He loves and is pleased with.

Many people’s outlook falls short of recognizing what Allah and His Messenger love concerning the benefits and harms affecting hearts and souls, the realities of faith that benefit them, and the heedlessness and desire that harm them. As Allah Most High said:

‘And do not obey one whose heart We have made heedless of Our remembrance, who follows his own desires, and whose affairs have exceeded all bounds.’

And He said, Most High:

‘So turn away from whoever turns away from Our remembrance and desires nothing but the life of this world. That is the extent of their knowledge.’

Thus, in many rulings, you find that many such people recognize only those benefits and harms that concern wealth and the body. When they go beyond this, the furthest many of them reach is to consider “governing the soul and refining character” within the limits of their knowledge. Similar discussions are found among would-be philosophers and the Qarmatians, such as the authors of the “Epistles of the Brethren of Purity” and others like them. They discuss governing the soul and refining character according to their knowledge of philosophy, adding to it whatever they suppose to be part of the revealed Law. Yet even at the furthest point they reach, they remain far below the Jews and Christians, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 15836

Some of those who discuss “the principles of jurisprudence” and explain the reasons for rulings of the revealed Law in terms of suitable attributes, when discussing this suitability and how the Lawgiver’s linking rulings to suitable attributes secures people’s interests and averts harm from them, regard benefit as being of “two kinds”: otherworldly and worldly. They identify otherworldly benefit with the wisdom found in governing the soul and refining character, and worldly benefit with whatever safeguards lives, wealth, chastity, reason, and the outward observance of religion.

They disregard the various forms of knowledge of Allah Most High, His angels, His Books, and His messengers found in inward and outward acts of worship, as well as the states and deeds of the heart: loving Allah, fearing Him, devoting one’s religion sincerely to Him, relying upon Him, hoping for His mercy, supplicating to Him, and other kinds of benefit in this world and the Hereafter. The same applies to what the Lawgiver has prescribed concerning fulfilling covenants, maintaining ties of kinship, the rights of slaves and neighbours, the rights Muslims have over one another, and the other things He has commanded and forbidden to preserve noble states and refine character. It thus becomes clear that this is only one part of the benefits brought by the revealed Law.

Likewise, anyone who attributes the prohibition of intoxicants and gambling solely to the wrongful consumption of wealth, and identifies their former benefit solely with obtaining wealth, resembles these people … (1) that these contests distract from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer by virtue of being activities, not by virtue of obtaining wealth. For in the latter respect, they distract from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer only as other ways of obtaining wealth do. It is well known that assets used to earn wealth are not prohibited without qualification merely because they distract from the remembrance of Allah and from

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in both originals.

---

## Page 15837

prayer; rather, only those that distract from an obligation are prohibited, as Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, when the call is made for prayer on Friday, hasten to the remembrance of Allah and leave off trade.”

He also says:

“Then, when the prayer is concluded, disperse throughout the land and seek Allah’s bounty.”

And He says:

“O you who believe, let neither your wealth nor your children distract you from the remembrance of Allah.”

And He says:

“Neither trade nor sale distracts them from the remembrance of Allah, establishing prayer, and giving zakat.”

Whatever distracts and occupies a person from the remembrance of Allah and prayer that Allah Most High has commanded is therefore prohibited, even if that kind of activity is not itself unlawful, such as selling, engaging in trade, and so forth.

Thus, if playing chess, backgammon, and similar games were permissible in itself, and were prohibited only when it involved consuming wealth wrongfully, its prohibition would be of the same kind as that of prohibited sales and leases involving the wrongful consumption of wealth, such as sales involving uncertainty. The prohibition of these transactions is not explained by their distracting people from obligatory remembrance of Allah and from prayer, since valid sales can also cause such distraction. Nor is the prohibition of invalid transactions explained by their distracting people from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer. It may therefore be said that, among valid transactions, those that distract from an obligation are prohibited.

It thus becomes clear that gambling is not prohibited merely because it is an invalid transaction. Rather, the activity itself is prohibited because of this harmful consequence, just as drinking intoxicants is prohibited. This is clear to anyone who reflects upon it.

Do you not see that when Allah prohibited usury because of the injustice and wrongful consumption of wealth it involves, He coupled this with mention of trade, which is just, and preceded it with mention of charity, which is an act of beneficence? Thus, towards the end of Sūrat al-Baqarah, He set out the rulings concerning wealth as they pertain to the beneficent, the just, and the unjust:

---

## Page 15838

He mentioned charity, trade, and usury. The injustice and wrongful consumption of wealth involved in usury are more evident than in gambling, for the usurer takes a guaranteed surplus from someone in need. Allah therefore punished him with the opposite of what he intended, saying:

“Allah destroys usury and gives increase to charity.”

The gambler, by contrast, may win and thereby wrong another person; the victim may be rich or poor. Wronging someone poor and needy is more serious than wronging someone rich. Injustice in which the powerful party is certain to be the wrongdoer is more serious than injustice in which the wrongdoer is not determined beforehand. For a wealthy, powerful person to wrong someone weak and helpless is more reprehensible than two wealthy, powerful people wronging one another when it is not known which will be the wrongdoer.

Thus, usury involves greater financial injustice than wagering. Nevertheless, its prohibition came later, and it was the last thing Allah Most High prohibited in the Quran. If gambling involved nothing more than wagering, it would have been less serious than usury, whose prohibition came later.

The Lawgiver has permitted certain forms of contractual uncertainty to meet a need. For example, He permitted stipulating that the fruit of date palms be included with the trees after pollination, as something subsidiary to them. He also permitted sales in bulk without exact measurement, and other such transactions. As for usury, none of it was permitted. Rather, He allowed quantities to be assessed by estimation instead of measurement by volume when needed, just as He allowed dry ablution out of need when water is unavailable. Estimation determines a quantity on the basis of probable judgment, whereas measurement by volume determines it with certainty. Resorting to probable judgment instead of certainty is permissible when needed.

It thus becomes clear that usury is more serious than wagering that involves nothing beyond the wrongful consumption of wealth. Gambling, however, is pursued for play and competition, and a person is forbidden to engage in it because it corrupts his mind as well as his wealth, including by turning him away from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer.

Both intoxicants and gambling engender enmity and hatred and divert people from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer to a greater extent than usury and other invalid transactions.

---

## Page 15839

It thus becomes clear that “gambling” involves “two kinds of harm”: harm to wealth, through its wrongful consumption; and harm arising from the activity itself, including financial harm, corruption of the heart and mind, and the breakdown of relations between people. Each of these harms independently warrants prohibition. Wrongfully consuming wealth is therefore prohibited in all circumstances, even without gambling, as in usury. Likewise, whatever diverts people from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer and engenders enmity and hatred is prohibited, even when no wealth is consumed.

When both harms occur together, the prohibition becomes more severe. Gambling that involves both is therefore more serious than usury. This is why it was prohibited before usury. It is well known that when Allah Most High prohibited intoxicants, He prohibited them even for someone drinking them as a remedy, as established in the authentic hadith. He also prohibited selling them to the People of the Book or anyone else, even though consuming the proceeds does not divert people from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer or engender enmity and hatred. For when Allah Most High forbids a people to consume something, He also forbids them its price. All this serves to ensure that it is thoroughly avoided.

The same applies to gambling: both aspects are prohibited. Someone who assists in gambling is like someone who assists with intoxicants, for this constitutes cooperation in sin and transgression.

Just as it is forbidden to assist with intoxicants by selling, pressing, serving, or otherwise, so too is it forbidden to assist in gambling—for example, by selling or hiring out its equipment, or by wavering between the two players while assisting one of them. Indeed, merely being present among people who are gambling is like being present among people drinking intoxicants. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day must not sit at a table where intoxicants are being drunk.”

A report was also brought before ‘Umar ibn

---

## Page 15840

‘Abd al-‘Azīz, may Allah be pleased with him, concerning a group of people drinking intoxicants. He ordered that they be flogged, but was told, “One of them is fasting.” He replied, “Begin with him.” Then he said, “Have you not heard the words of Allah Most High?”

“He has already revealed to you in the Book that when you hear Allah’s revelations being denied and mocked, you must not sit with them until they turn to another topic; otherwise, you would be like them.”

‘Umar cited this verse as evidence because Allah Most High placed those present when wrongdoing occurs on the same footing as those committing it. Indeed, even an invitation to a wedding feast must not be accepted if the feast involves wrongdoing, unless the host abandons it—even though accepting the invitation is a duty owed to him. How, then, could one attend a gathering involving wrongdoing when no such duty calls for one’s presence?

If it is asked, “If this is a form of gambling, how could some of the early Muslims have considered it permissible?” the answer is: Those among the early Muslims who permitted chess without stakes are like those who permitted backgammon without stakes. Both views are reported from some of the early Muslims. Moreover, the excuse of some who played chess has become clear. Al-Sha‘bī, for example, played it when al-Hajjāj summoned him to appoint him as a judge. He decided to play so that he would be judged morally unfit and would not have to serve as a judge under al-Hajjāj. He considered it preferable to bear such wrongdoing in order to avoid assisting someone like al-Hajjāj in his oppression of the Muslims. He regarded the latter as a far graver evil, and could find no way to excuse himself except by something of this kind.

It may further be said: It is well known that the early Muslims who considered the disputed nabīdh drinks lawful, and those who permitted exchanging one dirham for two, were more numerous and more eminent than these. Ibn ‘Abbās, Mu‘āwiyah, and others permitted exchanging one dirham for two, interpreting the prohibition of usury as applying only to deferred exchanges, not to exchanges made on the spot. The same applies to those who thought that the term “intoxicants”

---

## Page 15841

applied only to intoxicating drinks made from grape juice.

These people understood “intoxicants” to mean one kind but not another, and thought that the prohibition applied only to that kind. Yet “gambling” encompasses all its forms, just as “intoxicants” and “usury” encompass all their respective forms. No one may follow the scholars’ errors, just as no one may speak of people of knowledge and faith except in a manner befitting them.

Allah Most High has pardoned the believers for their mistakes, as He Most High says:

“Our Lord, do not take us to task if we forget or make a mistake.”

Allah said, “I have done so.” He has commanded us to follow what has been sent down to us from our Lord and not to follow protectors besides Him. He has commanded us not to obey any created being in disobedience to the Creator, and to seek forgiveness for our brothers who preceded us in faith, saying:

“Our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith”—and the rest of the verse.

This is obligatory upon Muslims in all such matters. We must honor His command by obeying Allah and His Messenger, and uphold the rights of Muslims, especially the people of knowledge among them, as Allah and His Messenger have commanded.

Whoever departs from this path has turned away from following the evidence to following his own desires through blind imitation, and has wrongfully harmed believing men and women. He is therefore among the wrongdoers. Whoever honors what Allah has made inviolable and treats Allah’s servants well is among Allah’s allies who fear Him. And Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 15842

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Two men disagreed about chess. One said, “It is forbidden.” The other said, “It keeps one from backbiting and looking at people, and it is lawful.” Which of them is correct?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

If chess is played for stakes or involves neglecting an obligation—such as delaying prayer beyond its prescribed time, neglecting its obligatory elements, or failing to attend to the needs of one’s dependants or other duties incumbent upon Muslims—then it is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. Likewise, if it involves lying, injustice, or other prohibited acts, it is forbidden by consensus.

When it is free of these things, the majority of scholars—including Mālik and his followers, Abū Hanīfa and his followers, Ahmad ibn Hanbal and his followers, and many of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers—hold that it is forbidden. These scholars said that al-Shāfi‘ī did not categorically declare it lawful; rather, he disapproved of it. It is also reported that he said, “Its prohibition has not become clear to me.”

Al-Bayhaqī, the most knowledgeable of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers in hadith and his staunchest defender, cited the Companions’ consensus on prohibiting it, reporting this position from ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, Abū Sa‘īd, Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, Abū Mūsā, and ‘Ā’isha—may Allah be pleased with them. He reported no disagreement among the Companions on this matter. Whoever reports that any Companion permitted it is mistaken. Al-Bayhaqī and other hadith scholars know the Companions’ statements better than those who transmit statements without chains of transmission.

Al-Bayhaqī said: “Al-Shāfi‘ī regarded playing chess as one of the matters concerning which there is disagreement

---

## Page 15843

with regard to the ruling that it does not disqualify a person from giving testimony. As for his disapproval of playing it, he explicitly stated this in the passage we cited earlier, and this is more consistent with his school and more fitting for it. Those who disapproved of it are more numerous, and among them are those whose statements carry authority.”

It was narrated through his chain of transmission, from Ja‘far ibn Muhammad, from his father, from ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib—may Allah be pleased with him—that he used to say:

“Chess is the gambling of the non-Arabs.”

It was also narrated through his chain of transmission from ‘Alī that he passed some people playing chess and said:

“‘What are these statues to which you are devoted?’ For one of you to touch a burning coal until it goes out would be better for him than to touch these pieces.”

It was narrated from ‘Alī—may Allah be pleased with him—that he passed a gathering of Taym Allah where people were playing chess. He said:

“By Allah, you were created for something other than this! By Allah, were it not that it would set a precedent, I would strike your faces with these pieces!”

Mālik said: “We have heard that Ibn ‘Abbās had charge of an orphan’s property and burned the chess pieces.”

It was narrated that Ibn ‘Umar was asked about chess and said:

“It is worse than backgammon.”

Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī said:

“Only a sinner plays chess.”

It was narrated that ‘Ā’isha disapproved of al-kayl even when no wagers were placed on it. Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī also disapproved of playing chess. These, then, are the statements of the Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—and no contrary view has been reliably established from any Companion.

Al-Bayhaqī also narrated that Abū Ja‘far Muhammad ibn ‘Alī, known as al-Bāqir, was asked about chess and said:

“Spare us this Magian practice.”

Al-Bayhaqī said:

“We have transmitted reports expressing disapproval of playing it from Yazīd ibn Abī Habīb, Muhammad ibn Sīrīn, Ibrāhīm, and Mālik ibn Anas.”

I say:

In the language of the early generations, “disapproval” frequently—and indeed usually—means prohibition. These scholars explicitly stated that their disapproval amounted to prohibition. Indeed, they explicitly stated that chess is worse than backgammon, and backgammon is forbidden even when no stakes are involved.

---

## Page 15844

It was also narrated through his chain of transmission, from Jāmi‘ ibn Wahb, from Abū Salama, who said:

“I asked al-Qāsim ibn Muhammad, ‘What is gambling?’ He replied, ‘Whatever distracts from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer is gambling.’”

Yahyā ibn Ayyūb said: “ ‘Abd Allah ibn ‘Umar told me that he heard ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd Allah say: ‘I said to al-Qāsim ibn Muhammad, “Backgammon is gambling. What about chess—is it gambling?” Al-Qāsim replied, “Whatever distracts from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer is gambling.” ’ ”

Ibn Wahb said: “Yahyā ibn Ayyūb told me that Abū Qays narrated to them from ‘Uqba ibn ‘Āmir, who said: ‘I would rather worship an idol that was worshipped in the pre-Islamic age of ignorance than play this gambling game.’” Al-Qaysī said: “It consisted of sticks with which people played on the ground.”

Through his chain of transmission, he also narrated from Fadāla ibn ‘Ubayd, who said:

“It makes no difference to me whether I play al-kayl or perform ablution with pig’s blood and then stand to pray.”

The report that ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib passed some people playing chess and said, “What are these statues to which you are devoted?” is reliably established from him. He was likening them to idol worshippers. This accords with Allah’s words:

“O you who believe! Intoxicants, gambling, sacrificial altars and divining arrows are nothing but defilement from Satan’s handiwork, so avoid them that you may succeed. Satan only seeks to sow enmity and hatred among you through intoxicants and gambling, and to turn you away from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer. Will you not then desist?”

“Gambling” includes backgammon and similar games. It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever plays backgammon has dipped his hand in the flesh and blood of a pig.”

In the Sunan collections, he is reported to have said:

“Whoever plays backgammon has disobeyed Allah and His Messenger.”

The position of the four imams is that playing backgammon is forbidden, even when no stakes are involved.

Ibn ‘Umar, Mālik ibn Anas and others said that chess is worse than backgammon. And it was stated by Abū Hanīfa

---

## Page 15845

and Ahmad ibn Hanbal, al-Shāfi‘ī and others that backgammon is worse than chess. Both views are correct in different respects. If backgammon is played for stakes and chess without stakes, then backgammon is worse, and in that case it is forbidden by consensus. If, however, both are played for stakes, or both without stakes, then chess is worse than backgammon, because it occupies the heart and distracts from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer more than backgammon does.

Hence it has been said: “Chess is based on the doctrine of human free will, while backgammon is based on the doctrine of determinism.” The backgammon player throws the dice and calculates afterwards, whereas the chess player plans, reflects and calculates his moves before making them. Chess therefore corrupts the heart more than backgammon does. Backgammon, however, was known among the Arabs, whereas chess became known only after the Muslim conquests: it originated in India and passed from the Indians to the Persians. This is why backgammon is mentioned in the hadith; otherwise, chess is worse when both are played for stakes or both without stakes. The answer to this question has been set out at length elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Concerning a man who plays chess and says that it is better than backgammon: is this correct? Is playing chess forbidden, whether for stakes or without stakes? What do the scholars say about it?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Playing chess is forbidden according to the vast majority of the scholars and imams of the Muslim community, just as backgammon is. It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever plays backgammon

---

## Page 15846

it is as though he had dipped his hand in the flesh and blood of a pig.” He also said:

“Whoever plays backgammon has disobeyed Allah and His Messenger.”

It is established that ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him, passed by some people playing chess and said: “What are these statues to which you are so devoted?” It is also reported that he overturned their board.

A group of the early generations said: “Chess is a form of gambling.” This is indeed as they said, for Allah has forbidden gambling. The scholars have unanimously agreed that playing backgammon and chess is forbidden when played for stakes, and that this constitutes the wagering and gambling that Allah has forbidden. Backgammon is forbidden according to the four imams, whether played for stakes or without stakes. Some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī, however, permitted it without stakes, believing that it would not then constitute gambling. Al-Shāfi‘ī himself, the majority of his followers, Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah and the other imams forbid it both with and without stakes.

Likewise, these imams—Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad and others—explicitly declared chess forbidden. They differed over which of the two was worse. Mālik and others said: “Chess is worse than backgammon.” Ahmad and others said: “Chess is less serious than backgammon.” For this reason, al-Shāfi‘ī withheld judgment concerning backgammon when it involved no forbidden elements. The source of uncertainty in this matter was that most people who play it do so for stakes, whereas chess is generally played without stakes. Some also thought that playing chess helped develop combat skills because it involved arranging two opposing forces.

The correct assessment is that, when both backgammon and chess are played for stakes, chess is worse than backgammon, for chess in that case is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. It is likewise forbidden

---

## Page 15847

by consensus when it involves anything forbidden, such as lying, a false oath, wrongdoing, an offence, speech that is not obligatory, or the like. The majority regard it as forbidden even when it is free of these prohibited elements, for it distracts from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer, and breeds enmity and hatred more than backgammon played for stakes does. When both are played for stakes, chess is worse in both cases. If, however, the stake comes from one of the two, that involves consuming wealth unjustly in a way that the other does not.

Allah Most High has mentioned gambling together with wine, sacrificial stones and divining arrows because they distract from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer. They also breed enmity and hatred. Indeed, excessive chess-playing veils the heart and distracts it from remembrance and prayer even more than wine does.

The Commander of the Faithful, ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, likened chess players to idol worshippers when he said:

“What are these statues to which you are so devoted?”

Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, likened a wine drinker to an idol worshipper in the hadith recorded in al-Musnad, in which he said:

“One who drinks wine is like one who worships an idol.”

As for the reports that Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr played chess, he explained his reason: al-Hajjāj sought to appoint him as a judge, so he played chess to discredit himself and thereby avoid the appointment. He considered serving under al-Hajjāj more harmful to his religion than playing chess. Actions are judged by intentions, and need may render permissible something even more strictly forbidden than this. This shows that they regarded playing chess as a reprehensible act, as is also reported from ‘Alī, Ibn ‘Umar and others.

For this reason, Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad and others said that a chess player should not be greeted with salām, because he openly displays disobedience. The two companions of Abū Hanīfah, however, said that he should be greeted.

---

## Page 15848

He was also asked—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—about the meaning of the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever plays backgammon is like someone who dips his hand into the flesh and blood of a pig.”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The statement:

“Whoever plays backgammon is like someone who dips his hand into the flesh and blood of a pig”

is an authentic hadith recorded by Muslim and others. Playing backgammon is forbidden according to the vast majority of scholars, even without stakes; playing it for stakes is forbidden by consensus.

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him—about playing with pigeons.

He replied:

Playing with pigeons is prohibited. The Sunan collections report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saw a man following a pigeon and said:

“A devil following a she-devil.”

Anyone who plays with pigeons and looks into people’s private quarters, or throws stones that land on his neighbours, must receive a disciplinary punishment that deters him from doing so, and must be prevented from continuing.
