This report from ʿUthmān is the strongest report from the Companions available to those who regard khulʿ as one irrevocable divorce. Yet, besides its weakness, a contradictory position is established from him through an authentic chain of transmission. The two cannot be reconciled, since doing so would conflict with the scriptural text and scholarly consensus.

---

## Page 15894

As for the reports from ʿAlī and Ibn Masʿūd, they are extremely weak. The report from ʿUmar is inconclusive and provides no evidence for this position. By contrast, the report from Ibn ʿAbbās that khulʿ is a separation, not a divorce, is among the most firmly established authentic reports, as agreed by scholars versed in transmitted reports. This lends further support to those who regard it as an annulment, such as Ahmad and others.

Those jurists who followed the reports from the Companions describing it as one irrevocable divorce assumed that those reports were authentic. They did not possess the expertise in scrutinizing reports and distinguishing authentic from weak transmissions that Ahmad and other specialists possessed. They therefore thought that the Companions who disagreed with Ibn ʿAbbās and others like him were more eminent than he and more numerous. They did not realize that no contrary position had been authentically established from any Companion. Moreover, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“O Allah, grant him understanding of the religion and teach him the interpretation of the Quran.”

What he deduced from the Quran on this question, and the evidence he drew from the Sunnah, reflected his consummate understanding of the religion and knowledge of Quranic interpretation. He also issued more legal opinions than any other Companion. Imam Ahmad was asked, “Which Companion issued the most legal opinions?” He replied, “Ibn ʿAbbās.”

He was the most knowledgeable and most learned in jurisprudence among the Companions of his generation. ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb would include him in consultation alongside the senior Companions—such as ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, Ibn Masʿūd, and others—something ʿUmar did not do for anyone else of his generation. Ibn Masʿūd said, “Had Ibn ʿAbbās reached our age, none of us would have been a tenth his equal”—that is, none would have attained a tenth of his level.

Those who transmitted his position on this question were among his most eminent students and those most familiar with his views, such as Tāwūs and ʿIkrimah. These two would visit him with his inner circle, unlike ʿAtāʾ, ʿAmr ibn Dīnār, and others like them, who would visit him with the general public. It is well known that a scholar’s closest associates

---

## Page 15895

possess knowledge from him that others do not, just as the closest Companions—such as the four Rightly Guided Caliphs, Ibn Masʿūd, ʿĀʾishah, Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, and others—possessed knowledge unavailable to those who did not enjoy the same closeness to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.

The point is that many people suppose that numerous Companions, or most of them, disagreed with Ibn ʿAbbās on this issue. They do not realize that the only positions authentically established from the Companions agree with his view rather than contradict it. Even supposing that some of them disagreed with him, disputes among them must be referred back to the Quran and Sunnah.

Those holding this position argue:

“The divorce for which Allah prescribed a limit of three is revocable divorce. Every divorce mentioned in the Quran concerning a woman whose marriage has been consummated is revocable, except the third divorce.”

Thus Ahmad said, according to one of his two opinions:

“I reflected upon the Quran and found that every divorce mentioned in it is revocable.”

They argue:

“Whoever divides the divorces counted toward the three into revocable and irrevocable has contradicted the Quran and Sunnah. Rather, any dissolution involving an irrevocable separation does not count toward the three divorces. If it is called an irrevocable divorce but is not counted toward the three, this conveys a sound meaning over which there is no dispute.”

They further argue:

“If khulʿ were divorce, it would not be permissible during menstruation, for Allah has forbidden divorcing a menstruating woman. Yet our opponents, or most of them, concede that khulʿ is permissible during menstruation. Moreover, the need for it may arise during menstruation.”

They also argue:

“Allah Most High made the woman unlawful for the man after the third divorce only as a punishment for him, so that he would not divorce without need. The basic rule concerning divorce is prohibition; it is permitted only to the extent needed, and three occasions suffice to meet that need. Likewise, breaking off relations is permitted for three days, mourning a death other than that of one’s husband for three days, and an emigrant’s stay in Mecca after completing his rites for three days. The basic rule concerning breaking off relations and an emigrant’s stay in Mecca is prohibition.”

---

## Page 15896

Those holding this position then differed over whether, for khulʿ to constitute an annulment, it must involve neither the wording of divorce nor an intention to divorce. They held three views:

The first is that it must involve neither the wording of divorce nor an intention to divorce. Thus, if someone effects khulʿ using the wording of divorce, or intends divorce thereby, it counts as one of the three divorces. This is the position of most later scholars of the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. They may hold that, whenever it involves neither explicit wording of divorce nor an intention to divorce, it is an annulment. Or they may hold that it constitutes an annulment only when expressed using the terms khulʿ, faskh (annulment), or mufādāh (release in return for compensation), to the exclusion of all other expressions, such as firāq (separation), sarāh (release), ibānah (severance), and the other expressions through which alone a man separates from his wife.

Yet Ibn ʿAbbās referred to it only as fidyah (ransom), firāq (separation), and khulʿ. He said, “Khulʿ is separation, not divorce.” Ibn ʿAbbās did not call it an annulment, nor is it called an “annulment” in the Quran or Sunnah. How, then, can the word “annulment” explicitly denote it, but not the word “separation”? Likewise, Ahmad ibn Hanbal most often calls it “a separation that is not divorce.” He sometimes calls it an “annulment,” because this term became prevalent in the usage of later scholars.

The second is that, when it is expressed without the wording of divorce—for example, using “khulʿ,” “release in return for compensation,” or “annulment”—it constitutes an annulment, whether or not divorce was intended thereby. This view has been mentioned by several scholars of the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

According to this position, does it constitute an annulment whenever explicit wording of divorce is absent, regardless of the expression or indirect wording used? Or is this restricted to the terms khulʿ, annulment, and release in return for compensation? There are two juristic views, corresponding to the two views under the first position.

---

## Page 15897

This view is more consistent with their legal principles than the preceding one. For when an expression explicitly denotes something in one legal context and is used again in that context, it cannot serve as an indirect expression for something else. Thus, according to the great majority of scholars, intending divorce through the wording of zihār (likening one’s wife to one’s mother in prohibition) does not effect divorce. This is what the Quran and Sunnah indicate.

Likewise, according to Ahmad, intending divorce through the word “unlawful” does not effect divorce, because it explicitly denotes zihār. This is especially so according to Ahmad’s principles. The terms “khulʿ,” “annulment,” and “ransom,” when accompanied by compensation, explicitly denote khulʿ. They therefore cannot serve as indirect expressions of divorce and cannot effect divorce under any circumstances. Moreover, the terms “khulʿ,” “release in return for compensation,” “annulment,” and “compensation” must either explicitly denote khulʿ, explicitly denote divorce, or indirectly denote both. If the first is maintained—and this is the correct view—these expressions do not effect divorce, even if divorce is intended.

If the second is maintained, it necessarily follows that “khulʿ,” “annulment,” and “release in return for compensation” are explicit expressions of divorce. They would therefore effect divorce when used on their own, just as the word “divorce” does. Yet no one has maintained this, nor has anyone counted them among the explicit expressions of divorce.

If it is said, “They explicitly denote divorce when accompanied by compensation,” the response is: This is invalid according to al-Shāfiʿī’s principles, since an expression that is not explicit, in his view, does not become explicit through the inclusion of compensation.

For this reason, al-Shāfiʿī and those scholars of Ahmad’s school who agree with him hold that a marriage cannot be contracted using expressions other than “giving in marriage” and “joining in marriage.” Other expressions are indirect; indirect expressions require intention, and intention requires attestation by witnesses, while marriage necessarily requires witnesses.

Thus, if someone says, “I have made her yours for a thousand,” “I have given her to you for a thousand,” or something similar, or “I have gifted her to you,” the inclusion of compensation is not treated as an indication that this constitutes marriage, since the words may mean transferring ownership of her person. Likewise, the expression “release in return for compensation” may mean ransoming someone from captivity. And if the term “annulment” constitutes divorce when accompanied by

---

## Page 15898

compensation, then it constitutes divorce without compensation as well. Yet none of al-Shāfiʿī’s followers has said that it explicitly denotes divorce without compensation; at most, it is an indirect expression. Although this view is closer to the truth than the first, it too is weak.

The third view

Khulʿ is an annulment, whatever wording is used, and does not count as one of the three divorces. Those who hold this view stipulate neither a particular expression nor the absence of an intention to divorce. This is what is transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās and his followers. It is also what is transmitted from Ahmad ibn Hanbal and his early followers concerning khulʿ, whether one expression or another is used—“divorce” or anything else. Indeed, their statements indicate that it is an annulment, whatever wording is used, more explicitly than the word “divorce” conveys its own exclusive meaning.

As for al-Shāfiʿī, he did not report that any of the early generations distinguished between the word “divorce” and other expressions. Rather, when discussing the position of Ibn ʿAbbās, his followers, and others, he reported that ʿIkrimah said:

“Any separation effected through payment is not divorce.”

Al-Shāfiʿī said:

“I think that those who do not regard it as divorce say this only when the word ‘divorce’ is not used.”

It was on this basis that Muhammad ibn Nasr, al-Tahāwī, and others stated that they knew of no disagreement concerning khulʿ effected with the word “divorce.” Yet it is well known that conjecture of this kind cannot serve as a basis for reporting the views of the early generations, or for setting aside their statements, their knowledge, and their clear proofs that all these expressions are treated alike. As for Ahmad, his statements clearly indicate that he does not make the particular wording a consideration or distinguish between one expression and another, and he follows Ibn ʿAbbās in this

---

## Page 15899

view and has followed his example.

Ahmad used to say:

“Beware of speaking on an issue without an imam to follow.”

His imam on this issue was Ibn ʿAbbās. Ahmad and others transmitted this view from Ibn ʿAbbās and his followers. It is thus clear that what matters, in their view, is that the woman offers compensation and requests separation. I have recorded their statements on this subject in the detailed discussion.

Furthermore, al-Bukhārī narrated in his Sahīh from Ibn ʿAbbās:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, spoke to Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās. He was the first man to conclude khulʿ during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. His wife had come to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said to him: ‘I find no fault with his character or religion, but I abhor disbelief after embracing Islam.’ She explained that she hated him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, asked her: ‘Will you return his garden to him?’ She replied: ‘Yes.’ He said: ‘Accept the garden and divorce her once.’”

It was Ibn ʿAbbās who narrated these words from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He also narrated:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed her to wait for one menstrual period to establish that she was not pregnant, and said: ‘No waiting period is required of you.’”

He also ruled that the divorce practised by the people of Yemen, which they called “release for compensation,” did not count as one of the three divorces. Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿd had said to him: “Most divorces among the people of Yemen are releases for compensation.” He replied: “Release for compensation is not divorce; it is merely separation, but people have given it the wrong name.”

The questioner thus informed him that their divorce was release for compensation. This plainly indicates that it was effected using the word “divorce”; at the very least, it encompasses both the word “divorce” and other expressions. Ibn ʿAbbās gave an unqualified answer

---

## Page 15900

and applied it generally, without excluding release for compensation effected through the word “divorce” or prescribing any particular wording for it, despite knowing that it was effected through the word “divorce” more often than through other expressions. Indeed, ordinary people do not know the terms “annulment,” “khulʿ,” and the like unless someone teaches them, nor do they distinguish between one expression and another. Many of them, when told, “Grant your wife khulʿ,” divorce her without compensation and say, “I have granted her khulʿ.” They do not distinguish between the expressions unless the intended purpose of one of them is explained to them.

Among the people of Yemen, to this day, a woman says to her husband, “Divorce me.” He replies, “Offer me compensation.” She then offers him her dowry or something else, and he divorces her. This is how most of their divorces take place. Ibn ʿAbbās ruled for them that this was release for compensation and separation, not divorce. He also restored a woman to her husband after two divorces and one release for compensation. Thus, what Ibn ʿAbbās transmitted, his legal ruling, and his reasoning from the Quran all support this view.

Just as this follows from Ahmad’s statements and principles, it also follows from the principles of Islamic law and the texts of the Lawgiver. For contracts are judged by their purposes and meanings, not their wording. If two expressions serve the same purpose, their legal rulings cannot differ. If a person could choose to make the same transaction a divorce or not a divorce, that would amount to manipulation, which is invalid.

They have raised the following objection: if the husband of an emancipated woman gives her the choice, she may divorce herself, or she may annul the marriage on account of her emancipation. They say: “She therefore has a choice between the two. Likewise, in return for compensation, a husband has the power either to effect an annulment or to effect a divorce.”

This analogy is weak. If this woman divorces herself, the divorce is only revocable; she therefore has the choice between

---

## Page 15901

effecting an irrevocable separation and effecting a revocable divorce. This is sound, just as a husband has the choice between granting her release for compensation, thereby effecting an irrevocable separation, and divorcing her without compensation by a revocable divorce. What contradicts the established principles is that he should have the power to effect an irrevocable separation and then, as he wishes, make it either an annulment or a divorce, although the purpose in both cases is the same—an irrevocable separation—with the choice of whether it constitutes divorce left to him. This is precisely the objectionable position: it entails that an individual may, at will, make the very same transaction either a divorce or something other than divorce, although its purpose is identical in both cases.

Furthermore, what is left to the individual is the intent behind his actions and the ends he seeks; legal rulings, however, belong to the Lawgiver. The Lawgiver assigns different rulings to different actions because their intended purposes differ. When a man’s intended purpose is the same, he cannot choose whether the legal ruling applies or not. It is well known that the intended purpose of the separation is one and the same, without any difference.

Furthermore, the meaning of securing release for compensation is present whenever she asks him to separate from her in return for compensation. Allah has attached the ruling of khulʿ to whatever constitutes such a payment for release. Wherever this meaning is present, it is the khulʿ mentioned in the Book of Allah Most High.

Furthermore, Allah has made the right to take one’s wife back an inseparable consequence of divorce in the Quran. Allah Most High never mentions the divorce of a woman whose marriage has been consummated without affirming this right. Thus, if release for compensation were divorce,

---

## Page 15902

it would entail the right to take her back. This would negate the meaning of securing release for compensation, and it is contrary to consensus. For we know of those who have said that, in khulʿ concluded without additional stipulations, the husband owns the compensation and retains the right to take her back. Some, however, have said that it is not binding: if he wishes, he may return the compensation and take her back.

The scholars disagreed over whether it is valid to stipulate the right to take her back while receiving compensation. There are two opinions, both reported from Mālik. The position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī, also held by the later scholars of Ahmad’s school, is that combining the two is invalid. Some of these scholars uphold the obligation to pay compensation but reject the right to take her back; others affirm that right but invalidate the compensation. These are two opinions within the schools of Ahmad and al-Shāfiʿī, although no explicit statement from Ahmad on this matter has been transmitted.

Reasoning by analogy from Ahmad’s school would establish its validity with this stipulation, just as when a woman offers money on condition that she be given control over her own marital status. He explicitly permitted that. Moreover, his governing principle is that contractual stipulations are permissible unless evidence from the revealed law establishes their invalidity. In his view, an invalid stipulation is not simply one that departs from what a contract would ordinarily entail in the absence of stipulations. Rather, it is one that conflicts with the Lawgiver’s purpose and contradicts His ruling, such as stipulating that the right of patronage belong to someone other than the emancipator, or that the seller retain the right to intercourse even though ownership belongs to the purchaser, and similar stipulations.

Furthermore, distinguishing between one wording and another in khulʿ is an unprecedented position, unknown from any of the early generations: the Companions, their Successors, or those who followed them. Al-Shāfiʿī—may Allah be pleased with him—did not transmit it from anyone. Rather, he stated that he thought the Companions made such a distinction. Clearly, this does not constitute transmitting the opinion of any member of the early generations.

Al-Shāfiʿī mentioned this in Ahkām al-Qurʾān (The Legal Rulings of the Quran), where he preferred the position that khulʿ is a divorce, not an annulment. He therefore did not accept the latter position because of what he considered an inconsistency among its proponents: namely, that when expressed in one wording, they treat it as an irrevocable divorce counting as one of the three

---

## Page 15903

and, when expressed in another wording, as not counting as one of the three. Because of this perceived inconsistency, he refrained from preferring that view.

Yet neither he nor anyone else reported this inconsistency from any of the early scholars who held that view, or from their followers, such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal and the early scholars of his school. Only some later scholars of Ahmad’s school maintained this distinction, having found that others distinguished between the wording of divorce and other expressions. Some of those scholars, such as Muhammad ibn Nasr and al-Tahāwī, stated that they knew of no disagreement on this matter.

Only some later scholars of Ahmad’s school maintained this distinction. What is transmitted from the early scholars, without exception, is either that khulʿ is an irrevocable separation, not a divorce, or that it is a divorce. I have not seen any statement from any of them distinguishing between one wording and another, or requiring the absence of an intention to divorce. Rather, they would say, as ʿIkrima did, “Any separation rendered valid by payment is not a divorce,” and use similar expressions. These show that they considered the purpose of the contract, not a particular wording. Distinguishing between one wording and another contradicts both established principles and the scriptural texts.

Those who regard all such separations as divorce invoke the invalidity of this distinction to reject the position supported by the Quran and Sunnah. Indeed, adopting this distinction would provide one of the strongest arguments against the position of those who consider khulʿ an annulment. This is why al-Shāfiʿī—may Allah be pleased with him—refrained from preferring that position: it appeared to him that its proponents made this distinction.

Moreover, the Sunan collections relate:

“Fayrūz al-Daylamī accepted Islam while married to two sisters. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him, ‘Divorce whichever of them you wish.’ He said, ‘I then turned to the one I had married first and parted from her.’”

This is a sound hadith. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus commanded him to divorce one of them. According to al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, this separation is irrevocable and is not

---

## Page 15904

counted as one of the three divorces. This shows that the word “divorce” can encompass an annulment that does not count as one of the three divorces.

It also shows that if someone embraces Islam while married to more than four women and says, “I have divorced this woman,” this constitutes separation from her and the choice to retain the other woman. This contradicts the position of some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, who maintain that if he says regarding one of the two, “Divorce her,” this constitutes choosing her. They argue that divorce can apply only to a wife. This position contradicts both the Sunnah and reason. A man who divorces a woman has no desire for her and wishes to part from her; how, then, can he be choosing her and wishing to retain her? What led them into this was their assumption that the word “divorce” is used only for a divorce that counts as one of the three. This is a false assumption, contrary to Islamic law, linguistic usage, and scholarly consensus.

Furthermore, the Lawgiver has not prescribed a particular wording for divorce. Rather, according to the Companions, the early generations, and scholars generally, divorce takes effect through any wording capable of conveying it. Only some later Shīʿī and Zāhirī scholars disputed this; no disagreement on it is known among the early generations. Thus, if a man says, “I have parted from you,” “I have released you,” or “I have let you go,” intending divorce, it takes effect. The same applies to all other indirect expressions.

If these indirect expressions are used in exchange for compensation—for example, if she says to him, “Release me,” “Let me go in return for a thousand,” “Part from me in return for a thousand,” or “Leave me in return for a thousand”—what difference is there between this and her saying, “Accept a thousand in ransom for my release,” “Grant me khulʿ in return for a thousand,” or “Annul my marriage in return for a thousand”? The same applies to all other indirect expressions. Moreover, when the terms “khulʿ” and “annulment” are used without compensation, with the intention of divorce, a revocable divorce takes effect. They are therefore among the indirect expressions of divorce. What distinction, then, can be drawn between one indirect expression and another?

---

## Page 15905

Scholars have also differed over the validity of khulʿ without compensation. There are two views, both reported from Ahmad.

The first holds that it is invalid. This accords with the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī and is the view preferred by most of Ahmad’s followers. The second holds that it is valid, as is the well-known position in Mālik’s school and the view preferred by al-Khiraqī. According to this view, the husband must intend divorce when using the term khulʿ. It then constitutes an irrevocable divorce, not an annulment, according to both reports. Ahmad—may Allah have mercy on him—expressly stated this. For if he allowed it to constitute an annulment without compensation, a man would have the power to annul his marriage on his own initiative without its counting as one of his three divorces. No scholar holds this view.

If that were permissible, it would necessarily mean allowing divorce without any numerical limit, as was the practice in the pre-Islamic period. In the early days of Islam, too, there was no limit to the number of divorces. If using the term “annulment,” or another expression, could effect separation without counting as one of the three divorces, that expression would be used instead of the word “divorce.” In substance, this would mean divorce without any numerical limit. This is untenable.

If it is argued, “It is an irrevocable divorce,” the reply is that this is even more untenable. For if the position is that a husband has the power to pronounce only a revocable divorce, not an irrevocable one, this argument fails. If, however, the position is that he has the power to effect an irrevocable divorce, then allowing him to do so using the term “annulment” without its counting as one of the three divorces would entail the very consequence that must be avoided: he could divorce his wife whenever he wished without its counting as one of his three divorces.

For this reason, scholars have not disputed that using the term khulʿ without compensation and without a request from the wife does not constitute an annulment. Their disagreement concerns only the case in which a woman asks her husband to grant her a single irrevocable divorce without compensation: is she entitled to this? There are two views.

---

## Page 15906

For scholars hold three views concerning irrevocable divorce.

The first is that a husband may pronounce either an irrevocable or a revocable divorce as he wishes, on the grounds that the right to take his wife back belongs to him: he may retain that right or waive it. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and one view reported from Ahmad. I believe it is also a view reported from Mālik.

The second is that he has no power to pronounce an irrevocable divorce on his own initiative, but may do so if she asks him to make the separation irrevocable. This is a well-known position reported from Mālik and one view reported from Ahmad, preferred by al-Khiraqī.

The third is that he has no power to make the separation irrevocable without compensation, whether she requests it or not; he may do so only in return for compensation. This is the position of most jurists versed in hadith. It is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and the apparent position of Ahmad’s school, followed by the majority of his adherents. It is also the view of Ishāq, Abū Thawr, Ibn al-Mundhir, Ibn Khuzaymah, Dāwūd and others. Most of the reliably transmitted reports from the majority of the Companions support it. This view is also supported by the Quran and Sunnah. For Allah has prescribed divorce only as revocable, and in Allah’s Book there is no irrevocable divorce that counts as one of the three except in return for compensation, not without it. Rather, every irrevocable separation falls outside the three divorces.

Furthermore, the imams agree that khulʿ and divorce are valid when expressed in a language other than Arabic. It is well known that non-Arabic languages have no wording that, when compensation is involved, distinguishes khulʿ from a divorce that is not khulʿ. The distinction rests solely on what is particular to khulʿ: the inclusion of compensation and the wife’s request for separation.

The word for divorce may also be used in relation to something other than a woman, as when people say, “I have renounced the world,” or “I have renounced your affection.” Even when used in relation to a woman, it may mean release from something other than her husband, as when one says, “You are released from bonds,” or “You are released from worries and sorrows.”

---

## Page 15907

If he explicitly qualifies the wording of divorce in this way, no divorce takes effect, without doubt. If he intends that meaning without expressing the qualification, his word is accepted as a matter between him and Allah, although whether it is accepted in court is disputed.

Thus, if he qualifies the wording of divorce by saying, “You are divorced in return for one thousand,” and she says, “I accept”; or she says, “Divorce me in return for one thousand,” and he says, “I divorce you,” this is a divorce qualified by compensation. It is not divorce in the unqualified sense used in Allah’s Book. Allah made that divorce revocable, prescribed for it a waiting period of three cycles, and set its number at three, thereby establishing three rulings for it.

Divorce in return for compensation, however, is not revocable, as established by the revealed text and scholarly consensus. Nor, according to the Sunnah, does the woman observe a waiting period of three cycles. It must therefore not be counted among the three divorces. This is because it does not fall within the meaning of “divorce” when that term is used without qualification; rather, it is described as divorce only with a qualification. Similarly, an oath expressed in the form of a vow is called a “vow of obstinacy and anger.” It is called a vow with a qualification because its wording is that of a vow, although in reality it belongs to the category of oaths, not vows, according to the Companions, the majority of the early generations, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others.

Likewise, the word “water,” when used without qualification, does not include semen, although semen may be called water with a qualification, as in the words of the Most High:

“He was created from gushing water, emerging from between the backbone and the ribs.”

Similarly, the term “leather sock,” when used without qualification, does not include one that has been cut down, although it may be called a “cut-down leather sock.” Thus, a cut-down leather sock is not included in the expression “wiping over the two leather socks”

---

## Page 15908

nor under the prohibition on wearing leather socks while in ihrām, according to the sounder scholarly view. This is why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, initially instructed the person in ihrām to cut down his leather socks: a cut-down leather sock is not a leather sock in the unqualified sense. Later, at Arafāt, he permitted wearing trousers and leather socks without requiring that the trousers’ seams be opened or the leather socks cut down.

Trousers with their seams opened and leather socks cut down in this way do not fall within the unqualified meanings of “trousers” and “leather socks.” Likewise, the unqualified term “sale” does not encompass the sale of wine, carrion or swine, although these transactions may be called sales when qualified accordingly. Similarly, “faith,” when used without qualification, refers only to faith in Allah and His Messenger. When qualified, however, Allah Most High says:

“Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scripture, who believe in superstition and false gods?”

Such belief is not included in “faith” in its unqualified sense. Likewise, “glad tidings,” when used without qualification, means conveying news that brings joy. When qualified, however, the Most High says:

“So give them tidings of a painful punishment.”

There are many such examples. Thus, “divorce,” when used without qualification in Allah’s Book, refers to divorce pronounced by the husband without compensation, in which he retains the right to take his wife back. Where compensation is involved, he has no right to take her back, and this does not fall within the unqualified meaning of divorce. Rather, it is a ransom by which the woman secures her release from her husband

---

## Page 15909

just as a female captive ransoms herself from captivity. This ransom does not count towards the three divorces, whether expressed as release for compensation, annulment, ransom, release, separation, divorce, irrevocable separation, or any other formulation.

For this reason, the four imams and the majority of scholars permit a third party to provide it. A third party may secure her release for compensation, just as he may ransom a female captive or offer compensation to a slave’s master to emancipate him. This should therefore be conditional upon his intending to free her from her husband’s bondage for her benefit, just as he would ransom a captive.

There is a view within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad that, if this release is considered an annulment, it cannot validly be arranged by a third party. They argue that it would then constitute rescission by mutual agreement, which cannot validly involve a third party. This is the view mentioned by Abū al-Maʿālī and others belonging to the Khurāsānī tradition. The correct position in both schools, however, is that, according to the view that it constitutes annulment, it remains an annulment even when arranged by a third party, as jurists from both schools have expressly stated. Although the author of Sharh al-Wajīz did not mention this, leading scholars of the Iraqi tradition did, including Abū Ishāq al-Shīrāzī in his Khilāf, among others.

This is because they treated it as analogous to ransoming a captive or paying to emancipate a slave, rather than to rescission by mutual agreement. Its purpose is to end the husband’s dominion over the woman so that she becomes free of his bondage once more; its purpose is not to transfer ownership to her. It therefore resembles emancipating a slave or freeing a captive, not mutually rescinding a sale. Accordingly, the imams agree that it is permissible for less than the stipulated dowry, while most scholars permit amounts exceeding the dowries. It is also permissible for compensation of a different kind from the dowry. Rescission by mutual agreement is not like this; rather,

---

## Page 15910

the purpose of rescission by mutual agreement is to return the exchanged considerations. If we consider taking more than her dowry disliked or forbidden, this is because the standard compensation for her release from the husband’s dominion is the amount stipulated in the marriage contract. Marital sexual rights cannot be sold, given away, or inherited as property can, or as rights of use can be leased, lent, or inherited. Trading and leasing property are permissible according to the scriptural texts and scholarly consensus.

As for merely trading in rights of use—for example, renting a house and subletting it for a higher rent without carrying out any work on it—the scholars hold two views, both reported from Ahmad. The better-known view reported from him is that this is permissible; it is also the position of most scholars, including Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. The second is that it is impermissible, as Abū Hanīfa held. Its proponents argue that it constitutes profit from something for which one bears no liability.

The first view is more correct, because the lessee does bear the risk of losing these benefits. That is, once the leased property has been delivered to him, if he does not use it, its benefits lapse at his expense as their owner. This differs from the destruction of the leased property itself, which is comparable to fruit perishing before it ripens.

The point here is that the permissibility of trading in rights of use, which can be inherited, is itself disputed. What, then, of marital sexual rights, which can neither be given away nor inherited according to the scriptural texts and scholarly consensus? It was the people of the pre-Islamic era who used to inherit such rights, and Allah abolished that practice. Thus, if a husband wished to part with his wife and marry her to another man in order to take her dowry, he would have no right to do so. If intercourse took place with her on grounds of presumed lawfulness, the dowry would belong to her, not to him. Hence the prohibition on taking more than the dowry.

If release for compensation is likened to rescission by mutual agreement, rescission in each contract is governed by the nature of that contract. These matters are discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 15911

The view we have mentioned—that release for compensation is a rescission effecting an irrevocable separation, whatever wording is used—is the correct view, supported by the scriptural texts and legal principles. Accordingly, if a man separates from his wife for compensation several times, he may marry her again, whether he used the word “divorce” or some other expression.

If it is said, “The word ‘divorce’ explicitly denotes one of the three divorces, so it cannot serve as an indirect expression for release for compensation,” the answer is: only the unqualified expression has that explicit meaning. When a qualification removes it from that meaning, it explicitly denotes the qualified meaning instead. For example, he might say, “You are released from bonds,” or “from worries and sorrows.” This explicitly denotes that release, not divorce from marriage.

If he says, “You are divorced in exchange for one thousand,” and she says, “I accept,” the expression is qualified by the compensation and explicitly denotes release for compensation. It cannot possibly count as one of the three divorces. If he intends it to count as one of the three, he has intended by the expression a meaning it cannot bear—just as if he intended release for compensation to make her unlawful for him until she marries another husband. His intention to produce that legal effect is invalid; likewise, his intention that it count as one of the three divorces is invalid.

The same applies if he intends divorce by zihār (declaring one’s wife as forbidden as one’s mother), or a deferred divorce by īlā’ (an oath to abstain from marital intercourse), even though the people of the pre-Islamic era regarded both zihār and īlā’ as divorce. Allah and His Messenger invalidated that practice. In the case of īlā’, He ruled that the husband must either retain his wife honorably or release her graciously, with a waiting period of four months. In the case of zihār, He ruled that if the husband goes back on his declaration—as He has stated—he must make expiation before intimate contact; no divorce takes effect through it.

For this reason, the view of anyone who treats īlā’ as a deferred divorce, or treats a declaration of unlawfulness equivalent to zihār as divorce, is the weaker view and bears some resemblance to their former practice, unlike one who

---

## Page 15912

distinguishes between the true nature of zihār, īlā’, and divorce. Such a person understands the limits of what Allah has revealed to His Messenger, neither including within those limits what falls outside them nor excluding what falls within them.

Likewise, “release for compensation” has a distinct nature that sets it apart from the three divorces. It is therefore impermissible to subsume divorce under release for compensation, or release for compensation under divorce. Even if someone describes one using the wording of the other, or intends one to produce the legal effect of the other, this is like intending, through a single divorce or release for compensation, to make his wife unlawful for him until she marries another husband. The intention to produce this legal effect is invalid; likewise, his intention that it count as one of the three divorces is invalid. Allah has only made her unlawful for him until she marries another husband after the third divorce. Whoever intends this legal effect through anything other than that divorce has intended something contrary to the ruling of Allah and His Messenger.

Likewise, whoever intends an irrevocable separation to use up one of the three divorces has intended something contrary to the ruling of Allah and His Messenger, and he has no right to do so. If he intends either of these effects out of ignorance of the ruling of Allah and His Messenger, he is like someone who intends, through any other contract, something contrary to their ruling. He is thus ignorant of the Sunnah and must be brought back to it, as ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb said: “Refer matters arising from ignorance back to the Sunnah.” Likewise, a group of the early generations said concerning someone who pronounces three divorces in a single utterance: “He is ignorant of the Sunnah and must be brought back to it.”

The Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to the husband granting release for compensation,

“And divorce her once,”

permit him a single divorce in exchange for compensation and forbid him from adding any more. It has already been explained that the Quran and Sunnah indicate that “divorce in accordance with the Sunnah” consists of pronouncing a single divorce, then either taking her back or leaving her until her waiting period expires, and that whenever he divorces her

---

## Page 15913

twice or three times before taking her back or entering into a new marriage contract, this constitutes a prohibited, innovated form of divorce according to the majority of earlier and later scholars. This is the position of Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and their followers, as well as Ahmad’s later position and the view preferred by most of his followers. Whether a prohibited divorce takes legal effect is disputed among earlier and later scholars.

This has been discussed at length elsewhere, along with the authentic report in the Sahīh from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said:

“During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the time of Abū Bakr, and the early part of ‘Umar’s caliphate, triple divorce counted as one. When people increasingly resorted to it, ‘Umar said: ‘People have acted hastily in a matter in which they had been allowed time. Why not enforce it against them?’ So he enforced it against them.”

We have discussed this hadith and what people have said about it at length elsewhere. We also mentioned the other hadith that agrees with it, reported by Imam Ahmad and others through Muhammad ibn Ishāq, from Dāwūd ibn al-Husayn, from ‘Ikrimah, from Ibn ‘Abbās:

“Rukānah divorced his wife three times. When he came to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—asked him: ‘In one sitting or in several?’ He replied: ‘In a single sitting.’ So he restored her to him.”

Ahmad ibn Hanbal authenticated this hadith and explained that it is more authentic than the version of Rukānah’s hadith stating that he divorced her ‘definitively,’ and that:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—required him to swear: ‘Did you intend only one divorce?’ He replied: ‘I intended only one divorce.’ So he restored her to him.”

The narrators of this latter report are of unknown standing: neither their integrity nor their accuracy in retaining what they narrated is known. For this reason, it was judged weak by Ahmad, Abū ‘Ubayd, Ibn Hazm, and others

---

## Page 15914

among the leading hadith scholars. By contrast, the report concerning triple divorce has a good chain of transmission. It is narrated by Ibn ‘Abbās and agrees with his hadith recorded in the Sahīh. Its transmitters were scholars versed in jurisprudence who acted upon its implications: Tāwūs, ‘Ikrimah, and Ibn Ishāq ruled that triple divorce counts as one. Some of them said: “This man has departed from the Sunnah and must therefore be brought back into conformity with it.”

As for Abū Dāwūd’s preference in his Sunan for the version mentioning “definitive” divorce, this was only because he did not mention this report of Dāwūd ibn al-Husayn, from ‘Ikrimah, from Ibn ‘Abbās. Instead, he mentioned another chain from ‘Ikrimah containing an unknown narrator. Thus, he preferred one unknown narrator’s report over another’s. This version narrated by Dāwūd ibn al-Husayn, however, takes precedence over that one by agreement of the specialists. Yet this chain of transmission did not reach most scholars, just as the hadith of Tāwūs is unknown to many jurists—indeed, to most of them.

This has been discussed at length elsewhere. The discussion of the rulings reported from ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, and other Companions, holding all three divorces binding, explained that this occurred when people began committing the prohibited act frequently and openly, so it was imposed as a punishment upon them. The scholarly discussion of “enforcing triple divorce” was also addressed: did those Companions who enforced it do so because it was a binding legal ruling established by the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or as a punishment for the open and widespread commission of wrongdoing?

If it is regarded as a punishment, does the ground for imposing it remain permanently, never ceasing, or does it vary with circumstances? It was explained that this cannot be a binding legal ruling or a binding punishment established through scholarly judgment. At most, it is a permissible but weaker scholarly judgment, or a temporary punishment

---

## Page 15915

sanctioned by Islamic law. Punishment applies only to someone who committed the act knowing that it was prohibited. As for someone who did not know it was prohibited and repented upon learning this, he does not deserve punishment. It is therefore impermissible to hold him bound by three divorces pronounced together; rather, only one divorce is binding upon him.

This applies when the divorce is without compensation. If it is in return for compensation, however, it is “a release for compensation,” as explained earlier. Here too, he is not permitted to pronounce three divorces in return for compensation. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded that only one divorce, and no more, be pronounced in return for compensation, just as only one divorce, and no more, may be pronounced without it. Divorce in return for compensation, however, is a qualified form of divorce: it is a release for compensation and an irrevocable separation. It is not the divorce referred to without qualification in the Book of Allah, for that is revocable divorce.

Thus, if he pronounces three divorces together in return for compensation, and the view is taken that three divorces without compensation count as one, whereas divorce in return for compensation is a release that does not count toward the three, then this separation is a release for compensation that does not count toward the three. The man who has separated from her may marry her under a new contract, and that separation in return for compensation is not counted against him toward the three divorces. The divorce is therefore not binding upon him, since it is prohibited: the two additional divorces are prohibited, whereas the single divorce is permissible. However, of the three, a single divorce in return for compensation is recommended, because it is a release for compensation, not one of the divorces that Allah has limited to three. Rather, he may marry the woman with all three divorces still available to him.

The “essence of the matter” is that irrevocable separation is of two kinds: “major irrevocable separation,” which results from pronouncing the three divorces that make the woman unlawful to him until she marries another husband; and “minor irrevocable separation,” whereby the woman ceases to be his wife, although he may marry her under a contract

---

## Page 15916

newly concluded, during the waiting period or after it. Release for compensation thus results in minor, not major, irrevocable separation. Major irrevocable separation resulting from three divorces occurs when he pronounces them in the permissible, prescribed manner: he divorces her once during a period of purity in which he has not had intercourse with her, or divorces her once when her pregnancy has become evident, and leaves her until her waiting period ends, then marries her under a new contract. He may also take her back during the waiting period. Once he has remarried her or taken her back, he may divorce her a second time in the prescribed manner.

If he pronounces three divorces in a single utterance, or in separate utterances without an intervening resumption of the marriage or a new marriage contract, this is prohibited according to the majority. This is the position of Mālik and, according to the well-known report from him, Abū Hanīfa. Indeed, even pronouncing the three divorces in separate periods of purity, without an intervening resumption of the marriage or a new contract, is prohibited according to Mālik and, according to the well-known report from him, Ahmad.

If he pronounces three divorces in a prohibited manner, do three divorces take effect, or only one? There are two well-known views among the early and later scholars, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

If the view is taken that the three do not take effect, then he has no power to bring about major irrevocable separation through a single utterance. Since he has no such power, she may not offer him compensation for something he has no power to effect. Thus, if she offers him compensation for the three prohibited divorces, she is offering him compensation for something that he is forbidden to do and has no power to effect. If he pronounces them, only what is permissible takes effect; and what is permissible in return for compensation is minor, not major, irrevocable separation.

Indeed, even if he has already divorced her twice and she offers him compensation for separation, whether expressed using the word “divorce” or otherwise, a third divorce does not take effect according to our position that separation in return for compensation is a dissolution resulting in minor irrevocable separation. If he separates from her in this situation, whether using the word “divorce” or otherwise, this brings about “minor irrevocable separation,” which is dissolution

---

## Page 15917

rather than major irrevocable separation. He may marry the woman under a new contract. But if she expressly offers compensation for the prohibited third divorce, intending to become unlawful to him until she marries another husband, then she has offered compensation for something other than minor irrevocable separation. This resembles her offering compensation for release on condition that he retain the right to take her back. His stipulating the right to take her back in a release for compensation resembles her stipulating, in that release, a divorce that renders her unlawful to him. In this situation, he has the power to pronounce the third divorce that renders her unlawful to him, just as he previously had the power to pronounce a revocable divorce. And Allah, glorified be He, knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

Praise be to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, with no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant them abundant peace.

Section: Separation That Counts toward the Three Divorces and Separation That Does Not

The division of separation into these two categories is, as far as I believe, agreed upon among Muslims. For if something were to arise between the spouses that made marriage between them permanently unlawful, without either of them choosing it

---

## Page 15918

as in the case of affinity through marriage, the resulting separation would count as a divorce. However, scholars have disagreed about many kinds of “separation,” such as “release for compensation,” “separation due to a difference in religion,” and “separation on account of a defect in the husband,” such as amputation of the penis, impotence, or similar conditions: does such a separation count as one of the three divorces or not? This disagreement concerns “refining” the “criterion distinguishing” divorce from other forms of separation. The positions of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad in this area are broader in scope than those of Abū Hanīfa and Mālik.

Thus, differing opinions are reported from both of them concerning release for compensation: is it a divorce or not? The well-known position reported from Ahmad is that it is not a divorce, in agreement with Ibn ʿAbbās, Tāwūs, and others. This is also one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two opinions. However, some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad distinguished between a release expressed using the word “divorce” and one expressed otherwise. If the word “divorce” is used, it counts toward the three divorces. If another expression is used with the intention of divorce, it is likewise a divorce. If neither the wording nor the intention of divorce is present, that is the disputed case.

This matter requires careful examination, just as the criterion underlying the distinction requires precise clarification. For this position rests on two premises: “The first” is that the word “divorce” cannot be used with an intention other than a divorce that counts toward the three. “The second” is a precise definition of the meaning of release for compensation that distinguishes it from a divorce that counts toward the three. Otherwise, if it is assumed that the word “divorce” can signify a divorce that counts toward the three, but can also convey another meaning, and he intends

---

## Page 15919

that meaning, no divorce counting toward the three takes effect thereby.

The jurists have stated that if a man says, “You are divorced,” intending release from bonds or divorce from a previous husband, no divorce takes effect between him and Allah. Whether his explanation is accepted in court is subject to two well-known opinions, both reported from Ahmad. Thus, the word “divorce,” when applied to a woman, may mean a divorce counting toward the three or something else.

The word “divorce” may also be applied to something other than a woman, as in the statement attributed to ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him:

“O worldly life, I have divorced you three times, never to take you back.”

Likewise, in the verse attributed to al-Shāfiʿī:

“Go, for your affection is divorced from mine.”

The objection to the foregoing restriction rests on what the Sunnah establishes: the word “divorce,” when applied to a woman, may signify separation without counting toward the three divorces. Thus, Imam Ahmad and the compilers of the three Sunan collections—Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasāʾī, and Ibn Mājah—narrated through Yazīd ibn Abī Habīb, from Abū Wahb al-Jayshānī, from al-Dahhāk ibn Fayrūz, that his father said:

“I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I have embraced Islam while married to two sisters.’ He replied, ‘Divorce whichever of them you wish.’”

This is Abū Dāwūd’s wording. He said: “Yahyā ibn Maʿīn narrated to us: Wahb ibn Jarīr narrated to us from his father, who said: ‘I heard Yahyā ibn Ayyūb narrating from Yazīd ibn Abī Habīb.’”

Abū Dāwūd also narrated through Hushaym and ʿĪsā ibn al-Mukhtār, from Ibn Abī Laylā, from Khumaysa ibn al-Shamardal, that Qays ibn al-Hārith said:

“I embraced Islam while married to eight women. I mentioned this to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and he said, ‘Choose four of them.’”

---

## Page 15920

Ibn Mājah also narrated it.

Ahmad, al-Tirmidhī, and Ibn Mājah narrated the following, with the wording being Ibn Mājah’s:

Ibn ʿUmar said: “Ghaylān embraced Islam while married to ten women. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him, ‘Keep four of them.’”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “I heard Muhammad say, ‘This version is not reliably preserved. The correct version is what Shuʿayb and others narrated from al-Zuhrī, who said: “I was told on the authority of Muhammad ibn Suwayd that Ghaylān …”’”—and he related the report.

Imam Ahmad’s wording includes:

“During ʿUmar’s caliphate, he divorced his wives and divided his wealth among his sons. When this reached ʿUmar, he said: ‘I suspect that Satan, while eavesdropping, heard of your death and planted the thought in your mind. Perhaps you have only a little time left. By Allah, you shall take back your wives and reclaim your wealth, or I shall ensure that they inherit from you, and I shall order your grave to be pelted with stones, just as the grave of Abū Righāl was pelted.’”

Mālik narrated this hadith in al-Muwattaʾ from al-Zuhrī with an incomplete chain to the Prophet. Al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad also narrated it in their respective Musnads through Muhammad ibn Jaʿfar and others, from Maʿmar, from al-Zuhrī, with an incomplete chain to the Prophet.

However, Imam Ahmad and others explained that this was among the reports in which Maʿmar erred when he came to Basra. He narrated it to them from memory, and Maʿmar would make mistakes when narrating from memory. Thus, the Basran narrators, such as Muhammad ibn Jaʿfar—Ghundar—and others, transmitted the erroneous version from him. His students who heard him narrate from his writings, such as ʿAbd al-Razzāq and others, transmitted it correctly.

Thus, in the hadith of Fayrūz, in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him:

“Divorce whichever of the two you wish,”

the intended meaning is not a divorce counting toward the three, according to al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others. Rather, the intended meaning is

---

## Page 15921

a separation that does not count toward the three divorces. He is not required to divorce her using the explicit formula for such a divorce; rather, according to them, he separates from her without using the word “divorce.” As for using that word, they have a discussion concerning it which we shall mention, Allah willing.

The same applies to what is reported in the hadith of Ghaylān:

“Keep four and part with the rest.”

He is not required to separate from them in a manner that counts toward the three divorces. The jurists among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad have differed …

There are several grounds demonstrating that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not intend that he divorce her using the explicit formula for a divorce counting toward the three, but intended separation instead.

First: In the other hadith, he said:

“Choose four of them.”

This indicates that choosing four of them is sufficient, without any need to initiate a divorce of the remaining women. Had separating from them counted toward the three divorces, he would have needed to perform an act that effects divorce. This is like saying, “By Allah, I shall divorce one of my two wives.” He must then actually effect a divorce of one of them. If he merely said, “I choose this one,” that alone would not constitute a divorce of the other—unless, of course, it is argued that such words could effect a divorce of the other wife when accompanied by that intention.

Second: Any wives beyond four are forbidden to him by Islamic law, and a woman who is forbidden to him by Islamic law does not require a divorce. However, since the woman who is forbidden has not been individually specified, he has the authority to determine which one she is.

---

## Page 15922

Third: It may be said that Allah has described in His Book the distinctive features of divorce, which are absent from this separation. Allah Most High says:

“Divorced women shall wait for three cycles,”

up to His words:

“And their husbands have a greater right to take them back during that period.”

He thus gives the husband of a divorced woman the greater right to take her back during her waiting period. Yet he cannot choose any of the wives beyond four during their waiting period—unless someone were to say that, during that period, he may take back one of the women from whom he has separated and divorce another in her place. I know of no one who holds this view.

Fourth: Allah says:

“Divorce is twice; then either retain her honourably or release her graciously.”

Thus, after two divorces, He allows the husband either to retain her honourably or to release her graciously. This option is not available to him regarding the wives beyond four once he has separated from them—unless it is said that he may take one back on condition that she replaces another wife.

Fifth: Allah says:

“When you divorce women, divorce them at the prescribed time for their waiting period.”

This separation, however, is not tied to the waiting period. Rather, upon embracing Islam, he must part with any wives beyond four. This is clear evidence.

Sixth: He says:

“Do not turn them out of their homes, nor should they leave, unless they commit a blatant indecency.”

This separation is not of that kind.

Seventh: He says:

“When you divorce women and they have reached the end of their waiting period, do not prevent them from marrying their former husbands if they mutually agree on honourable terms.”

This separation is not of that kind.

---

## Page 15923

Eighth: Separation from one of two sisters and from any wives beyond four is specifically required by Islamic law. Allah, however, has never made divorce itself the sole obligation; rather, He has required either retaining the wife honourably or releasing her graciously.

Ninth: Divorce is, in principle, disliked. For this reason, Allah has permitted it only three times and has made the wife unlawful to her husband after the third divorce, as a punishment intended to deter him from divorcing. Here, however, separation is something Allah and His Messenger have commanded. How, then, can something Allah and His Messenger love be classified as something they dislike?

This is comparable to shunning fellow Muslims: it was originally prohibited, but the Lawgiver permitted it for three days. As for shunning that is commanded—such as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his Companions shunning the three who were left behind for fifty nights—this was something Allah and His Messenger loved. It therefore does not belong to the category of disliked conduct of which three days were permitted out of need.

Likewise, since mourning other than that of a wife for her husband was originally prohibited, three days of it were permitted out of need. As for a wife’s mourning for four months and ten days, since it is something Allah and His Messenger have commanded, it does not belong to the category of mourning that Allah disliked but permitted for three days out of need. Similarly, separation commanded by Allah and His Messenger does not belong to the category of divorce that Allah and His Messenger dislike, but have permitted three times out of need.

Khulʿ (marital dissolution in return for compensation) falls into this category. Al-Bukhārī narrated in his Sahīh, through Khālid al-Hadhdhā’, from ʿIkrimah, from Ibn ʿAbbās:

“The wife of Thābit ibn Qays came to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I find no fault with Thābit ibn Qays in either his character or his religion

---

## Page 15924

, but, as a Muslim, I dread falling into unbelief.’ The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, asked, ‘Will you return his garden to him?’ She said, ‘Yes.’ The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said, ‘Accept the garden and divorce her once.’”

Thus, this report includes ʿIkrimah’s narration “from Ibn ʿAbbās that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said, ‘Accept the garden and divorce her once.’”

Yet it is established that Ibn ʿAbbās, ʿIkrimah and others did not count khulʿ as one of the three divorces. Ahmad ibn Hanbal said: “Yahyā ibn Saʿīd al-Qattān narrated to us, from Sufyān, from ʿAmr ibn Dīnār, from Tāwūs, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: ‘Khulʿ is a separation, not a divorce.’”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ahmad said: “I saw my father adopt Ibn ʿAbbās’s position.” This is also the position of Ishāq, Abū Thawr, Dāwūd and his followers, apart from Ibn Hazm.

ʿAbd al-Razzāq narrated from Ibn ʿUyaynah, from ʿAmr ibn Dīnār, from Tāwūs, that Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿd asked him about a man who had divorced his wife twice, after which she obtained khulʿ from him: could he marry her again? Ibn ʿAbbās said: “Yes. Allah mentions divorce in the verse and at its end, with khulʿ between the two.”

ʿAbd al-Razzāq narrated from Ibn Jurayj, from Ibn Tāwūs, who said: “My father did not regard release in return for compensation as divorce, and he would give him the choice between the two.”

Ibn Jurayj said: “ʿAmr ibn Dīnār informed me that he heard ʿIkrimah, who heard Ibn ʿAbbās say: ‘Any separation secured by payment is not divorce.’”

Here, then, ʿIkrimah states that every separation effected in return for payment does not count as one of the three divorces. That is because this is precisely the meaning of the release in return for compensation mentioned in Allah’s Book.

---

## Page 15925

and “release in return for compensation” does not count as one of the three divorces, as Ibn ʿAbbās explained—even though Ibn ʿAbbās and ʿIkrimah are the very narrators through whom al-Bukhārī transmitted the hadith concerning the wife of Thābit ibn Qays, as mentioned above … (1).

He said: “The hadith they cite is narrated by ʿIkrimah with the Companion omitted from its chain.” Abū Bakr ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: “It is weak, with the Companion omitted.”

The response is that this applies to some of its chains of transmission; the others do not contain this omission. Moreover, this particular version has been transmitted with a connected chain by a narrator equal, if not superior, in standing to the narrator who omitted the Companion. In such cases, the connected version takes precedence over the version containing that omission. Al-Hākim also narrated this hadith in his collection of authentic reports, entitled al-Mustadrak, and said: “This hadith has an authentic chain of transmission, except that ʿAbd al-Razzāq narrated it from Maʿmar with the Companion omitted.” Al-Qushayrī also included it in his Ahkām, in which he stipulated that he would transmit only hadiths whose narrators had been declared trustworthy by a leading authority in assessing hadith transmitters, and which were authentic according to the method of some of the leading hadith scholars and critical jurists.

He said: “The position of ʿUthmān and Ibn ʿAbbās is contradicted by that of ʿUmar and ʿAlī, who said: ‘Her waiting period is three menstrual periods.’ As for Ibn ʿUmar, Mālik narrated from Nāfiʿ that he said: ‘The waiting period of a woman who obtains khulʿ is the same as that of a divorced woman.’ This is the more authentic report from him.”

The response is: As for what is reported from ʿUmar and ʿAlī … (2). Even assuming that disagreement among the Companions is established, the matter over which they disagreed must be referred back to Allah and the Messenger. The Sunnah has made clear that what is required is one menstrual period … (3). Further evidence clarifying this is that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed the wife of Thābit ibn Qays to menstruate

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2, 3) Blank in the original.

---

## Page 15926

and wait for one menstrual period, then return to her family. Had he divorced her with one of the three divorces, she would have been obliged to observe the waiting period of a divorced woman, according to the explicit text of the Quran and the agreement of the Muslims. Khulʿ, however, is different: it is established that several scholars, both early and later, held that it entails no prescribed waiting period, but only verification through menstruation that the woman is not pregnant. The disagreement over this issue is well known.

As for the hadith transmitted with a connected chain, it was narrated by the compilers of the Sunan. Al-Nasāʾī said:

Muhammad ibn Yahyā al-Marwazī narrated to us: Shādhān ibn ʿUthmān, the brother of ʿAbdān, told me: My father narrated to us: ʿAlī ibn [al-Mubārak] (1) narrated to us, from Yahyā ibn Abī Kathīr: Muhammad ibn ʿAbd al-Rahmān informed me that al-Rubayyiʿ bint Muʿawwidh ibn ʿAfrāʾ had informed him.

Al-Nasāʾī also narrated it from Yaʿqūb ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿd: My paternal uncle told me: My father narrated to us, from Ibn Ishāq. Ibn Abī ʿĀsim narrated it from Muhammad ibn Saʿd and from Yaʿqūb ibn Mahrān, from al-Rubayyiʿ bint Muʿawwidh. Ibn Mājah narrated it from ʿAlī ibn Salamah al-Naysābūrī: Yaʿqūb ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿd narrated to us: My father told me, from Ibn Ishāq: ʿUbādah ibn al-Walīd narrated to us, from ʿUbādah ibn al-Sāmit. Both relate:

“Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās struck his wife and broke her hand. She came to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, after dawn—she was Jamīlah bint ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy—and her brother came to complain to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, about him. He sent for him and said to him: ‘Take what you owe her, and let her go.’ He replied: ‘Yes.’ The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, then instructed her to wait for one menstrual period and return to her family.”

That is, after one menstrual period. It was also narrated by

---

## Page 15927

Abū Dāwūd in his Sunan, al-Tirmidhī in his Jāmiʿ, and Abū Bakr ibn Abī ʿĀsim in his Book of Divorce. All three narrated it from Muhammad ibn ʿAbd al-Rahmān al-Baghdādī: ʿAlī ibn Yahyā al-Qattān narrated to us: Hishām ibn Yūsuf informed us, from Maʿmar, from ʿAmr ibn Muslim, from ʿIkrimah, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who reported:

“The wife of Thābit ibn Qays obtained a khulʿ from him, and the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prescribed a waiting period of one menstrual period for her.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “A good, singular hadith.” Al-Hākim also narrated it in his Sahīh.

Abū Dāwūd said: “ʿAbd al-Razzāq narrated this hadith from ʿAmr ibn Muslim, from ʿIkrimah, from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.”

Al-Tirmidhī also narrated from al-Rubayyiʿ bint Muʿawwidh ibn ʿAfrāʾ:

“She obtained a khulʿ during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed her—or she was instructed—to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “The correct version of al-Rubayyiʿ’s hadith is that she was instructed to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period.”

Al-Nasāʾī, Ibn Abī ʿĀsim, and Ibn Mājah narrated that al-Rubayyiʿ bint Muʿawwidh ibn ʿAfrāʾ said:

“I obtained a khulʿ from my husband. Then I went to ʿUthmān and asked what waiting period I was required to observe. He replied: ‘You are not required to observe a waiting period unless he has recently been intimate with you, in which case you must wait until you have had one menstrual period.’”

Ibn Mājah’s wording is: “You must remain with him until you have had one menstrual period.” Al-Nasāʾī and Ibn Abī ʿĀsim, however, did not include the words “with him.”

She said: “In this, he was simply following the ruling of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, concerning al-Mughāliyah, who had been married to Thābit ibn Qays and obtained a khulʿ from him.”

These, then, are three chains of transmission for the hadith concerning the wife of Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās, with whom he concluded a khulʿ: “The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed her to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period.” It was also narrated by Abū Bakr

---

## Page 15928

ibn Abī ʿĀsim in his Book of Divorce through four chains of transmission back to the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. Thus, the hadith has five or six chains of transmission. He cited al-Rubayyiʿ’s hadith mentioning Maryam al-Mughāliyah (*), but did not cite al-Rubayyiʿ’s earlier hadith concerning Thābit’s beating of his wife Jamīlah—which Ibn Hazm and others graded authentic.

He related: Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn ʿUmar narrated to us: ʿUmar ibn Yūnus narrated to us, from Sulaymān ibn Abī Sulaymān, from Yahyā ibn Abī Kathīr, from Muhammad ibn ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Thawbān, from al-Rubayyiʿ:

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed the woman who had obtained a khulʿ to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period.”

He also said: Muhammad ibn Sulaymān narrated to us: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yūsuf narrated to us: Ibn Lahīʿah narrated to us: Abū al-Aswad narrated to us, from Yahyā ibn al-Nazr and Yazīd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qusayt, from Abū Salamah ibn ʿAbd al-Rahmān and Muhammad ibn ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Thawbān, from al-Rubayyiʿ bint Muʿawwidh ibn ʿAfrāʾ:

“She heard the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, relate concerning the wife of Thābit ibn Qays that there had been some discord between her and her husband, who was a hot-tempered man. She came to the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, and spoke to him. He sent for Thābit, who then accepted compensation from her, and she thereby secured her release from him. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed her to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period.”

Abū Bakr ibn Abī ʿĀsim said: “Among the evidence that khulʿ is an annulment, not a divorce, is the following report transmitted through an established chain: Muhammad ibn Musaffā narrated to us: Suwayd ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz narrated to us
