---

## Page 15929

—that is, Yahyā ibn Sa'īd—from 'Amrah, from Habībah bint Suhayl, who said:

‘The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, had intended to marry a woman, but Thābit ibn Qays proposed to her and married her. Thābit had a harsh disposition, and he struck her. She came to the door of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, at dawn, while it was still dark. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, came out and asked, “Who is this?” Habībah replied, “It is I, O Messenger of Allah. Thābit and I cannot remain together.”

Before long, Thābit arrived. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, asked him, “Did you strike her?” He replied, “Yes, I struck her.” The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, told him, “Take compensation from her.” She said, “O Messenger of Allah, I still have everything he gave me.” He then spoke, and Thābit took it from her, while she remained in her own home.’”

Ibn Abī 'Āsim said: “He did not mention divorce.”

He said: “The reference to ‘one menstrual period’ is evidence that she was not a divorced woman. The same is indicated by her observing her waiting period in her own home. Had she been a divorced woman, she would have been entitled to accommodation and maintenance.”

I say: This follows the view of those who hold that khul' constitutes a revocable divorce if it is classified as divorce, as Abū Muhammad reports from the majority of hadith scholars and Dāwūd. Ibn Abī 'Āsim agrees with them on this point: his position is that an irrevocably divorced woman is entitled to neither maintenance nor accommodation, based on the hadith of Fātimah bint Qays.

Ibn Abī 'Āsim said: “Among those who held that her waiting period is one menstrual period were 'Uthmān ibn 'Affān and Ibn 'Umar. Among those who held that it is an annulment, not a divorce, were Ibn 'Abbās and Ibn al-Zubayr.”

I say: Ibn al-Mundhir related that Ahmad ibn Hanbal deemed weak all reports from the Companions that contradicted Ibn 'Abbās’s position.

---

## Page 15930

Shaykh Abū Muhammad also mentioned in his al-Mughnī this report from Ahmad, which Abū Bakr 'Abd al-'Azīz recorded in al-Shāfī. Abū Muhammad took it from that work, though it is also found in other books. He wrote:

“Most scholars hold that the waiting period of a woman who obtains khul' is the same as that of a divorced woman. They include Sa'īd ibn al-Musayyib and a group of scholars, among them Mālik and al-Shāfi'ī.”

He said:

“It is reported from 'Uthmān ibn 'Affān, Ibn 'Umar, Ibn 'Abbās, Abān ibn 'Uthmān, Ishāq and Ibn al-Mundhir that the waiting period of a woman who obtains khul' is one menstrual period. Ibn al-Qāsim transmitted this from Ahmad, just as Ibn 'Abbās reported: ‘The wife of Thābit ibn Qays obtained khul' from him, and the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prescribed one menstrual period as her waiting period.’ This was narrated by al-Nasā'ī. A similar report from al-Rubayyi' bint Mu'awwidh was narrated by al-Nasā'ī and Ibn Mājah.”

He said:

“Our evidence is the statement of Allah Most High: ‘Divorced women shall wait for three menstrual cycles.’ Moreover, since this is a separation after consummation while both spouses are alive, its waiting period is three menstrual cycles, as in khul'.”

He then said:

“As for the verse, it cannot be adduced as evidence until it is established that a woman who obtains khul' is a divorced woman—and that is precisely the point at issue. Even supposing that the text includes her, the specific evidence qualifies the general. Several categories of divorced women have been excepted from this verse: a woman whose marriage has not been consummated, a pregnant woman, a slave woman, and a woman who has not menstruated. It applies only to a divorced woman whose husband has the right to take her back.

“As for the analogy mentioned, our response is: We do not concede that the effective cause in the original case consists merely of the characteristic just mentioned. Nor do we concede that the ruling holds in all cases involving people. Moreover, it is countered by the case of a woman who separates from her husband. The Sunnah indicates that what is required in both cases is a waiting period to establish that the womb is free of pregnancy.”

---

## Page 15931

As for the report: was she Jamīlah bint Ubayy, Sahlah bint Suhayl, or another woman? The reports differ on this point. Either there were two or three separate incidents, or one of the two narrators mistook her name. This does not undermine the established account, for the ruling does not depend on his wife’s name. The account of his separation from his wife through khul' is established by mass transmission and is agreed upon by the scholars. Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā'ī narrated:

“Habībah bint Sahl al-Ansāriyyah reported that she was married to Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, went out for the dawn prayer and found Habībah bint Sahl at his door while it was still dark. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, asked, ‘Who is this?’

“She replied, ‘I am Habībah bint Sahl, O Messenger of Allah.’

“He asked, ‘What is the matter with you?’

“She replied, ‘Thābit ibn Qays and I cannot remain together,’ referring to her husband.

“When Thābit arrived, the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said, ‘Habībah bint Sahl has said what Allah willed her to say.’

“Habībah said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, everything he gave me is still with me.’

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said to Thābit, ‘Take it from her.’ He took it from her, and she stayed with her family.”

Ibn Hazm cited this hadith, along with the hadith prescribing a waiting period of one menstrual period, among the arguments of those who hold that khul' is an annulment. He wrote:

“They said: ‘This makes clear that khul' is not a divorce, but an annulment.’”

He mentioned Ibn 'Abbās’s hadith only through the disconnected chain of 'Abd al-Razzāq. He said:

“As for the report of 'Abd al-Razzāq, it is to be rejected because its chain is disconnected;

---

## Page 15932

and its chain includes 'Amr ibn Muslim, who is of no standing. As for the reports of al-Rubayyi' and Habībah, had no other reports been transmitted, they would have constituted decisive proof. However, they were also narrated through al-Bukhārī.”

He then cited the Prophet’s previously mentioned words, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Accept the garden and divorce her once.”

He said: “This report contains an addition to the two reports mentioned above, which cannot be disregarded. Since it is a divorce, and Allah has prescribed the waiting period for divorce, this adds to what is contained in the hadith of al-Rubayyi'; such an addition cannot be disregarded.”

The response to him is as follows:

As for your statement that the report of 'Abd al-Razzāq has a disconnected chain, Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī narrated it through Hammām ibn Yūsuf with a connected chain, as noted above. According to your own principle, this is an addition supplied by a trustworthy narrator and must therefore be accepted. Moreover, al-Tirmidhī graded the hadith as good.

As for your remarks about 'Amr ibn Muslim, the response is that Muslim included his narrations in his Sahīh, as did al-Bukhārī in The Book of Human Acts, and Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī and al-Nasā'ī. Ibn Hibbān included him in The Trustworthy Narrators. According to the account transmitted by Ibrāhīm ibn al-Musnid, Yahyā ibn Ma'īn said: “There is nothing wrong with him.” Abū Ahmad ibn 'Adī said: “He has no highly objectionable hadith.”

As for the other hadith, whose authenticity you acknowledged and which you deemed decisive proof were it not for the conflicting report, it explicitly settles the issue: the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed her

“to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period and return to her family.”

---

## Page 15933

As for your claim that the other chain of transmission contains an addition—namely, that he instructed him to divorce her once, and that a divorced woman must observe a waiting period—this is not an addition. Rather, unless “a single divorce” here means an annulment, this narration contradicts the other one. The other narration explicitly states that she should return to her family after one menstrual period. Even if it contained only the words:

“He instructed her to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period,”

this would clearly mean that he instructed her to observe one menstrual period, not more. Had he instructed her to observe three, it would not have been permissible to report only that “he instructed her to observe one menstrual period.” How much clearer, then, when it also says, “and return to her family”!

Moreover, all the narrations transmitted through the other chains support or agree with this. They are further supported by the practice of 'Uthmān ibn 'Affān, one of the Rightly Guided Caliphs. Some chains of transmission for the report concerning him have already been cited, along with the fact that, in this matter, he followed the Sunnah concerning the wife of Thābit ibn Qays.

Furthermore, even supposing that the other narration stated, “He instructed her to observe a waiting period of three menstrual periods,” this would constitute a conflict between the narrations, requiring consideration of which of the two chains was more authentic. How, then, can that be claimed when it contains only the words, “And divorce her once”? Those who transmit this are Ibn 'Abbās and his companion, both of whom also narrate that “he instructed her to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period.” Both also hold that khul' is a release obtained by compensation and does not count toward the three divorces.

As for the words, “And divorce her once,” if this wording, together with what precedes it, is reliably preserved as the words of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, then one of two explanations must apply: either it must be said that divorce

---

## Page 15934

in return for compensation does not require a waiting period of three months; this would be an exception to the wording of the Quran. If this is said of divorce in return for compensation, then it applies all the more to khul'.

Alternatively, it must be said that what he meant by “Divorce her once” was khul', and that the Lawgiver makes no distinction between the terms khul' and divorce when compensation is involved. This is a release obtained by compensation, not the divorce referred to without qualification in Allah’s Book, as some of the early generations held. This comes back to the first meaning. In any event, if the Lawgiver has prescribed no waiting period in such a case, we know that it does not count toward the three divorces, for the Quran explicitly states that any divorce counted toward the three entails a waiting period.

Moreover, there is consensus on this as far as we know. We know of no one who disputed it and maintained that khul' counts as one of the three divorces yet entails no waiting period. This further supports the view that khul' is an annulment. Some of the reports from 'Uthmān and others have already been cited.

Yahyā ibn Bukayr reported: Al-Layth ibn Sa'd narrated to us, from Nāfi', the freedman of Ibn 'Umar, that he heard al-Rubayyi' bint Mu'awwidh ibn 'Afrā' telling 'Abd Allāh ibn 'Umar that she had obtained khul' from her husband during the caliphate of 'Uthmān. Her uncle came to 'Uthmān and said:

“Mu'aydh’s daughter obtained khul' from her husband today. May she move out?”

'Uthmān replied:

“Let her move out. Neither inherits from the other, and she owes no waiting period. However, she must not marry until she has menstruated once, lest she be pregnant.”

'Abd Allāh ibn 'Umar said:

“Indeed, 'Uthmān is the best and most knowledgeable of us.”

Ibn Hazm said:

“Thus, 'Uthmān, al-Rubayyi'—who was a Companion—her uncle—who was one of the senior Companions—and Ibn 'Umar all held that annulment entails no waiting period.”

---

## Page 15935

If it is objected:

It has been reported from 'Uthmān and Ibn 'Umar that khul' is a divorce. Hammād ibn Salamah narrated from Hishām ibn 'Urwah, from his father, from Jumhān, that Umm Bakrah al-Aslamiyyah was married to 'Abd Allāh ibn Asīd and obtained khul' from him. They both regretted it and brought their case before 'Uthmān ibn 'Affān, who approved it and said: “It counts as one divorce, unless you specified a particular number, in which case it is as you specified.”

Mālik also narrated from Nāfi', from Ibn 'Umar, who said:

“The waiting period for a woman who obtains khul' is the same as that of a divorced woman.”

Abū Dāwūd narrated:

“'Uthmān ibn Abī Shaybah narrated to us: 'Affān narrated to us: Hammām narrated to us, from Qatādah, from 'Ikrimah, from Ibn 'Abbās, that Barīrah’s husband was a black slave. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) gave her the choice and commanded her to observe a waiting period.” (1)

Ibn Abī 'Āsim likewise narrated it:

“Hudbah ibn Khālid narrated to us: Hammām narrated to us, from Qatādah, from 'Ikrimah, from Ibn 'Abbās, who said: ‘The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) issued four rulings concerning Barīrah: he instructed her to choose and commanded her to observe a waiting period.’”

He also said:

“Al-Halwānī narrated to us: 'Amr ibn . . . (2) narrated to us: Hammām narrated to us, from Qatādah, from 'Ikrimah, from Ibn 'Abbās, mentioning the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). He said: ‘I believe the wording included: “Observe the waiting period for khul'.”’”

Thus, this was an annulment for which he required a waiting period. For this reason, Ibn Hazm said that no annulment entails a waiting period except this one, because he does not accept analogical reasoning, and the text does not stipulate a waiting period for an annulment.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) Text missing in the original.
> (2) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 270) writes: “Perhaps the intended name is ‘Amr ibn ‘Āsim.”

---

## Page 15936

But in their usage, the expression “observing a waiting period” can mean waiting for a single menstrual period, as in the hadith concerning the woman who obtained khul', transmitted through several routes: “He commanded her to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period.”

'Ā’ishah explained Allah’s words, “And married women, except those whom your right hands possess,” as meaning that these women become lawful to you once their waiting periods have ended. What is meant here is “ascertaining freedom from pregnancy,” since a woman taken captive is required only to wait for one menstrual period to establish that she is not pregnant. Thus, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said concerning the women taken captive at Awtās:

“A pregnant woman must not be approached sexually until she gives birth, nor a woman who is not pregnant until one menstrual period has established that she is not pregnant.”

The report also states that Allah then revealed: “And married women, except those whom your right hands possess.” Likewise, the well-known hadith of Abū Sa'īd al-Khudrī concerning the women taken captive at Awtās, transmitted through Abū al-Khalīl, states: “They become lawful once their waiting periods have ended.” (*) Concerning this, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“A pregnant woman must not be approached sexually until she gives birth, nor a woman who is not pregnant until it has been established that she is not pregnant.”

Abū Sa'īd narrated both reports.

According to both hadiths, a slave woman who has borne her master a child observes a waiting period of one menstrual period. As for 'Amr ibn 'Āsim’s statement, “And I believe he said: ‘She observes the waiting period of a free woman,’” this is uncertainty upon which no proof can be based.

Two opinions are reported from Ahmad concerning the waiting period of a woman who obtains khul'. Abū Bakr mentioned both in his book al-Shāfī. Abū Bakr said in al-Shāfī: “Chapter: The Waiting Period of a Woman Who Obtains Khul', a Woman Separated by Mutual Imprecation, and imra'at 'asbā.”

He narrated through his chain of transmission from al-Athram and Ibrāhīm ibn al-Hārith that Abū 'Abd Allāh was asked: “Is the waiting period of every divorced woman three menstrual periods?”

He replied: “Yes, except for a slave woman.”

He was asked: “Does that include a woman who obtains khul', a woman separated by mutual imprecation, and the wife of an apostate?”

He replied: “Yes. Every separation entails a waiting period of three menstrual periods.” It is also reported from Abū Tālib that

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd writes (p. 238):
> The hadith referred to is that narrated by Muslim (1456) and others, from Abū ‘Alqama al-Hāshimī, from Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī: “On the day of Hunayn, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) dispatched an army to Awtās. They encountered an enemy, fought and defeated them, and took women captive. It seems that some of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) felt scruples about having intercourse with these women because they had husbands among the polytheists. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, then revealed concerning this: ‘And married women, except those whom your right hands possess’—meaning that they are lawful for you once their waiting periods have ended.”

---

## Page 15937

Abū 'Abd Allāh said concerning a woman who obtains khul': “She observes a waiting period of three menstrual periods, like a divorced woman.”

It is also reported through Ahmad ibn al-Qāsim that Abū 'Abd Allāh said: “The waiting period of a woman who obtains khul' is one menstrual period.”

'Abd al-'Azīz said: “The report of al-Athram and al-'Abbādī is the one to be followed: whenever a free woman is separated from her husband, her waiting period is three menstrual periods. The hadith stating that a woman who obtained khul' was commanded to observe a waiting period of one menstrual period is weak, because it is transmitted from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) through a disconnected chain. I adopt the view I have stated, which is also the view of 'Uthmān ibn 'Affān.”

I say: Ibn al-Qāsim frequently transmits Ahmad’s later opinions, which he adopted after revising his earlier views. For example, he reported from Ahmad that issuing three pronouncements of divorce together is forbidden, mentioning that Ahmad had retracted his view that it was permissible, and that he had reflected upon the Quran and found that the divorce mentioned in it was only revocable divorce.

Likewise, Ahmad may have accepted the report concerning a woman who obtains khul' as authentic and revised his view accordingly. His statement, “Her waiting period is one menstrual period,” could only have been made if he had accepted the hadith as authentic; and if he had accepted it as authentic, he would not have abandoned it. Ahmad’s followers hold two views concerning intercourse mistakenly believed to be lawful—

Similarly, Ibn 'Umar initially held that her waiting period was three menstrual periods. When he learned of 'Uthmān ibn 'Affān’s view that she should wait for one menstrual period to establish that she was not pregnant, Ibn 'Umar adopted it. What Abū Bakr related from 'Uthmān is a less authoritative report. The well-known report from 'Uthmān is that she observes a waiting period of one menstrual period. This is also the view of Ibn 'Abbās and the later of the two views reported from Ibn 'Umar. No contrary view has been authentically established from any Companion: the contrary view is reported from 'Umar and 'Alī through a weak chain of transmission. The view that one menstrual period suffices was also held by Abān ibn 'Uthmān, 'Ikrimah, Is'hāq ibn Rāhawayh, and other jurists versed in hadith.

---

## Page 15938

Al-Bukhārī also narrated in his Sahīh that Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“The polytheists fell into two categories in relation to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and the believers: those at war, whom he fought and who fought him, and those under treaty, whom he did not fight and who did not fight him. When a woman emigrated from among those at war, no marriage proposal was made to her until she had menstruated and become pure. Once she became pure, she was permitted to marry. If her husband emigrated before she married, she was returned to him. If a male or female slave belonging to them emigrated, they became free and enjoyed the same rights as the emigrants.”

Then, regarding those under treaty, an account similar to Mujāhid’s report was mentioned: “If a male or female slave belonging to the polytheists under treaty emigrated, they were not returned, but their owners were reimbursed their value.”

This hadith establishes that a woman who emigrated from a territory at war was permitted to marry once she had menstruated and become pure. She was therefore required only to establish that she was not pregnant by waiting for one menstrual period, not three menstrual periods. Her waiting period followed intercourse with a husband, but her marriage to him had ended through her acceptance of Islam. This shows that separation resulting from a difference in religion—such as when a disbeliever’s wife accepts Islam—requires only one menstrual period to establish that she is not pregnant. Such a separation is an annulment, not a divorce.

This undermines the general claim that every separation during the spouses’ lifetimes, after consummation, requires a waiting period of three menstrual periods. The woman in this case is a free Muslim woman, although her waiting period follows intercourse with a disbeliever.

---

## Page 15939

Scholars have differed over whether a disbeliever’s wife must observe a waiting period or merely wait to establish that she is not pregnant. There are two well-known opinions; the position of Abū Hanīfa and Mālik is that she is not required to observe a waiting period.

This hadith’s mention of returning female slaves belonging to those under treaty is analogous to reimbursing the dowries of women who emigrated from among people under a truce. These are the women whose faith was to be tested, concerning whom Allah said:

“When believing women come to you as emigrants, test them”—to the end of the verse.

As for its statement that, if her husband emigrated before she remarried, he had a greater right to her, this represents one of the opinions on the matter. When a disbeliever’s wife accepts Islam, does separation take effect immediately in every case? Or is a distinction drawn between a consummated and an unconsummated marriage? Or does the matter remain pending so long as she does not remarry, such that, if he accepts Islam, she remains his wife?

The hadiths support only this last opinion. They include this hadith and the hadith concerning Zaynab, the daughter of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). The established report states that he returned her to her husband under their original marriage contract after six years. Ahmad narrated it in his Musnad, as did the compilers of the Sunan—Abū Dāwūd and others—and al-Hākim in his Sahīh, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) returned Zaynab to Abū al-‘Ās under their original marriage contract, without instituting anything new.”

One version adds, “after six years.” Its chain of transmission includes Ibn Ishāq. Al-Tirmidhī also narrated it and said, “There is nothing objectionable in its chain of transmission.”

Abū Dāwūd and al-Hākim, in his Sahīh, also narrated from Ibn ‘Abbās:

“A woman accepted Islam during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and then remarried. Her husband came to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I had already accepted Islam, and she knew that I had done so.’ The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) then took her away from her second husband and returned her to her first husband.”

Its chain of transmission includes Simāk.

---

## Page 15940

Thus, he returned her when her husband stated that he had accepted Islam and that she knew of his conversion, without asking him for further details: Had they accepted Islam together? Or had she accepted Islam before the waiting period expired? Not requesting further details indicates that the answer applies generally and without qualification to all the circumstances encompassed by the question.

This is because whatever a person possesses when he accepts Islam remains his. If he accepts Islam while inheritances remain undistributed, they are distributed according to Islamic law. Likewise, contracts under which possession has not yet been taken are governed by Islamic law. If a slave belonging to a non-Muslim living under Muslim protection accepts Islam, the owner’s ownership does not cease automatically. Rather, he is ordered to relinquish his ownership and is prevented from retaining control over the slave, having sexual relations with his female slaves—whether a slave who has borne him a child or another—or employing their services.

Likewise, when a woman accepts Islam, she and her husband are kept apart. If he accepts Islam before another man acquires a right to her, his case is like that of an owner who accepts Islam before his slaves are sold: he has the greater right to them. Maintaining an existing bond has a stronger claim than establishing a new one.

Moreover, the view that separation takes effect immediately contradicts what is established through mass transmission from the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). The view that the matter remains pending until the waiting period expires does so as well. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) set no such time limit for the women and men who accepted Islam during his lifetime, despite their considerable number. Nor is there any relevant connection between the waiting period and marital entitlement arising from either spouse’s acceptance of Islam. Drawing an analogy between this and taking back a divorced wife is, in numerous respects, among the most invalid forms of analogy.

Furthermore, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said concerning female captives:

“A pregnant woman must not be approached sexually until she gives birth, nor a woman who is not pregnant until she menstruates.”

This hadith implies that nothing is required for establishing freedom from pregnancy except menstruation, or pregnancy in the case of a young girl who does not menstruate; and this cannot be envisaged in the case of a slave woman. Thus, the hadith does not impose a waiting period to establish freedom from pregnancy upon a woman who does not menstruate.

---

## Page 15941

and imposing such a requirement is far removed from sound analogy. Hence, those who advocate it have differed unsettledly among several positions, each of which is open to refutation.

“Furthermore,” no one has reported that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) ordered a waiting period to establish freedom from pregnancy in any other case. These women had husbands and had been having intercourse. As for the slave women sold during his lifetime, they were not ordinarily used for intercourse; rather, they were generally employed for service. This implies that a slave woman whose master has not had intercourse with her need not undergo such a waiting period, just as she need not undergo it when she marries. If it is not required for marriage, then there is even stronger reason not to require it when taking her as a concubine.

Ibn ʿUmar said: “A Muslim woman need not undergo a waiting period to establish freedom from pregnancy.” This is because she has intercourse. A woman who is required to observe neither a marital waiting period nor a period to establish freedom from pregnancy when she is married off is likewise not required to observe the latter when intercourse takes place with her by virtue of ownership.

Al-Layth ibn Saʿd likewise said: “If she is among those who cannot become pregnant, no waiting period to establish freedom from pregnancy is required for her, whether measured by menstruation or pregnancy.” This accords with the scriptural text.

Abū Hanīfah said: “If they establish that she is not pregnant … (1) a waiting period incumbent upon him.” Mālik said: “If she is in his possession as a deposit for safekeeping or the like, and he knows that she has not had intercourse, he does not need a waiting period to establish freedom from pregnancy if he has already observed such a period for her (2).” The same applies to the view of the one who said that this waiting period is required only for a pregnant woman or one who has had intercourse. Al-Rūyānī inclined to this view.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A lacuna in the original.
> (2) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 15942

What the scriptural text indicates is that a waiting period to establish freedom from pregnancy is prescribed whenever pregnancy is possible. For he commanded this waiting period for pregnant and menstruating women among the female captives whose condition was unknown. When it is known that the womb is free from pregnancy, however, such a waiting period serves no purpose. The report of Ibn Shihāb in al-Muwattaʾ is mursal: it is not linked to the Prophet through a Companion.

The Quran prescribes a waiting period of three cycles only for divorced women—not for a woman separated from her husband by means other than divorce, nor for a woman who has had intercourse under a mistaken assumption of its lawfulness, nor for a woman with whom illicit intercourse has occurred. Since the Sunnah establishes that a woman divorced by khulʿ (divorce in return for compensation) need only wait for one menstrual period, which establishes freedom from pregnancy, this applies with even greater force to a woman who has had intercourse under a mistaken assumption of lawfulness or one with whom illicit intercourse has occurred. This accords with one of the two positions reported from Ahmad concerning both a woman divorced by khulʿ and a woman with whom illicit intercourse has occurred. There is less reason to require a full waiting period of a woman who has had intercourse under a mistaken assumption of lawfulness than of either of those two women. Whichever of them her case is treated as analogous to, she need only wait for one menstrual period, as one of the two juristic views holds.

Reasoned consideration also supports this position. A divorced woman’s husband has the right to take her back, while she is entitled to a compensatory gift upon divorce, as well as maintenance and accommodation during the waiting period. It is therefore fitting that she should be commanded to wait for three cycles in recognition of her husband’s right, allowing him to take her back during that time. He thus has a right served by the length of the waiting period, as Allah Most High says: “When you marry believing women and then divorce them

---

## Page 15943

before you have consummated the marriage with them, you have no waiting period to count against them.” Thus, He, glorified be He, explains that, when a waiting period is obligatory, it is a right the man holds over the divorced woman. Once he has consummated the marriage with her, he is entitled to require her to observe the waiting period on account of that consummation, and he has the right to take her back. In return, she is entitled to maintenance and accommodation, just as she is entitled to a compensatory gift on account of the divorce.

As for a woman whose separation was not by divorce, if she is entitled to neither maintenance, accommodation, nor a compensatory gift, and the husband has no right to take her back, freedom from pregnancy can be established by a single menstrual period, just as it is for enslaved women. Her being free makes no difference. This is demonstrated by the fact that, according to most jurists, a slave woman who has borne her master a child observes a waiting period of one menstrual period after her husband’s death, as Ibn ʿUmar and others held, even though she is free. A woman who has had intercourse under a mistaken assumption of its lawfulness is in no better position than she is. Likewise, a woman separated from her husband by means other than divorce, who is entitled to neither maintenance, accommodation, nor a compensatory gift, and whom he has no right to take back, is in the same position.

If it is objected: “This is contradicted by the case of a woman divorced for the third and final time. She is entitled to neither maintenance nor accommodation, and her husband has no right to take her back; yet she observes a waiting period of one menstrual period?”

The answer is: Such a divorced woman is entitled to a compensatory gift according to al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad in one of the two reports from him, and many—or most—of the early generations. She is entitled to maintenance according to Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and many jurists of the Hijaz; this is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad. In addition, she is entitled to accommodation according to many jurists of Iraq, such as Abū Hanīfah and others. Thus, according to the general body of scholars, she must be entitled to either a compensatory gift or accommodation. Requiring her to observe the waiting period in return for this therefore has a rationale that is absent when it is required of a woman entitled to neither a compensatory gift, maintenance, nor accommodation. It is also established that “the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) instructed Fātimah bint Qays

---

## Page 15944

, when her husband divorced her for the third and final time, to observe a waiting period. He instructed her to observe it in the house of Ibn Umm Maktūm, then instructed her to move to the house of Umm Sharīk.” Although the hadith does not explicitly state that she was to observe a waiting period of three menstrual periods, this is the established position among the scholars whose views have reached us.

If there is consensus on this, then it is the truth, for the Muslim community does not agree upon error. If, however, some scholars have held that a woman divorced three times need only establish freedom from pregnancy, rather than observe a waiting period of three menstrual periods, this position has a strong rationale: the length of the waiting period would correspond to the husband’s right to take her back, this being the reason it was set at three cycles. Thus, a woman whom her husband has no right to take back would not have to wait three cycles. The apparent meaning of the Quran contains only what accords with this position, not what contradicts it. Likewise, its apparent meaning contains only what accords with the established position, not what contradicts it. Whichever of the two positions the Sunnah establishes is therefore true and consistent with the apparent meaning of the Quran.

The established position among scholars is the first. This differs from the case of a woman who obtains a separation in return for compensation, for the Sunnah has prescribed for her what was mentioned above. This is also established from leading Companions and several of the early generations, and is the position of several leading scholars. Nothing in the Quran contradicts it; rather, it accords with it. The two cases therefore cannot be treated as analogous. If we understand the reasons for distinguishing a waiting period of three cycles from merely establishing freedom from pregnancy, well and good; otherwise, it suffices to follow what the pure, well-known scriptural evidence indicates.

This is further clarified by the case of captive women who are newly enslaved. Reference has already been made to the hadith of Abū Saʿīd, which states that Allah permitted the Muslims to have intercourse with them when they felt scruples about

---

## Page 15945

having intercourse with them, and He revealed concerning this:

“And married women, except those whom your right hands possess.”

The report also states that the time for intercourse with them is when their waiting period has ended. It is reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said concerning the captives of Awtās:

“A pregnant woman must not be approached sexually until she gives birth, nor a woman who is not pregnant until her freedom from pregnancy has been established.”

Another narration reads: “until she has menstruated once.”

Scholars generally require only one menstrual period to establish freedom from pregnancy in this case. Yet this is a waiting period following intercourse with a husband to whom the child’s lineage is attributed and whose intercourse has a legally protected status, even if he is an unbeliever at war with the Muslims. His being at war permits his killing, the seizure of his property and the enslavement of his wife—subject to scholarly disagreement and detailed qualifications. Nevertheless, there is no disagreement that his child’s lineage is established through him and that his semen has a legally protected status. By Muslim consensus, no one may have intercourse with his wife before her freedom from pregnancy has been established.

Indeed, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, cursed anyone who did so, as related in the authentic hadith in Muslim:

“He came upon a woman near childbirth at the entrance to a tent and said, ‘Perhaps her master is having intercourse with her?’ They replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘I have almost resolved to curse him with a curse that would accompany him into his grave. How can he make the child his heir when that is not lawful for him? How can he enslave the child when that is not lawful for him?’”

He also “forbade a man to water another man’s crop with his own semen.”

However, this woman’s husband did not part with her of his own choice, whether through divorce or otherwise. Rather, her enslavement transferred his right over her to the one who enslaved her, or confusion of her husband with another man extinguished that right. Thus, it is clear that this is not a marriage that has ended for a woman, for that entails a waiting period of three cycles. If an unbeliever and his wife were to bring a dispute concerning the waiting period before us, and he then divorced his wife

---

## Page 15946

we would require her to observe three cycles.

It is thus clear that a divorced woman must observe three cycles without qualification, whereas this woman, whose marriage ended otherwise than through divorce, was not required to observe three cycles. One cannot therefore say that every woman observing a waiting period following separation from a living husband must observe three cycles. Rather, this case disproves that claim on the basis of the revealed texts and scholarly consensus.

Section

The Quran, the Sunnah, and reports from leading Companions—such as ‘Uthmān and others—indicate that the waiting period of a woman who secures release from marriage in return for compensation is one menstrual period. This resolves the difficulty concerning “the overlap of two waiting periods,” as when a woman marries during her waiting period and the man has intercourse with her.

What is reported from Companions such as ‘Umar and ‘Alī is that she completes the waiting period for the first husband, then observes a waiting period on account of intercourse with the second. She must therefore complete the first husband’s waiting period and observe another for the second. This is the position adopted by the majority of jurists, including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad.

‘Umar and ‘Alī differed over whether she becomes lawful for the first man after completing both waiting periods. ‘Umar said, “He may never marry her,” and Mālik adopted this position. ‘Alī said, “He is one suitor among the other suitors,” and al-Shāfi‘ī adopted this position. Two opinions are reported from Ahmad.

Abū Hanīfah, however, holds that she need observe only one waiting period, for the second man, with the remainder of the first husband’s waiting period included within it. Some of his followers mentioned that this opinion is reported from Ibn Mas‘ūd, but we know of no chain of transmission for it.

We therefore say that the two waiting periods overlap, for the waiting period is a right owed to him: if the husband wished to waive it, he could not do so. Thus, one period is subsumed within the other, as with prescribed punishments and expiations, for

---

## Page 15947

if a man stole and then stole again, only one hand would be cut off. Likewise, if he drank intoxicants and then drank again, he would incur only one prescribed punishment. Prescribed punishments are incurred on account of the kind of offence, not its extent. Thus, the punishment applies whether the amount stolen is large or small, and whether the amount drunk is little or much, because what incurs it is the kind of offence, not its extent. Since the ruling does not differ according to whether the quantity is small or large, it likewise does not differ between a single occurrence and repeated occurrences, for all these differences concern quantity. The same applies to expiation for intercourse during Ramadan when a man has intercourse and then repeats it before offering expiation.

Those who hold that the two waiting periods overlap therefore say:

The waiting period of a divorced woman falls into this category, for if it is occasioned by intercourse, it is unlike the waiting period following a husband’s death, which is occasioned by the marriage contract. It is obligatory whether intercourse occurred seldom or often, because what occasions it is intercourse as such. It makes no difference whether there was one man or two. A further application of this reasoning is that if someone purchases a slave woman with whom several men have had intercourse, she need observe only one waiting period to establish that she is not pregnant, even though several men had intercourse with her.

They have, however, been challenged regarding this case. It has been said that, while she remains jointly owned by two men, she must observe one such waiting period for each of them.

If, however, they sell her to someone else, the purchaser is required to have her observe only one such period. We know of no one who has said that a slave woman acquired through capture, purchase, inheritance, or the like must observe multiple periods to ascertain non-pregnancy, corresponding to the number of men who had intercourse with her. Likewise, if a man purchases a slave woman and sells her before having her observe this period, the second purchaser is required to have her observe only one such period.

The jurists said: We do not say that he must have her observe it twice. Some justified this by saying that the requirement to ascertain non-pregnancy is occasioned by a multiplicity of ownership, whereas ownership had not become multiple. For this reason, they do not require such a period if he frees her and marries her, provided that the seller had not had intercourse with her; yet they require it if he does not free her. This is unlike the prescribed waiting period, which is occasioned by slave status. Discussion of the waiting period for ascertaining non-pregnancy belongs elsewhere.

---

## Page 15948

“The point” here is that this period does not become multiple, and we know of no one who has said that it does. If anyone has said so, the Sunnah refutes him, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, required only that it be ascertained that she was not pregnant, saying:

“A pregnant woman must not be approached for intercourse until she gives birth, nor a woman who is not pregnant until it has been ascertained that she is not pregnant.”

He thus made the permissibility of intercourse dependent solely on ascertaining that she was not pregnant, without drawing any distinction. Since the period for ascertaining non-pregnancy is of the same kind as the prescribed waiting period, and does not become multiple because several men have had intercourse with her, the prescribed waiting period is likewise. This is the argument that may be advanced in support of Abū Hanīfa, may Allah have mercy on him.

The majority, however, said that the prescribed waiting period involves a human right. They cited the words of the Most High:

“When you marry believing women and then divorce them before you have touched them, you have no waiting period to count against them. So provide for them…”

They said: Allah has thus denied that men have any right to require a waiting period of women in this situation. Nor is there a waiting period here owed to anyone other than the men. It therefore follows that, wherever the waiting period is obligatory, it involves a right belonging to men. Otherwise, denying that men have a right to require a waiting period would not rule out a waiting period owed to Allah. If the waiting period were exclusively a right of Allah, He would not have said:

“You have no waiting period to count against them.”

For men would have no right to a waiting period, whether in this situation or any other. Moreover, if there were two kinds of waiting period—one owed to Allah and another involving a right belonging to husbands—then negating the waiting period owed to husbands would not negate the other waiting period. The Quran therefore indicates that, wherever a waiting period is obligatory, it involves a right belonging to husbands.

Accordingly, if the waiting period involves rights belonging to two men, the right of one cannot be subsumed under that of the other, for human rights do not overlap. This is like two men each being owed a debt by one person, or having property in his possession that he holds in trust or has wrongfully appropriated: he must give each person what is rightfully his. This is the position stated by the majority of the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, and others.

---

## Page 15949

They also argued against Abū Hanīfa on the basis of his own position:

“If a Muslim marries a non-Muslim woman under Muslim protection, she must observe the waiting period solely as a right owed to her husband, since she is not held accountable for rights owed to Allah. Hence he does not require her to observe it if her husband is also a non-Muslim under Muslim protection and they do not believe that the waiting period is obligatory.”

This argument that the majority advanced against him is sound. It accords with what the Quran indicates and with the judgments of the Rightly Guided Caliphs, especially since no contrary position has been established from anyone else. Even if a contrary position were established, when others disagree with the Rightly Guided Caliphs, the caliphs’ position carries greater weight, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the Rightly Guided Caliphs after me. Hold fast to it and bite onto it with your molar teeth. Beware of newly introduced matters, for every innovation is misguidance.”

Yet for the waiting period to constitute a right of the husband in the full sense, he must have a right over the woman during it—namely, the right to take her back. As the Most High said:

“Divorced women must wait for three cycles. It is not lawful for them to conceal what Allah has created in their wombs, if they believe in Allah and the Last Day. And their husbands have a greater right to take them back during that period.”

He thus commanded women to wait and gave the husband the greater right to take his wife back during that waiting period. Every divorce mentioned in the Quran is revocable, except the third divorce mentioned in His words:

“If he divorces her, she is not lawful to him thereafter until she marries another husband.”

That divorce makes her unlawful to him: she cannot become lawful to him merely through a marriage contract concluded with her consent and that of her guardian. How, then, could she become lawful through his taking her back?

As for a woman who may lawfully return to her husband during the waiting period, her husband has the greater right to take her back during it without a marriage contract. The Quran contains no irrevocable divorce whereby she may become lawful to him through a marriage contract while he has no greater right to her. Rather, whenever she is lawful to him, he has the greater right to her.

---

## Page 15950

On this basis, it becomes clear how the waiting period constitutes a right of the husband: during it, he is entitled to take his wife back. This is unlike requiring a waiting period after an irrevocable divorce following which she may become lawful to him through a marriage contract. In that case, he has no such right, since marriage is permissible only with the consent of both parties.

For this reason, Abū Hanīfa applied his principle consistently. Since, in his view, divorce is either irrevocable or revocable, and a husband may pronounce an irrevocable divorce without his wife’s consent, he regarded the right to take her back as belonging exclusively to the husband: he may relinquish it or retain it. The waiting period, by contrast, is not something he may waive, so it cannot constitute a right belonging to him.

This supports the view that khulʿ (marital separation in return for compensation) is not divorce, for this entails treating the two alike. It also supports the view that, in khulʿ, the husband has no right to require the woman to observe a waiting period as he does in divorce. Rather, she must wait for one menstrual period to establish that her womb is clear. This requirement is a right owed to Allah, intended to establish that the womb is clear, and is therefore indispensable for every woman who has had intercourse, whether within a valid marriage, an invalid marriage, or through slave ownership. It is required to establish that her womb is clear of the first man’s semen, lest his semen mix with another man’s.

Likewise, according to the sounder of the two scholarly views, this is required of a woman who has had illicit intercourse, on account of the second man’s semen, lest it mix with that of the man who committed illicit intercourse with her. This is the position of Mālik and Ahmad.

If a woman separated by khulʿ is required to observe only one menstrual period as her waiting period, this applies all the more to a woman in an invalid marriage, since the man has no right to take her back and she has no entitlement to maintenance.

If it is objected:

“In the report concerning Tulayha, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb said: ‘Any woman who marries during her waiting period must complete the waiting period for her husband if the second man has not consummated the marriage with her. If he has consummated it, she must complete the remainder of her waiting period for the first husband and then observe a waiting period for the second.’

“Likewise, it is reported that ʿAlī ruled that she must complete the remainder of her waiting period for the first husband and then observe a fresh waiting period for the second. Once her waiting period has ended, she may marry if she wishes, or refrain from marrying if she wishes?”

---

## Page 15951

The reply is:
Yes. However, in the language of the early generations, the term “waiting period” applies both to the three cycles and to establishing that the womb is clear by waiting for one menstrual period, as illustrated by the examples already given. Thus, if ʿUmar and ʿAlī held that a woman separated by khulʿ and others in similar circumstances must wait for one menstrual period, then that is what they meant here. If, however, they held that she must wait for three cycles, then the Companions held two views on this matter, for it is established that ʿUthmān held that a woman separated by khulʿ must wait for one menstrual period.

If it is said:
“We may instead hold that a woman separated by khulʿ must wait for one menstrual period, whereas a woman in an invalid marriage must wait for three cycles.”

Then anyone adopting this position must explain the distinction between the two cases.

If it is objected:
“ʿUmar and ʿAlī differed over whether she becomes lawful for the second man to marry. ʿUmar said, ‘He may never marry her,’ whereas ʿAlī said, ‘Once her waiting period has ended—meaning the waiting period arising from the second man—she may marry if she wishes, or refrain from marrying if she wishes.’ If intercourse with the second man were like intercourse under a mistaken belief of lawfulness, it would not prevent the first man from marrying her. For if a man’s wife has intercourse under a mistaken belief of lawfulness, his marriage does not thereby end, by scholarly consensus. Rather, he must refrain from intercourse with her until she completes her waiting period. Likewise, if a revocably divorced woman has intercourse under a mistaken belief of lawfulness, nothing extinguishes her husband’s right?”

The reply is:
First, this objection has no bearing on the length of the waiting period. Whether it consists of establishing that the womb is clear by waiting for one menstrual period or of waiting for three cycles, the objection arises in either case.

There is no doubt that if the husband who divorced her, and on account of whose intercourse she observes a waiting period, has divorced her for the third time, she has become unlawful for him until she marries another husband. He therefore cannot take her back during her waiting period following divorce from him. But if he has separated from her irrevocably, as in khulʿ, and she marries during her waiting period following separation from him—for example, before her womb has been established to be clear by one menstrual period—then, if he wishes to marry her during her waiting period, he may do so only under a marriage contract

---

## Page 15952

that is newly concluded; but he may not marry her under a new contract while she is observing a waiting period on account of another man. A waiting period on account of another man prevents the initiation of a marriage, but not its continuation. No one may marry a woman observing such a waiting period, whether it results from intercourse under a mistaken belief of lawfulness, an invalid marriage, or even unlawful intercourse. Yet if his wife has intercourse under a mistaken belief of lawfulness or engages in unlawful intercourse, his marriage is not invalidated. Rather, he must refrain from intercourse with her until he establishes that her womb is clear, after which he may resume intercourse with her.

If it is said:
“But this woman is observing a waiting period because of intercourse. Why, then, is he prevented from marrying her during that waiting period?”

The reply is:
First, this has no bearing on the length of the waiting period. Second, neither a revealed text nor scholarly consensus permits every woman observing a waiting period to marry during it. Rather, consensus has been established concerning cases such as a woman separated by khulʿ, since she owes no waiting period on account of anyone other than the man who would marry her.

If, however, she must observe a waiting period on account of another man, then the impediment here is precisely that she is observing a waiting period on account of someone else. This would likewise prevent him from marrying her after her waiting period on his account had ended. He may marry her when she is free of her waiting period, but not when she is observing one on account of another man. Thus, the waiting period neither bars the marriage nor renders it lawful. The absence of one impediment does not make something permissible when another impediment exists. Someone may nevertheless suppose that the waiting period on his account was prescribed to make his marriage contract lawful. This is mistaken.

If, however, the first divorce was revocable, his taking her back during the remainder of her waiting period on his account is just like his taking her back if she had intercourse under a mistaken belief of lawfulness during her waiting period following a revocable divorce. There is no difference between the two cases.

---

## Page 15953

Alī likewise ruled that the second man may not marry her until her waiting period on his account has ended. This is also the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. According to al-Shāfiʿī, however, the second man may marry her during her waiting period on his account, just as a man who has intercourse with a woman under a mistaken belief of lawfulness may marry her during her waiting period on his account. Likewise, anyone who has contracted an invalid marriage with a woman may marry her during her waiting period on his account. There are two reported views from Ahmad on this underlying issue.

“The first” is that this is not permissible. This is Mālik’s position, intended to distinguish semen deposited through intercourse under a mistaken belief of lawfulness from semen deposited through entirely lawful intercourse.

“The second” is that it is permissible, as al-Shāfiʿī holds, because paternity is established in both cases. On the basis of this reported view, some of Ahmad’s followers permitted the second man to marry her during her waiting period on his account, as al-Shāfiʿī holds, just as this reported view permits marriage to any woman observing a waiting period arising from an invalid marriage.

Others disputed his explicit ruling and said concerning this case:

He used to mention in her case a waiting period on account of the first intercourse. She did not begin observing her waiting period on account of this second man immediately after he separated from her. Rather, the waiting period on account of the first man intervened between the second man’s separation from her and her waiting period on his account. She was obliged to observe two waiting periods on account of the two men. The first man’s waiting period took precedence only because his right arose earlier. Otherwise, had she given birth to a child whose paternity was attributed to the second man, the second man’s waiting period would have preceded that of the first. Thus, during her waiting period on account of the first man, the second man still has a right over her; and while she is observing the waiting period

---

## Page 15954

on account of the second man, the first man still has a right over her. The evidence is that, if she gave birth after completing her waiting period on account of the first man, and either man could have fathered the child, the child would be presented to experts in identifying family resemblance. Since the first man retains a right during her waiting period on account of the second, the second may not marry her during that waiting period. This is the best-known position on this question. It is transmitted from the Companions, is Ahmad’s explicit ruling, and is followed by most of his adherents. My grandfather—may Allah have mercy on him—also followed it in his al-Muharrar.

As for the length of the waiting period, we have mentioned two views reported from Ahmad concerning a woman whose marriage has ended through khulʿ (divorce in exchange for compensation). If there is no legally recognized distinction between her and a woman in an invalid marriage—otherwise, it must be said concerning a woman in an invalid marriage: she need only observe one menstrual period, as established by the Sunnah. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a man who quarrelled with his wife, with a single divorce involved in their marriage. She said to him, “Divorce me.”

He replied, “If you release me from your claims, you are divorced.”

She said, “May Allah release you from the claims women make against men.”

He then said to her, “You are divorced,” believing that he was thereby released from his obligations. He follows the Shāfiʿī school.

He answered:

Yes, he is released from the claims women make against men, provided she is legally competent.

---

## Page 15955

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man whose wife said to him:

“Divorce me, and I release you from all my claims against you. I shall take the girl and provide for her; she shall be owed one hundred dirhams by you, at a rate of one-sixth of a dirham each day.”

Upright witnesses attested to this, and he divorced her on that basis, under the terms of the release or guarantee. May she subsequently demand an assessed maintenance allowance for the girl, or not?

He answered:

If he grants her a khulʿ on condition that she release him from her claims, take the child into her care and undertake his support, and not demand maintenance from him, this is valid according to the great majority of scholars, including Mālik, Ahmad—according to the well-known position in his school—and others. According to the majority, khulʿ is valid in exchange for something that does not yet exist but is expected to come into existence, such as the offspring her slave woman may bear and the produce her trees may yield. As for maintenance during her pregnancy, nursing her child, and his maintenance, the basis for their arising and permissibility has already been established.

The same applies if she says to him: “Divorce me; I release you from my claims, I shall take the child and undertake his support, and I release you from his maintenance,” or uses similar wording that conveys the intended meaning.

If someone who considers this kind of khulʿ valid—such as a Mālikī judge—effects the khulʿ between them on these terms, no other judge may overturn it, even if he considers it invalid; nor may he award the child

---

## Page 15956

maintenance payable by him after this. If the first judge acts in this way, he has thereby issued a judicial ruling, according to the sounder of the two scholarly views.

Whenever a judge concludes a contract or effects an annulment in a matter open to independent juristic judgment, no other judge may overturn his decision.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a man who said to his father-in-law:

“If you bring me my document and release me from the obligations it records, your daughter is divorced three times.”

The father brought him a document other than his own, and the husband tore it up without knowing whether it was his document or not. The wife’s father then said:

“Bear witness concerning him that my daughter is under my guardianship, and bear witness that I have released him from the obligations recorded in her document.”

He did not specify what the document contained. He then waited a while, and the wife’s father came in the presence of the witnesses and asked him:

“What did you say, husband?”

The husband replied: “Bear witness that this man’s daughter is divorced three times.”

The husband subsequently claimed that this explicit pronouncement of divorce had been based on the assumption that the earlier release was valid. Does the divorce take effect or not?

He answered:

His first statement was conditional upon being released from his obligations. If the father did not release him, the divorce did not take effect. His second statement, however, was an acknowledgment based on the assumption that the first divorce had taken effect. If the first divorce had not taken effect, then the second statement did not bring about any divorce.

---

## Page 15957

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man’s father-in-law swore that he would not let his daughter remain with her husband. He beat her and said, “Release him from his financial obligations.” She released him, and her husband divorced her once. She then claimed that she had released him only out of fear of her father. Does this divorce take effect or not?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah. If she released him under wrongful coercion, the release was invalid, and the divorce conditional upon it did not take effect. But if she was under her father’s legal guardianship and he considered this to be in her interests, then this is permissible according to one of the two scholarly views, as in Mālik’s school and one opinion within Ahmad’s school.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A married orphan girl under legal guardianship was told by her husband, “If you release me from the obligation to pay your dowry, you are divorced three times.” Because of the severe beating and her terror, she gave it to him. She then changed her mind and regretted it. May she revoke the gift without his being considered to have broken his oath, or not?

He answered:

If he coerced her into making the gift, or if she was under legal guardianship, the gift was invalid and the divorce did not take effect. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15958

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man provided his wife with costly adornments, such as worked gold and silver, jewellery, necklaces and similar items, beyond the value of her clothing provision. She requested a divorce in exchange for compensation, while he owed her a considerable sum. He asked her for some jewellery to help him meet his obligations to her or other expenses, but she denied it. He knows that she will swear an oath and retain the items he says are in her possession, while he remains liable for the price. He has no evidence against her. What is the ruling?

He answered:

If he gave her these items beyond what he was obliged to provide, intending to transfer ownership to her, they became her property. If he then initiates the divorce, he has no right to demand them back.

However, if she is the one who dislikes remaining with him and seeks a divorce in exchange for compensation, she should return those items and the dowry he paid her, and relinquish the balance he still owes her, so that he may grant her that divorce. This accords with the established Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in the case of {the wife of Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās, whom he instructed to return what her husband had given her}.

But if he provided these things for her to adorn herself with—just as he might let her ride his animal or place his slave at her service, and the like—without transferring ownership of the items themselves, they remain his property, and he is entitled to reclaim them

---

## Page 15959

whenever he wishes, whether he divorces her or not. If they dispute whether he gave them to her as her property or merely permitted her to use them, and no established custom settles the matter, his word is accepted upon his swearing that he did not transfer ownership to her. If they dispute whether he gave her anything at all, and there is no evidence upon which judgment may be given in his favour—neither a single witness, nor an admission, nor anything else—her word is accepted upon her swearing that he gave her nothing.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man sold some of his belongings, and his wife quarrelled with him because he had sold them. Ill feeling arose between them over this. While they were quarrelling, some of her relatives arrived, and the man told those present: “If this woman will not behave like other people, I shall leave her and marry someone else.”

He then said: “If you hand your document over to this man for me, you are divorced three times.” His intention was that she should release him from liability. She became angry and gave the document to the man. Does the divorce take effect or not?

He answered:

If he intended the document to be handed over as a release from liability, but she merely handed it over without releasing him from the obligation it recorded, no divorce takes effect thereby.

And if he says, “I intended the handing over in that sense”—for his only purpose is release from liability, and surrendering the dowry document prevents a claim based upon it, whereas merely depositing it for safekeeping serves no purpose for him. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15960

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man who follows the Mālikī school had a dispute with his wife’s father and appeared before the judge. The husband said to his wife’s father, “If your daughter releases me from liability, I shall divorce her.” Her father replied, “I release you.”

The husband and his wife’s father then appeared before some jurists, and her father released him from liability without her presence or permission. Does the divorce take effect or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah.

The underlying issue is disputed among the scholars. The position of Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, according to their well-known, explicitly transmitted rulings, is that a father has no authority to secure a divorce for his daughter in exchange for any of her property, whether or not she is legally restricted from managing it. This is because doing so constitutes a gratuitous disposal of her property, which he has no authority to undertake, just as he has no authority to waive other debts owed to her.

Mālik’s position is that he may secure a divorce in exchange for compensation on behalf of his minor daughter, whether she is a virgin or not, because he has guardianship over her property. It is also reported from him that a father may do so on behalf of his virgin daughter without restriction, because he has the authority to compel her to marry.

It is further reported from him that a father may secure such a divorce on behalf of his daughter without restriction, just as he may marry her off for less than

---

## Page 15961

the dowry customary for a woman of her standing, when this serves her interests. Some Shāfiʿī jurists have explicitly advanced a view within his school that, in the case of a minor virgin daughter, her father may secure her divorce by waiving half her dowry, if we hold that the person in whose hand is the marriage bond is the guardian. Others have judged this view mistaken, because he has authority to waive it only after divorce. Yet if he has authority to waive her entitlement after divorce without any benefit to her, then doing so for her benefit—by securing her release from her husband—is all the more permissible. Hence, all these scholars permit him to secure her release from her husband in exchange for some of his own property. Likewise, she may obtain a divorce from him in exchange for her own property if the husband guarantees that. If, then, he may secure her release, and no harm remains to her other than the forfeiture of half her dowry…

Mālik’s position can be derived from Ahmad’s principles in several ways. One is that, according to one of the two reported positions, a father may pronounce a divorce or arrange a compensated divorce on behalf of his young son, as groups among the early generations also held. Mālik permits compensated divorce, but not divorce without compensation, because compensated divorce involves an exchange.

Ahmad holds that the father may pronounce a divorce on his son’s behalf because this may serve the son’s interests by freeing him from his wife’s claims and the harm she causes him. Likewise, in waiving his rights, there is no distinction between financial and nonfinancial rights.

Furthermore, according to one of the two reported positions, an arbitrator in a marital dispute may arrange the wife’s release in exchange for some of her property without her permission, and pronounce a divorce on the husband’s behalf without his permission, as is the position of

---

## Page 15962

Mālik and others. Likewise, he permits a father to marry his daughter off for less than the dowry customary for a woman of her standing. According to one of the two positions reported from him, the father holds the marriage bond in his hand and may waive half the dowry.

His position is that a father may take ownership of his child’s property insofar as this does not harm the child. Thus, if he marries his daughter off and stipulates that he himself receive part of the dowry, this is permissible. Given this authority to dispose of property and take ownership of it, all that remains is his seeking the dissolution of her marriage, and he has the right to do so by the consensus of the Muslims. He also permits a father to partially emancipate a slave owned by the person under his guardianship when this serves a beneficial purpose.

It may therefore be said: the stronger view is that, if the woman is under her father’s guardianship, he may arrange a compensated divorce as an exchange and a means of ransoming her from her husband. The father has this authority just as he has authority to conduct other exchanges and to ransom her from captivity. He may do so, however, only when it serves her interests.

It may also be said: divorce may not be in her interests, but the husband has the right to divorce her, and the father cannot prevent him. If someone other than her offers the husband compensation, she cannot prevent that person from offering it. Waiving her dowry and the rights to which marriage entitles her, however, may harm her. Moreover, the father’s purpose in securing her release may be to serve his own interests rather than hers, and there is agreement that he has no authority to waive her rights merely for his own benefit. According to those who uphold the waiver, both the waiver and the divorce take effect. According to those who do not permit the father to make such a waiver, if he guarantees it, the divorce takes effect without dispute, and the father owes the husband

---

## Page 15963

an amount equal to the dowry, according to Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, Ahmad, and al-Shāfiʿī’s earlier position. According to al-Shāfiʿī’s later position, he owes only the dowry customary for a woman of her standing.

If, however, the father did not guarantee it, and the husband made the divorce conditional upon being released from liability, saying to him, “If you release me from liability, she is divorced,” Ahmad’s explicit position is that the divorce takes effect if the husband believed that he would thereby be released. The husband may then recover an amount equal to the dowry from the father, because the father misled him. This is also one of the two positions transmitted within Abū Hanīfa’s school. According to the other, no divorce takes effect. This is al-Shāfiʿī’s position and also one position within Ahmad’s school, because the husband was not, in fact, released from liability.

Those who hold the first view say: the waiver took place, and the father can be held liable on the basis of that waiver. If, however, the husband pronounced a divorce without making it conditional upon the waiver, the divorce takes effect. According to Ahmad, however, the father is liable to the husband for the dowry because he misled him. According to al-Shāfiʿī, the father owes him nothing, because he was not bound by any obligation. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman’s husband divorced her three times, and she released him from his marital obligations before learning that she was pregnant. When the pregnancy became apparent, she demanded that he provide an allowance for the pregnancy. Is she entitled to do so?

He answered:

If the circumstances are as described, maintenance for the pregnancy was not included in the waiver, and she is entitled to claim it. Even if she knew of the pregnancy and released him solely from his marital obligations, this would not

---

## Page 15964

include maintenance for the pregnancy, since it is due after the marriage has ended. According to the stronger of the two scholarly views, it is owed to the unborn child, like payment for breastfeeding. According to the other view, it is owed to the wife on account of the pregnancy and thus falls under spousal maintenance. The correct view, however, is that it falls under maintenance for relatives, like payment for breastfeeding.

The exception is where the waiver was intended to ensure that no claims would ever remain between them after the marriage. If that was the case, and they intended a mutual release such that no claim of any kind would remain for the Hereafter, then this includes waiving maintenance for the pregnancy.

> **Footnotes:**
> End of Volume Thirty-Two.
